## INTRODUCTION {#cuaderno.8.0}

Our life in the Work is a long journey that lasts all the days of our earthly life. It is a journey which began in love, which is nourished by love, and which will find its fulfilment, if we are faithful, in Love without end. One idea that describes very well the whole purpose of our existence is that we are wayfarers on a journey towards God. Through our self-surrender to God in Opus Dei each of us has responded with a loving commitment to that divine call: come, follow me.1 Since then, though nothing has changed externally, inwardly we find everything bathed in a new light and a new melody. Our daily work, our relationships – family, friends, social and professional – have all become, thanks to Christ’s imperative command, our path to holiness. As our beloved Father often taught us, it all becomes sanctified, and it, in turn, sanctifies us. But along this road the scenery varies considerably, all the more so when the journey is a long one: as well as dreamlike landscapes full of colour, there are others which are arid and rocky. With the passage of time it is not uncommon for one’s initial enthusiasm to wane somewhat; then, tiredness begins to make itself felt, the sunny days are interspersed with days of mist and fog, the weather turns cold, or perhaps everything becomes covered with snow. Something similar can happen on our journey to God. It is part of the wayfarer’s lot, and so we ought to be ready for it when it happens.

1 Mt 19:21

Along the Path to Love 10

The wrong thing to do in these circumstances would be to slacken our pace or, worse still, to come to a halt altogether and cast aside all that divine grace, all those hours of sacrifice and of effort, of fidelity and of cheerfulness, precisely when we are much closer to the end than when we started. To help us travel gracefully along the path to God, without shortening our stride when the going gets difficult, this volume of the Notebook series offers the reader a compendium of articles from our internal publications, all of them impregnated with our holy Founder’s spirit and words. Through his heroic fidelity to God’s will, he has opened for us and for many other souls a divine pathway through the world of human endeavour, and, by travelling it himself faithfully to the end, has left us a shining example to guide our steps. We have recourse to his intercession, praying that his daughters and sons in Opus Dei, and all those other people who are nourished by that same spirit, may travel to the end along this path which leads to Love, bringing with us a great multitude of souls. Cor Mariae dulcissimum, iter serva tutum!

## THE ONE THING NECESSARY {#cuaderno.8.1}

We are here together in Christ’s sheepfold for a very specific reason. Haec est enim voluntas Dei, sanctificatio vestra: this is the will of God, your sanctification (1 Thess 4:3). We are here to become saints. Our vocation is a vocation to holiness: not any holiness whatever, not a generic sort of holiness, not even an outstanding holiness, but heroic holiness. I am not using the word ‘holiness’ here in its strictly theological meaning, our Father wrote, but in the commonly accepted sense whereby people say about someone who appears to be exemplary: ‘He’s a saint!’ Heroic holiness: that’s what our calling implies. We have to be really authentic saints; otherwise we have failed.1 We must never lose sight of the fact that only one thing is necessary:2 holiness. Seek first his kingdom and his righteousness, our Lord has told us, and all these things shall be yours as well.3 The first and most essential thing of all, the one that cannot take second place to anything else, is the quest for holiness. This calling, as we well know, entails constant, cheerful application, and a sporting attitude to life. In calling us to the Work, God our Lord wants us to fight to become saints ... But don’t get distracted by

1 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 9 2 Lk 10:42 3 Mt 6:33

Along the Path to Love 12

thoughts of great heroics. Don’t look beyond the reality of each day, eagerly seeking perfection in your ordinary work. That’s where God awaits us. Every day we have a chance to respond affirmatively. And that affirmation must indeed be heroic, trying to surpass ourselves, without setting limits.4

The desire for holiness No difficulty can bridle our zeal. It is understandable that we may sometimes feel weighed down by trouble or fatigue, such poor stuff are we made of.5 At times our objective seems to fade into the horizon, and the divine brightness that shone when we started off seems to decline entirely. But when that happens it is also logical that we would turn to our Lord, in the intimacy of our prayer and in the fraternal talk, asking him confidently to make us see clearly that idea our Father told us: only one thing is necessary (Lk 10:42), personal holiness. Moreover, in that daily struggle of ours, which is ordinarily made up of many little things, when we really desire to please God and manage to do so to some small extent, I assure you, my children, that nothing can go wrong. All that toil, no matter how hidden or insignificant it may be, acquires the power of God’s own life.6 In spite of all the difficulties we may encounter, be they internal or external, be they weariness or discouragement, when things work out or when they don’t, we mustn’t forget that God has said to everyone: Be holy, for I am holy.7 If we keep this eagerness alive at all times, then no obstacle can block our way. “Christ has become for us the way; can we lose the hope of our destination? This road cannot come to a dead end

4 From our Father, Get-together, 6 October 1968 5 From our Father, Meditation, 6 April 1965 6 From our Father, Get-together, 6 October 1968 7 Lev 11:44 13 The one thing necessary

nor can it be blocked; neither can rains or floods wash it away, nor can it be ambushed by bandits. Walk on, safely in Christ, walk on. Don’t stumble, don’t fall, don’t look back, don’t tarry along the way, and don’t leave it. As long as this remains your aim, your arrival is assured.”8 We can surmount difficulties just as surely as clear mountain streams penetrate all resistance: inter medium montium pertransibunt aquae:9 the waters shall pass through the middle of the mountains. When there is interior life, the apparent obstacles are no problem: nothing can restrain a soul seeking only to fulfil God’s will. My children, have a lot of interior life; then everything turns out well, and is seen to be God’s work.10

Holiness: the means Since Christ first called mankind to a life of Christian perfection – you, therefore, must be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect11 – the world has witnessed an almost incredible growth in culture and progress:12 science and technology have transformed the material conditions of society and made life more bearable. Yet the means to holiness are the same as they have always been, the ones Jesus himself left us: as Christians we don’t have any others at all.13 The reason is that in the spiritual life there is nothing left to be invented; all we have to do is fight to identify ourselves with Christ, to be other Christs – ipse Christus – to fall in love with Christ and to live in him who is the same yesterday and today and forever: Iesus Christus heri et hodie,

8 St Augustine, Sermon 170, 11 9 Cf Ps 104:10 10 From our Father, Get-together, 6 October 1968 11 Mt 5:48 12 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 6 13 From our Father, Meditation, 25 December 1968

Along the Path to Love 14

ipse et in saecula (Heb 13:8). So do you see why I tell you so often, over and over again, that I have no other prescription to offer you than this: personal holiness? There is no other, my children, no other.14 To help us reach this goal, our Lord gives us his grace and has left us the sacraments. “The followers of Christ, called by God not in virtue of their works but by his design and grace, and justified in the Lord Jesus, have been made sons of God in the baptism of faith and partakers of the divine nature, and so are truly sanctified. They must therefore hold on to and perfect in their lives that sanctification which they have received from God.”15 My children, our Father comments, baptism makes us ‘fideles’, faithful; along with that other word ‘saints’, the first followers of Christ used it to refer to one another, as we still do in speaking of the Church’s faithful. After baptism, the other sacraments, which are signs instituted by Christ, the footsteps or traces of his divine passage through the world, give us sanctifying grace ex opere operato, by their own power, if we don’t hinder their action; and if we are already in the state of sanctifying grace, they increase it further. They also give us sacramental grace, a divine assistance that is proper to and characteristic of each sacrament. If we don’t hinder their action, I said. That is, for the sacraments to be truly effective the recipient needs to be well disposed. And what does one need in order to be well disposed? I’ll tell you very clearly: good works, deeds of one’s own. Holiness is not something communal; if someone says it is, it’s not true. Holiness is a personal encounter of the soul with God, and every soul has to put everything it can into it: good works, personal effort. Let me remind you of those words of the epistle of St James: sic et fides, si non habeat opera, mortua est in

14 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 6 15 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 40 15 The one thing necessary

semetipsa (Jas 2:17). So faith, if not accompanied by deed, is dead. It’s useless ... ! Deeds! Deeds! Deeds! Deeds! The personal effort to respond to grace!16 If we couldn’t produce any evidence of specific acts of service for our brothers and sisters and for all mankind, or of a spirit of prayer and penance, there would be reason to doubt the genuineness of our desire for holiness.

Fulfilling the Norms lovingly We have to be really convinced that holiness is the one thing necessary. But the conviction alone isn’t enough: we need to make use of the means to get there. How can we be saints? By being consistent with our Christian vocation, and in particular, as Christians in Opus Dei, which means we are Christians who have experienced a specific call from God, a call that does not take us out of our place, uproot us from our surroundings, or remove us from the world, from our state in life or from our work situation. And once we heard that call, we answered our Lord for the most supernatural of reasons: ‘because we wanted to’

– ecce ego quia vocasti me (1 Sam 3:9), here I am, Lord, for you have called me ... And how are we supposed to achieve the holiness proper to Opus Dei? How can we attain the goal? ... The way is to fulfil our Norms, for that is a sign of strong faith, of certain hope, and of burning, sacrificing love.17 But it is not enough just to ‘fulfil’ the Norms as if it were a routine chore. We have to do them lovingly and tenderly, as carefully as we can, for in them we meet God, who gives himself to us totally yet in exchange asks for our whole life, our whole heart. I am always telling

16 From our Father, Meditation, 25 December 1968 17 Ibid.

Along the Path to Love 16

you this: our Lord is a jealous lover who wants all our love, everything we have. He wants us to seek him out, in the Bread and in the Word: in the Eucharist, so badly treated these days, and in prayer. We must unite ourselves intimately with this God who submits himself to us, who gives himself to be our food. Let us tell him passionately: Lord, I love you, I’m crazy about you. This love causes us to be filled with confidence; seeing our misdeeds, we say to him with the humble simplicity of the leper in the Gospel (cf Mt 8:2): ‘Make me more faithful’. Cleanse me that I may follow you more closely, that I may walk by your side, that I may never leave you.18 We have to rejuvenate that call to holiness, feeling it always present and alive in our souls. Our vocation is not an isolated event that once happened to us. Our meeting with Christ was not a chance encounter, lost now in the dimness of time. Jesus is constantly knocking at the door of our heart: behold, I stand at the door and knock,19 he keeps telling us. God has captured our heart, our whole life. One day, because of his infinite goodness, we felt the heavenly dart that conquered us forever. And we must see to it that this love endures, and becomes more intense and purified every day.20 We shouldn’t presume that we have already travelled far enough, “we shouldn’t linger at whatever point we may have reached; rather must we use it to go further. You see that we are travellers and you say: what does it mean to travel? I will answer very briefly: to travel is to make progress ... Examine yourself and never be content with what you are, if you want to become what you still are not. Wherever you felt satisfied, that was where you stopped. If you ever say: ‘That’s enough,’ you’ll be

18 From our Father, Get-together, 18 August 1968 19 Rev 3:20 20 From our Father, Get-together, 18 August 1968 17 The one thing necessary

finished. Increase always, make progress always, advance always.”21 This was always our Father’s teaching. In our heart we discover struggles, victories and defeats, feelings that drag us down and inspirations pushing us up again. So you see, my children, how by using the means available, we are to travel on the unconfined road of God’s children in his Opus Dei ... Do you know what happens to a road that’s never used? There are some people here from Brazil. You probably know that I once heard how, somewhere in your country, a road had been made with all the latest techniques, but was eventually obliterated again by the jungle. If there had been a lot of traffic on it – nowadays perhaps there would be

– then the jungle wouldn’t have triumphed ... Each person’s pathway is very personal: within the spirit and the general direction the Work provides, we each have our own way. If you don’t walk upon it every day, if you don’t make the effort to fulfil the Norms all along the way, the track will begin to disappear and you’ll end up not knowing where to go. I would like our resolution today to be the following: when you are each doing your prayer, whenever it happens to be – today, tomorrow, later on – think about that personality of yours that the Work not only respects, but wants to develop more and more. And then, think too about that commitment, also a very personal one, that you have before God – I have it too, just like you – to make sure our track doesn’t disappear. This will help us to ensure that not only will we not get lost individually, but that the road itself will be kept nice and flat, and will remain unobstructed and in good condition: the jungle won’t win. I also want you to draw a lesson from this: none of you, no more than myself – I’m just one more – nobody in Opus Dei can opt out of keeping the way clear. And to keep it clear it has to be walked on, it

21 St Augustine, Sermon 169, 18

Along the Path to Love 18

has to be used. We all have the same duty, the same obligation.22

Holiness is urgently needed The Church today needs our holiness and the holiness of all Christians very badly indeed. She expects us to tell “the society around us, people near and far, that we believe in Christ and that we want to follow him; that in following him we do not walk blindly, in the darkness, but in the light of his word, his example, and his grace.”23 At a time when interior life and holiness seem to have been forgotten about, when men speak only of action, statistics and techniques, it is our mission to remind them that holiness is the one thing necessary. You ought to bear in mind that heroism, holiness and daring require constant spiritual preparation. We can’t give what we haven’t got: if we are to give God, we have to be close to him, live his life, and give ourselves to him.24 Here is one more reason to get us to renew our desire for holiness. No one in Opus Dei can rest easy, feeling no concern for the faceless masses: I once described them to you as a flock, a drove, a herd. But there are so many noble feelings, so many possibilities, hiding under that apparent indifference! We have to serve them all, laying our hands on each one, as Jesus did – singulis manus imponens (Lk 4:40)

– bringing them back to life, curing them, enlightening their minds and strengthening their wills; in short, making them useful. Then we will turn the flock into a legion, the drove into an army, and from the herd we will draw, purified, those who no longer wish to be unclean.25 With holiness absent from the lives of so many lukewarm Christians, these words of the Apocalypse resound with new vigour:

22 From our Father, Get-together, 6 January 1971 23 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 19 March 1967 24 From our Father, Get-together, 6 October 1968 25 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 9 19 The one thing necessary

Let the evildoer still do evil, and the filthy still be filthy, and the righteous man still do right, and the holy still be holy. Behold, I am coming soon, bringing my recompense, to repay every one for what he has done.26 We need to be very holy, because we have to drown in an abundance of good the wicked seed that flourishes in so many places: where sin increased, grace abounded all the more.27 The best way to do so is to redouble the effort to improve our interior life. Show God your gratitude for being called, our Father tells us, by always trying harder to improve. I think the time in which we are now living is a great opportunity for holiness within the world, because, although all this present confusion is leading many souls astray, he has given us a very clear vision of things and a great many resources to bring him all over the world once more. It is, then, a time for holiness; in other words, each of us must resolve to respond faithfully to that divine call.28

Renewing our love It is worth making the effort to revitalize the interior life, to renew once and again that decision we one day made to follow Christ forever. Never tire of loving, no matter what happens. Our life on earth is a bad night in a bad inn, but everything unpleasant eventually comes to an end. What endures is God’s love.29 There is only one difficulty that can prevent us reaching the goal to which God calls us, and our Father tells us what it is: Along the way – yours and mine – I see only one difficulty, appearing in different forms, which we have to fight against constantly ... that difficulty is the danger of becoming bourgeois, either in professional life or in the spiritual life: the danger

26 Rev 22:11-12 27 Rom 5:20 28 From our Father, Get-together, 6 October 1968 29 Ibid.

Along the Path to Love 20

of feeling like bachelors or old maids, lonely people without love.30 That bourgeois mentality – forgetting that we are in love and that God’s Love has to be our whole reason for being – could foil our desire for holiness and apostolate, as well as causing us to be unhappy here on earth and possibly also in the next life. Consequently, if we should ever detect its symptoms, we should react immediately and reject vigorously anything that might separate us from God forever. The plain fact is that there is no alternative. On earth there are two ways to live: either supernaturally or like an animal. And you, son of God in the Work, can live no other life but God’s life, the supernatural life. Quid prodest? (Mt 16:26). What does it profit someone to have all the goods of the earth, all the extravagances of a satiated heart, all the aspirations of mind and will? What is it worth if it all comes to an end, if it all crumbles away, if everything in this world is mere stage scenery, if afterwards there is eternity forever, forever, forever? My son, this adverb ‘forever’ made Teresa of Avila great. Once, as a child, accompanied by her brother Rodrigo, she left the walls of her city through the Adaja gate, bound for the land of the Moors in order to be beheaded for Christ; when her brother got tired she took him in her arms and carried him on her shoulders saying: Forever, forever, forever ... 31 Perhaps at times we too have to do the same thing: to fix our gaze on heaven and live by faith and hope. That is the moment to ponder those words of Christ in the Gospel as he invites us to feel responsible for our own salvation: for what will it profit a man, if he gains the whole world and forfeits his soul?32 Our Founder’s advice to think about eternity is meant for just such occasions. My son, I too consider quite often that if here on earth love can give so much joy, what will it

30 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 84 31 From our Father, Meditation, 12 October 1947 32 Mt 16:26 21 The one thing necessary

be like in heaven when all God’s splendour, all his wisdom, all his beauty, when all that vivacity, all that colour and all that harmony, cascades into this little clay vessel which we are? It is indeed worth while! It is worth while saying ‘No’ to earthly loves for the sake of Love. I like to put it another way too, positively: ‘because I want to’: I want to be faithful to the love of Christ.33

Personally responsible Porro unum est necessarium! (Lk 10:42), our Lord has said. Only one thing is necessary: personal holiness. This is the secret of the joy that we are passing on to the world, of the peace that we are sowing in all walks of life.34 Don’t forget, my children, that holiness brings happiness on earth, even in the midst of all kinds of difficulties. Quite a number of your brothers and sisters have had a vivid experience of that. Why? As I am constantly telling you, if you want to be happy, be holy; if you want to be happier, be holier; if you want to be very happy, be very holy.35 We cannot waste even a second of this short life; it is so short it goes by while we are waiting for eternal life. Hence all flesh is like grass and all its glory like the flower of grass. The grass withers, and the flower falls, but the word of the Lord abides forever (1 Pet 1:24-25). Consider too that statutum est hominibus semel mori (Heb 9:27), men die only once: some, in infancy; others, as young people like yourselves, or in full maturity, or when they have reached old age. Time is so short we can’t fritter it away: we must really persist in the business of our sanctification and apostolic endeavour.36

33 From our Father, Get-together, 20 October 1968 34 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 9 35 From our Father, Meditation, 25 December 1968 36 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 43

Along the Path to Love 22

Mankind has a right to expect us to surpass ourselves in our vocation to holiness. We are obliged to seek the fulness of Christian life, to be saints. Otherwise we would cheat not only God, who has deigned to choose us for himself, but also all those people who expect so much from our apostolic efforts.37 Our Lord has selected us from among men – I chose you and appointed you that you should go and bear fruit38 – and has placed us in the midst of the multitude to be its leaven, salt, and light. But if salt has lost its taste, how shall its saltness be restored? It is no longer good for anything except to be thrown out and trodden under foot by men.39 We cannot cheat all those souls. Leaven, salt, light! That’s what you shall be, and that’s what I will be, if we don’t shrink from loving the Church with deeds! When our Lord reaches out to us, we have to make the effort – ‘because we want to’, as I said – to grasp that hand which comes to us from heaven.40 Porro unum est necessarium;41 only one thing is necessary: personal holiness. Beloved daughters and sons, our Father said many years ago, think about all the things our Lord, the Church and all mankind expect of Opus Dei, which is still just a tiny seed hidden in the furrow. Consider how wonderful your vocation is, and love it more and more every day. Resolve to be the instrument our Lord needs, optimistic, cheerful, and sensitive to the supernatural. On you go, my children: Jesus and the Church expect a lot of you. But keep one thing very clear in your mind and in your heart: we will get nowhere if we are not saints.42

37 Ibid., 57 38 Jn 15:16 39 Mt 5:13 40 From our Father, Meditation, 25 December 1968 41 Lk 10:42 42 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1943, 62

## ALONG THE WAY {#cuaderno.8.2}

“Recall with me, brethren,” writes St Augustine, “how the Lord has said, ‘While we are at home in the body we are away from the Lord, for we walk by faith, not by sight’ (2 Cor 5:6-7). And Christ Our Lord, who said, ‘I am the way, and the truth, and the life’ (Jn 14:6), willed us to walk not only by him, but towards him. Wherefore shall we walk, but by the Way? And whither are we headed, but to the Truth and the Life, that is, to eternal life, the only one which merits being called life?”1 Our life on earth is a pilgrimage towards the homeland Christ won for us by his death on the Cross. So we are always of good courage; we know that while we are at home in the body we are away from the Lord ... So whether we are at home or away, we make it our aim to please him.2 To direct all our senses and potencies towards God calls for unremitting effort, repeated acts of self-denial, continuous mortification, and constant prayer.

Difficulties along the way The difficulties we encounter in our journey towards our heavenly home are not insurmountable. They can be overcome if there is love, because, as St Augustine says, “the person who loves, runs; and the more intensely he loves, the faster he runs ... Let us run, then, by virtue

1 St Augustine, Sermon 346, 1 2 2 Cor 5:6, 9

Along the Path to Love 24

of love and charity, disregarding the things here below. On this road we cannot afford to be lazy, but strong, for on it abound the assaults of temptation. The devil lies in wait at the bends, trying by every means he can to arrest and gain mastery over the wayfarer. And once he succeeds in doing this, he leads his victim astray or detains him; he gets him to turn back, he hinders his advance, and even drags him off the road.”3 Among the more obvious difficulties we have to face, the first is the disordered love of self which forever accompanies us and causes us to appropriate for ourselves what really belongs to God. Our passions too are ready for the slightest opportunity to slip surreptitiously into everything we do. And we shouldn’t forget for a moment the presence and activity of the devil, who never ceases in his hatred of God and is forever looking for ways to make us come to grief. Be sober, be watchful, says St Peter. Your adversary the devil prowls around like a roaring lion, seeking some one to devour.4 Other events too, things like someone else’s bad example, can also be occasions for not keeping to the straight and narrow path. The shoals and obstacles of life, and the effort to avoid them, are, to a greater or lesser degree, normal everyday occurrences. Cross, toil, anguish: such will be your lot as long as you live. That was the way Christ went, and the disciple is not above his Master.5 The interior life is actually strengthened by hardship. God uses difficulties for chipping off the rough edges, shaping us as he desires, with blows of the hammer and chisel,6 to fashion his image in us. With God’s help the soul emerges from these trials humbled, purified and overflowing with love. Suns of heaven are needed and personal

3 St Augustine, Sermon 346 B, 1, 4 4 1 Pet 5:8 5 The Way, 699 6 Ibid., 756 25 Along the way

efforts, small and constant, to shake off those inclinations, those vain fancies, that depression: that mud clinging to your wings. And you will see yourself free. If you persevere, you will ‘rise’.7

The danger of discouragement A natural consequence of having to make a daily effort to persevere in our way is that at times we get tired, so it shouldn’t come as any surprise when it happens. On occasions it can be simply a question of physical exhaustion, which is nothing to get worried about, because as long as our heart is firmly fixed on God we can easily solve it just by being sincere and doing what we are told. It could happen, though, that it is not caused by something physical, but by a form of spiritual depression – discouragement. Discouragement, my children, is an enemy of perseverance. The soul can encounter moments of struggle, both at your age, and also in the middle years and even later – it happens to me too – moments when the soul feels moved to echo with St Paul that cry which for me is a great consolation: I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members. Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death? (Rom 7:23-24). One feels then the inroads of pride and sensuality and a longing, not for freedom, but for licentiousness. And if we are not forewarned, that is when discouragement makes its appearance.8 Discouragement is born when the love of God is allowed to evaporate by not replacing the energies spent in the daily struggle; because, just like the body, the interior life also needs to be fed. When Elijah, fleeing from Jezabel, had to walk for many days at a stretch, he

7 Ibid., 991 8 From our Father, Meditation, 4 March 1960

Along the Path to Love 26

eventually reached the stage that he had no energy left at all, and he collapsed exhausted, unable to take another step, saying: It is enough; now, O Lord, take away my life; for I am no better than my fathers. And he lay down and slept under a broom tree; and behold, an angel touched him, and said to him, ‘Arise and eat.’ And he looked, and behold, there was at his head a cake baked on hot stones and a jar of water. And he ate and drank, and lay down again. And the angel of the Lord came again a second time, and touched him, and said, ‘Arise and eat, else the journey will be too great for you.’ And he arose, and ate and drank, and went in the strength of that food forty days and forty nights to Horeb the mount of God.9 We too have to replace lost energies by making use of the means – the Norms and Customs – that enable us to keep going. If we didn’t, we shouldn’t be surprised to feel discouraged, because it would be a logical consequence of being spiritually run down. But it is not enough for the human body just to be fed, particularly in the case of small children: it also needs to get the right food, in the correct quantities, and at the proper time; and it has to be well absorbed into the bloodstream and pass from there to the various organs. The same happens in the spiritual life. It is not enough for us to do the Norms just to get them done. We have to fulfil them lovingly and considerately, at the right time, and ensuring that they don’t suffer because of distractions, disorder or poor dispositions.

Lukewarmness This undermining of the powers of the soul, if it is not put right in time, gives rise to lukewarmness, which St Thomas describes as “a form of sadness which causes man to be reluctant to perform spiritual

9 1 Kg 19:4-8 27 Along the way

acts because of the effort they require.”10 The soul feels unable to carry on the fight and, on seeing the apparent uselessness of its own efforts and its inability to overcome the obstacles once and for all, it tends to give way to despair. When the soul begins to fall into this condition, it ceases to identify with those words of the Apostle: Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation, or distress, or persecution, or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or sword? ... No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us.11 The soul then begins to recoil from anything unpleasant; it avoids the effort to do good because it sees it as something hard and difficult to achieve. It begins to make increasingly greater concessions to venial sin, until eventually, unless it reacts in time, it becomes quite indifferent to the things of the spirit. The soul that is apathetic and doesn’t want to do anything is in a bad way. It should at least want to do something. And if it doesn’t, it should want to want to, because that in itself is at least a beginning.12 The real enemy is an unwillingness to struggle, the loss of the desire for holiness, a deficient spiritual diet, and the unchallenged presence of harmful germs that habitually cause the soul to think, desire and act in a sickly and reluctant fashion. All this leads to an impoverishment of the love of God. This enemy of perseverance is called lukewarmness. Lukewarm individuals have hearts of clay, wretched hearts of flesh. From time to time I have encountered hard hearts – with apologies to the doctors: I was going to say that I actually touched them – hearts that are hard and cold, but noble; on coming into contact with the warmth of the Heart of Christ they dissolve like bronze into tears of love and atonement and are set alight! And there are other hearts

10 St Thomas, S. Th., I, q.63, a.2 ad 2 11 Rom 8:35-37 12 From our Father, Crónica, July 1961, pp.13-14

Along the Path to Love 28

which are made of clay: these crack and crumble into dust. Disgusting! My children! O Jesus! Keep lukewarmness well away from us! No to lukewarmness!13 Lukewarmness is not a consequence of a single fall, however great it may be, because when the soul is humble and repentant and truly sorry, then it rises from its falls quickly and with even greater strength than before. Lukewarmness arises when the soul abandons the struggle through neglecting the supernatural means: since it is not firmly seated and rooted in God, it tends to operate as if everything depended on its own strength. This can happen when, after the first steps in its long journey of self-surrender, it reaches a narrower stretch where perhaps its initial enthusiasm disappears; its spiritual practices begin to seem dry and arid, and it realizes that it is still very far from holiness. Then, if it doesn’t take advantage of the spiritual diet God provides through prayer and the sacraments, and doesn’t persevere in practices of mortification and good works, it can easily waver. In the supernatural life the same happens as in the case of “seeds of barley or wheat. When they are scattered on the ground they don’t begin to germinate immediately; only when the winter and the winds are over, do they in their proper time ripen into mature ears ... The same is true in spiritual affairs, which are so sensitive and profound. Man progresses little by little to the state of ‘mature manhood, to the measure of the stature of the fulness of Christ’ (Eph 4:13).”14

Symptoms of lukewarmness In order to fight against lukewarmness we have to be on our guard, and be able to recognize its causes and symptoms and how it can get hold of us.

13 From our Father, Meditation, 4 March 1960 14 Pseudo-Macarius, Homilies 15, 41 29 Along the way

The first cause is self-love. When the soul sees its faults and limitations, and how difficult it is to root them out, it can fall prey to a sense of despair and tend to give up trying. Lukewarmness can also come from a feverish form of activism whereby someone neglects the Norms or does them routinely and haphazardly, perhaps with the excuse that he has a very important and time-consuming apostolate to attend to. Lukewarmness has another ally too: a stupid little enemy, but very effective nonetheless, namely, a lack of diligence in examination. The examinations of conscience have been included among our Norms for a very good reason. If you ever get scrupulous about the examination you should try the following very short one. What have I done wrong? Forgive me, Lord. What have I done well? Thank you, Lord. What could I have done better? A resolution. Just two minutes, or half a minute. Then there is the particular examination, that specific area where you have been bringing the enemy to battle, just to make sure he doesn’t manage to choose the terrain to your disadvantage. Never omit it!15 Without a courageous and vigorous examination the soul ceases to notice small concessions and ends up being careless about little things: these are the little foxes that spoil the vineyards.16 The beginning of lukewarmness tends to be characterized by a habitual neglect of little mortifications, the appearance of disorder and routine in the plan of life, voluntary distractions in the Norms, resolutions unfulfilled through lack of real interest, and a general air of lack of effort which is expressed in the mechanical fulfilment of practices of piety. This initial state tends of itself to get worse, and so our Father reminds us: it hurts me to see the danger of lukewarmness in which you place yourself when you do not strive seriously for

15 From our Father, Meditation, 4 March 1960 16 Cant 2:15

Along the Path to Love 30

perfection in your state in life.17 Lukewarmness is like a slippery slope which causes the soul to slide further and further away from God. It arises from a desire not to strain oneself, to stay within comfortable limits, just enough not to fall into mortal sin, while not really fighting against venial sin. And so, little by little the soul acquires a completely mundane outlook, it doesn’t try to avoid occasions of sin, and time-wasting becomes second nature to it: a member of the Work who has time on his hands is quite clearly lukewarm; supernaturally speaking, he is a cripple.18 When a soul grows lukewarm it begins to regard the whole life of piety as an awkward imposition that prevents it from feeling relaxed, and it becomes saddened by not being able to enjoy forbidden satisfactions. This, if it doesn’t react vigorously, eventually leads to “indolence as regards the commandments, and the drifting of the mind towards unlawful things.”19 Practices of piety are carried out reluctantly or are omitted altogether, and zeal for winning apostles languishes. Not to be interested in winning apostles would be a symptom of wavering, of sickness and perhaps even of death.20 For a soul dedicated to God, if lukewarmness is not dealt with in time it eventually leads to the loss of vocation. The words of the Amen, the faithful and true witness, the beginning of God’s creation. I know your works: you are neither cold nor hot. Would that you were cold or hot! So, because you are lukewarm, and neither cold nor hot, I will spew you out of my mouth.21

17 The Way, 326 18 From our Father, Meditation, 9 January 1956 19 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 31, 9 20 From our Father, Crónica, May 1960, p.10 21 Rev 3:14-16 31 Along the way

Remedies for lukewarmness In the Work it is difficult for this sort of thing to happen. God has given us so many excellent means that, if we use them, we are certain to reach our journey’s end safely. The Norms, the Confidence, fraternal correction and the other means we have are all a safe shield, and also a trusty weapon to fight with. They maintain us in a state of joyful vigilance and deeply in love with God our Lord and his Blessed Mother. If, in spite of everything, it ever happened that a person were about to become lukewarm, the remedy is easily to hand: unmask it valiantly and have recourse to our Lord. Say with me: I don’t want to be lukewarm! Confige timore tuo carnes meas, pierce thou my flesh with thy fear: grant me, my God, a filial fear that will make me react.22 And have recourse to the Blessed Virgin with the filial confidence of children: love for our Mother will be the breath that kindles into a living flame the embers of virtue hidden beneath the ashes of your indifference.23 This is the moment to remember that we are pilgrims and need to cling tightly to God’s outstretched hand and keep going. “In effect, those who love, progress; however, one doesn’t progress towards God by walking, but through the affections. Our Way himself seeks out the wayfarers. But there are three classes of individuals whom he detests: the one who stops, the one who turns back, and the one who leaves the path. With God’s help, may our journey be free and immune from these three types of misfortune. It is quite a different matter that some wayfarers travel more slowly and others faster; both types, nevertheless, make progress. Those who halt have to be spurred on, those who turn back have to be turned around again, and those who leave the path have to be brought back to it once

22 The Way, 326 23 Ibid., 492

Along the Path to Love 32

more. The dawdlers have to be encouraged and the speedy imitated.”24 For us in the Work this continual progress along the path of holiness can be summed up in the effort to do the Norms lovingly. My children, you must always be on the lookout and stay alert. You are well able to teach those other souls who are with you in Opus Dei what being watchful means, because you know that we are vigilant by the mere fact of fulfilling our Norms. I’ve said from the beginning that I am utterly convinced of the salvation – the holiness – of anyone who fulfils the Norms of life of Opus Dei. And this is true even though at times an upright soul may have to go against the grain and feel that its life is a bit of a farce; it then has to make an act of faith and keep praying and working until the last stone of the day has been set in place. Don’t fall asleep! Because if you do, in the midst of good fortune evil will come upon you.25 If we are resting squarely on our Norms and Customs, centred on the Eucharist, and breathing a spirit of divine filiation and trust in God, then no difficulty can cause us to founder. And if we ever get tired, we can cope with it because we are children of God: and after you have suffered a little while, the God of all grace, who has called you to his eternal glory in Christ, will himself restore, establish, and strengthen you.26 Being truly contrite in Confession, being sincere in the fraternal talk, being humble enough not to conceal any expression of our abandonment, are irreplaceable and efficacious remedies for our condition, as well as being expressly willed by God; through them we get encouragement and counsel from the people qualified to give it, and the support of their prayer and sacrifice on our behalf. With God’s help, we also have to strive humbly to improve at

24 St Augustine, Sermon 306 B, 1 25 From our Father, Meditation, 4 February 1962 26 1 Pet 5:10 33 Along the way

praying and fight distractions, “because the more we meditate on spiritual things, the more they please us and the quicker do we lose the tedium that a superficial knowledge of them can provoke.”27 We also have to keep continually practising small mortifications, doing them for Love, and to rely on the help of others, because we never fight alone. Just by doing this much, for Love, we banish any possible danger of lukewarmness, because as long as ascetical struggle is present, there is interior life. And that is what God asks of us: the will to love him with deeds, with little deeds.28 When a soul truly longs for holiness and is humble enough to realize that by itself it can achieve nothing, it infallibly attracts God’s grace and wipes out any trace of lukewarmness. I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and humble spirit, to revive the spirit of the humble, and to revive the heart of the contrite ... I have seen his ways, but I will heal him; I will lead him and requite him with comfort, creating for his mourners the fruit of the lips. Peace, peace to the far and to the near,29 says the Lord. We must have confidence, therefore. The Lord is nigh, beside us, to help us, to comfort us, to stretch us out his hand when we fall. With this firm conviction, with the humility of little children who don’t trust in their own strength but in the merciful power of their Heavenly Father, we can overcome all obstacles. And at the end of the road we will find our Lord awaiting us with open arms to offer us all his Love. Behold, I stand at the door and knock; if any one hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in to him and eat with him, and he with me. He who conquers, I will grant him to sit with me on my throne.30

27 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.35, a.1 ad 4 28 From our Father, Crónica, December 1954, p.12 29 Is 57:15-19 30 Rev 3:20-21

## FORGETTING ABOUT SELF {#cuaderno.8.3}

If any man would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me. For whoever would save his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake will find it. For what will it profit a man, if he gains the whole world and forfeits his soul?1 Our Lord’s invitation is clear and compelling: Christ’s follower must be ready to give himself unconditionally. What Jesus asks of us is made easier by his wonderful promise. It is not sufficient, however, merely to give up certain material goods or benefits, or one’s family, or to be ready to do as much as possible to spread the saving influence of his message. Jesus asks for much more than that: he asks for everything. We must not keep anything for ourselves, not one thought, not one desire, which is not that of following him. We must give him our lives that we might live for God2 wherever he happens to place us.

Killing off our selfishness The decision to give ourselves completely and absolutely, was at the heart of the response that we each made to the call to follow Christ in Opus Dei. You do not come to the Work to seek anything. You come to give yourselves, to renounce any personal ambition for the love of

1 Mt 16:24-26 2 Gal 2:19 35 Forgetting about self

God. Everyone has to leave something if they want to be effective in the Work and work as God asks us to, like a faithful donkey, ut iumentum! The donkey’s only ambition is to serve, to be useful; and the only reward he looks for is the one God has promised: Quia tu reddes unicuique iuxta opera sua (Ps 61:13), for the Lord rewards each one according to his works. Dearly beloved daughters and sons, you are here in the Work because our Lord has placed in your hearts an unblemished and generous desire to serve, a true zeal that makes you willing to make any sacrifice, working away quietly for the Church without seeking any earthly compensation. Fill yourselves with these noble ambitions and strengthen this holy disposition in your hearts, because the work is immense.3 The dispositions of dedication and service our Founder asked us to foster are ultimately based on a humble attitude of forgetting about self. We have a distinctive spiritual life that is proper to us, and in some ways unique to us. And the spiritual life of the members of the Work has to give each of us a particular way of looking at things, one characteristic of which is to seek actively the holiness of others. We do not love God if we think only about our own holiness. We have to think about others, about the holiness of our brothers and sisters and of all souls.4 The obstacles to following Christ can be many and varied, but they all ultimately stem from a disordered love of self. When this is present it gives rise to an interior monologue where one’s own interests and aspirations run wild: conflicts are imagined or blown up, objectivity is totally lost and the self is always given undue importance. The soul

3 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 85 4 From our Father, Meditation The sign of interior life, 3 March 1963, in In Dialogue with the Lord, pp. 63-64

Along the Path to Love 36

aspiring to holiness has to snap out immediately of this stifling, subjective attitude. It is important for it to open up – which is not the same as getting lost in externals. It has to seek the interior presence of that divine friend, the Blessed Trinity, dwelling within it through grace. It is not possible to follow our Lord without first renouncing self: If any man would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me.5 Another sign of self-centredness is excessive concern for oneself: health, professional matters, rest and relaxation, what the future has in store. This attitude makes no sense in a soul dedicated to God. Don’t get obsessed with your state of health, or your reputation, or your career. This is mine, that’s yours ... mine, yours ... But you haven’t got anything at all! If you’ve really given yourself, then what should be important for you are our things, God’s things, everybody else’s things. Mine, mine, mine ... How tiresome! If you go around feeling humiliated all the time – don’t forget that pride is the worst aspect of the fomes peccati – thinking perhaps that you’re right, or because you’re this, because you’re that, because this or that, all the time because you, you, you ... ! Bad, very bad! You’re wasting time and forcing us to kill off your selfishness.6

Life in Christ Renouncing self is indispensable for holiness. Our Father has written: God asks us to fill our hearts with apostolic zeal, to forget about ourselves in order to be concerned, in earnest sacrifice, with the whole of mankind.7 Without this disposition we would not attain real holiness nor would we be truly effective. In our situation none of us

5 Mt 16:24 6 From our Father, Meditation, 9 January 1956 7 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 22 37 Forgetting about self

can afford to be wrapped up in self or to carry around personal worries. We must be concerned solely with the glory of God and the good of souls.8 Our Founder at times spoke to us about those people whose interior life drifts aimlessly along in response to the buffeting of the elements: they suffer uselessly because they never manage to discover the secret of true serenity and peace of soul. Almost all those who have personal problems, he told us, have them because of selfishly thinking about themselves. What we have to do is to give ourselves to others, to serve others for the love of God: that’s the way to make our sorrows disappear. Most of our trials are caused by forgetting about the duties we owe the rest of mankind and getting too caught up in our own ego.9 Forgetting about self is not simply an ascetical technique. It is a divine gift that radically transforms the soul, making it understand and love, for God, with God and in God. To forget about self means gradually to displace our ego in accordance with that motto of John the Baptist: He must increase, but I must decrease.10 This substitution is brought about by our own response to grace and by the motions of the Holy Spirit: every good endowment and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights.11 For as many of you as were baptized into Christ have put on Christ.12 Baptismal grace imprints on the Christian soul an image and likeness of its Lord and Master. Frequent reception of the sacraments, the eager desire to imitate Christ’s example, the practice of Christian virtue and the use of the means most suited to one’s own vocation, all

8 From our Father, Letter 8 August 1956, 8 9 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 15 10 Jn 3:30 11 Jas 1:17 12 Gal 3:27

Along the Path to Love 38

help to deepen and improve this image. The soul not only becomes likened to God, however; as our Father put it so graphically, it actually becomes divinized: joined to Christ, it begins to live like Christ. He has granted to us his precious and very great promises, that through these you may ... become partakers of the divine nature.13 The soul is thereby formed in its way of thinking, feeling and loving, according to the model of the Only-begotten Son. The natural faculty of knowledge is not lost, but rather is perfected by faith and supernatural outlook. The natural inclinations of the soul are not suppressed, but purified and placed at the service of God and of our fellow men. The will is not annihilated, but on the contrary, is strengthened and confirmed in goodness. As St Paul said to the Philippians, God is at work in you, both to will and to work for his good pleasure.14 The conclusion I come to every day at the examination of conscience is always: pauper servus et humilis: a poor and humble servant! That is, when I don’t have to say instead: Lord, Josemaria is not very happy with Josemaria. But since humility is truth, it also happens that very often I have to add, as no doubt you do too: Lord, today I haven’t thought about myself at all! I’ve been thinking only about you, and for your sake I’ve been concerned only about working for others! Then our contemplative soul exclaims with the Apostle: vivo autem iam non ego: vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20); it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me.15 The immediate result of really living in Christ is to forget about ourselves. For if the life I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God,16 then my ego, my own personal affairs, everything that is not

13 2 Pet 1:4 14 Phil 2:13 15 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 90 16 Gal 2:20 39 Forgetting about self

Christ, no longer counts. Any plans, ambitions or feelings I have are mine only insofar as they are Christ’s. This radical transformation is rich in consequences. It shouldn’t matter at all, then, to become as the refuse of the world, the offscouring of all things,17 if I have no other reason for happiness and glory except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom the world has been crucified to me, and I to the world.18 Not even the experience of one’s own limitations can take away the inner joy of a person who doesn’t think about self. As St Paul said: I will all the more gladly boast of my weaknesses, that the power of Christ may rest upon me. For the sake of Christ, then, I am content with weaknesses, insults, hardships, persecutions, and calamities; for when I am weak, then I am strong.19

Charity and humility In order to foster and accelerate this whole process our personal cooperation is needed. Managing to forget about oneself is a gift from God, but it is also something that is laboriously won day after day through the ascetical struggle, inspired and sustained by Christ’s grace. And so, as well as frequent reception of the sacraments – which have the power to divinize the soul according to the dispositions with which they are received – we have to practise the Christian virtues, especially charity and humility. Why charity? Because the perfection of love consists in going outside oneself in order to become identified with the person loved. As St Thomas says, “in the love of friendship, the lover is in the person loved, insofar as he considers the good things or the bad things of the person loved to be his own, and insofar as he considers the will of the

17 1 Cor 4:13 18 Gal 6:14 19 2 Cor 12:9-10

Along the Path to Love 40

person loved to be his own, in such manner that he suffers the same evils as his friend and enjoys the same good things.”20 This change in the centre of gravity of one’s joy and sorrow implies a forgetfulness of self which is the clearest sign of love of God, and of others for God’s sake. My children, if any of you wants to know if he or she really loves others, I’ll tell you the touchstone. Charity, holy affection, consists in forgetting about self and being concerned about others. You are nothing; the people around you are everything in Christ ... How well are you doing as regards charity with your brothers or sisters? If your charity isn’t warm-hearted then it’s meaningless. You need have no fear of being kind and affectionate with people as long as it all passes through the heart of Christ. Besides, kindness is shown by sacrifice. So ask yourself what you do to put yourself out for your brothers or sisters, and how hard you try to make our journey together easier. Do you work at it every day, and is it a recurring feature of your day? 21 As well as charity, we especially need to cultivate the virtue of humility. Without humility we can never serve effectively, because we wouldn’t feel compelled to abandon ourselves trustingly to the action of grace and to have constant recourse to God as our only source of strength; nor would we succeed in obtaining from him the favours he has reserved for us, for our own sanctification and that of our companions: quoniam excelsus Dominus, et humilia respicit: the Lord is high, he regards the lowly (Ps 138:6).22 Besides, humility leads us to acknowledge that there is nothing good in us that does not come from God, and that it is the personal element that keeps getting in the way. It is the soul itself that is the

20 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.28, a.1 21 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 22 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 90 41 Forgetting about self

principal hindrance to its becoming identified with Christ. Consequently, humility leads us to want to give ourselves, to deny ourselves completely, to make way for supernatural life according to our Lord’s promise: whoever loses his life for my sake will find it.23 We have to learn to disappear, to annihilate ourselves, to forget about ourselves. For the love of men, and for the love of God, we have to burn before God like lamps flickering before the altar, consuming themselves until they are completely spent. My children, if I am bringing you along more elevated ways, it is because they are guaranteed to lead somewhere. And I want you all to practise the penance of self-renunciation. We only succeed in being totally God’s when we forget about ourselves and learn to serve others. This way is indeed a divine way, because it is based on humility. And God rewards it. When the soul really becomes aware of all the limitations of the human condition and surrenders itself totally to God, that is when our Lord stretches forth his all-powerful hand and gives it – he assures it – its vocation, its holiness, its heaven of love. For me, love seems to mean more than heaven. They’re really the same thing, but to me love sounds better, and I lay hold of God’s love with all my strength. My children, it’s worthwhile forgetting about ourselves and instead being truly concerned about others.24 The insistence of our Father on this point is meant to sharpen our realization that one of the main functions of the ascetical struggle is to get us to forget about ourselves in order to have time only for God, for his things, for the mission he himself gave us by calling us to the Work. Not only must we avoid harbouring vain or useless desires, but we must also make the positive effort to fill all our thoughts and affections with

23 Mt 16:25 24 From our Father, Meditation, 16 February 1964

Along the Path to Love 42

God. It is a wonderfully effective medicine. Listen, some time or other the thought will suddenly strike you: ‘What on earth happened to the afternoon prayer ... ?’ And when you stop to think about it you suddenly realize that you were praying all afternoon, and in fact all day! Be sure to keep taking this medicine, this constant conversation with God.25 When life revolves around God’s things, then our natural reaction is to tend towards others and we have no time to get absorbed by personal problems: the only thing of interest is the situation of our brothers and sisters. This was the example our Father always gave us. My dearly beloved children, he wrote on one occasion, now more than ever our Lord is making me feel very soft-hearted. I find I love you all so eagerly and so intensely that I want to help you and – I must admit – really to put myself out, to sacrifice myself, both for your sake and for God’s sake.26 And on another occasion he added: I think that all my children’s personal problems are solved if, when they come to do their examination of conscience, they can truly say: ‘Jesus, I haven’t been worrying about myself, I haven’t been thinking about myself at all.’ Just think of all the disappointments and anxiety this would spare us! “Lord, I have been totally taken up with my brothers and sisters, with others, for you, for your Love, for your Glory.” And I remind all the sons and daughters of mine who can say that, and there are now very many of them, about St Paul’s words: vivo autem, iam non ego, vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20), it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. If you behave like that, you are a contemplative soul, you are ipse Christus.27

25 From our Father, Meditation The King’s Banquet, 13 October 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 208 26 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 23 27 From our Father, Meditation The King’s Banquet, 13 October 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 207 43 Forgetting about self

All these methods of our Father for keeping selfishness in check are embraced much more easily and spontaneously when we are truly ready to be last in everything ... and first in Love.28 To aspire to be last means to accept wholeheartedly and cherish as treasures all the humiliating and hurtful experiences that come our way; not only that, it means seeing them as something we actually deserve, since we have so much to be forgiven and so little to forgive. To want to be last means not wanting to have any rights other than the right to love God, who alone is worthy … to receive glory and honour and power.29 If some time you feel this is the way you live your life, which is the life of Christ – and I’m sure you must have felt it, or are now feeling it – then thank God for it.30

Interior mortification In order to rise above ourselves in a surge of love for God and souls, we need to foster a spirit of mortification. You have put off the old nature with its practices and have put on the new nature, which is being renewed in knowledge after the image of its creator.31 It is impossible in this mortal life to free ourselves completely from our old nature and its concupiscence. Self-worship, the disordered love of self, will always have to be fought with. Still, even though there may be lots of failures along the way, we can be certain of final victory if we work at using the weapon of interior mortification: that is, not to consent to any thought, image or recollection that makes us the centre of our own or others’ attention. We should be very quick to get rid of anything that

28 The Way, 430 29 Rev 4:11 30 From our Father, Meditation The King’s Banquet, 13 October 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 208 31 Col 3:9-10

Along the Path to Love 44

smacks of self-satisfaction, as well as any feelings of hurt or bitterness which have more to do with humbled pride than with true humility, and which rob the soul of its inner peace. Brooding over personal problems is not only a waste of time. At times it is a concealed form of self-indulgence, much more difficult to eradicate than impure thoughts because it is more difficult to admit their existence or to recognize their evil origin. Thinking about oneself is, at the very least, the cause of insensitivity of heart and the origin of lots of omissions in the service of souls. If self-centredness is not rooted out, it leads to a kind of hypocrisy which is all the more difficult to unmask the more it covers everything with a veneer of apparent righteousness. Our interior mortification has to be something solid, sacrificed and constant. In the Father’s bedroom our Founder had the following inscription put up, as a perpetual ‘alarm clock’: Separate from me, O Lord, all that separates me from You. The goal may seem difficult, but we mustn’t forget that we’re not fighting alone: we have the grace of God, the loving protection of the Blessed Virgin and of St Joseph, the assistance of our Guardian Angel and the help of our brothers and sisters. Let us not be fooled. Satan disguises himself as an angel of light.32 Some thoughts we get may seem very good: ‘I’m being useful, I’m effective, I’m becoming a saint, I can’t do anything about my limitations ...’ However, if they don’t lead us to God and cause us to be better, but instead make us introverted and self-centred, then they have to be seen for what they are: a temptation from the devil. In these situations it can help us a lot to consider that, even though we see ourselves as weak and crippled, we are nevertheless of service to our Lord, or at least he does manage to find some use for us. That thought should be enough for us to keep going full of peace and joy. Moreover,

32 2 Cor 11:14 45 Forgetting about self

the same thing holds in relation to other people: what we have to do is to ask our Lord for the one thing we hold dear, namely, to contribute to our brothers’ and sisters’ holiness and their work for the glory of God: we ourselves are not important at all. This form of behaviour is indeed love with no ulterior motives. Let me stress this: the solution I have for all personal problems is to forget about oneself and be concerned about others, for God’s sake. That’s how we have to travel along the paths of the earth, building the paths of the Lord.33

The source of joy When the soul obtains from God the gift of forgetting about itself, the things that once led to bitterness now disappear. It is not a question of becoming insensitive, but of being so much in love that it is always able to rest in the loved one. Diligam te, Domine, fortitudo mea: I love you, O Lord, my strength (Ps 17:2). Quia tu es, Deus, fortitudo mea: for you, O God, are my strength (Ps 42:2). I rest in You! I don’t know how to do anything, big or small, if You don’t help me – and if I do them for Love then nothing is small! But if I really try, God’s strong arm comes to strengthen, to temper, and to carry that sorrow; and then that weight no longer overwhelms us. Think about it carefully, my children. Consider your own personal circumstances, and see how the things that apparently don’t work out and that make life difficult, are far more valuable to you than the ones that seem to work effortlessly. If we don’t have clear ideas about this, when difficulties do arise they will leave us bewildered and distressed. On the other hand, if we absorb this whole spiritual psychology properly and learn to see the will of God precisely in those circumstances, even if it is hard, and if we love Jesus Christ, realizing

33 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967

Along the Path to Love 46

that we are co-redeemers with him, then we will not lack understanding, peace of soul and the strength to do our duty.34 Lord, since what invigorates our life, and gives us this wonderful sense of duty and the ability to forget about ourselves, is the effort to serve You, then nothing and nobody can take away our peace, our serenity and our joy.35 It is worth while being firmly resolved to fight on, to take hold of Christ’s Cross. In that extraordinary suffering whereby he gave up his life in order to give us Life, Christ offers us the ultimate proof of love. There too we find the safe embrace of our Holy Mother Mary, who knows how to make everything easy and agreeable for us. It is worth while because, despite all your own weaknesses and failings, the divine weight of caring for the holiness of others falls on each of you, and likewise the divine weight of protecting the holiness of our Mother the Work, the duty of defending before the world the Christian honour of all your kindred in Holy Church, and the sublime obligation of cooperating in the task of winning souls for God, a work which is so great that in the beginning it is hardly noticed, but which has no limits. How many great things depend upon us!36 We have to fill the world with light, because ours is a service that has to be performed joyfully. Wherever a son of God in his Work happens to be he has to have a sense of humour, which is the fruit of interior peace and of dedication. Giving oneself to the service of others is so efficacious that God rewards it with a humility full of spiritual joy.37

34 From our Father, Meditation The sign of interior life, 3 March 1963, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 65 35 From our Father, Crónica, 1970, p.204 36 From our Father, Letter 16 June 1960, 27 37 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 22

## CONTEMPLATIVES IN THE MIDDLE OF THE WORLD {#cuaderno.8.4}

“All the faithful, whatever their condition or state (…) are called by God, each in his own way, to that perfect holiness whereby the Father himself is perfect.”1 Since our Lord called us to the Work, we know very well that our ordinary work is the raw material we must sanctify; it is what sanctifies us, and what we must use to sanctify others.2 As ordinary Christians, “by reason of their special vocation it belongs to the laity to seek the kingdom of God by engaging in temporal affairs and directing them according to God’s will.”3

Contemplatives in everyday life We live in the world, where we carry out a daily occupation. It was there that God called us, and it is there that he has left us to seek holiness. Our ultimate destiny does not lie in work itself or in temporal achievements. As our Lord says: Do not labour for the food which life.4 perishes, but for the food which endures to eternal “Contemplation is promised to us as the purpose of all our works and

1 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 11 2 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 128 3 Lumen gentium, 31 4 Jn 6:27

Along the Path to Love 48

the eternal perfection of our joys.”5 Contemplation is to love and enjoy God, seeing him directly without the intervention of any created thing. All mankind is called to this loving contemplation which “will be perfect in the life to come, when we shall see God face to face (1 Cor 13:12), and it will make us perfectly happy. But now the contemplation of divine truth is possible for us imperfectly, in a mirror dimly. Hence it bestows on us a certain foretaste of happiness which begins now and will reach its perfection in the life to come.”6 This grace, which is a preparation for the vision of heaven, is not denied by God to those who make the effort to obtain it. St Gregory says: “The grace of contemplation is not given to great folk and denied to the lowly. Rather, many great people receive it, and also many of humbler state. It is received both by those who live alone and by those who are married. Since no state in life of the faithful is excluded from the grace of contemplation, he who guards his heart interiorly can be illumined by the grace of contemplation.”7 This teaching, which has its roots in the Gospel, has been taught by our Founder since the 2nd of October 1928, because that was God’s will. Contemplation is not something for a privileged few. Some people with only an elementary knowledge of religion think that contemplatives spend the whole day in ecstasy. But this shows great naïveté. The monks in their monasteries are busy all day with hundreds of jobs: they have to keep the house clean and work hard to earn their living. I often get letters from contemplative religious, both men and women, expressing lots of affection and enthusiasm for the Work and how much they pray for us. They understand something many people do not, namely, our secular way of life as contemplatives

5 St Augustine, De Trinitate, 1, 8 6 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.180, a.4, c 7 St Gregory the Great, In Ezechielem homiliae, 2, 5, 19 49 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

in the middle of the world, in temporal activities. Our cell is the street, that’s our enclosure. How can salt be hidden away? We have to make sure nothing is left insipid. That’s why all the things of the world are our seclusion.8 We are called to work in temporal affairs. “When man’s mind is intent on temporal things to seek its fulfilment in them, it lowers itself to their level. But when it is intent on them in order to attain eternal happiness, it is not lowered by them, but rather raises them to a higher level.”9 It turns them into means of holiness and apostolate. Thanks to our vocation, all our affections, intentions and achievements are fused in a unity of life. We strive to unite our daily work with ascetical struggle and with contemplation. This may seem impossible, but it is necessary if we are to reconcile the world to God. And we try to change this ordinary work into a means of personal holiness and apostolate. Isn’t that a great and noble ideal for which it is worth giving your whole life? 10 The spirit of the Work requires us to be contemplatives in work and in relaxation, in the street and in family life. We raise our souls to God in all of our occupations, in everything we do: he sang praise with all his heart, and he loved his Maker (Sir 47:8). We keep up a dialogue, a loving conversation, with our heavenly Father and with our Mother the Blessed Virgin.11 We must be contemplatives, our Father insisted. If our lives are full of love, then the rest follows naturally.12

8 From our Father, Get-together, 30 October 1964 9 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.83, a.6 ad 3 10 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 33 11 From our Father, Letter 29 July 1965, 1 12 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.592

Along the Path to Love 50

A way of prayer and of work With God’s help, we must each transform our professional work and the fulfilment of our obligations of state into a prayer that is ever more continuous, until in all our actions we acquire the habit of contemplation. This is our goal. Our life is to work and to pray, and the other way around, to pray and to work. Because the time comes when one cannot distinguish between these two concepts, these two words, contemplation and action: in the mind and in one’s awareness of them they end up meaning the same thing. Look what St Thomas says: “When of two things the first is the reason for the second, the attention of the soul to the second does not hinder or lessen its attention to the first ... And since God is apprehended by the saints as the reason for all things that will be done or known by them, their efforts in perceiving sensible things or in contemplating or doing anything else will in no way hinder their contemplation of God, nor conversely” (S. Th., Suppl., q.82, a.3 ad 4).13 This form of contemplation, which St Thomas speaks of in the context of the blessed in heaven, can also take place to a certain extent during this life on earth. Daily work can be a continual contemplative dialogue of love with God, because for a person in the state of grace working with a right intention, God is the complete and only reason for acting. Serve Jesus with such refinement, our Father writes, that the very service you perform is already contemplation. Where I come from, a relationship that is not very refined is described as being ‘not very contemplative’.14 If we work entirely for God we can be aware of his presence and relate to him lovingly in the intimacy of our hearts. Our work then

13 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 14 14 From our Father, Letter 29 July 1965, 44 51 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

becomes an act of love for God and we really learn to ‘be contemplative’ towards him. Through the work we do in God’s sight – in his presence – we pray uninterruptedly because, in working according to our spirit, we practise the theological virtues that crown the Christian life. Through this constant dialogue with the Trinity dwelling in the centre of the soul – contemplative life – we give expression to our faith. By persevering in our work we practise the virtue of hope: semper scientes quod labor vester non est inanis in Domino (1 Cor 15:58), knowing that in the Lord your labour is not in vain. By striving to imbue all our actions with God’s love, giving ourselves in generous service to our fellow men and to all souls, we practise the virtue of charity.15 Contemplation is a conversation with God that makes us fall in love and fills the soul. It doesn’t demand a long reasoning process or great efforts of the imagination or other faculties, because it is God himself who gives it: You give them drink from the river of your delights.16 This reception of God in the soul does not hinder work in any way. On the contrary, you let yourself be absorbed by what you are doing only in order to make it divine, because with our spirit everything earthly becomes divine and everything temporal becomes eternal. We keep our eyes on heaven, even though this earth, coming as it does from God’s hands, is beautiful and we love it greatly. We are not worldly people but we must love the world: that is where we want to be. Neither do we separate contemplation from action. I contemplate because I work, and I work because I contemplate. In this way our interior life infuses new vigour into the task we must perform. It makes it more perfect, more noble, more dignified, more

15 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 24 16 Ps 36:8

Along the Path to Love 52

pleasant. It does not take us away from our temporal concerns, but instead it leads us to do them better.17 Since it is a gift from God, contemplation is to a certain extent passive. But it also demands an effort and a struggle, just like any activity of the human spirit in the natural order, like trying to think, or the effort involved in waiting. Contemplation also calls for an unremitting attempt to purify the senses and faculties of anything that is not divine. If we are to receive God’s light and love we need to turn continually towards him and to die to ourselves. It is the task of a lifetime, and of its nature it is never-ending, because the theological virtues that unite us to God are always capable of further growth.18 It requires time and constant effort, as our Lord gradually leads the soul along the ways of light and love. We start with vocal prayers which many of us have been saying since we were children. They are made up of simple, ardent phrases addressed to God and to his Mother, who is our Mother as well. Is this not, in some way, a beginning of contemplation, an evident expression of trusting self-abandonment? First one brief aspiration, then another, and another ... till our fervour seems insufficient, because words are too poor ... : then this gives way to intimacy with God, looking at God without needing rest or feeling tired. We begin to live as captives, as prisoners. And while we carry out as perfectly as we can (with all our mistakes and limitations) the tasks allotted to us by our situation and duties, our soul longs to escape. It is drawn towards God like iron drawn by a magnet. One begins to love Jesus, in a more effective way, with the sweet and gentle surprise of his encounter.19

17 From our Father, Get-together, 2 November 1964 18 Cf St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.24, a.7 c 19 Friends of God, 296 53 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

Supernatural outlook in work We would never manage to grow in unity with God in everyday life, nor would we ever succeed in becoming contemplatives, if we were to neglect striving for perfection in our work or were to allow any trace of selfishness or self-indulgence to creep into it. The spirit of the Work demands, as an indispensable condition to reach contemplation, the very highest standards in work. We have to finish it down to the last detail, bringing into play all the virtues which that effort implies. In this way the work itself, even on a natural level, mirrors God’s own perfection and can be the object of contemplation. This is what our Father teaches so graphically in describing those expeditions up the spires of Burgos cathedral to give his visitors a close view of the carving at the top. There they saw a veritable lacework of stone that must have been the result of very patient and laborious craftsmanship.
