As I chatted with the young men who accompanied me, I used to point out that none of the beauty of this work could be seen from below. To give them a material lesson in what I had been previously explaining to them, I would say: ‘This is God’s work, this is working for God! To finish your personal work perfectly, with all the beauty and exquisite refinement of this tracery stonework.’ Seeing it, my companions would understand that all the work we had seen was a prayer, a loving dialogue with God.20 Neither is it possible to have a contemplative spirit if one’s motives are imperfect: if the intention is not right the will is not purified and is incapable of receiving God’s love. And so we must strive for genuine moral uprightness in our life and in our work, knowing that God sees all: laborem manuum mearum respexit Deus: God saw the labour of my hands (Gen 31:42). Therefore our work should be something holy

20 Ibid., 65

Along the Path to Love 54

and worthy of Him: not only finished down to the last detail, but also done with a right intention, with integrity, honestly, faithfully and with a sense of justice. In this way your daily work will not only be upright and holy, it will be prayer.21 It is part of God’s design for us that of its very nature our work would be apostolic, so that we work with a desire to win souls for God and sanctify the human relationships it involves. Practise this spirit yourselves, and instil it in others. Let it be seen in every aspect of your lives: in the way you pay attention to colleagues who share their problems with you; in the silent help, which may perhaps pass unnoticed, that you give when they are unable to cope with everything they have on hand; in the disinterested advice that helps them improve their performance.22 Seeking perfection in work requires a spirit of self-denial and gives rise to constant opportunities for thinking about God and others and forgetting about oneself. I have always taught you that any mortification which affects your work adversely, or disrupts the fulfilment of your professional and social obligations, is not true mortification, but rather a disorder. The mortification our Lord asks of us lies in being methodical and punctual, in paying attention to small things in our work, in the faithful fulfilment of the smallest duty of our state even when it calls for sacrifice; in doing what we are obliged to do, avoiding the tendency to take the easy way out. We don’t persevere in our work because we feel like it, but because it must be done, and then we do it wholeheartedly and cheerfully.23

21 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 26 22 Ibid., 27 23 Ibid., 25 55 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

Fulfilling the Norms faithfully I have distinguished as it were four stages in our effort to identify ourselves with Christ: seeking him, finding him, getting to know him, loving him. It may seem clear to you that you are only at the first stage. Seek him then, hungrily; seek him within yourselves with all your strength. If you act with determination, I am ready to guarantee that you have already found him, and have begun to get to know him and to love him, and to hold your conversation in heaven (cf Phil 3:20).24 In the living complexity of each individual the various stages of spiritual progress are not always clearly defined. They are intimately united and interrelated in such a way that there is always room for further growth in humility and for overcoming the difficulties created by sensuality and pride; it is always possible to advance in virtue and to become more docile to the action of the Holy Spirit. The soul needs to direct its faculties towards God once and again, to meditate on the truths of faith and our Lord’s life, making use of the imagination and other external reminders where appropriate. Certain fixed times have to be set aside for prayer. It is always necessary to begin again. “Through prayer our Lord seeks to inflame our desires so that we can understand what he has prepared for us.”25 With the same insistence with which I previously invited you to work, and work well, without fear of tiredness, I now invite you to have interior life our Father has written. I will never tire of repeating it: our Norms of piety, our prayer, come first. Without ascetical struggle our life would be worth nothing, we would be ineffective, sheep without a shepherd, blind leading the blind (cf Mt 9:36; 15:14). My daughters and sons, if at any stage work – even disguised as

24 Friends of God, 300 25 St Augustine, Letter 130, 8, 17

Along the Path to Love 56

apostolic zeal – were to prevent you fulfilling with loving fidelity the Norms of our plan of life, it would no longer be Opus Dei: it would have become the devil’s work, opus diaboli.26 And our Founder stressed: Never forget that Opus Dei’s weapon is not work; it is prayer. That is why we turn work into prayer and are contemplative souls.27 We have a very specific way to achieve this. The means? The Norms. The fulfilment of the Norms. When a son of mine struggles to fulfil the Norms, he has interior life, he has presence of God – more or less – and is on the way to being a contemplative soul, if he is not one already.28 To have presence of God in work, especially at the beginning (and sometimes later on as well), we need to make greater use of external reminders and interiorly recite vocal prayers. All this can help us keep conversation with God alive. From time to time, especially at the start, in order to deepen our conversation with God we have to make use of external reminders. But don’t forget that crutches of this sort are not always needed. When one isn’t lame they’re not necessary and only get in the way.29 But relying on external reminders would be of little use if we were to neglect the Norms, especially the Mass and the prayer. And on the contrary, when we do the Norms faithfully and lovingly, really wanting to converse with our Lord and eager to love and contemplate him, then dialogue comes naturally: it becomes much simpler and more spontaneous. We have to find our nourishment in the Mass, which is the centre of our interior life, in our encounter with Christ in the Gospel and in the Blessed Eucharist, in loving trust in the Blessed Virgin Mary, in

26 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 21 27 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.594 28 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 9 29 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 70 57 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

docility to the inspirations of the Holy Spirit, in a filial relationship with our Father God. In a word: by fulfilling our Norms of life, in accordance with the spirit of the Work.30

Seeking God’s presence Turning work into prayer, contemplation, conversation with God, is not a question of techniques or special systems, but rather is simply a matter of dedicated and generous love. Love will lead you to look after little things scrupulously well, which doesn’t mean being inflexible about them. You will take care of every detail with the proficiency of acquired habit – of Christian virtue – and at the same time with the living spontaneity of someone forever seeking fresh opportunities for believing and loving.31 From this it follows that dialogue with God during work cannot be reduced to a few more or less effective formulae. Throughout the day you should keep up a constant conversation with our Lord which draws on the incidents of the task on hand. In your heart go to the tabernacle and offer him the work you are doing. Go to our heavenly Mother and to our Father and Lord St Joseph. Ask their help to overcome the little set-backs that crop up. Entrust your companions and your colleagues to their Guardian Angels. Renew your apostolic intention, taking advantage of breaks in your work. Ask the Holy Spirit for light to tackle the problems you’re trying to solve.32 We can gauge our readiness to be true contemplatives by examining how we look after the Norms of always: for a soul in love with God they are absolutely essential. They are means that have sprung up naturally; we need them to ask our Lord for help, and to love Our

30 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 21 31 From our Father, Letter 29 July 1965, 62 32 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 22

Along the Path to Love 58

Lady. There is nothing artificial about them; they have grown out of life itself: first we put something into practice and only later do we create the norm. There is nothing unnatural here: everything is divine, and at the same time human, because if the human element is missing it becomes more difficult to get to God.33 Our Founder affirmed that it is not possible for someone to acquire interior life without spending years trying to make many acts of love of God, and many mortifications and aspirations. Therefore, as I used to tell those first brothers of yours, it’s a good idea to dedicate each day of the week to a particular devotion: the Blessed Trinity, the Eucharist, the Passion, Our Lady, St Joseph, the Guardian Angels, the holy souls in Purgatory.34 We must struggle, when necessary using external reminders – alarm clocks our Father used to call them – which can never be dispensed with entirely. My daughters and sons, do you fulfil the Norms for me? Do you try to live in the presence of God? Do you try to say aspirations and make acts of love and contrition? Ask your Director what stratagems to use to achieve it. It’s something very personal, like taking medicine: the dose depends on the person.35 When we make an effort to work well, for love of God and to draw souls closer to him, a moment comes when it is impossible to distinguish between work and contemplation. You can’t say you spend this much time in prayer, and that much time at work. You spend all your time in prayer, contemplating in God’s presence. Though our vocation may appear to be to the active life, we end up where the great mystics did: I flew aloft so high, so high, that in the end I seized my prey. I flew to the heart of God. Lose if you want to win, sink if you want to rise, suffer if you want to rejoice, die if

33 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.597 34 From our Father, Get-together, 12 January 1955 35 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.597 59 Contemplatives in the middle of the world

you want to live. In the street, nel bel mezzo della strada, one reaches this: I flew aloft so high, so high ... One becomes a contemplative, in constant conversation with God. That’s why I can’t imagine a son of mine ever being bored. All of us are engaged in a divine conversation. That is why we feel our faults so keenly and turn immediately to ask God’s forgiveness. And what is that but contemplative life?36 All of this leads to unity of life, which presupposes a constant struggle to live each day, each hour, each moment, as your conscience directs. And if you aren’t fully content, at least you see an improvement every day. It’s like the dissatisfaction of an artist, who exclaims, on finishing a painting, ‘It’s not exactly what I wanted! There’s still something missing!’37 “Man was created to raise his mind to the heights of contemplation, so that no distraction might lead him away from his path of love.”38 Our calling is such that we cannot persevere in our vocation if we are not contemplatives, if we don’t turn our lives into Love.39 If you are not a contemplative, a soul of continuous prayer, then the light will be darkness; if you are not immersed in God, the salt will lose its savour and will be fit only to be trampled upon by men.40 The way is to work as well as we can, for love of God. The marvellous reality of our vocation, whereby we seek our Lord by doing our ordinary work perfectly, enables us to receive the graces to turn our entire life into constant prayer.41 For us there should be no separation between active and contemplative life, as there was none in Christ.

36 From our Father, Get-together, 30 October 1964 37 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.598 38 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 8, 10, 18 39 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 130 40 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 35 41 From our Father, Letter 29 July 1965, 1

Along the Path to Love 60

“When our Redeemer became incarnate, living both, he united them in himself.”42 We follow his lead through our Norms and our Customs. Fulfil them, our Father said, and the time will come when you can say, feeling this truth in the depths of your soul: It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me (Gal 2:20).43 We will then be truly contemplatives, with a genuine foretaste of heaven. “Contemplation begins here and is perfected in our heavenly home (...) Contemplation never ends, because when the light of this world disappears it reaches its perfection.”44

42 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 28, 13, 33 43 From our Father, Meditation, 12 April 1954 44 St Gregory the Great, In Ezechielem homiliae, 2, 2, 9

## AT THE TIME OF PRAYER {#cuaderno.8.5}

Christ, my children, teaches us to pray by his example. He would often withdraw from his followers’ company, on one occasion for as long as forty days together, and call upon his Father. And he always acted like that: before the great miracles, and during his intensive journeying from village to village; before undergoing his Passion, and throughout the torments of his death. See how he makes sure the evangelists tell us about his prayer to God the Father.1 The Gospel tells us how, on a certain occasion, in the morning, a great while before day, he rose and went out to a lonely place, and there he prayed.2 And our Father comments: I am deeply moved by our Lord’s great need – St Mark records that it was long before daybreak – to speak with his Eternal Father and entrust to him all the apostolate with souls he was going to do that day. ... We have no other option. At the start of each day’s labour at Christ’s side, ministering to the souls who seek him, the one thing we must do is turn to our Lord in prayer: only in prayer will we learn to serve others.3

1 From our Father, Meditation A suit made to measure, 25 February 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 194 2 Mk 1:35 3 From our Father, Meditation A suit made to measure, 25 February 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 195

Along the Path to Love 62

Mental prayer During our meditation we try to place ourselves in God’s presence, trying to maintain a conversation with him and put aside for the time being the other concerns of the day. This is what the Apostles and the first Christians did, and, during these twenty centuries of the Church’s history, their example has been followed by all those who want to live close to our Lord. In describing the cure of the man who was lame from birth, Sacred Scripture notes in passing that the Apostles Peter and John were going up to the temple at the hour of prayer, the ninth hour.4 Peter is told to open the doors of the Church to the Gentiles while praying on the roof of the house of Simon the tanner in Joppa.5 The first Christians are described as devoting themselves to the apostles’ teaching and fellowship, to the breaking of bread and the prayers.6 While St Paul is in prison at Philippi he spends the entire night praising God;7 and in the midst of the storm which is about to shipwreck his vessel, he does not abandon his prayer, and it is because of this prayer that he and the crew are eventually saved.8 There is no other way to practise Christianity. You and I, our Father used to say, want to reach holiness: that’s why we’ve come to Opus Dei. To achieve it, we must identify ourselves with Christ, we must clothe ourselves with Christ. Induimini Dominum Iesum Christum! Put on the Lord Jesus Christ (Rom 13:14). We must all be ipse Christus, my beloved children, Christ himself. However, we each have to work out how to put on that garment the Apostle speaks about. We each

4 Acts 3:1 5 Cf Acts 10:9 6 Acts 2:42 7 Cf Acts 16:25 8 Cf Acts 27:9ff 63 At the time of prayer

have to have our personal conversation with our Lord ... So then, what are you and I going to do...? We’re going to converse with our Lord, look for him, like Peter, to have an intimate conversation with him. Note that I say “conversation”, that is, a one-to-one dialogue, face to face, without taking refuge in anonymity. We need this personal prayer, this intimacy, this direct relationship with God our Lord.9 We should try to keep up this conversation with our Lord all day long. But, particularly during the periods in our plan of life dedicated to meditation, we must enkindle and feed the fire of dialogue with God, so that we can be effectively and truly contemplative for the rest of the day. A saint, without prayer? I don’t believe in such sanctity.10 Your apostolic life is worth what your prayer is worth.11 In the Work we have no ready-made methods of prayer. There is no such thing as a set approach. We children of God don’t need a method, an artificial system, to talk with our Father.12 Each one has to discover the way best suited to the needs of his or her soul. As our Father used to say: I don’t want to dictate how each person should pray, because it’s a very personal thing. I just give general indications. Then each one can follow his or her own path, which is different from everybody else’s. And this diversity doesn’t alter the fact that we all have the same spirit and are all travelling along the same road.13 Among our Father’s general recommendations, which are useful for everyone, is the advice to get to know our Lord’s Sacred Humanity. Jesus Christ is perfectus Deus, perfectus homo. Use your mind to dialogue with him, for we can’t use it like angels or animals, since

9 From our Father, Meditation A suit made to measure, 25 February 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 195 10 The Way, 107 11 Ibid., 108 12 Friends of God, 255 13 From our Father, Get-together, 6 September 1973

Along the Path to Love 64

we’re neither one nor the other: we’re human beings!14 Mental prayer is a task that demands the use of all the faculties of the soul, but especially the intellect and the will, under the influence of divine grace. To facilitate prayer, we should ‘materialize’ even the most spiritual things, as the parables do. This teaching is divine. Doctrine must reach our mind and our heart by way of the senses.15 This is the ordinary way to talk to God in mental prayer. When you begin your meditation, frequently, depending on the circumstances, you imagine the scene or the mystery you want to contemplate. Then you apply your understanding and you immediately begin to talk to our Lord in an affectionate manner, full of love and sorrow, of thanksgiving and of desires to improve. If you follow this path you should come to a prayer of quiet in which it is our Lord who speaks, and you listen to whatever he has to say.16 In order to achieve this wordless form of conversation, if and when God grants it, the normal thing is to persevere for years trying to achieve and maintain dialogue in prayer. This means struggling against distractions and other difficulties, and making use of practical techniques. And even when these latter become less necessary, we still have to continue using other means, because only in Heaven is the contemplation of God continuous and perfect. Very often the peaceful, unhurried consideration of the Our Father or the preparatory prayer our Founder taught us, can be useful. In those words we can find many things to improve our presence of God. Tell him: My Lord and my God, I firmly believe that you are here, listening to me. He is in the Tabernacle, really present under the

14 Ibid. 15 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954, , in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 18 16 Ibid.

65 At the time of prayer

sacramental species, with his Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity. And he is present in our soul in grace, as the mover of our supernatural thoughts, affections, desires and deeds. You can’t imagine what peace it gave my soul and how much good it has done me, and so many other people since, when I added those words to the prayer: that you see me, that you hear me! And then immediately we greet him, as we do when we are talking to a fellow human being. We greet God by adoring him: I adore you with profound reverence! And if we have ever offended someone, if we have treated them badly, we ask forgiveness. Well, the same holds true with God our Lord: I ask you for pardon of my sins and grace to make this time of prayer – of conversation with you – fruitful. In this way we are already praying, we are already absorbed in intimacy with God. Moreover, what would we do if this illustrious person whom we want to speak to has a mother, and she is a mother who loves us? We’d try to get her approval, wouldn’t we, we’d ask her to put in a good word for us! Well, then, we should invoke the Mother of God, who is also our Mother and who loves us so much: my Mother Immaculate! We go to St Joseph, the foster father of Jesus, who also has great influence with God: St Joseph, my Father and Lord! We go to our Guardian Angel, that heavenly prince who helps us and protects us: Intercede for me! Having completed the preparatory prayer and gone through the greetings that are normal in polite society, then we can talk to God. About what? About our joys and sorrows, our hardships and desires, about our disappointments and the things that excite us ... about everything! We can also tell him simply: ‘Lord, here I am like a dummy. I don’t know what to tell you ... I want to talk to you, to pray, to immerse myself in the intimacy of the Heart of your Son Jesus. I know I am right beside you, and I still can’t manage to say a word. If Along the Path to Love 66

I were with my mother, or with that person I love so much, I’d be well able to talk about this and that; and here I am with you, and my mind is gone blank.’ My beloved daughters and sons, this is real prayer! Make sure you stay there, before the Tabernacle, like a little dog at his master’s feet, for the entire period fixed beforehand for prayer. ‘Lord, here I am! I find it hard! I’d prefer to leave right now, but I’m staying here because I love you, because I know that you see me and are listening to me and are smiling at me’.17

Difficulties in prayer One of the commonest difficulties in prayer is distractions. The memory begins to churn and the imagination conjures up images that draw us away. At times it takes a great effort to subdue them. The important thing, however, is to keep alive the decision to remain in God’s presence, and not to allow the prayer to become a monologue with oneself or with uninvited visitors. On occasions, rather than trying to banish the distraction from our mind, the thing to do is to follow our Father’s advice: If the distractions are about good persons or good things, ask our Lord for the virtues of those good people. If they have to do with things that are indifferent or with people who are doing evil things, then ask our Lord to help the people concerned to shed their indifference or wickedness. In this way you use your time well, you pray, and you do a great act of charity.18 When praying, one’s imagination and memory have to be placed at God’s service. We need our imagination: it is extremely useful in order to pray well and to think about the concerns of God.19 To keep

17 From our Father, Crónica, 1976, pp.1410-1411 18 From our Father, Get-together, 21 April 1973 19 From our Father, Get-together, 12 April 1974 67 At the time of prayer

the imagination under control, it is vitally important to use the Gospel. When you pray using the words of the Gospel, you contemplate how Christ relates to all sorts of people: simple folk, individuals, crowds, rich and poor ... Try not to be passive. Imagine yourself as one of the characters in those Gospel scenes: with your weaknesses, with my littleness and with yours, and also with all the noble qualities we have ... Follow Christ, walking where he walked, and you will have his life, his strength, his eagerness to redeem.20 There are times when tiredness, either physical or mental, makes prayer difficult. When you’re tired and don’t know what to say to our Lord, tell him: Lord, I’m tired!21 At times like these it is almost impossible to concentrate on anything. Your mind is sluggish: you try to collect your thoughts in God’s presence, but it’s useless: there’s a complete blank! Don’t try to force yourself, and don’t worry. Look: such moments are for your heart.22 Meditation doesn’t consist of flowery speeches. Sometimes prayer is looking at an image of our Lord or his Mother. At other times it can be a petition formulated in words. And yet again it is expressed in those good deeds that are the consequence of faithfulness. Just like a soldier on guard duty, we keep watch at our Lord’s door: that’s prayer. Or like a little dog at his master’s feet. Don’t mind saying to him from time to time: ‘Lord, here you have me, like a faithful dog.’ Or better still: ‘Lord, like a little donkey who won’t kick the person who loves him.’ We can use all the resources of human relationships for prayer and friendship with God.23

20 From our Father, Get-together, 9 January 1969 21 From our Father, Get-together, 14 April 1974 22 The Way, 102 23 From our Father, Crónica, 1970, p.754

Along the Path to Love 68

The most serious obstacle to progress in prayer is discouragement. Our Lord warns us: Watch and pray that you may not enter into temptation.24 The Apostles abandoned Jesus in Gethsemane and left him on his own because they fell asleep and neglected prayer. Don’t ever omit the mental prayer ... When a soul starts thinking it doesn’t know how to pray, or that what the Father teaches is very difficult, or that our Lord is not saying anything or is not listening, then the thought might occur to him: ‘Well, if it’s like this, I might as well give it up altogether and just stick with vocal prayers.’ That would be a serious temptation. No, my daughters and sons! Persevere in your meditation. Don’t be slow to unburden yourselves to our Lord in your prayer. If needs be, keep saying the same aspiration for the whole half hour: Jesus, I love You; Jesus, teach me to love; Jesus, teach me to love others for You. Persevere like this one day after another, for a month, a year, another year, and finally you’ll hear our Lord saying to you: ‘Don’t be so silly, I’ve been here right beside you all the time!’25

Persevering in prayer Dryness in prayer, as such, is quite normal. What is important is the mental determination to keep on praying. If at the beginning things are easy, it is because God helps to make the first steps delightful and pleasant. However, as time goes on and divine Love establishes itself more firmly in the soul, our Lord purifies it by taking away any emotional consolation. Then we have to go against the grain and rectify our intention, and prepare ourselves for that more intimate union to which God is calling us. Our Lord treats us just like a mother treats her children. At first a mother gives her infant child her breast, or the

24 Mk 14:38 25 From our Father, Get-together, September 1973 69 At the time of prayer

bottle, or something sweet, easy to swallow and digest. Later on, when his teeth have come, she gives him biscuits, a piece of bread, or some meat ... 26 Once a person has matured in the interior life God takes away those consolations and, above all, any sensation of progress. At the same time he bestows on the soul a greater sensitivity to sins and imperfections, and leaves it with nothing to rely on except God himself: I will betroth you to me in righteousness and in justice, in steadfast love, and in mercy. I will betroth you to me in faithfulness.27 My children, if our Lord gives us a rapturous and palpable devotion, and we find ourselves sighing like an old lady in a dark corner of a cathedral, then praise be to God! But if, on the other hand, he treats us like mature adults, if we feel nothing in the prayer, and come to no resolutions either, if we are as dry as a well without water, we must persevere just the same, telling him in all simplicity everything that’s happening to us, all we have in our heart.28 That is the moment to exercise our faith, our hope and our love, and to show with deeds our readiness to slake our thirst in the living waters he has promised. Like the Samaritan woman, we cry out: Sir, give me this water.29 Sometimes the effort to drink, to reach the fountain, to converse with him, can be costly. Don’t get discouraged. That effort, if it is genuine, is already love, it is already perseverance, it is already faithfulness.30 In any case we cannot afford to abandon mental prayer. The devil wants us to put prayer aside, so as to leave the soul without nourishment and make it weak and feeble, so that instead of being

26 From our Father, Get-together, 16 April 1974 27 Hos 2:19-20 28 From our Father, Crónica, 1976, p.1407 29 Jn 4:15 30 From our Father, Crónica, 1973, p.810

Along the Path to Love 70

concerned for the things of God, we would find ourselves being dragged downwards.31 We must be on our guard. The danger we face, when, as we write, the lines come out crooked, is to say: forget that ruled paper, throw away the guideline! Out goes the prayer! My daughters and sons, you must reject that temptation at once, as it is particularly diabolical. We’ve got to be contemplative souls, and so we can never forsake our meditation. Without prayer, without meditation, without interior life, we could do nothing but evil. Particularly at the present time, when, within the Church, individuals who ought to preach the faith are publicly proclaiming their immoral lives, their lack of faith, their own doubts ... Now it seems that we are even more obliged to be truly souls of prayer, offering generously to our Lord everything we do and never abandoning our conversation with him, no matter what happens. If you behave in this way, you will be attentive to God all day long, and you will make a serious effort to pray very well during those two daily half-hour periods. Don’t forget, my children: there will always be vocations as long as you are faithful and loyal, as long as you abhor sin, as long as you are souls of prayer, as long as you love Jesus Christ and his Blessed Mother. The day you abandon your prayer and become careless, you will go, just like anybody else, rapidly downhill along the road to hell.32 Dryness is not always a trial from God which purifies the soul like gold in the furnace.33 Sometimes it can be due to negligence, through not being mentally prepared or not having topics for the conversation

31 From our Father, Get-together, 16 April 1974 32 From our Father, Crónica, 1976, p.1408 33 Wis 3:6 71 At the time of prayer

with our Lord. Dryness can also occur if one doesn’t have a right intention. So when it appears, we cannot remain inactive: we have to be sincere, to ask for advice, and improve our spirit of penance so that our dispositions, both habitual and actual, are those of a clear aversion to sin.34 We should never complain about our lot, because in the last analysis, if our soul seems to be empty, we can always offer the feeling in atonement for our sins. Perhaps one of you might say to me: Father, I am looking within myself, in my soul, and I don’t find anything. Well, I would tell you in return: maybe it is that you haven’t had much interior life up to now, or perhaps your interior life is in fact robust but now our Lord wants to test it. Your soul feels like an empty cistern, does it? Well, then, look for the love of God! Seek the Lord and his strength; seek his presence continually (1 Chr 16:11). Do what someone does when trying to win another’s heart in good and noble courtship. That’s the way to keep up your conversation with God.35 This is the moment to put into practice our Father’s advice. So, when you are not able to go on, when you feel that your fire is dying out, if you cannot throw on it sweet-smelling logs, throw on the branches and twigs of short vocal prayers and ejaculations, to keep the bonfire burning,36 the bonfire of your conversation with God. The whole half hour can go by with your head in a whirl and your body rebelling like a wild donkey ... Well, my daughters and sons, it doesn’t matter. Say as your closing prayer: ‘Lord, I’m sorry, I’m really sorry. I’ll be back again and You’ll have to help me out.’ This can happen to us one day after another.37 God, who always hears us,

34 Friends of God, 243 35 From our Father, Crónica, 1973, pp. 810-811 36 The Way, 92 37 From our Father, Get-together, 25 August 1973

Along the Path to Love 72

will eventually make his presence known to the soul who perseveres in this way. What do men do in places where they don’t have any water? They build a cistern and empty buckets of water into it one after another. When you find it impossible to be recollected for prayer, prepare for it by fetching water to the cistern: that means acts of love and reparation, spiritual communions, appeals to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, to Our Lady, to St Joseph and to our Holy Guardian Angels. All this is water that has to be carried by hand to the cistern.38 The fidelity and effort this supposes are not based on nature alone: the Holy Spirit, hidden in the centre of the soul, works away helping us. He is always present at the drama we stage in our efforts to possess him. We may have to work like this for a long time. However, if we persevere, the time will come when we won’t need to fetch the water, because a well will have formed. There may not be very much water there at first, but it is a well of living water (Cant 4:15). There it is, in the depths of the soul. You don’t know where it springs from, nor how it forms a pool, nor where it flows to ... , but there’s always enough to drink from. If you persist, the level of the well rises and rises until it forms a spring of clear water where you can drink with both hands, with your mouth wide open, when you are thirsty. Do you follow me, my children? You can always find water. With the help of God, One and Triune, hidden in the soul, each of you can make sure not to be an empty cistern, but rather a well which rises and rises until it becomes a fountain of clear and splendid water, water of love. However, your commitment to this task has to be wholehearted.39 One aspect of this task is to make use of anything that makes it easier to pray: notes taken at the examination of conscience, at the Brief Circle

38 From our Father, Crónica, 1973, p.811 39 Ibid.

73 At the time of prayer

or at any moment of the day; prayers which the Church uses in her liturgy; things we have written down so as to think them over in our conversation with our Father God,40 a good book ... I too normally bring a book to the prayer. It’s not that I always need it, but ... just in case. Do the same yourselves. Try to follow the ideas it contains, and then close it when you are able to reason on your own.41

United in prayer Perseverance is what really matters. We ought always to pray and not lose heart.42 If we do, then we really manage to help other souls and are at peace ourselves. When you have interior life, you share a little, and more than a little, in God’s power, because prayer is allpowerful. It makes me very happy to think that, at all hours of the day or night, somewhere in the world there are people of Opus Dei praying.43 When difficulties are particularly intense – even though they always pass – then considerations like these can encourage us to persevere in prayer. When you are spending that time in mental prayer, and during the rest of the day too, remember we are never alone, even though materially speaking we might be quite isolated. In our lives, if we are faithful to our vocation, we are always united to the saints in Paradise, to the souls that are being purified in Purgatory, and to all your brothers and sisters who are still carrying on the fight here on earth. Moreover – and I find this thought very consoling, because it expresses admirably the continuity of Holy Church – you can unite yourselves to the prayer of all Christians of any age: those who have

40 Cf The Way, 97 41 From our Father, Crónica, 1976, pp.1409-1410 42 Lk 18:1 43 From our Father, Get-together, 25 August 1973

Along the Path to Love 74

gone before us, those who are alive right now, and those who will be born in centuries to come. So, as you experience this marvellous communion of saints, which is an unending hymn of praise to God, and in spite of the fact that you don’t feel like it or find it hard – you’re bone dry – you pray with an effort, but with total confidence. It should make you very happy to consider that your prayer is united to the prayer of those who lived with Jesus Christ, to the ceaseless petition of the Church militant, the Church suffering and the Church triumphant, and to the prayer of all Christians yet to come. So when you find that your prayer is dry, make a valiant effort and tell our Lord: ‘My God, I don’t want my voice missing from that chorus of permanent and endless praise directed to You’.44

44 From our Father, Get-together, 6 September 1973

## A TIME FOR PENANCE {#cuaderno.8.6}

I see in my members, writes Saint Paul, another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin.1 All men, even the holiest, feel this law, because everyone comes into the world with original sin on their souls, and so everyone suffers its consequences: Behold I was brought forth in iniquity, and in sin did my mother conceive me.2 As if this were not enough, we have added our own personal sins to that sin with which we were conceived. Logically enough, a sincere soul is keenly aware of the times it has offended God personally and directly in its past life: Against you, you only, have I sinned, and done that which is evil in your sight.3

Reasons for penance Having repented and confessed our sins, it is true to say that God forgives us. However, sin always leaves an imprint on the soul. Once the guilt has been forgiven, the remnants of sin, the dispositions caused by preceding acts, remain. Yet they remain weakened and diminished so they do not dominate the human person. They are more akin to dispositions than to habits; they are like the ‘fomes peccati’ which

1 Rom 7:23 2 Ps 51:5 3 Ps 51:4

Along the Path to Love 76

remain after Baptism.4 The sins of our past life continue to have an effect on the present, and therefore provide another reason to be contrite. The Psalmist says: Wash me thoroughly from my iniquity, and cleanse me from my sin.5 Besides, even a righteous man falls seven times,6 many times. These sins may perhaps be due to weakness, but they still need to be atoned for because they do offend our God. Since you first gave yourself to God, you have come quite a distance. And yet, isn’t it true, our Father asks, that there are still many aspects of pride and lack of virtue remaining undiscovered? There are still corners of your soul that need cleaning. Perhaps you still allow yourself to dwell on thoughts or ideas that have no place in the divine path you have chosen to follow.7 If we don’t pay any attention to failures, omissions or hidden sins, it is either because we lack a spirit of examination, or because our conscience has become hardened, or we are insensitive in our dealings with God. But, nevertheless, they are there: they offend God and are often the hidden cause of our going astray. And if the root is bitter and is not dug up, then the fruits will continue being bitter, since the bad tree bears evil fruit.8 Another reason for doing penance, and one we shouldn’t forget, is the consideration of the sins of others, since those who commit them are our brothers and sisters, to whom we are closely linked by a mysterious solidarity. We should try to make amends for these offences, since they are not as foreign to us as our pride might suggest. All the saints have learned to weep for the sins of others.

4 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.86, a.5 c 5 Ps 51:2 6 Prov 24:16 7 From our Father, Meditation, 2 March 1952 8 Mt 7:17 77 A time for penance

Re-awakening the sense of sin Who can deny that God is offended? Yet it can sometimes happen that the human heart becomes hardened and insensitive and deforms the meaning of sin. First of all, sin is referred to as error; next it is described as mere weakness; and finally it is called liberation, so that eventually something which is really sinful ends up being glorified. Only when the soul has a clear conscience and sees things very clearly can it really make its own the words of the psalm: for I know my transgressions, and my sin is ever before me.9 The sorrow that then arises is truly supernatural, and enables the soul to appreciate what sin really is, and who the sinner is, and who the Person offended is. Love-sorrow. Because he is good. Because he is your friend, who gave his life for you. Because every good thing you have is his. Because you have offended him so much ... Because he has forgiven you ... He! ... you! Weep, my son, with Love-sorrow.10 Yes, we do weep on seeing how God behaves towards us. He keeps on calling us lovingly, inviting us to share his holiness, his glory and his goodness. We behold his mercy, his readiness to forget our offences and to come to our aid once again, looking for a response. Do you remember the parable of the rich man who prepared a banquet, and how the guests kept finding excuses to avoid attending the wedding? Just imagine the Heart of Jesus Christ as he described it! Haven’t we too often looked for excuses and said No to Jesus? … To his heartfelt invitation – all those inspirations of the Holy Spirit, all the gifts and graces, and all the insights which God our Lord quietly implants in

9 Ps 51:3 10 The Way, 436

Along the Path to Love 78

the heart and mind.11 If every offence is hurtful to our Lord, then it must be particularly painful when it is caused by a friend, one who is chosen and dearly loved, even if ordinarily the matter of itself is not so serious. It is not an enemy who taunts me, then I could bear it, says the Psalmist, but it is you, my equal, my companion, my familiar friend.12 And our Father comments: a contemptuous glance doesn’t matter too much if the persons concerned are strangers, but it is particularly hurtful if it is from a son to his mother.13 And Jesus’ love is greater, and is infinitely more intense and generous, than that of all the mothers of the world together. My child, I’m sure you’ve seen what a mother’s love is like. You’ve witnessed their sacrifices, their self-denial, their anxiety and sleepless nights beside the cot of a sick child. Do you think that the children, with all their self-centredness and forgetfulness, really deserve such care? Then think of Christ’s love for us in bringing us to Opus Dei. Do you think we deserve it? Do you try to do at least as much for him as human beings do when they truly love someone?14 In spite of all God’s fatherly care for us, he is nevertheless very badly treated. One sees a great coldness in people’s response to his generous call, and this should make us feel more or less directly responsible: perhaps if we acted more decisively, or prayed more intensely, or were more constant in mortification, then things would be different. Don’t be content to ask Jesus pardon just for your own faults: don’t love him just with your own heart ...

11 From our Father, Meditation Lessons of Ordinary Life, 9 January 1959, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 85 12 Ps 55:13-14 13 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 14 From our Father, Meditation Prayer of the senses, 13 April 1954, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 54 79 A time for penance

Console him for every offence that has been, is, or will be done to him. Love him with all the strength of all the hearts of all those who have most loved Him.15 Realizing that our God is badly treated everywhere should help us transcend the narrow horizons of our own individual sorrow, and make us want to partake in the sorrow that fills the heart of every person who has ever been, is, or will be in love with Christ.

Union with Christ’s redemptive suffering Sins against justice, as well as requiring repentance, also demand restitution. “There is no man who is not in debt to God (...) Who is not in his debt, except he who has not sinned in any way?”16 St Augustine is not referring here to the general debt every human being owes God for the free gift of everything they have, but to that other debt that springs from injustice, insult and dishonour, the one our Lord illustrates with the parable of the servant who could not pay.17 The sinner is a servant who offends his generous creditor, who tries to rob him and insult him. So much so that Holy Scripture vividly describes sinners as persons who crucify the Son of God on their own account.18 Just as it is difficult for the human mind, even aided by grace, to appreciate how justice and mercy are harmonized in God, neither can it understand how the profound displeasure sin causes him can be reconciled with his perfect happiness. Sin is the mysterium iniquitatis: the mystery of evil, the inexplicable evil of the creature whose pride leads him to rise up against God. The story is as old as mankind. It began with the fall of our first parents; then came the

15 The Way, 402 16 St Augustine, Sermon 83, 3 17 Mt 18:25 18 Heb 6:6

Along the Path to Love 80

unending depravities which punctuate the behaviour of mankind down the ages; and, finally, our own personal rebellions. It is very difficult to realize just how perverse sin is and to understand what our faith tells us. We should remember that even in the human context the scale of an offence is frequently determined by the importance of the injured party – his social standing, his qualities. But with sin man offends God, the creature repudiates his Creator.19 Through the Incarnation of his Son, God has helped us discover, to some degree, the infinite malice of sin. The magnitude of the expiation God demanded of his Only-Begotten Son gives us some notion of how serious sin really is. That deep sorrow, which was overwhelming and well-nigh infinite, caused in Christ by the sins of men, and the sadness which weighed him down on the Mount of Olives as he lay there, crushed and exhausted, help us understand sin’s malice. His bloody sweat, the trembling his human nature experienced as he faced the price of our ransom, and the accumulation of sufferings he had to undergo, one by one, can help us put sin into its proper perspective. Jesus has made atonement for our sins. Divine justice demanded appropriate reparation, and only God himself could do so sufficiently: that is why he became man. Our sorrow and our reparation are acceptable to God through Jesus’ merits, since all the value, merit and effectiveness of Christian penance derive from Christ’s redemptive sacrifice. This redemptive task can absorb our entire existence, enabling the spiritual priesthood we received at Baptism to blossom into deeds. Besides, at Mass we can unite our satisfaction to Christ’s own sacrifice and direct it to God, thereby making it really worthy. Christ has not dispensed us from expiation. Rather, he has made it possible and has given it a value it could never have of itself. We can now say with the Apostle: in my flesh I complete what is lacking in Christ’s afflictions

19 Christ is passing by, 95 81 A time for penance

for the sake of his body, that is, the church.20 We are members of Christ’s body, and therefore our deeds of penance are his deeds.

Love-sorrow Any serious offence against God implies, firstly, a turning of the will away from him: aversio a Deo et conversio ad creaturas. In any sin the heart withdraws from God and opts for creatures. Therefore, the first debt to be paid is that of the will, of the heart. It must return to him and draw back from what led it astray. The sacrifice acceptable to God is a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise.21 What our Lord looks for above all is that sacrifice of the will, a contrite and humble heart, true sorrow. Then you will remember your evil ways, and your deeds that were not good; and you will loathe yourselves for your iniquities and your abominable deeds ... Be ashamed and confounded for your ways.22 Without contrition, sin remains. Sorrow is so fundamental to penance that “more than sin itself, what irritates and offends God is that sinners feel no sorrow for their sins.”23 For this reason too, the first gift God’s mercy bestows on the sinner is a sensitive heart, capable of sorrow and easily moved to contrition: I will give them a new heart, and put a new spirit within them; I will take the stony heart out of their flesh, and give them a heart of flesh, that they may walk in my statutes and keep my ordinances and obey them; and they shall be my people, and I will be their God.24 The beginning of conversion is the ability to recognize the presence

20 Col 1:24 21 Ps 51:17 22 Ezech 36:31-32 23 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 14, 3 24 Ezech 11:19-20

Along the Path to Love 82

and malice of sin by means of an examination of conscience. My son, how are things going? Are you trying to be more discerning in your examination? Are you asking our Lord for grace to get to know him and to get to know yourself, and so be converted once more?25 A clear, deep, sincere examination is always hard. It is a grace we need to ask God for, if we want to discover all those nebulous things hidden away in the recesses of the soul. Behold you desire truth in the inward being; therefore teach me wisdom in my secret heart.26 Then, when we have seen our failings, we try to appreciate their malice and how much they offend the divine goodness. Nothing helps this better than contemplating Christ on the Cross, because there we see the direct result of our sins: even a single mortal sin would have been enough to place Jesus on the Cross. Let us meditate on our Lord, wounded from head to foot out of love for us. Using a phrase which approaches the truth, although it does not express its full reality, we can repeat the words of an ancient writer: The body of Christ is a portrait in pain. At the sight of Christ bruised and broken – just a lifeless body taken down from the Cross and given to his Mother – at the sight of Jesus destroyed in this way,27 acts of contrition and reparation flow more easily from the depths of the soul: Pauper servus et humilis! Cor contritum, et humiliatum, Deus, non despicies! Domine, tu omnia nosti; tu scis quia amo te! Begin again, our Father asks us. I’m sure the same thing happens to you, but I find myself beginning again every day, every hour. Every time I make an act of contrition, I begin again.28 Every day, and as often as necessary, whenever we need to come back to our Lord, we

25 From our Father, Meditation, 2 March 1952 26 Ps 51:6 27 Christ is passing by, 95 28 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 83 A time for penance

have to begin again. The Church has set aside certain liturgical periods of the year, like Advent and Lent, that provide us with actual graces from God. Our Lord is always ready to give us his grace but particularly at these times; the grace for this new conversion, for raising the soul higher in the supernatural life, for greater dedication and progress in perfection, inflaming us with love.29

Deeds of penance As well as being sorrowful and repentant, we also have to make up to God for our sins by means of what are called deeds of penance, expiation. Make friends quickly with your accuser, while you are going with him to court, lest your accuser hand you over to the judge, and the judge to the guard, and you be put in prison; truly I say to you, you will never get out till you have paid the last penny.30 The debt has to be paid, in this life or in the next. That is why we pray each day for spatium verae paenitentiae, a time for true penance, and why we ask ourselves often if we have tried to increase our spirit of penance. We have a great need to make atonement – for ourselves, and for all mankind – to make up to God, to console Christ and share his suffering. There is no shortage of opportunities; we’ll find them if we look for them: in small everyday things, in work that is intense, constant and methodical. Little things that won’t harm our health but will keep our love of God alight. Mortifications at mealtime. Heroic minutes throughout the day. Punctuality. Tidiness. Guarding our sight on the street, without doing anything odd … Dozens and dozens of features and opportunities to take advantage of!31

29 From our Father, Meditation, 2 March 1952 30 Mt 5:25-26 31 From our Father, Meditation Prayer of the senses, 13 April 1954, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 50

Along the Path to Love 84

We can use constant mortification of the internal and external senses: the imagination and memory, our likes and dislikes, what we enjoy looking at, our comfort ... And after the ordinary mortifications come the extraordinary ones, with the Director’s permission, at those moments – so easy to recognize – when God is asking for more. You should always ask your Director for permission because he is the one to moderate them. But moderating them doesn’t always mean lessening them. It also means increasing them if he thinks it appropriate ... The spirit of penance is not something one just talks about, nor is it present in someone who does great penances some days and forgets about it altogether on others. The person who is able to conquer self every day, offering things for love without showing off, that person has the true spirit of penance. That indeed is a love full of sacrifice, a pure love that does not look for applause.32 The penitent soul also finds thousands of occasions for expiation in things that arise unexpectedly and can be difficult to bear: sickness, not feeling well, discomfort, weather being too hot or too cold, the consequences of poverty, difficulties in the apostolate. “We can make satisfaction to God, through Jesus Christ, not only through the sufferings we accept spontaneously in reparation for sin and those imposed by the judgment of the priest in accordance with our guilt, but also – and this is the greatest proof of love – through the temporal punishments with which God afflicts us and which we suffer patiently;”33 in a word, by cheerfully bearing the cross of every day.

Sacrifice is fruitful This spirit of penance is the beginning of a new life. Jesus began his preaching with a call to penance: Repent, for the kingdom of heaven is

32 Ibid. 33 Council of Trent, Decree De sacramento paenitentiae, ch. 9 85 A time for penance

at hand.34 St Peter likewise began again after he had denied our Lord three times: and he went out and wept bitterly.35 And St Paul did the same after Jesus had thrown him to the ground and he was led blind into Damascus: and for three days he was without sight, and neither ate nor drank.36 This is the good foundation on which all the saints have begun the task of their sanctification. The edifice of the interior life is unstable until the soul sees clearly its own iniquity and until, in Confession and in the fraternal talk, there is a continual and heartfelt expression of contrition. You are crying? Don’t be ashamed of it. Yes, cry: men also cry like you, when they are alone and before God. Each night, says King David, I soak my bed with tears. With those tears, those burning manly tears, you can purify your past and supernaturalize your present life.37 A day of recollection or a retreat provides a great opportunity to get to know oneself better, to be converted again and to increase one’s sorrow and contrition. Hide your face from my sins, and blot out all my iniquities.38 God is moved. He forgets all our sins and wipes them out with his grace. “The Almighty happily forgets that we have been bad. He is ready to consider our penance as innocence.”39 The new edifice erected by God’s grace is reinforced by deeds of penance. “The sufferings of satisfaction greatly withdraw the soul from sin and hold it like a brake, and make it more cautious in the future. They also provide a remedy for the remains of sin, and through the action of the virtues they take away bad habits.”40 That is why it is so

34 Mt 4:17 35 Mt 26:75 36 Acts 9:9 37 The Way, 216 38 Ps 51:9 39 St Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels, 25, 10 40 Council of Trent, Decree De sacramento paenitentiae, ch. 8

Along the Path to Love 86

regrettable that nowadays the word penance is almost unknown, and whenever it is mentioned it frightens many people, like a bad dream from the past. Nevertheless, the only foundation for true peace and happiness lies in humility, love-sorrow and contrition. My daughters and sons, I want you to be happy. I am, although I shouldn’t be, when I see my poor life. But I am, because I realize that God is looking for us once again, that he is still our Father; and I know that you and I will discover what has to be uprooted in our souls, and we will make up our minds to uproot it; and we will see what has to be burned and we will burn it, and what has to be given up and we will give it up.41 May those who sow in tears reap with shouts of joy.42 When we see ourselves in the presence of God, and we realize he is moved by our efforts, that he forgives and loves us, why wouldn’t we be happy? Restore to me the joy of your salvation.43 Our Lord gives us the joy of salvation abundantly. And together with joy he gives us the intimate peace of his friendship. For the moment all discipline seems painful rather than pleasant; later it yields the peaceful fruit of righteousness to those who have been trained by it.44 Our true contentment lies there in God’s mercy; he is a good Father and he purifies our miseries with the fire of his Love. Have mercy on me, O God, according to your steadfast love; according to your abundant mercy blot out my transgressions.45 When, repentant, we raise our voice and call on the divine mercy, expressing our contrition with penance, we bring to life the parable of the prodigal

41 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 62 42 Ps 126:5 43 Ps 51:12 44 Heb 12:11 45 Ps 51:1 87 A time for penance

son: conscience-stricken, he arose and came to his father. But while he was yet at a distance, his father saw him and had compassion, and ran and embraced him and kissed him.46 The penitent soul is also grateful because it is humble. Thank our Lord, he is our protector and liberator. Don’t stop to consider whether your faults are big or small. Consider the vastness of God’s forgiveness. Consider that the guilt could have been great, and give thanks, because God has been – and is – ready to forgive.47 Understanding, tasty fruit of the mortified soul,48 is expressed in having for others the same merciful compassion God has towards us. With this new love we can easily see beyond the differences between us. We treat our neighbour with an affection that wins even the most wary heart. Then I will teach transgressors your ways, and sinners will return to you.49 The spirit of penance, which is love for Christ on the Cross, is a condition for apostolic effectiveness. Like the grain of wheat, my children, we need to die to be fruitful. You and I don’t want to be alone. We want to multiply our family and leave a furrow that is bright and deep ... To be apostles, we have to carry Christ crucified within us, as St Paul describes.50 Like Christ on the Cross, we have to make expiation for those who do not do so, to merit for those who do not earn merit, and to support those who collapse. We need a spirit of mortification and penance, then, as a “means of purification and of true and solid spiritual progress; as a practical demonstration of love for Jesus Christ, qui dilexit me et tradidit

46 Lk 15:20 47 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 48 From our Father, Crónica, February 1962, p.16 49 Ps 51:13 50 From our Father, Meditation Prayer of the senses, 13 April 1954, in While he spoke to us on the way, pp. 52-53

Along the Path to Love 88

semetipsum pro me, who loved us and gave himself up on the Cross for each one of us; and finally, they are a preparation for all apostolate and a means for the perfect carrying out of any apostolate.”51 May our Mother Mary, Refugium peccatorum, Mater misericordiae, obtain for us from our Lord a sensitive conscience, a deep and supernatural vision of things, a heart that abounds in love-sorrow, and a spirit that is generous in deeds of penance.

51 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 123

## THE VALUE OF DIFFICULTIES {#cuaderno.8.7}

When we joined the Work it was made clear to us from the start that holiness and apostolate were not going to be an easy business. As far as recent vocations are concerned, our Father wrote that it is important to tell them clearly that, on coming to the Work, they’re not coming to Mount Thabor: they’re coming to Calvary. Non est discipulus super magistrum, a disciple is not above his teacher (Mt 10:24). They will be the butt of gossip and criticism. Their noble behaviour may become the object of snide remarks. It may happen either to them, or to the Work, or to us all – it has happened before, God allows it – that they encounter the opposition of the good, who, with the best will in the world, go around inventing and spreading falsehoods.1 The universal disorder due to sin is the reason why we can expect trials and difficulties of all sorts, and we would be deceiving ourselves if we were to think otherwise. The normal thing is that sooner or later they make their appearance, because no great ideal is ever obtained without effort. It is precisely in this struggle that we acquire and develop virtue, which forms the character and tempers the will.

An everyday occurrence No man, whether he be a Christian or not, has an easy life. To be

1 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935, 283

Along the Path to Love 90

sure, at certain times it seems as though everything goes as we had planned. But this generally lasts for only a short time. Life is a matter of facing up to difficulties and of experiencing in our hearts both joy and sorrow. It is in this forge that man can acquire fortitude, patience, magnanimity and composure.2 We have to avoid falling into the situation described by St Gregory the Great: “There are some who want to be humble, but without being despised; to be content with their lot, but without suffering want; to be chaste, but without mortifying their bodies; to be patient, but without being ill-treated. In trying to acquire virtue, while fleeing the toils that virtue supposes, it is as if, not wanting to experience anything of the strife of battle, they hoped to win the war while dwelling comfortably in the city.”3 It is naïve to expect victory without struggle. In the crypt of the Prelatic Church of Our Lady Queen of Peace our Father placed the following inscription, inspired by a phrase of St Paul: semper in corde habentes quod nemo coronabitur nisi qui legitime certaverit,4 always remembering that no one will be crowned without having fought honourably. Because, as St Augustine states, “in this pilgrimage of our present life there cannot fail to be temptations, because our improvement is achieved through temptation, and nobody knows himself unless he is tempted, nor can he be crowned unless he has conquered, nor can he conquer unless he has fought, nor can he fight unless he has had temptations, and an enemy to fight with.”5 Just like in the apostolate itself, there are always difficulties in the spiritual life: “because although the piety of good people desires the

2 Friends of God, 77 3 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 7, 28, 34 4 Cf 2 Tim 2:5 5 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos, 60, 3 91 The value of difficulties

conversion of the wicked, and indeed, through God’s merciful grace, does achieve the conversion of many, nevertheless, the snares of the evil spirits against the saints never cease, and so, either surreptitiously or openly, they lay waste the ambitions of the faithful of good will.”6 Things have always been like this: the history of all great apostolates is also the history of the great struggles and tribulations of God’s faithful servants. That is how it has been since the Redemption wrought by Christ on Calvary, and that is how it will be as long as the world lasts.

Supernatural outlook Since Christ overcame sin on the wood of the Cross, things like pain, tribulation and difficulty all have a positive meaning for the Christian. And yet there is a kind of fear around, a fear of the Cross, of our Lord’s Cross. What has happened is that people have begun to regard as crosses all the unpleasant things of life, and they don’t know how to accept them as God’s children should, which is with supernatural outlook. So much so that they are even removing the roadside crosses set up by our forefathers ... In the Passion, the Cross ceased to be a symbol of punishment and became instead a sign of victory. The Cross is the emblem of the Redeemer: in quo est salus, vita et resurrectio nostra: there lies our salvation, our life and our resurrection.7 Our Founder commented that, even though things are as clear as that, at times the Christian people forget it. And I believe that that was why our Lord chose, on that 14th of February 1943, to place the Cross in our hearts; he chose to crown the façade of his Work with the Cross, and wished us to bear that symbol, the Cross, placed at the heart of the world.8

6 St Leo the Great, Homilies, 70, 5 7 The Way of the Cross, Second Station, 5 8 From our Father, Crónica, November 1960, p.8

Along the Path to Love 92

God’s son or daughter, Christ’s brother or sister, has to embrace the Cross lovingly and see pain as the true expression and touchstone of love, as St Peter announced: beloved, do not be surprised at the fiery ordeal which comes upon you to prove you, as though something strange were happening to you.9 The path Christ has shown us passes via the Cross: there it becomes possible to co-redeem. This is what our Father taught us: Is it not true that as soon as you cease to be afraid of the Cross, of what people call the cross, when you set your will to accept the Will of God, then you find happiness, and all your worries, all your sufferings, physical or moral, pass away? Truly the Cross of Jesus is gentle and lovable. There, sorrows cease to count; there is only the joy of knowing that we are coredeemers with Him.10 Moreover, the Christian has to love pain because it is a means of purification, atonement and penance, both for his or her own sins and for the sins of others. The Christian has to view hostility in the light of faith and bear it with the help of the theological virtue of hope. It is not just a question of waiting for the Cross to arrive: it has to be sought out actively. This revolution in outlook is itself a guarantee that one will manage to bear up, and that in fact everything is quite achievable.

Sportsmanship and optimism in the ascetical struggle The spirit of the Work teaches us to bear difficulties readily and cheerfully. We know well that among the virtues we have to cultivate especially are “optimism, cheerfulness, toughness and courage”.11 These four virtues determine our attitude to obstacles, from within and from without.

9 1 Pet 4:12 10 The Way of the Cross, Second Station 11 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 129 93 The value of difficulties

Our Father wanted us to have a sporting attitude to life, both in earthly affairs and in the spiritual sphere, so as to embark on all the undertakings of soul and body with the eagerness of a person who looks forward to winning. To you, who like sports, the Apostle’s argument should appeal: Nescitis quod ii qui in stadio currunt omnes quidem currunt, sed unus accipit bravium? Sic currite ut comprehendatis. All the runners in the stadium are trying to win, but only one of them gets the prize. You must run in the same way, meaning to win.12 It is a whole attitude to life – cheerful, composed, lively, youthful – whereby we perceive the goal, and keep up our daily training, knowing that everything contributes to the final victory. Nowadays there is so much interest in sport that it has become practically a cult ... and the athletes all train to keep fit. We too keep fit by fighting in little things. If we don’t, we haven’t a chance. We have to be fit! Life has all the appeal and excitement of sport.13 Difficulties, great or small, should act as a spur, a hurdle that has to be jumped. Brethren, wrote St Paul, I do not consider that I have made it my own; but one thing I do, forgetting what lies behind and straining forward to what lies ahead, I press on toward the goal for the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus.14 And, after many years of hardships, he again wrote: I have fought the good fight, I have finished the race, I have kept the faith. Henceforth there is laid up for me the crown of righteousness, which the Lord, the righteous judge, will award to me on that Day, and not only to me but also to all who have loved his appearing.15 It is easier to overcome the obstacles in our path if, in addition to

12 The Way, 318 13 From our Father, Crónica, November 1960, p.10 14 Phil 3:13-14 15 2 Tim 4:7-8

Along the Path to Love 94

being sportsmanlike, we have a good sense of humour. Moreover the joyful love which makes the soul glad is based on suffering, a sense of duty, and the happiness of going against the grain.16 The joyful good humour with which we try to bear difficulties is evident, naturally, in our Centres and in our lives; this, the omnia in bonum which our Father taught us, is a very powerful instrument in apostolate and winning vocations. That is how God has to be served. Without joy we cannot serve. Can you imagine someone who weeps and wails being of use to you? In our central house in Rome I have had placed an inscription with the words servite Domino in laetitia, serve the Lord with gladness (Ps 100:2). Be happy always, even at the hour of death: happy to live and happy to die. With God’s grace we shouldn’t be afraid of life, nor should we be afraid of death either. We have no reason whatsoever to be sad: we leave that to those who are not children of God!17 Nobody should think that this joy of ours is a function of temperament or is perhaps acquired with time. For us cheerfulness is essentially supernatural: it is not connected with temperament nor with one’s state of health. It has equally to imbue youth, maturity and old age and, as our Father often put it so graphically, it is a joy whose roots are in the shape of a cross.18 In a word, our cheerfulness is based on divine filiation, it thrives on love, under the light of faith, and it is protected by hope. And so in our spirituality to lose our good humour is something serious.19

16 Alone with God, 236 17 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 69 18 Christ is passing by, 43 19 From our Father, Crónica, November 1960, p.11 95 The value of difficulties

Being objective In addition to a spirit of sportsmanship and cheerfulness, we also need to be straightforward enough to avoid complicating life unnecessarily by inventing crosses for ourselves. Although in the normal course of events we can always expect to have problems, at times we could tend to exaggerate things somewhat and give too much importance to difficulties in the apostolate or in our own interior life. It could happen, for example, that at a given moment prospects seem particularly bleak, or we have the impression that nothing is being done properly, or that in the case of a particular individual or apostolic undertaking nothing seems to be happening. In summary, we could become completely pessimistic. This reaction can be attributed principally to having lost the awareness of divine filiation, and often too our objectivity, or because we are too impressionable. In a word, it is due to a lack of perspective. It is like looking at a small cut under a microscope: all one can observe is the small area under the lens, and the rest of the body, the healthy part, is not seen at all. Because of our distorted perspective we fail to appreciate all the positive features of the individual or the apostolate in question, and we also lose sight of the full extension and apostolic fruitfulness of the Work. The less humble one is, the more one tends to rely on one’s own powers; as a result, the more helpless one then feels when beset by problems and the more one exaggerates their importance and thereby justifies shying away from them. Our Father warned us: there is one enemy you have to watch out for: pessimism. It can afflict people who are children of God but who, in extreme cases provoked by pride or a sort of esprit de corps, fail to realize that they are ut iumentum, like a donkey. You know what I say about donkeys: the more blows they get the more they work. Our attitude has to be such that if at times there Along the Path to Love 96

are problems, we don’t get discouraged.20 This is true in working with souls, and in the tasks entrusted to us, and also in our own spiritual life. Discouragement is a consequence of an excessively worldly way of looking at events. That is why we have to ask our Lord for light to make us share in his vision of things. We need a more elevated, wideranging and far-seeing viewpoint to help us appreciate all the positive aspects of things, to study the problem in its proper perspective, and to come up with the best means of overcoming it. This supernatural insight lets us study things in context and see the relative importance of events so we can assign them their proper significance. Above all it helps us identify the positive features of something which at first sight might seem to be an unmitigated disaster, and thereby avoids us becoming totally pessimistic. We can then examine the situation calmly, which is a characteristic of mature people and something our Founder wished all of us to have, even the youngest. You have to have the poise, the fortitude and the sense of responsibility that many get only with the passage of time, in old age. You will have all that, even in youth, if you don’t lose the supernatural sense of being a son of God; because it is He who will give you, more than to your elders, the qualities you need for your task as an apostle.21 Then one tries calmly to pinpoint the precise aspect which isn’t going quite as well as it should: it may be, for example, that along with many good qualities, the soul has a particular defect that is hindering its progress towards holiness. This has to be identified, though without losing one’s optimism and good spirits. My children, you have to see the positive side of things. The things that appear most depressing in life are never really quite as bad as they seem. If you examine them

20 From our Father, Meditation, 9 June 1960 21 From our Father, Meditation, 17 February 1959 97 The value of difficulties

closely enough you won’t come to negative conclusions. Just as a good doctor never tells a patient that his situation is hopeless, I ask you, for the love of Christ sake, to be optimistic. Don’t make negative comments without seeing the other side too. Just because someone is sick doesn’t mean that he’s a corpse ready for the cemetery; let’s try to give him the proper treatment and heal him. In the spirit of the Work we have all the medication we need.22 The ailment is tracked down and, once it is diagnosed, is given the proper treatment. This applies also to apostolic tasks when, together with lots of grace and perhaps flourishing developments, there is some obstruction, either internal or external, which hasn’t been taken into account sufficiently and which can be improved.

Everything can be cured The causes of unhappiness and lack of peace for the most part exist only in the mind and are generally related to a lack of humility. Our spirit requires us to be simple enough not to see calamities where they don’t exist, to evaluate difficulties objectively, not to give undue importance to things that don’t warrant it, and to trust always in our Father God. To help someone who is going through a bad patch we have to act as doctors do. We each have to encourage the people around us and give life to the work entrusted to us. We are there to cure people: we wouldn’t be needed if everything were dead or if everything were healthy, because those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick.23 God has conferred on us, as on his first Apostles, the power to heal every disease and every infirmity24 in the spiritual

22 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 14 23 Lk 5:31 24 Mt 10:1

Along the Path to Love 98

sphere, a power which we exercise with the help of the Holy Spirit, to whom the Church directs this triple invocation: Heal our wounds, our strength renew; on our dryness pour your dew; wash the stains of guilt away.25 The first thing a doctor does is examine the patient. The sick person isn’t dead, he’s alive, but neither is he totally healthy, because there is something giving rise to a general lack of well-being. The doctor can’t be blindly or foolishly optimistic, and neither can we. Faith, cheerfulness, optimism. But not the idiocy of closing one’s eyes to reality.26 The doctor tries to identify the source of the infection (if infection it is) by a detailed analysis and a complete examination, and only then does he give his diagnosis of the malady and the treatment to be followed. He then prescribes the correct medicine to combat the infection directly or to strengthen the body’s defences. The choice of treatment is followed by its application, and it lasts as long as necessary until the sick person is completely cured. It is important, though, to be patient with invalids, because it is only natural that at times they find things difficult, all the more so if the treatment is an unpleasant one. Those in attendance have to be forgiving and understanding, with a patience that arises from the love of souls itself. Love is patient and kind,27 and “without charity there cannot be true patience.”28 Patience is also needed when trying to find a remedy for problems in the apostolate, because apostolic activities have their own biology too: at times they take a while to get going, and may need to be allowed to mature until they reach their proper level of development.

25 Sequence Veni, Sancte Spiritus 26 The Way, 40 27 1 Cor 13:4 28 St Augustine, De patientia, 23 99 The value of difficulties

Benefits of this way of acting There are many, indeed innumerable, benefits to be obtained from difficulties. If we are faithful nothing can harm us. Now who is there to harm you if you are zealous for what is right? But even if you do suffer for righteousness’ sake, you will be blessed.29 God always gives us all the grace we need, not only to survive unscathed, but to be very successful.
