This is especially so given that we have entered the fray for love of his Name. “The Christian has been born to fight, and the more hard-pressed he finds himself, the surer he is, with God’s help, of obtaining the victory.”30 In the spiritual life the struggle against difficulties strengthens the soul. “Trees that grow in shady places protected from the wind superficially seem to grow very well, but they become soft and yielding and can be very easily damaged; trees that grow on the tops of very high mountains, by contrast, where they are buffeted by frequent and strong winds, constantly exposed to all sorts of inclement weather, struck by mighty storms and frequently covered with snow, become hardier than iron.”31 The soul beset by hardship is strengthened by divine grace and becomes generous, patient, unconquerable, robust and constant. We always have to see the trials of life as an unmatchable opportunity for growth. Count it all joy, my brethren, when you meet various trials, for you know that the testing of your faith produces steadfastness. And let steadfastness have its full effect, that you may be perfect and complete, lacking in nothing.32 The same happens in our apostolic activities. In apostolate and proselytism we will always encounter difficulties and misunderstand-

29 1 Pet 3:13-14 30 Leo XIII, Encyclical Sapientiae christianae, 19 31 St John Chrysostom, Homilia de gloria in tribulationibus 32 Jas 1:2-4

Along the Path to Love 100

ings, but we should remember that obstacles are part of God’s providence for strengthening those who receive a vocation to Opus Dei, and for sanctifying all.33 And, indeed, opposition is the best guarantee of soundness in the apostolate. A task which presents no difficulties lacks human appeal – and supernatural appeal too. If you find no resistance when hammering a nail into a wall, what can you expect to 34 Apostolate done this way, overcoming obstacles, hang on it? cultivated in the shadow of Christ’s Cross and participating in his merits, is assured; it is blessed by God. And so for us opposition is something positive; it fills us with optimism and makes us react vigorously, with a sporting and cheerful spirit, with love and faith, with hunger for the fruit. Love! Suffer joyfully. Strengthen your soul. Harden your will. Assure your self-giving and, with it, your effectiveness.35

33 From our Father, Crónica, November 1960, p.13 34 The Forge, 245 35 From our Father, Meditation, 15 April 1954

## ACTS OF CONTRITION {#cuaderno.8.8}

God in his love has chosen us, my beloved daughters and sons, for this way of the Work, a way forever youthful and new, a human and divine adventure, a means of co-redemption with Christ, and a close and intimate participation in Jesus’ impatient longing to spread the fire that he had come to cast on the earth (cf Lk 12:49).1 With God’s grace, with our own effort, and the help of all our brothers and sisters in the Work, we gradually achieve that identification with Christ which is the goal of all holiness. The task is so lengthy it cannot be completed in a few years: it is the work of a lifetime. With every day that passes the contemplative soul resembles Christ more and more and becomes more deeply identified with him through prayer and the sacraments. The soul becomes deified. Its life is a complete contrast with what it was before and what it so often encounters around it!2 But it knows that only in heaven will that identification be perfect. During our earthly voyage we sometimes encounter spells of fair weather, internal or external, at times prolonged; but only in heaven is peace definitive and tranquillity complete.3 For as long as we travel we feel the burden of our condition: shortcomings to be sorted out and tackled, character defects apparently overcome but

1 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 1 2 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 3 3 Ibid., 9

Along the Path to Love 102

which suddenly seem to sprout anew, and invitations of grace to which we seem incapable of responding generously. As a consequence of our fallen nature we find ourselves hesitating between good and evil, between sin and virtue, between selfcentredness and self-surrender. We cannot forget, our Father reminds us, that original sin makes us proni ad peccatum, prone to sin. We are grafted onto that first fallen couple. We have a great propensity to fall. We can taste in our mouths the full flavour of that inheritance. Propter peccata, on account of our sins, original and personal, we experience all those feelings which are not of peace; those faults, in fact, because human nature is very prone to error.4 But the correct treatment is also readily available: acts of atonement and contrition, one of the norms of always of our plan of life.

Awareness of our own wretchedness As a consequence of the law of sin man bears within him, it may happen that, after many years serving God and trying to fulfil the Norms well, someone suddenly experiences all the wretchedness of the human condition particularly intensely. Obedience, which up to then had been exemplary, or at any rate willing, could become an enormous burden that clashes with the uncontrollable urge to revolt. Perhaps too, critical spirit, which seemed to have been definitively beaten, springs up again, mordant and full of bitterness. Small aspects of self-surrender could become extremely irksome. At the same time the heart may find itself being attracted to things which it had previously renounced, and keeping it entirely for God may entail continuous struggle and effort. Nor would it be unusual if character defects – one’s own and other people’s – began to loom so large as to become practically unbearable.

4 From our Father, Meditation, 5 November 1967 103 Acts of Contrition

The sudden awareness of our wretchedness should never surprise us. It is a normal occurrence in the spiritual life, one we are quite likely to experience at some stage or other. But we have to be on our guard, because there is a danger of reacting in a merely human way, and the consequences of that would be disastrous. One could easily tend to place all the blame for failure on the environment or some particular circumstance. The soul that succumbs to this temptation makes itself incapable of being helped, because it blocks the path of humility, which is its only route to God; consequently, all its resolutions to improve are fruitless again and again. Furthermore, the pressure of having to fight ceaselessly while seeming to get nowhere creates a state of stress which extinguishes peace, serenity and joy. The examination of conscience can then easily give rise to anxiety. This usually happens when sin hurts not so much because it is an offence against God, but rather because in it the soul directly experiences its own wretchedness. We have been warned of this danger by our Father: In this intimate struggle, in this ascetical war that we wage within ourselves, my children, we may lose our peace because of the faults we commit. If you find yourselves thinking about those faults and infidelities, consider also what our Lord tells you through the mouth of Jeremiah: ego cogito cogitationes pacis et non afflictionis (Jer 29:11); my thoughts are thoughts of peace and not of affliction. He says these words to you because you need them. To think anything else would be pride.5 God wants us to be happy and contented. Our ascetical struggle is not supposed to make us anxious and scrupulous but to fill us with peace. When we are not at peace we should try to find the reason for it, and that reason is pride. For it is possible to detest sin and all its fruits, and be fighting to reach perfection, and yet be more or less consciously

5 Ibid.

Along the Path to Love 104

motivated by the unhealthy desire to have everything ‘in order’ with God, to owe him nothing.

Growing in humility The right way to act, in such a situation, does not consist in redoubling one’s efforts and continuing to struggle with all one’s might to construct the edifice of virtue, come what may. It would call for a titanic effort which in any case would be impossible to maintain for long, because sooner or later sadness and discouragement would set in. The right way to go about it is in fact just the opposite: to dig down into the foundations and lay as the base of everything a true and deep humility. If your humility makes you feel like that – filth, a heap of filth! – we may yet turn all your weaknesses into something really great.6 There is no point trying to fight harder and harder unless we are completely ready to acknowledge our faults, because God opposes the proud, but gives grace to the humble.7 Happiness, peace and joy arise only when there is sincere repentance and deep sorrow. We have to admit, without trying to make excuses, that we are really and truly sinners and have offended God in this thing and that. And this should be so not just in special circumstances, like after a spectacular fall, but constantly, as a reaction to the smallest lapse of generosity or forgetfulness. We should be deeply grieved by the least sign of lack of love for God, who loves us so much and who always forgives us whenever we return to him with a humble and contrite heart.8 When I declared not my sin, says the psalmist, my body wasted away through my groaning all day long. For day and night your hand

6 The Way, 605 7 1 Pet 5:5 8 Cf Ps 51:17 105 Acts of Contrition

was heavy upon me; my strength was dried up as by the heat of summer. I acknowledged my sin to you, and I did not hide my iniquity; I said, ‘I will confess my transgressions to the Lord;’ then you did forgive the guilt of my sin.9 Our faults then cease to be a burden which makes us falter. And if in spite of everything these thoughts still haunt us, perhaps because we are out of sorts, or tired, or short of sleep, then it is the moment to reflect on what God tells us: invocabitis me, et ego exaudiam vos (Jer 29:12): call upon me and I will hear you. Is there any father or mother who has a bigger or more wonderful heart than that?10 There is also the danger that, while acknowledging our shortcomings readily enough, we might come to regard them as inevitable. This would be tantamount to shirking our responsibility; we would be making excuses for ourselves. It might conceivably permit us acquire a certain degree of equanimity, but it would be merely apparent, because it could never be the gaudium cum pace which is the fruit of humility and truth. If one is not humble, deeply humble, says our Father, it is easy to get to the stage of deforming one’s conscience. It could well happen that, through weakness, we make mistakes at times. But let’s have clear ideas and a clear conscience. What we cannot allow is to do things that are wrong and say they are holy.11 The way we should react to our faults is to humble ourselves and ask God for forgiveness with sincere repentance. In what can we glory? asks our Father. All I have are my inadequacies. If I have anything of value it is God’s. And this is not false humility. With my human intelligence, in the presence of God, all I can see is wretchedness. I see it as clearly as two and two are four. But God’s grace is there too:

9 Ps 32:3-5 10 From our Father, Meditation, 5 November 1967 11 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 37

Along the Path to Love 106

omnia possum in eo qui me confortat: I can do all things in him who strengthens me (Phil 4:13). So I fight to make them divine. Any one who struggles is on the road to holiness. My children, make good use of those insights from God because, as you have heard me say many times, our Lord does not will our defects, but he does make use of them for our humility and our sanctification.12 Our faults make us see ourselves as we really are: you are wretched, pitiable, poor, blind, and naked.13 They make us feel completely inadequate, like the servant in the parable who was unable to pay his lord.14 And so we need a basic element of humility, so we can say after every fall, big or small: miserere mei, Deus: have mercy on me, O God, according to thy steadfast love.15 The humble soul realizes it should ask God’s pardon many times a day. Every time it strays from the right path it feels the need to turn to God with genuine sorrow, like the prodigal son in the parable: Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you; I am no longer worthy to be called your son; treat me as one of your hired servants.16 And God, “who is close to those of contrite heart,”17 hears that humble prayer. “He would have died the most wretched of deaths,” says St John Chrysostom of the prodigal son. “But since he repented and did not lose hope, after such great degradation he returned to his former eminence. He was clothed with the finest garments, and received greater honour than the son who had never fallen.”18 For God’s mercy is without limits. In the Work no one has the job of mortifying any one else. If

12 From our Father, Get-together, 9 November 1966 13 Rev 3:17 14 Cf Mt 18:23-27 15 Ps 51:1 16 Lk 15:18-19 17 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus, 15, 25 18 St John Chrysostom, Exhortatio ad Theodorum lapsum 1, 7 107 Acts of Contrition

someone humiliates us without meaning to, without realizing it, and receives fraternal correction from someone else and comes to apologize for that little thing, how wonderful! We receive him with open arms ... Well, it’s unthinkable that God our Lord would be more petty-minded than you or I! God wants us to have peace, and he is waiting for us to turn to him anew and tell him we’re sorry. That’s what makes me so happy.19

The path of contrition We cannot be blind to the existence of our faults, nor lose peace of mind because of them, nor accept them with a shrug just because we are tired of fighting against them. Humility gives us a firm foundation that permits us to fight on without getting discouraged or giving in. And to attain it we have a sure path, which is the path of contrition. To this royal road the soul of true interior life returns constantly throughout life; it is always completely indispensable, both in youth and in old age. This is exactly what our Father taught us: I have often told you that acts of contrition are the best of all devotions, and that I always have to come back like the prodigal son. We have no reason to drag a trail of wretchedness behind us. Put it in God’s hands, and, like St Peter after his denials, say to him in all humility: Domine, tu omnia nosti, tu scis quia amo te! (Jn 21:17), Lord, you know that I love you in spite of my weaknesses. That way our miseries don’t separate us from God. Instead, they lead us to him, just as the child who falls headlong doesn’t get separated from his mother. Mummy! he shouts, and runs to her arms; or, if he is a little older, to his father’s, which he knows are stronger. If we have done

19 From our Father, Meditation, 5 November 1967

Along the Path to Love 108

something wrong, big or small, let’s go running to God! Cor contritum et humiliatum, Deus, non despicies: a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise (Ps 51:17).20 The bad thing is not to be full of failings, but rather to stop fighting against them. Do not be surprised, writes our Father in one of his Letters, for a righteous man falls seven times, and rises again (Prov 24:16). In our spiritual combat there will be no shortage of failures. But when faced with our blunders and mistakes, we should react immediately by making an act of contrition, which comes to our heart and lips as quickly as blood rushes to a wound, successfully resisting the foreign body, the germ of infection.21 If we have the misfortune to offend our Lord gravely – for our wretchedness can go that far – and even if we fall many times, once the moment of darkness or weakness has passed, we will always go to our brother the priest, who will pardon us in the name of Jesus Christ. And with the grace of the sacrament we will return, stronger than ever, to do battle once more. But we must go to the sacrament of penance truly repentant and contrite, and resolved to use the means to avoid falling again into the faults we confess. The awareness of our divine filiation makes it easy for us to return gratefully, certain of being received by our Father God.22 And the experience of always being received with fatherly benevolence makes us keenly aware of what it is to be a son or daughter of God and moves us to an even greater sorrow. Just as a mother forgets about her son’s previous thoughtlessness when he greets her affectionately, so too does Jesus overlook the things we have done wrong when we finally go to Him affectionately, repentant, purified by the sacrament of penance.23

20 Ibid. 21 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 11 22 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 28 23 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 59 109 Acts of Contrition

Drawing strength from one another The holy lives of our brothers and sisters, who struggle to serve God, are also an encouragement to us to desire to be better and have recourse to the divine mercy. When you feel yourself truly humiliated by your faults, our Father wrote, do what Moses did (cf Exodus 32). He placed the merits of others before the Lord; he offered the merits of the people. Many times I offer, in the presence of God, the virtues and acts of service of my children. I usually say: Lord, ne respicias peccata mea, sed fidem: look not upon my sins, but upon my faith. Afterwards I add: Look at these sons and daughters, see how they love you, how joyfully they serve you, how zealously they spread your Name and your Love throughout the world, and how readily and faithfully they obey Holy Church and the Pope!24 When humility leads us to see the virtues of others contrasted with our own defects, we are then better able to appreciate how affectionately and compassionately people treat us and how little we deserve it. And we in turn become more tolerant with their shortcomings and love them as they are, with their defects. We learn to have a big heart, which precisely because of its own imperfections knows how to make allowances for other people’s. This is the only way to be charitable – really tolerant – to be continually working away at our own defects and failings and those of others. And we can even make use of these defects and failings as a means of helping the apostolate.25 This ensures that we never feel unconcerned about other people’s defects or react disapprovingly to them, but instead feel moved to help them with our prayer, mortification and affection, and are saddened by them only insofar as they signify a lack of love of God. What should really hurt us about sin, our own as well as others’, is that it offends

24 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 26 25 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 46

Along the Path to Love 110

God. My children, said our Father: let us ask God for forgiveness for our mistakes, for the mistakes of those who form our pusillus grex, our little flock. Miserere mihi, Deus; attende, Domine; miserere, quia peccavimus tibi: have mercy on me, O God; give heed, O Lord; have mercy, for we have sinned against thee. We are obliged to be ipse Christus, and he is the Lamb of God, who takes away the sins of the world. Our dedicated life should be a means whereby we blot out from God’s sight our own sins and those of others.26

Peace and happiness Contrition eliminates the victim mentality from our lives. There is but one Victim, Christ our Lord on the Cross.27 It causes us to forget about ourselves and leaves us wanting only to make atonement. That noble desire restores our peace and joy if we ever lose them. Our Founder often mentioned this to us: How do we avoid losing our peace? I have told you before: by having a right intention. And now I add: by making acts of contrition. Acts of contrition improve the spiritual life and confer peace and serenity, and at times can even improve one’s physical well-being. I think that this is why those who have interior life according to our spirituality are necessarily well-balanced people and are less inclined to psychological illnesses, because they place themselves in the hands of God, who leads them along the right path.28 And he adds: Do you want to be more at peace? Well, make acts of contrition. Do you want to feel even better physically? Make more acts of contrition.29 Peace and joy, gaudium cum pace, is the reward God bestows on

26 Ibid., note 122 27 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 15 28 From our Father, Meditation, 5 November 1967 29 Ibid.

111 Acts of Contrition

the repentant and contrite soul. My daughters and sons, I want you to be happy. I am, although I shouldn’t be, when I see my poor life. But I am, because I realize that God is searching us out once again, that he is still our Father.30 We cannot but be optimistic. In this way not even the lost battles are lost. On the contrary, each new wire brace is like a medal, and we should be humble enough not to hide them: earthenware vessels that have been repaired with wire braces are even more charming in God’s sight than new ones (...) And what do we gain from this peace and serenity, this state of soul that feels itself a child of God? It leads us to live abundantly the charity of Christ and communicate it to others.31 It doesn’t matter if we make mistakes provided that, having repented, we are willing to begin anew as often as necessary. I have told you many times, and I will never tire of repeating it, that the spiritual life contains many incidents like those of Naim. To be raised from the dead: what is that except to begin and begin again? In the spiritual life we are continually coming to life again through acts of contrition, sorrow and reparation.32 Acts of atonement are one of the norms of always, and they need to begin taking shape in our lives from the very start of our vocation, because they safeguard interior peace and perseverance. God tends to lead souls, above all at the beginning, and at times perhaps for years, through less stormy seas, in order to consolidate them in their initial decision, without asking them at the beginning for what they cannot give, because they are still sicut modo geniti infantes (1 Pet 2:2), like newborn babes.33 But this doesn’t exclude the possibility that, as

30 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 62 31 From our Father, Meditation, 5 November 1967 32 From our Father, Crónica, July 1965, pp.45-46 33 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 5

Along the Path to Love 112

they progress in the love of God and in self-knowledge, they begin to see more clearly faults which before they gave less importance to. And so recent vocations should learn from the beginning to practise a spirit of penance, because otherwise they could fall into that situation our Father warns us about: My daughters and sons, I cannot conceal from you the fear that in some cases that deification, without a deep basis of humility, could give rise to presumption, to the corruption of true hope, to pride, and sooner or later to spiritual ruin, on being faced with the unexpected experience of one’s own frailty.34 The best way to prevent possible conflicts and to assure temporal and eternal happiness is always to see ourselves as poor sinners before God and make frequent acts of contrition. By making many acts of contrition every day, especially at the examination of conscience, we keep our dedication always youthful and vibrant and forestall any unease and tension that could kill the desire to be saints. Always end your examination, our Father recommends us, with an act of Love – of Love-sorrow: for yourself, for all the sins of mankind. And consider the fatherly care of God in removing the obstacles in your way lest you should stumble.35 The soul’s final goal is identification with Christ. This is the course we must always steer, both in fair weather and in storms. As our Father said: I like to see gardening being done. I have often seen a public square turned into a flower garden in forty-eight hours! We too are a wilderness that must be turned into a flower garden, a little branch that should give sweet and abundant fruit. And for that, my children, we must graft ourselves onto the tree that is Jesus Christ. We need to live in Christ, with Christ, for Christ, in spite of our frailties.36

34 Ibid., 4 35 The Way, 246 36 From our Father, Get-together, 7 January 1964 113 Acts of Contrition

The best way to attain that identification with Christ is by acts of contrition. And if our faults ever seem to weigh heavily on us, we should have recourse to Mary, because she never abandons us, on this path of service to her Son Jesus in the midst of the world.37 Let us go, then, to Our Lady, so that she obtains for us from the Blessed Trinity more grace, the grace of faith, hope, love and contrition, so we can exclaim with St Peter: Domine, tu omnia nosti, tu scis quia amo te: Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you (Jn 21:17).38

37 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 63 38 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 118

## RECEIVING HOLY COMMUNION FRUITFULLY {#cuaderno.8.9}

I have earnestly desired to eat this passover with you before I suffer.1 With these words our Lord showed the Apostles, on the night of the Last Supper, the extent of the love that moved him to institute the sacrament of the Eucharist. For it is in this sacrament, under the appearances of bread and wine, that he willed to offer himself, Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity, as food for our souls. With these same sentiments he now invites every Catholic to approach Holy Communion. Contemplative souls have always been greatly inspired by the thought of Christ’s immense love in the Eucharist; it has helped them prepare to receive him worthily in the sacrament and to make a fervent thanksgiving afterwards. From an early age our Father experienced this lively, loving faith; it was part of the legacy he left us of intense love for the sacrament of the altar. Since I was a small child I understood perfectly the ‘why’ of the Eucharist. It is a feeling we all have: to want to remain always with those we love. It’s how a mother feels for her child: I could eat you with kisses, she says. I could eat you up: I could transform you into my own being. Our Lord has also said to us: Take me and eat! Nothing could be

1 Lk 22:15; cf Jn 13:1 115 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

more human than that. But we do not make God our Lord human when we receive him. It is he who makes us divine, who enriches us and exalts us. Christ can do what for us is impossible: he supernaturalizes our lives, our actions, our sacrifices. We become deified. For me, that’s more than enough: it’s the whole explanation of my life. Thank you, Jesus, thank you for having stooped so far, to the point of satisfying all the needs of our poor hearts, of these poor human hearts which can sink to unthinkable depths of betrayal and depravity.2

The wedding garment “The eternal Word of God, the nourishment and sustenance of rational creatures, by becoming flesh has also given himself as food to man.”3 Our Lord himself points out that to share in this banquet of eternal life one must approach the altar clothed in the nuptial garment of sanctifying grace.4 And St Paul, referring to this sacrament, warns: any one who eats and drinks without discerning the body eats and drinks judgment upon himself.5 Throughout the centuries the Church has insistently reiterated this law of adoration and loyalty: “lest this great sacrament be received unworthily and thus be received unto death and condemnation, this holy Council has determined and decreed that those who have mortal sin on their consciences, no matter how contrite they may think they are, must necessarily make a sacramental Confession before receiving.”6 This requirement is perfectly logical, since the Bread we receive is

2 From our Father, Crónica, 1970, p.491 3 Urban IV, Bull Transiturus de hoc mundo, 11 August 1264, Dz 847 4 Cf Mt 22:12 5 1 Cor 11:29 6 Council of Trent, Session 13, Decree De Eucharistia, canon 11, Dz 893 (1661); cf John Paul II, Letter Dominicae Coenae, 24 February 1980, 7

Along the Path to Love 116

holy and “ought not to be thrown to the dogs.”7 The soul, when bereft of grace, is dead to the supernatural life; it is like a corpse, incapable of absorbing food. Hence our Father preached insistently that we should love Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament of the Eucharist. And a sign of this love is to make a good Confession so you can approach him without the slightest hesitation. Always go to Confession first rather than risk receiving our Lord with the slightest shadow of doubt. Is this clear? If you hear anything different, be sure it is a lie.8 In addition to purity of soul, the fruitful reception of Holy Communion requires the recipient to have an adequate knowledge of the faith, and specifically, the doctrine regarding this august sacrament. The Church also requires, as a sign of respect for this food of the soul, a period of fasting. Under the present regulations this is one hour, except in the case of illness. This fast facilitates interior recollection and is an expression of the importance the Bread of heaven should have in our lives. “Live in such a way that you can receive every day,”9 exhorts St Augustine. Mindful of those who receive Communion frequently, even daily, the Church stresses the great importance of being prepared interiorly by having “a right intention and true devotion”.10 Holy Communion is certainly not a reward for virtue, but food for the Christian during this earthly pilgrimage, and that is why the Church exhorts us to receive frequently. We should never allow routine or vanity to creep into our reception of the Blessed Eucharist, because it would deprive us of a great part of the benefits to be gained from it. Strictly speaking, no one can be said to be worthy to receive this heavenly food. Moreover, neither can any one, even if habitually in the

7 Sequence Lauda Sion 8 From our Father, Get-together, 20 May 1970 9 St Augustine, Sermon 4, 1 10 St Pius X, Decree Sacra Tridentina Synodus, 20 December 1905 117 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

state of grace, avoid every possible venial sin. Like the centurion of Capernaum, a Christian becomes worthy by acknowledging his unworthiness: “Lord, I am not worthy to receive you; but only say the word and I shall be healed.”11 Our Lord knows us well. He only asks us to do what is in our power, namely, to be sorry for these lesser faults and determined to avoid them. Go to Communion. It doesn’t show lack of respect. Go this very day when you have just got over that ‘spot of trouble.’ Have you forgotten that Jesus said: It is not by those who are well, but by those who are sick, that the physician is needed.12 Among the means we have for nourishing our eucharistic piety is the night period, that time for recollection whose chief objective is to help us prepare for prayer and Mass the following day. We have been given us a lot of advice by our Father about how to prepare ourselves as well as possible. Have you ever thought how you would prepare to receive Jesus if you could only go to Holy Communion once in your life? When I was young and frequent Communion wasn’t very common, people used to prepare themselves very carefully before receiving. First, with a good Confession. Then, with a new suit, if they could; clean from head to toe. They prepared soul and body, like people in love. Nowadays we have to be grateful to our Lord for having made it so easy for us to go to Communion. But the way to thank him is to prepare ourselves very well to receive him.13 We should approach our Lord in Holy Communion adorned and spotless in all our faculties: soul and body, memory, imagination and affections. My son: tell our Lord that, from now on, every time you

11 Ordo Missae (cf Mt 8:8) 12 The Way, 536 13 From our Father, Meditation, 14 April 1960

Along the Path to Love 118

celebrate or attend Mass, every time you administer or receive the sacrament of the Eucharist, you will do so with a great faith, with a burning love, as if it were the last time in your life. Make every single word and action come alive! May you identify yourself with this Jesus-Host who is offered on the altar! Feel very close to your brothers and sisters! And so there may be nothing on earth that can separate us from God.14

Spiritual Communions During the night period, and also throughout the day, a good way to prepare ourselves for our daily sacramental encounter with Christ is by making spiritual Communions. What a source of grace there is in spiritual Communion! Practise it frequently and you’ll have more presence of God and closer union with him in your life.15 The sincere desire to receive Holy Communion worthily leads us to share in its effects and thereby prolongs the benefits of our previous eucharistic Communion. Spiritual Communion is, in a thousand different ways, an expression of faith, hope and love. Through it we can make atonement for the times when perhaps we received our Lord coldly or carelessly, for all those who receive Communion sacrilegiously, and for those who are indifferent or forgetful of Christ in the Sacrament of the Altar. “Have a great love for Jesus in the Eucharist. That’s how to exercise our faith in his Real Presence. It leads us to make many spiritual Communions and thereby increases in us the virtue of charity, at the same time as it also fills us with hope. This way the three theological virtues are at work in us. God, who is so good, has been waiting for us, as our Father wrote in The Way, for twenty centuries. He waited for us

14 Ibid. 15 The Way, 540 119 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

to be born and to reach the moment of our First Holy Communion. And he continues waiting for each soul, right to the end of time. It’s a wonder of love!”16 We should strive to do better every day, because our longing will never match that of Christ who lovingly awaits us in the tabernacle. Our Father, drawing on his personal experience and zeal, told us not to have any inhibitions in the way we express our love for God. We should feel free to ask Jesus: Teach me to receive you as you want me to. And keep saying things like that. Little by little you will learn to do it very well, for your Communion will have a continuous effect right throughout the day.17 The first and last thought of the day, which we try to give to God, should often refer explicitly to the Eucharist: a thought of love, of gratitude, of contrition, which we can then repeat during the night if we happen to wake up. You sleep soundly at night, don’t you? But there are some people, who are young too, who don’t manage to sleep uninterruptedly at night. They wake up four, five or maybe six times, and in those periods while they are awake start preparing for the following day’s Communion. It’s a good method, an excellent method, to follow, whatever your state in life is: single, married, widowed or priest. It works well for everyone! So, if you want to improve your preparation and your thanksgiving, do try it.18

Thanksgiving after Holy Communion It is logical that the more sensitive our dispositions are, the more unworthy we feel to receive such great benefits. Our Father, who struggled resolutely to avoid even the slightest faults, grasped, like all

16 Don Alvaro, Get-together, 25 November 1984 17 From our Father, Get-together, 12 April 1974 18 From our Father, Get-together, 21 April 1973

Along the Path to Love 120

saintly people, the infinite void between Our Saviour’s holiness and the lowliness of creatures. It was this acute awareness that set him on fire in his thanksgiving after Holy Communion. On one occasion he told us: after saying Mass yesterday I said some prayers kneeling down. They are not prescribed, they are ad libitum, at the priest’s discretion, but in the Work we usually say them. Then I sat down and began my thanksgiving. Lord, I said, I still haven’t learned to give thanks to you. Can it be that I’ve grown accustomed to having you with me, and that I’m not aware of who you are and what I am, a dunghill ... ? And that’s as far as I got, because ... I made no further considerations.19 Seeing all this in the context of our own circumstances helps to put ourselves in our proper place, certainly the very lowest, but the one most suitable for our soul to reflect the brilliance of the light of grace. At times we may speak to Jesus without words or stammer through a dialogue in which we never manage to express ourselves properly. This is how I made my thanksgiving yesterday: telling Jesus I didn’t know how to, that I still hadn’t learned to thank him. And I repeat it again now, completely ashamed. My children, look, our Lord is always present in the Tabernacle. It may seem that he’s not listening, but he is: he listens lovingly, with the affection of a father and a mother, hiding his Divinity and his Humanity. He is a Lord that speaks when he chooses, when we least expect it, and tells us very specific things. Then he becomes silent again, for he desires the response of our faith and our loyalty.20 Unworthy to receive him, incapable of properly thanking him, bereft of merit and virtue: that’s how we should see ourselves before our Redeemer.

19 From our Father, Get-together, 18 June 1972 20 Ibid.

121 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

‘I am worth nothing, I can do nothing, I have nothing, I am nothing ...’ But You have ascended the Cross so that I may make your infinite merits my own. There I also take on – they are mine, because I am their child – the merits of the Mother of God, and those of St Joseph. And I make my own the virtues of the saints and of so many dedicated souls ... Then, I steal a glance at my own life, and I say: Alas, my God, it is all night and full of darkness! Only now and then can one see a few points of light sparkling, due to your great mercy and to my inadequate response ... All this I offer to you, Lord; I have nothing else.21 Love for Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament should move us to find our own ways of thanking him, even when we feel spiritually dry. At the very least we should try to work hard at our thanksgiving and, following our Founder’s example, have recourse to our heavenly intercessors. After Holy Mass, he told us on a certain occasion, I didn’t know how to give thanks. I invoked my Guardian Angel and my ministerial Archangel, so that they, in my name, would intensely adore our Lord, who had come down to this dunghill of my breast. Afterwards I sought the aid of St Joseph, and I sought the aid of our Mother who is the Mother of God, forever pure and spotless, so they would give thanks in my name. There I was, like an idiot: what am I going to say? I don’t know what to tell you, I don’t know anything ... Then I remembered something from the time I was four or five years old. Both my grandmothers, paternal as well as maternal, were very old when they died: one was eighty six and the other eighty something. This latter one, my father’s mother, whenever she didn’t have visitors

21 The Way of the Cross, Thirteenth Station, 5

Along the Path to Love 122

was always saying the Rosary. And just yesterday I remembered something she taught me, and so I was able to give thanks in very simple language, like something a child would say. I was very young when I learned it, but my granny must have felt like a child herself, because she used to say it too. It was: For you I am, for you I came to be; what, my Jesus, do you want of me? It made me very happy, this sort of doggerel, very happy indeed: I had made my thanksgiving.22 Even distractions can help us improve our thanksgiving. Your Communions were very cold: you paid little attention to the Lord: you were distracted by the smallest trifle. But ever since you began to realise during an intimate dialogue with God that the angels are present, your attitude has changed. You say to yourself: ‘Let them not see me like this.’ And see how, as a result of thinking, ‘What will they say?’ – this time, for a good motive – you have advanced a little towards Love.23 When love is true it makes the time of thanksgiving after Mass seem short. We ought, therefore, make the maximum use of it, recalling that, while our Lord is sacramentally present within us, the Eucharist’s abundant benefits flood the soul. Like our Founder, we should feel inspired to turn our whole day into a period both of thanksgiving for having received Christ and of preparation for the Communion to follow. On one occasion, commenting on a text of medieval literature, that was precisely what he suggested. I have here a verse from a Christmas carol which was composed sometime between the years 1200 and 1300: With whom can I share my sorrows, my sweet love? With whom can I share my sorrows, save with Thee? What I’m really telling you is how to give thanks after Communion; it may seem short

22 From our Father, Get-together, 19 March 1973 23 Furrow, 694 123 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

– at times barely ten minutes – but it lasts the whole day long, for we are always in the presence of God.24

A dialogue of love When we receive our Lord in Holy Communion, what he looks for from us is a trusting dialogue. When we don’t know what to say, it can be very helpful to say the prayers recommended by the Church for use after Mass. They are prayers that have flowed, not so much from pens, as from hearts on fire with love for the Blessed Eucharist. This was our Founder’s advice, and he himself used to make use of them in his thanksgiving. This brings us to another piece of advice from him which, when circumstances warrant, we do well to follow. I see Jesus in the Eucharist as a King, as a Doctor, as a Teacher, and as a Friend. He is a King, and he wants to reign in your Christian heart, as a son of God. Tell him: Yes, I want to serve with all my soul. But we have to receive him like some important celebrity: with decorations, with lights, with new clothes. And if you ask what cleanliness, what decorations and what lights you should have, let me tell you: cleanliness in your senses, each and every one; decorations in your talents, each and every one; and light in all your soul. He is a Doctor, and he comes to cure your ailments, your infirmities. But you have to tell him the truth: I have this tendency, this is how I feel ... You cannot hide anything from the divine Doctor. You have to show him your sores, and if you notice that pride is whispering to you to hide something, say: Lord, you who have cured so many souls, make me see you in the Eucharist as the divine Doctor! He is a Teacher. Tell him: teach me to love. Do you remember,

24 From our Father, Meditation, 26 March 1964

Along the Path to Love 124

when we were children, how the teacher used to take our hand as we wrote to help us make the letters? And when we let ourselves be guided, they turned out so well! Tell him: Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament, help me to write, for I am drawing my first letters in this divine story. May I let myself be led. He is a Friend, a friend who gives his life, for love. My son, your life is not yours, it doesn’t belong to you, you can’t keep it for yourself. You belong to Jesus. Feel proud to be his friend, and, as a friend, offer him confidently all you have. He understands you. He, who cried for Lazarus, understands you. Tell him: Jesus, I don’t want to make you cry.25 If we cultivate these dispositions of soul our Communions will be extremely fruitful, both in ourselves and in those around us: the Blessed Eucharist will fill our entire being and reign over it, thereby extending Christ’s presence on earth. In the Blessed Eucharist – ordinarily, I don’t say the Eucharist, I say the Blessed Eucharist – the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity, who became Man for our sake, is really present: Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity. That’s how he is when we actually receive him, but our body at once begins to destroy the sacramental species, and when that happens his Eucharistic presence disappears. Even then God still remains in us as long as we don’t oust him by mortal sin. The Holy Spirit dwells in us through grace and, as a consequence, so does the entire Trinity, for there is but one God in three distinct Persons. Wherever one Person is acting, the Blessed Trinity – the one and only God – is present.26 This is how to become contemplatives and learn to rejoice in the presence and action of the Trinity dwelling in our soul in grace. This

25 From our Father, Meditation, 14 April 1960 26 From our Father, Get-together, 13 April 1972 125 Receiving Holy Communion fruitfully

indwelling is accentuated by each sacrament and reaches its summit in the gift of the Paraclete, who remains in us after we receive the Eucharist. This action of the Holy Spirit makes it possible for us to live a supernatural life and to keep ablaze the light of our Christian life. Without him we would live like animals; thanks to him we live as children of God. Everything we do, desire or think is turned into treasure for the soul. Now do you understand why we should have devotion to the Holy Spirit?27 Our holy Mother Mary, who always teaches us to come to Jesus, to recognize him and to find him in all the different situations of our day,28 is the best teacher of piety; from her we learn to receive Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament with “the purity, humility and devotion” with which she received him at the moment of the Annunciation.

27 From our Father, Get-together, 27 March 1972 28 Christ is passing by, 94

## OUR HOLY MOTHER MARY {#cuaderno.8.10}

Standing by the cross of Jesus were his mother, and his mother’s sister, Mary the wife of Clopas, and Mary Magdalene.1 We have often contemplated this Gospel scene. Jesus is comforted anew by the quiet, loving presence of his Mother. Mary does not shout; she does not run about frantically. Stabat: she is there, standing next to her Son. It is then that Jesus looks at her, and then turning his gaze to John he exclaims, Woman, this is thy son. Then he said to the disciple, This is thy mother (Jn 19:26-27). In the person of John, Christ is entrusting all men to his Mother, and especially his disciples: those who were to believe in him.2 Through her motherly concern for all mankind, and especially for Christians, the Blessed Virgin continues to offer her Son Jesus the same care and attention she offered him while he was on earth, for we are all members of his Mystical Body. Furthermore, as Co-redemptrix, she feels compelled to lead mankind definitively into the divine life. “Taken up to heaven she did not lay aside this salvific duty, but by her manifold intercession continues to bring us the gifts of eternal salvation. By her maternal charity, she cares for the brethren of her Son, who still journey on earth surrounded by dangers and difficulties, until they are led into the happiness of their true home. Therefore the

1 Jn 19:25 2 Friends of God, 288 127 Our Holy Mother Mary

Blessed Virgin is invoked by the Church under the titles of Advocate, Helper, Benefactress, and Mediatrix.”3

Motherly vigilance We have a wonderful experience in the Work of Our Lady’s motherly protection! On the 11th of October 1964, which in those days was the feast of Our Lady’s Maternity, our Father said: My children, you and I have often reflected on the fact that each step in the legal journey of the Work has been taken under the protection of the Mother of God. Now, as we celebrate her divine Maternity, I cannot help recalling that the first time the Holy See officially took notice of the Work was on this feast day, many years ago. I was very young, and your brother Álvaro, who was with me, said: Father, you must be pleased that tomorrow is the feast of Our Lady of Pilar. I replied: All the feasts of the Blessed Virgin make me very happy; to me they’re all wonderful. But if I had to choose, I would prefer today’s, the feast of the Maternity. At that moment I was unaware that the Mother of God had interceded for this Work of God and it had been given its first approval.4 The Blessed Virgin has shown herself to be the Mother of the Work in all circumstances, to such an extent that our Father could write: our Opus Dei was born and has developed under Our Lady’s mantle. She has been a good Mother to us, consoling us, smiling at us and encouraging us in all the trying moments of the blessed struggle to bring forth this army of apostles in the world.5 Each of us is well aware of our heavenly Mother’s loving hand in our spiritual life, in our apostolate, and even in the very development of

3 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 62 4 From our Father, Meditation, 11 October 1964 5 From our Father, Crónica, August 1964, pp.9-10

Along the Path to Love 128

our vocation. Egredietur virga de radice Iesse, et flos de radice eius ascendet (Is 11:1): a shoot will spring from the root of Jesse and a flower will bloom from its stem. Even though these words of Holy Scripture are a poetic way of describing Mary’s divine maternity, my children, they are also a very real way of saying that in the depths of our heart, in the midst of the turbulence of our passions, She has caused to bloom, not only the first flower, but also the fruit of deeds which are rooted in Love.6 A mother’s love is sweet, attentive, understanding and completely faithful; so too is the affection Mary has for each of us, but infinitely more powerful through grace. And this is so not in any sort of vaguely spiritual or disembodied manner, but with the warmth of a human, motherly heart. There is no heart more human than that of a person overflowing with supernatural sense. Think of Holy Mary, who is full of grace, Daughter of God the Father, Mother of God the Son, Spouse of God the Holy Spirit. Her heart has room for all humanity and makes no distinction or discrimination. Every person is her son or her daughter.7 I am the mother of fair love, of fear, of knowledge, and of holy hope. Come to me, you who desire me, and eat your fill of my produce.8 The liturgy places these words on Our Lady’s lips. Our Father has helped us to understand vividly what the Blessed Virgin’s maternity means for each of us. If I were a leper my mother would kiss me. She would kiss my wounds without fear or hesitation. Well then, what would the Blessed Virgin Mary do? When we feel we are like lepers, all full of sores, we have to cry out: Mother! And the protection of our Mother will be like a kiss upon our wounds, which

6 From our Father, Meditation, 11 October 1964 7 Furrow, 801 8 Sir 24:18-19 129 Our Holy Mother Mary

will then be healed.9 A mother’s capacity for renunciation and self-forgetfulness is never more evident than when she tenderly nurses a sick or handicapped child, or when she corrects her wayward offspring firmly but affectionately, or receives back with open arms a rebellious son or daughter who had left the parental home. Our heavenly Mother does the same. Since her children are all handicapped, sick, wayward and rebellious, in all ages Christians have felt compelled to seek her merciful help. This too was our Founder’s message: My Mother! Mothers on earth look with greater love upon the weakest of their children, the one with the worst health, or who is least intelligent, or is a poor cripple. Sweet Lady, I know that you are more of a Mother than all other mothers put together. And since I am your son, since I am weak, and ill, and crippled, and ugly ... 10

The daily struggle Every day, at the end of the Liturgy of the Hours, among the Marian antiphons the Church recommends to her priests is the following: “Mother of Christ, hear thou thy people’s cry! Star of the deep, and portal of the sky! Mother of him who thee from nothing made, Sinking we strive, and call to thee for aid.”11 Such is man’s state in this world: a constant struggle to overcome the disorder of sin. But, as St John Paul II has written, “the words of this liturgical antiphon also express the truth of the ‘great transformation’ which the mystery of the Incarnation establishes for man (...) It is an unending and continuous transformation between falling and

9 The Forge, 190 10 The Forge, 234 11 Antiphon Alma Redemptoris Mater Along the Path to Love 130

rising again, between the man of sin and the man of grace and justice.”12 On the occasion of the Marian Year in 1987, St John Paul II invited the faithful to contemplate the fundamental truths that govern human and Christian living. “Mankind has made wonderful discoveries and achieved extraordinary results in the fields of science and technology. It has made great advances along the path of progress and civilization, and in recent times one could say that it has succeeded in speeding up the pace of history. But the fundamental transformation, the one which can be called ‘original’, constantly accompanies man’s journey, and through all the events of history accompanies each and every individual. It is the transformation from ‘falling’ to ‘rising’, from death to life.”13 Between this falling and rising again there intervenes the forgiving and healing action of divine grace, the gift of the Holy Spirit. All these supernatural benefits reach us through the agency of our holy Mother Mary, Mediatrix of all graces. “As she goes forward with the whole of humanity towards the frontier between the two Millennia, the Church, for her part, with the whole community of believers and in union with all men and women of good will, takes up the great challenge contained in these words of the Marian antiphon: ‘the people who have fallen yet strive to rise again’, and she addresses both the Redeemer and his Mother with the plea: ‘Assist us’. For, as this prayer attests, the Church sees the Blessed Mother of God in the saving mystery of Christ and in her own mystery. She sees Mary deeply rooted in humanity’s history, in man’s eternal vocation according to the providential plan which God has made for him from eternity. She sees Mary maternally present and sharing in the many complicated problems which today beset the lives of individuals, families and nations; she sees her helping the Christian people in the

12 St John Paul II, Encyclical Redemptoris Mater, 25 March 1987, 52 13 Ibid.

131 Our Holy Mother Mary

constant struggle between good and evil, to ensure that it ‘does not fall’, or, if it has fallen, that it ‘rise again’.”14 Every human being readily experiences this inner tension between sin and grace, between passion and virtue, between the desire to love God and the allurements of this world. But we are equally well aware from personal experience how much this struggle is aided through recourse to the Blessed Virgin’s motherly protection. Gratias tibi, Deus, gratias tibi! Thank you, O Lord, Father, Son and Holy Spirit, because the piety, confidence and love you have given us for the Mother of God, has been matched by a generous response on her part. She knows the clay we are made of; she understands our defects, and after we have gone astray she has restored us to your love with that wonderfully gentle and affectionate manner she has.15 We should pray that we never lack that filial abandonment, especially in the hour of temptation. When we were little, we kept close to our mother in a dark alley or if dogs barked at us. Now, when we feel temptations of the flesh, we should run to the side of Our Mother in Heaven, by realising how close she is to us, and by means of aspirations.16 Mother! Call her again and again. She is listening, she sees you in danger, perhaps, and, with her Son’s grace she, your holy Mother Mary, offers you the refuge of her arms, the tenderness of her embrace. Call her, and you will find yourself with added strength for the new struggle.17 We should also seek the protection of our Mother the Blessed Virgin whenever the awareness of our wretchedness threatens to

14 Ibid. 15 From our Father, Meditation, 11 October 1964 16 Furrow, 847 17 The Way, 516

Along the Path to Love 132

suffocate us; although when we think this way, it is generally exaggerated. It is certainly true that we are sinners, but it is also true that where sin increased, grace abounded all the more.18 If we are humble the balance is always positive, because God’s mercy is infinitely greater than our capacity to offend him. All the sins of your life seem to rise up against you. Don’t lose confidence. Rather, call on your holy Mother Mary, with the faith and abandonment of a child. She will bring peace to your soul.19 We turn to our Mother just as confidently whenever we feel so weak that, if she were to let our hand go, we would end up committing the most dreadful atrocities. The holy Virgin Mary, Mother of Fair Love, will bring relief to your heart, when it makes you feel that it is of flesh, if you turn to her with confidence.20 And our Father adds: she will obtain for you abundant grace to conquer in your daily struggle. And the enemy will gain nothing by those foul things that continually seem to boil and rise within you, trying to engulf in their fragrant corruption the high ideals, the sublime determination that Christ himself has set in your heart. Serviam! – I will serve!21 There are moments in the spiritual life when one’s strength seems to falter. God permits this to happen so we grow in humility and come to rely completely on his power. What wonderful progress we can make on this path of abandonment when we learn to put everything in Mary’s hands! Our Father told us a story about a statuette of the Blessed Virgin which he had during the early years of the Work and which he used to call Our Lady of the kisses. In the first residence which we had, I

18 Rom 5:20 19 The Way, 498 20 The Way, 504 21 The Way, 493 133 Our Holy Mother Mary

never went in or out without going to the Director’s room, where the statue was, so I could kiss it. I don’t think I ever did it mechanically. My kisses were human kisses, the kisses of a son who was afraid ... But since I’ve said so often that I’m not afraid of anyone or anything, let’s not say I was afraid. They were kisses from a son who felt far too young and who needed all the tenderness of Our Lady’s affection. All the strength I needed, I sought from God through the Blessed Virgin.22

‘Returning’ to Jesus through Mary If, through lukewarmness, neglect, or even mortal sin, our Lord ever allowed us to desert him, that would be the moment to remember that we go to Jesus – and we ‘return’ to him – through Mary.23 If we are ever so unfortunate as to get lost, then the Most Sweet Heart of Mary is the shortest, safest, and most lovable route back home. In the unfathomable designs of God’s mercy, guilt sincerely repented for becomes the source of great benefits. Felix culpa (Easter Vigil, Praeconium), the Church sings. Happy fault, that has brought us so great and wonderful a Redeemer. Happy fault, we could add, which has merited that we should receive Mary as our Mother. Now we are safe. Nothing should worry us now, because Our Lady, the crowned Queen of heaven and earth, is omnipotent in her supplication before our Father God. Jesus cannot deny anything to Mary, nor to us, who are children of his own Mother.24 The soul in love with Mary continually discovers, at the hand of this wonderful mother, new treasures of contrition and repentance. One of the Fathers of the Church addresses himself to Mary in words that we could well make our own. “Who, after your Son, is so

22 From our Father, Meditation, 11 October 1964 23 The Way, 495 24 Friends of God, 288

Along the Path to Love 134

concerned for mankind as you are? Who so unceasingly protects us in our tribulations as you do? Who so quickly frees us from temptation? Who takes such pains to plead for sinners? Who comes to the defence of the forsaken cases to excuse them?”25 We can truly say that, thanks to the Blessed Virgin Mary, the earth is full of the steadfast love of the Lord.26 Recordare, Virgo Mater Dei, dum steteris in conspectu Domini, ut loquaris pro nobis bona. A personal resolution: never to worry about anything at all, no matter how catastrophic it may be, as long as we go to God through the Blessed Virgin. And you know how to go: through the Confidence and Confession. Then we’ll always find a haven of peace. At times our Lord will smile to see us acting foolishly, and he’ll pardon us, thinking: now that he’s learned his lesson, from now on he’ll be more faithful.27 This requires humility, simplicity and spiritual childhood. If a little child does something naughty, breaking a valuable object, for instance, he sometimes tries to escape from his mother’s arms. He doesn’t realize that she doesn’t always want to catch him to spank him, but to console him, to press him against her heart and consume him with kisses. This is what I’m trying to tell you too: trust our Lord completely and don’t ever try to run away. The best sign that we’re not trying to escape is to be sincere in the Confidence and open in Confession. Don’t ever despair when you do something wrong, for that’s not sorrow but pride. Keep your head and return to your Father’s house; there we always find the marvellous understanding and compassion of our holy Mother Mary.28

25 St Germain of Constantinople, Homilia in Sanctae Mariae zonam 26 Ps 33:5 27 From our Father, Meditation, 11 October 1964 28 Ibid.

135 Our Holy Mother Mary

Hand in hand with our Mother As a sure point of reference in the spiritual life, we should take the spotlessly pure image of our heavenly Mother, who is blessing us. What I’m trying to tell you is that, even though this unwavering trust in our Mother is a central feature of our spirit, with the passage of time we continually discover more reasons for it. The evidence of her intercession, within the normal course of God’s Providence, is so overwhelming as to seem miraculous. It is really wonderful: it seems unbelievable and yet it is true.29 Our Father also told us that when this sweet smell of devotion blooms in the souls of all my children, it necessarily engenders great confidence in this Mother of ours who has smiled upon us before, and who never ceases to do so now.30 What security, what peace, what human and supernatural assurance fills the soul which in all circumstances turns to the Blessed Virgin with the simplicity and confidence of a child with its mother! Such a soul is truly in love with Jesus and identified with him. Another Father of the Church has said: “In my poverty and wretchedness I would like to become the servant of the Mother of my Lord for my own reparation (...) I would like to be subject to the Virgin Mother, wholly dedicated to her service, as a docile instrument in the hands of the most high God. Grant this to me, O Jesus, true God and Son of Man. Confer it upon me, Lord of all and Son of your Handmaid (...) Grant that I may serve your Mother in such a manner that you recognize me as your servant; may she be my sovereign on earth so that you may be my Lord for all eternity.”31 Still, we must get down to details and examine ourselves carefully, because we are so lacking in talent that even our devotion to the Blessed Virgin can suffer from routine, lukewarmness and disorder. If

29 Ibid. 30 Ibid. 31 St Alphonsus of Toledo, Liber de virginitate perpetua Sanctae Mariae, 12

Along the Path to Love 136

you feel proud to be a son of Our Lady, ask yourself: How often do I express my devotion to the Virgin Mary during the day, from morning to night?32 “My daughter, my son, ask yourself: How do I fulfil the Marian Norms? Do I put new love each day in my encounters with our Blessed Lady? Do I make a sincere effort to say the Rosary well, without rushing, contemplating each of the mysteries? Do I say the Angelus or Regína Coeli attentively? Do I greet my mother, Holy Mary, in a filial way when I enter or go out of my room or when I discover images of hers in the street? Do I go to her daily, asking for the cleanliness of souls? Do I commend to her the crusade of purity which the world needs? Do I spread devotion to our Lady, learning from her humility? Do I entrust to our Mother the peace and unity of the home?”33 There is a subtle danger we have to watch out for: unless we are ready to examine ourselves sincerely and rectify as often as necessary, we will never have the strong filial relationship with Our Lady which we ought to have. If our spiritual life is not as alive as it should be, it is almost certainly because we are not treating Our Lady as true children ought. Invoke the Blessed Virgin. Keep asking her to show herself a Mother to you – monstra te esse Matrem! As well as drawing down her Son’s grace, may she bring the clarity of sound doctrine to your mind, and love and purity to your heart, so that you may know the way to God and take many souls to him.34 Let us return to the scene of Golgotha. After St John describes how Jesus gave him his own Mother to be his Mother too, he concludes: and from that hour the disciple took her to his own home.35 Let us too

32 The Forge, 433 33 Don Alvaro, Family Letters I, 164 34 The Forge, 986 35 Jn 19:27 137 Our Holy Mother Mary

receive her in our souls more lovingly, because “the Marian dimension of the life of a disciple of Christ,” St John Paul II wrote, “is expressed in a special way precisely through this filial entrusting to the Mother of Christ, which began with the testament of the Redeemer on Golgotha. Entrusting himself to Mary in a filial manner, the Christian, like the Apostle John, ‘welcomes’ the Mother of Christ ‘into his own home’ and brings her into everything that makes up his inner life.”36 When the Blessed Virgin is well and truly present in our lives, we enter deep within the recesses of her Most Sweet Heart, and therefore, within the Sacred Heart of her Son Jesus. And as a result we become happier, more apostolic, and more supernaturally effective.

36 St John Paul II, Encyclical Redemptoris Mater, 45

## LEARNING TO LOVE {#cuaderno.8.11}

In his loving Providence, God wanted man to be born into a home; he wanted his very existence to be the fruit of love. Thus, from birth man is surrounded by a unique and personal atmosphere of affection which accompanies him throughout life. In such loving surroundings he develops humanly and supernaturally, and becomes an adult. I don’t mind repeating it often, our Father tells us. Everybody needs affection, and in the Work we need it too. You must always be trying to grow in affection for your brothers, without falling into sentimentality. Anything that affects any son of mine should be very much ours, and very genuinely so. The day we live as strangers, or indifferent to one another, we have killed Opus Dei.1

Jesus’ love for men Everybody needs to be loved: it is part of human nature, and we see it too in the life of Jesus Christ our Lord, perfectus Homo,2 who assumed our nature fully. Jesus was born into a family where love was the only guiding principle. He grew up protected by the warmth and affection of Mary and Joseph, and in adulthood he surrounded himself with a group of friends to whom he offered the human and supernatural affection that they needed.

1 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 2 Athanasian Creed 139 Learning to love

Jesus’ life teaches us to be attentive to people and treat them properly as individuals. Christ’s affection is not directed at an anonymous multitude, nor is it diluted by the great number of his followers. He addresses each individual intimately and personally: Now when the sun was setting, all those who had any that were sick with various diseases brought them to him; and he laid his hands on every one of them and healed them.3 The closer people were to Jesus, the greater was the Master’s concern for them. And so, since Jesus loved Martha and her sister and Lazarus,4 we see him upset and weeping at the death of his beloved friend: Jesus wept. So the Jews said, ‘See how he loved him!’5 My children, our Father comments, our Lord’s whole life, and especially this scene at Bethany, is an invitation to us to be good friends to our friends. Never be ashamed of being warmhearted. If you weren’t you wouldn’t be human, nor – since we are human beings – could you be divine. Don’t be afraid to love one another truly, putting your heart into it, as Jesus did.6 On another occasion our Lord is concerned about the disciples’ rest: Come away by yourselves to a lonely place, and rest a while.7 But, when necessary, that same affection brings him to correct them: You do not know of what manner of spirit you are.8 And he is continually giving them an example of how they ought to serve others. Shortly before the Passion, St John sums up our Lord’s life as follows: When Jesus knew that his hour had come to depart out of this world to the Father, having loved his own who were in the world, he loved them to the end.9

3 Lk 4:40 4 Jn 11:5 5 Jn 11:36 6 From our Father, Crónica, May 1965, p.34 7 Mk 6:31 8 Lk 9:55 9 Jn 13:1

Along the Path to Love 140

There is nothing that attracts like affection, our Father tells us. Serve with love. So many passages of the Gospel remind us that Christ let himself be won over by love! And you already know, because we have meditated on it many times, that when he wants to highlight the trait which will identify his disciples, he says to them: Love one another. By this all men will know that you are my disciples (Jn 13:34-35).10

Supernatural charity and natural affection God’s love for men, manifested in Christ, is what we ought to imitate. Love one another greatly, our Father tells us. And when I say this, I am saying something that is at the very heart of Christianity: Deus caritas est (1 Jn 4:8), God is love. Remember John our patron, who is also a good model for learning to love Jesus Christ? When he was very old, although he must have still felt very young, and was hardly able to speak because his body no longer responded, he used to say to his disciples: Filioli, diligite alterutrum: my little children, love one another (St Jerome, In ep. ad Galat. comm. 3, 6). Children of my soul, love one another, because everything else is nonsense. Forget about likes and dislikes and behave supernaturally. Love one another, really and truly!11 Fraternal affection is particularly required in Opus Dei because we are a family with supernatural bonds, in which those words of Jesus are fulfilled: ecce mater mea et fratres mei: quicumque enim fecerit voluntatem Patris mei qui in coelis est, ipse meus frater et soror et mater est (Mt 12:49-50). Here are my mother and my brethren, for whoever does the will of my Father in heaven, he is my brother and sister, and mother.12

10 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 11 From our Father, Get-together, 19 March 1964 12 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 76 141 Learning to love

This family of ours, with supernatural bonds stronger than all the ties of blood, requires a superabundance of charity. “If the simple fact of being from the same town is enough for many people to become friends,” wrote St John Chrysostom, “what sort of love must there be among us who share the same house, the same table, the same road, the same door, the same lifestyle and outlook, the same shepherd and king, the same master and judge, the same Creator and Father?”13 My children, our Father adds, feel in your souls this blessed fraternity which we show by loving one another truly, more than if we were related by blood. Besides, we do have a blood relationship, because we are children of God, washed and purified by his Blood, and called with an identical vocation.14 How are we to express this affection which God expects of us and which our brothers and sisters have a right to? In the first place by being always very human, my children. To be very spiritual, very supernatural, you must try to be very human and compassionate in your approach to life. God our Lord does not build on the disorder of an insensitive nature. We must be understanding and be able to make life pleasant for those we live with. We need to be quick to forgive; and we shouldn’t be scandalized by anything or anyone. That’s how to encourage your brothers or sisters and make it easy for them to avail of your assistance.15 What is expected of us is to make life pleasant for others and not to insist on our own preferences, however legitimate they may be, when satisfying them might indicate a lack of affection for our brothers or sisters. When I tell you that you have to be like a carpet so that others may tread softly, our Father says, it’s not just a nice turn of phrase. It

13 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 32, 7 14 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 15 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 34

Along the Path to Love 142

must be a fact. This is difficult, just as holiness is difficult; but it is at the same time easy, because holiness is within everyone’s reach.16 It is also a matter of overlooking what we don’t like in people around us. When you are truly human yourself, our Father says, you are able to pass over small defects in others, and with motherly understanding you will always see the good side of things. Jokingly, and by way of an example, I’ve commented how people form different impressions of the same event depending on whether they view it sympathetically or not. Do you remember the story about the child (excuse me, but it’s rather graphic) who puts his finger up his nose? The visitors say: What a dirty habit! But his mother says: Oh, he’s going to be a research scientist. My daughters and sons, you understand me: we must be good at forgiving. Don’t show your displeasure over unimportant little trifles, be they spiritual or material. Be kind to one another, and you too will come to the very charitable conclusion that we are all research scientists.17

The vigilance of the good shepherd Each of us, if we truly love our brothers or sisters, should feel keenly the anxiety for all the churches18 that weighed on the heart of St Paul: a loving solicitude which is always alert and concerned for the spiritual and material well-being of others. Who is weak, and I am not weak? Who is made to fall, and I am not indignant?19 My children! Children of my soul! Don’t forget that, through the love of Christ, each of you has entered through the gate. You are all

16 From our Father, Crónica, July 1955, p.5 17 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 35 18 2 Cor 11:28 19 2 Cor 11:29 143 Learning to love

sheep of the same sheepfold, and at the same time each one has to be, in a certain sense, a good shepherd to the other sheep. And just as we must allow ourselves to be led and to answer when we are called by name, we have an equally serious duty to contribute to the holiness and the perseverance of our brothers and sisters.20 We do this through good example, through fraternal correction, and through the holy attraction of our mortified life. Christ reminds us in the Gospel that the Good Shepherd knows his sheep one by one. He goes before them, and the sheep follow him, for they know his voice.21 His affection, which shows itself in many different ways, is directed to the good of the whole flock. I came that they may have life, and have it abundantly.22 This is the model we should imitate. I remember as a child in the Pyrenees, our Father has written, on freezing winter days when everything was covered in snow, seeing shepherds, wrapped in their sheepskin jackets, making their way along the valleys of my native land. The shepherd would be accompanied by his ever-faithful dogs, and a donkey, unbelievably laden with all his utensils. On top of it all the donkey carried a big pot in which the master cooked his food and prepared the dressings he put on the animals’ injuries. If one of the sheep had ‘taken a knock’, as they say there, if it had hurt a leg, I have seen the shepherd bring the Gospel parable to life (cf Lk 15:1-7) and carry the injured sheep on his shoulders. Other times I have seen him carry a newborn lamb lovingly in his arms. My children, this is beautiful! This is what we too must do: ask our Lord to forgive us and to forgive others; to be understanding and take on our shoulders the one who is going astray or who has ‘taken a

20 From our Father, Meditation, 12 March 1961 21 Jn 10:4 22 Jn 10:10

Along the Path to Love 144

knock’. And if that sheep has lost its sense of direction, then the ones at the back have to be sent forward and take the lead. We should also be good at picking up and looking after those who are newborn in the Work, giving them the warmth of our arms.23 This is an obligation of justice that we joyfully assume when we come to the Work, because God is relying on the support of our affection to help our brothers or sisters to become saints and be happy and faithful. In the Work we are obliged to be deeply concerned about others, in an on-going way our Father reminds us. The very nature of our calling requires us to have this deep affection, which is both natural and supernatural, because our vocation makes us belong more to God and, at the same time, to be more truly human. We cannot sanctify ourselves in the Work if we are not ready to serve others continually, and, in the first place, our brothers or sisters.24

God’s grace and man’s effort Nevertheless it is not easy to be affectionate always and with everybody, although it may be easier for people who are friendly by nature. It always takes an effort to be affectionately outgoing towards others and to forget oneself, because it is not just a task of the heart, although the heart does have a role to play; nor is it exclusively the fruit of sentiment. A love that is based solely on feelings, apart from not being properly supernatural, is likely to be a cloak for self-love or for wanting to be loved, instead of being an occasion for generous self-giving to others. It would not be true affection, because it would lack a fundamental characteristic, that is, giving oneself to one’s brethren for love of God. It would be a selfish friendship, and it could even militate

23 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 122 24 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 145 Learning to love

against the unity of the Work and the precept of charity. On the other hand, a person who simply observes certain formalities in dealing with others cannot be said to be charitable either. Christ’s charity is not a matter of technique: that would be attempting to turn love into the mere externals of courtesy and politeness, and would destroy charity at its very root. True charity comes from within, from one’s heart and soul. The affection that is asked of us in the Work is, by contrast, universal, open to all, because it proceeds from a heart enlarged by divine grace. “We are not mistaken,” wrote St John Chrysostom, “if we say that Paul’s heart embraced the whole world. It was capable of encompassing entire cities, peoples and nations. ‘Our heart is wide open,’ he said (2 Cor 6:11). And this love that so enlarged his heart made him say: ‘I wrote to you out of much affliction and anguish of heart and with many tears’ (2 Cor 2:4). His heart reached up to heaven. It was as large as the world; it was filled with light like the sun, burning like fire, and as solid as a diamond. Rivers flow from him: ‘Out of his heart shall flow rivers of living water’ (Jn 7:38). There was a new kind of life in that heart, not our kind of life: ‘It is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me’ (Gal 2:20).
