That heart merited to love Christ as no other heart loved him; so much so that he cared nothing for death or gehenna. And yet, that heart was full of tender love for his brethren.”25 Our charity has to flow from a heart in which the Holy Spirit dwells; it helps us to be conscious of the fact that we are children of God, and to live as such, because all who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God;26 and it fills us with its fruits: love, joy, peace patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, self-control.27

25 St John Chrysostom, Super Epistola ad Romanos homiliae, 32 26 Rom 8:14 27 Gal 5:22

Along the Path to Love 146

Learning to love If being affectionate towards others is not a question of temperament or of technique, but rather of grace and personal effort, the clear consequence is that we all can and should develop this virtue and work at being warm and kind-hearted, humanly and supernaturally. Our affection should show itself in deeds: remembering our brothers or sisters in our prayers; being interested in the things they are interested in; and being aware of their likes and dislikes, their joys and their sorrows. If a brother or a sister is ill-clad and in lack of daily food, writes St James, and one of you says to them, ‘Go in peace, be warmed and filled,’ without giving them the things needed for the body, what does it profit?28 The very real and affectionate concern our Father had for each of us offers us an eloquent example. Once, when he was on a journey, he was told that a son of his was going to have an operation. A short while ago, he said, I was told that a brother of yours is going to have an operation which he possibly may not pull through, and as a result I am quite distraught. Join me in my prayer, in my petition, for our Lord to make him well again. Keep insisting, tenaciously, so that our Lord may will it, bearing in mind always that we love and accept his most holy Will, even when we find it difficult. We must have this same concern in spiritual things. We must be alert to ensure that our brothers have divine life in their souls. They rely on our help, our kindness, our fraternal correction; and this makes their journey through life more pleasant. Always pray with faith, and when you are praying for a special intention, keep saying to our Lord, ‘If you want to, you can! Will it, Lord!’ Pray, I urge you, pray with faith and confidence. Now, going back to the case I mentioned earlier, let us pray for this brother of yours, and let us pray

28 Jas 2:15-16 147 Learning to love

for everyone always. Acquire the supernatural habit of loving your brothers, with deeds, with prayer, and in truth.29 Our Founder’s paternal and maternal affection always acts as a call to greater responsibility, urging us truly to love our brothers or sisters with our heart and with our head. My dearly beloved children, now more than ever our Lord is making me feel very soft-hearted. I find I love you all so eagerly and so intensely that I want to help you and – I must admit – really to put myself out, to sacrifice myself, both for your sake and for God’s sake.30 I can offer myself as an example in only a few things. But, in the midst of my many personal shortcomings, I think that I can offer myself as an example of a man who knows how to love. Your worries, your sorrows, your anxieties are forever on my mind. How I desire, with this heart of mine, the heart of a father and of a mother, to carry everything on my own shoulders.31

Showing love with deeds “There is no sign or mark that better distinguishes the Christian than the care he has for his brethren.”32 This is how it has been in the Work from the beginning, and how it always has to be. I don’t want to dwell now, our Father wrote in 1940, on the wonderful supernatural charity and genuine human affection which from the start has been so typical of the Work. Many souls have discovered the Gospel in the Christian warmth of our Centres, where no one ever feels alone or experiences the coldness of indifference.33

29 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 30 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 23 31 From our Father, Get-together, October 1968 32 St John Chrysostom, Homilies, 6, 3 33 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 7

Along the Path to Love 148

Affection for our brothers or sisters leads us, in the first place, to help them safeguard their vocation, which is the most splendid treasure God has given us. Charity motivates us to play our part in helping them have a solid interior life and become saints.34 In one of the Instructions our Father reminds us that when charity is present, that is, human and supernatural affection, it is very easy to be aware of the spiritual and material needs of those who live with us, and to be attentive to them. I have often added that if someone ever left the Work for reasons we weren’t able to explain, I would not excuse the Directors, and those who lived with that person, from sin – and possibly even grave sin.35 This concern for others ought to be expressed in constant prayer and mortification, in fraternal correction, in good example, and in the encouraging word, whenever necessary. If you know that a brother of yours has been delaying the prayer, or forgets to go to Confession on the appointed day, why not help him? You could say for example: ‘Let’s do the prayer?’ Or: ‘Are you coming to Confession? It’s our day today.’ If you know that someone finds mortification difficult, couldn’t you smooth the way for him with an affectionate word and good example? 36 Fraternity does not depend on circumstances, on whether we get on well with someone or not, on whether we’re from the same part of the country, or so forth; over and above all the differences there is an unbreakable bond of holy affection that binds us together. No one should ever feel isolated in the Work; and if any one does, it is because they choose to be,37 because they do not want to receive the fraternal affection and support of the others. What happens to our brothers or

34 From our Father, Meditation, 29 March 1956 35 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 97 36 From our Father, Meditation, 29 March 1956 37 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 73 149 Learning to love

sisters is not immaterial to us. Rejoice with those who rejoice, weep with those who weep. Live in harmony with one another.38 May no one feel alone in the Work! Everybody has to be convinced that they are understood. If any one has a problem, we are quick to make allowances for it and offer a helping hand; and our combined strength is available to be the support of their weakness.39 We also show affection by being aware of people’s material needs. If you notice that someone needs clothes and doesn’t say so, though they really should, then you mention it. If someone is not eating well, you bring it to the Director’s attention.40 The same applies when it is a case of sickness or old age. Whenever a son of mine gets sick, I always tell the people looking after him: ‘My children, don’t let him remember that his mother is far away.’ By this I mean that, while he is sick, we have to be like his mother to him, being as affectionate and attentive to him as she would be.41 But it is in family life, in daily living together, that we normally have the opportunity of showing fraternal affection and fostering a pleasant atmosphere: being attentive to material things, like tidiness and cleanliness in the Centre; helping to make the get-togethers attractive and restful, perhaps by preparing some topic of interest; or by being prepared to overlook little areas of friction which occasionally arise when people live together. When something unimportant comes up for discussion, we say what we think – at times we may even say nothing – and we let other people express their opinion, which is different from ours, and we let it pass unchallenged, without giving our point of view again. Out of prudence, we say nothing, as long as

38 Rom 12:15-16 39 From our Father, Meditation, 15 April 1954 40 From our Father, Meditation, 29 March 1956 41 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 12

Along the Path to Love 150

no harm is being done, even though what is being said may not be accurate. What difference does it make, for example, if someone says you’re twenty-eight when in fact you’re thirty? And there are so many other things like that.42 It is customary in the Work to have a little celebration for birthdays. We do whatever we can to make it special. In 1956 our Father said: For the past twenty-eight years we have had an unbroken tradition in the Work that, in order to make our brothers or sisters enjoy themselves, we are prepared to sing, or dance, or do whatever we’re asked; nobody has ever said ‘I don’t know how’ or ‘I’m not able’.43 Don’t be afraid to love one another as brothers or sisters. Love one another truly, my children. Strengthened by this love, by this charity of Christ, there will be no difficulty you cannot overcome, and you will be faithful. You will support one another, and if anyone feels about to fall they will be held up by the others: alter alterius onera portate, et sic adimplebitis legem Christi: bear one another’s burdens, and so fulfil the law of Christ (Gal 6:2).44 The fruits of human and supernatural fraternal affection in Opus Dei are quite obvious in the holy lives of our brothers and sisters. That is why our Father never tired of repeating: Love one another, supernaturally, genuinely, with God in all your affection and crowning everything. The more you love one another with that holy affection, the more you love the Work.45 By fulfilling our Lord’s new commandment of love, we make the life of dedication to God more pleasant for others, and become ever more faithful disciples of Christ. If you love each other, each of our Centres will be the home that I

42 Ibid., 77 43 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p.495 44 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 133 45 From our Father, Meditation, 29 March 1956 151 Learning to love

have seen. They will be what I want our Centres to be. And your brothers and sisters will have a holy longing to come home after their day’s work. And then, they will want to go out once more, to this holy war, this war of peace.46

46 Ibid.

## CORRECTING OUT OF LOVE {#cuaderno.8.12}

In the Work we learn to love Christ’s most holy humanity more and more. As we contemplate Jesus in the pages of the Gospel, we fall in love with his teaching and the way he lived. You have often been amazed spectators – protagonists – with me in those scenes. From the beginning I have taught you to put yourselves into the pages of the Gospel and, through them, to live in fraternal union with the first Twelve, because you too are apostles, specially chosen by Christ. That is why you are familiar with the way our Lord dealt with the Apostles during that period of formation. It has been engraved on your souls as strongly as your most intimate memories, so much so that it has become second nature to you.1 Those were moments of loving conversation between our Lord and the Apostles, periods of friendship and trust. I don’t need many words, our Father wrote, to describe to you, the lengths to which Jesus went in explaining the deeper meaning of his parables to the Twelve (cf Mt 13:9-23; 36-43), how careful he was in correcting their overly human reaction when they harvested the first fruits of their apostolic sowing (cf Lk 17:20), or how persistent he was in repeating the same lesson again and again (cf Mt 6:9-13; 7:7-11; Lk 11:1-13). Nor do I have to remind you of his fortitude in correcting their ambitions and their superficial notion of the kingdom of God (cf Mt 16:21-23; 20:20-28;

1 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 2 153 Correcting out of love

Lk 22:24-28); or the gentleness with which, to encourage them, he asked them to lend a hand when he performed his great miracles (cf Jn 6:5-10); and the tenderness with which he provided for their rest (cf Mk 6:31).2

Fraternal correction begins in the Gospel The Apostles live with our Lord. In their simplicity they show themselves as they really are. And Jesus, who loves them as God, and with his human heart as well, teaches them and corrects them. He wants them to be holy, to identify themselves with his Father’s will, and so he draws attention to their bouts of impatience, to their inclination to laziness, or to the tendency to envy that lurks beneath their apparent zeal. ‘Teacher, we saw a man casting out demons in your name, and we forbade him, because he was not following us.’ But Jesus said, ‘Do not forbid him, for no one who does a mighty work in my name will be able soon after to speak evil of me.’3 Sometimes our Lord’s corrections were made immediately and forcibly. Peter was sharply reprimanded – Get behind me, Satan!4 – when he mistakenly tried to dissuade Jesus from His readiness to suffer for our salvation. Other times Jesus corrects differently. He uses the request of the sons of Zebedee to help them appreciate better the consequences of their dedication: ‘You do not know what you are asking. Are you able to drink the cup that I am to drink?’ They said to him, ‘We are able.’ He said to them, ‘You will drink my cup, but to sit at my right hand and at my left is not mine to grant, but it is for those for whom it has been prepared by my Father.’5

2 Ibid., 3 3 Mk 9:38-39 4 Mt 16:23 5 Mt 20:22-23

Along the Path to Love 154

The Apostles have seen our Lord’s kind face grow stern when he has to correct them; but they also experience vividly the affection that accompanies his words. It is love that moves Christ to correct them. As the Good Shepherd, he goes ahead of them by word and example along the right path, so that the flock can follow safely. He steers them away from perilous tracks and poor pasture. The sheep make their way confidently because the Good Shepherd’s hand guides them expertly. Have this mind among yourselves, which was in Christ Jesus,6 St Paul urges us. In the Work each of us has to be a good shepherd for the others; together with the Lord of the flock, we are committed to looking after all his sheep. To do that we have to love our brothers or sisters deeply, so that we correct them out of love and with love, whenever the good of their souls or the good of the Work requires it. It is a pleasant duty because, through the grace of our vocation, we love them with Christ’s own heart, and we ask him to guide us in that love. Fraternal correction is a refined expression of our affection and concern for their formation. God has chosen fraternal correction as a way of safeguarding the spirit of Opus Dei and of sanctifying its members. I ask you, our Father urges us, for the love of God, to be concerned about the things of the Centre, the things of the Work. Put your heart into the things of your brothers, as well as in your own ... give fraternal correction to one another.7 Fraternal correction embraces anything that takes away from the supernatural or human tone of family, professional, or social life: things like character defects, for example, or faults in the spiritual life or apostolate. Usually they are small but recurring points that need to be improved; but at times we may also need to give deeper corrections, the kind that can bring about a complete change in a mistaken way of

6 Phil 2:5 7 From our Father, Brief Circle, 4 November 1962 155 Correcting out of love

behaviour or point of view. Our affection gives us a kind of instinct for understanding our brothers or sisters. Knowing that they are depending on us in their weakness makes us determined not to deny them our help, especially when they need that help to go more deeply into things. Corrections of this sort are remembered even more gratefully, because they often give rise to important new spiritual discoveries and fill our life with supernatural light. If we want to live harmoniously with people in general, our Father says, we have to do so first of all among ourselves. That is the order of true charity. Each of us has many defects, which we’re well aware of and are struggling against. But we also have many other defects that we’re not aware of. Unfortunately, that’s the way things are! Our brothers or sisters draw our attention to some of them through fraternal correction – only some of them, so as not to pester or discourage us. They point them out to us because they love us, and because in the Work we have the affectionate spirit of a true Christian family. To live harmoniously together means that you must all love one another, you must be understanding and forgiving towards one another. But there are things which we shouldn’t let pass, even though we forgive them: these are the things we should correct. Hence the need and the duty we have to bring to the Directors’ attention those things which are harmful to our brother’s or sister’s soul, and to the Work as a whole.8

How to give fraternal correction The kind of disposition we should have in the practice of this custom has been spelled out for us by our Father. “When making fraternal correction, a member of the Work should have a genuine

8 From our Father, Crónica, 1970, p.795

Along the Path to Love 156

supernatural outlook, so that at no stage during the brief conversation with the other person do they lose sight of the reason for making the correction. Fraternal correction ought to be made:with a humble attitude, because we can all fall into the same faults.”9 This is what St Paul recommends when he writes: Brethren, if a man is overtaken in any trespass, you who are spiritual should restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Look to yourself, lest you too be tempted.10 Fraternal correction, done with supernatural outlook and deep humility, is necessarily “accompanied by prayer, so that it benefits the person corrected,” says St Augustine. “Indeed, when men return to the right path because of a correction, it is God who really brings about growth in their hearts, no matter who it is who sows, waters or cultivates the ground.”11 Supernatural outlook and humility should be accompanied by true affection. Then we will always find the right words and the best way of making the correction. Correction isn’t done according to some generic and impersonal technique or regulation. It must be adapted to the person in question and to their particular circumstances at the time. St John Chrysostom teaches: “You should follow the example of good physicians in this regard. They do not try just one remedy. When they see that the first prescription doesn’t work, they try another; and afterwards, perhaps, something different again. Sometimes they cut; other times they bandage. As physicians of souls, you should do the same; use all the different kinds of treatment which are in keeping with the laws of Christ. And your reward will be your own happiness, and that of others.”12

9 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 225 10 Gal 6:1 11 St Augustine, De correptione et gratia, 14, 43 12 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 29, 3 157 Correcting out of love

We consult fraternal correction with the local Director because we want it to be fully effective. It is a norm of prudence and of love. “This consultation is not in any sense tale-bearing, because there is nothing more foreign to the spirit of the Work, but is done to avoid a number of people making the same correction to the same person.”13 Besides, the Director will judge whether the correction is opportune, and may also suggest the best time, and perhaps even the most appropriate words to use, so as to help our brother or sister understand fully the implications of the correction and accept it gladly. Taking into account what the Director has said to us, we reflect on the correction again in the presence of God, asking for the grace to do it as our Lord wants us to. We say things nobly and clearly to one another, so that there is no misunderstanding; but at the same time, we try to be sensitive, just as we would like others to be sensitive when correcting us. Love always knows how to find the best way. Christ himself taught us this. His words, everything he says, are an expression of affection, even when he is making it clear to someone that they have done something wrong or are mistaken about something. We see it in his look and in his gestures. That is why they encourage and console people so much, and at the same time open new horizons to their self-giving. That was the spirit that prompted our Father to remind us that “fraternal correction should be done one to one, never in the presence of others, and never with added comments or jokes. The correction is given and then forgotten about.”14 We try to be sensitive and straightforward in the way we mention the point; our attitude and tone are friendly and encouraging; it all helps the other person realize that they are not struggling alone. From now on they are going to fight in a new area, but knowing that they are being backed up. The fact that we

13 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 227 14 Ibid., 295

Along the Path to Love 158

correct the fault is a reassurance that we really care: we suffer when they suffer, and we support them in their struggle with our prayer and mortification. Who is weak, and I am not weak? Who is made to fall, and I am not indignant?15 The Good Shepherd is continually on the alert to watch over the safety of the sheep. He is attentive and vigilant, and as soon as he notices any danger he drops everything and runs to their assistance. Once we have consulted a correction we mustn’t delay making it. Love urges us on. And, in any case, the effectiveness of a correction also depends on the promptness with which it is done. A correction registers much more deeply when the details of the action in question are fresh in the person’s mind; it is less helpful if the incident referred to happened some time ago. If someone seems to take a while to react properly to a correction, we should come to their aid with prayer and mortification, with the holy coercion of our dedicated life, with our good humour and our evident affection. St John Chrysostom urges us to “repeat the same advice and exhortation again and again, but never sharply; always be pleasant and graceful ... Have you not noticed how at times painters carefully erase their brush-strokes, and how at other times they add touches here and there, as they strive to reproduce a beautiful face? Let us not be outdone by the painters. If they put such care into painting a bodily image, how much more ought we to do as we strive to form the image of a soul. We can’t leave a stone unturned to bring out that image as perfectly as possible!”16 At the same time this very urgency cautions us to be patient, to leave things in our Lord’s hands, and not to repeat the same correction too often. “One should rely on supernatural means and the passage of time.”17

15 2 Cor 11:29 16 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 30, 5 17 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 225 159 Correcting out of love

A consequence of love We can sum up all the characteristics of fraternal correction, and the virtues which we have to practise in carrying it out, in one: it must be done lovingly, without hurting or offending, with a supernatural spirit; it must be the fruit of the affection we have for our brothers or sisters. As St Augustine wrote: “We must correct out of love. Not wanting to hurt in any way; we simply want to help our brother change for the better. If we do it like that, we fulfil the precept which says: ‘If your brother sins against you, go and tell him his fault, between you and him alone’ (Mt 18:15). Why do you correct him? Is it because you are hurt at having been offended by him? That is not what God wants. If you correct him out of self-love, you are achieving nothing. But if you correct out of true love, you are doing something excellent. ‘If he listens to you,’ our Lord adds, ‘you have gained your brother.’ Correct with love, then, so as to win him.”18 Our Father often spelled out for us the need to do fraternal correction. He used to say that whenever he went to a Centre he could always tell how well the people there cared for one another simply by asking how many fraternal corrections were being done. This supernatural practice is an infallible thermometer of our affection. Any laziness or lack of interest in correcting people’s faults, or any other excuse that leads to neglect in this area, is always a sign of carelessness and latent selfishness. Our Father alerts us to these excuses, which “usually arise from a mistaken human respect or from an excessive spirit of comfort.”19 Hence, they never justify the failure to fulfil our obligation to do fraternal correction. Whenever the thought occurs to us, for example, that a particular correction is liable to upset someone, we know we have to put it aside

18 St Augustine, Sermon 82, 4 19 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 229

Along the Path to Love 160

as an invalid excuse; it probably just indicates that we don’t want to complicate our own life. Our Lord himself says: those whom I love, I reprove and chasten.20 Love sometimes means causing pain to the loved one. The correction may hurt initially, but the other person always ends up coming to the conclusion that they needed it, and, relying on God and on our affection, they face up to the situation and fully accept the correction. When we find it difficult to accept a correction, we should listen to the following words of Holy Scripture, as though written especially for us: My son, do not regard lightly the discipline of the Lord, nor lose courage when you are punished by him. For the Lord disciplines him whom he loves, and chastises every son whom he receives ... God is treating you as sons; for what son is there whom his father does not discipline ... ? For the moment all discipline seems painful rather than pleasant; later it yields the peaceful fruit of righteousness to those who have been trained by it.21 Hence, my daughters and sons, our Father writes, on occasions we may have no choice but to give ourselves a bad time and to give others a bad time too, if we want them to be better.22 To neglect this means of formation for any reason of this sort would be to deprive our brothers or sisters of something very important, namely, the grace that God attaches to it. And it would also imply that we are unjustly doubting their good spirit, all the more so since the Directors can always judge whether the correction is opportune or not. As St Augustine says, “it often happens that our brother is saddened initially when he is corrected, and he resists and struggles interiorly against it. But then he silently reflects on the matter, and, now, alone

20 Rev 3:19 21 Heb 12:5-11 22 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 73 161 Correcting out of love

before God and his conscience, he is not worried about displeasing men because they had to correct him; rather he is afraid of displeasing God if he doesn’t change his ways. Then he resolves not to do any more what he was corrected about, and the more he hates his sin, the more he loves his brother for having been the enemy of his sin.”23 Another excuse for not correcting others can be the fear of losing their esteem or affection. This would imply that our love is selfish and thinks only about receiving and not about giving. What we have to be concerned about is their holiness and the fullness of their dedication, and not about our own comfort and our desire to be esteemed and loved. Our mutual relationship is based on Christ’s charity, on our common dignity as Christians and on the vocation we share, and not simply on getting on well with one another. Hence fraternal correction makes our affection more supernatural. Another error we could fall into would be to recognize someone’s defects and faults all right, but seeing them as foibles which are so deeply ingrained that they are practically impossible to change. Whereas superficially we might seem to be making excuses for the person concerned, in fact it means that we are condemning him or her irrevocably, which is completely unfair. It would be a serious lack of true love, because real affection would enable us find some way of helping them see the defects for what they really are. True love doesn’t give up, even though there may be many reasons for doing so. As St John Chrysostom writes, “Who was more perceptive than King David? And yet he did not realize that he had sinned gravely (...) He needed the light of the prophet, and with his words he became aware of his fault. Similarly, God our Lord wants somebody to approach the sinner and tell him what he has done.”24

23 St Augustine, Letter 210, 2 24 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 60,1

Along the Path to Love 162

Excuses for omitting fraternal correction always stem from some kind of misconceived charity. We have to be truly affectionate and forget ourselves so as to work for the happiness of others. This is the spirit we need if we are to be always on the alert and ready to help others keep their dedication alive and cheerful, urging them to ever greater heights along the path to holiness. That is the first duty of charity to others, our Father tells us, because the first expression of charity is to help one another to be better. And that is not easy. It is much easier to sit back and not get involved. It is more comfortable, but it is not supernatural. And we would have to render an account to God for those omissions.25 To ensure that our affection is alive and active, we need to be conscious of our own pride: how it intervenes and clouds our vision. Pride can make us think that the fact that we also fall – perhaps even more frequently – into some defect, justifies tolerating the same defect in others. In these cases we have to be very humble so as to recognize the fallacy in the argument and realize that we do not correct others on the basis of our own moral authority: we are all made of the same clay, dust from the ground.26 Rather, we correct in virtue of a duty that God has entrusted to us. No matter how obvious our defects may be, they never excuse us from making fraternal correction. Isn’t it the case that a doctor who is sick himself, even chronically sick, is still able to heal others? Isn’t he able to treat people who suffer from the same illness as himself? Of course he is, and he can cure them too! All he needs is to be trained and to put his knowledge to good use. You may make mistakes – I make them too, lots of them – but they are not important if you really struggle to overcome them, even though you may not

25 From our Father, Brief Circle, 23 September 1962 26 Gen 2:7 163 Correcting out of love

appear to be making any progress. But even with your shortcomings, you can and ought help others overcome their faults and failings. Not to do so is a lack of prudence. It’s pride.27 This mistaken humility can at times disguise itself under the belief that we have fewer virtues ourselves, or that we are less supernatural, or are not as holy as the person who needs to be corrected. Or the person may be a director or be a long time in the Work. However, in all these cases our obligation to make the correction is all the greater, if such were possible, because of our affection for the person concerned and our love for the Work: the fact is that the Directors need extra help and support if they are to fulfil their responsibilities faithfully and efficiently. On doing the Fidelity we undertake especially to correct the Directors whenever they need it, but in fact from the moment we join the Work we all try to do that anyway. We know that the Directors always appreciate the corrections they receive.

A sense of responsibility We should take the topic of fraternal correction to our prayer often, so as to learn in conversation with our Lord how to help our brothers or sisters more effectively. The most important aspect of proselytism is to ensure that none of those who are already with us, in the boat of the Work, are lost. Our charity must be ordered: you and I, in the Work, are especially close to each other. We are in each other’s hands, and in the bleeding hands of Christ. Let us help one another then, always speaking the truth to one another, nobly, courteously, elegantly, and with supernatural affection. In a word, my daughters and sons, defend one another’s vocation because, after the gift of faith, it is the greatest treasure they have received.28

27 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 13 28 Ibid., note 34

Along the Path to Love 164

The thought that the holiness and the happiness of our brothers and sisters depends to a great extent on us personally, should encourage us particularly when we find it harder to be affectionate and concerned for them. If, for example, we find it difficult at some stage or other to make a fraternal correction, it may help us to consider what our Father says: Fraternal correction is always fruitful and supernaturally effective. This is so because you mortify yourself without mortifying others, and you speak clearly. In short, your intention is upright and you are not discourteous to anyone.29 To love, and to correct out of love, is a duty that helps us put ourselves in the background. What really matters to us is the situation of others, their joys and their sorrows, and the spirit of the Work which we want to pass on to all our brothers and sisters in its entirety. Our Father asks us: What’s your charity like? Charity makes us forget ourselves and think about others. If you are truly concerned about others, there comes a time when your daily examination can be quite short, because you haven’t been thinking about yourself: you’ve been thinking about our Lord and about others. Then, you won’t have time to be self-centred; you won’t have time for pride. All you will see will be opportunities to serve others.30 My daughters and sons, love with the love of God. Be objective, don’t be sentimental. That’s how to contribute effectively to fulfilling the Scriptures once again, so that what we read in the Acts of the Apostles about the first Christians can be said of us all: credentium erat cor unum, et anima una, those who believed were of one heart and soul (Acts 4:32).31

29 Ibid., note 24 30 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 133 31 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935, 41

## UNIVERSAL AND PERMANENT {#cuaderno.8.13}

From the very beginning, when the Work was still like a mustard seed, the smallest of all seeds,1 our Father already had his sons and daughters reflect on the fact that the Work didn’t arise just by accident (...) nor is it meant to be the answer to the needs of a particular country or historical moment, because right from the start Jesus wanted his Work to be catholic and universal.2 Its spirit transcends all geographical, historical, social and cultural divides and even the very unfolding of history. Our Lord doesn’t want the Work to be something merely transitory; he wants us to have a character that lasts forever.3 God has established that our spirit, which is based on the universal and permanent call to holiness, should have as its raw material something which is common to the human race in every place and time, namely, ordinary work, man’s daily professional activity. As a result, as long as there are men on this earth, Opus Dei will exist too. There will always be room for this phenomenon of people of every profession and trade seeking holiness in their state, in their own profession or trade, and being contemplative souls in the middle of the world.4 To enable us attain the goal of our vocation regardless of the

1 Mt 13:32 2 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 14-15 3 Ibid., 28 4 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 92

Along the Path to Love 166

circumstances of place or time, our Lord has placed at our disposal a tangible means to ensure we never get lost. This is our internal Law, which by God’s will contains everything we need for our sanctification and our effectiveness.5 That is why it is holy, unchangeable and everlasting. In our internal Law everything is so divinely fashioned that I assure you it is not my doing. It belongs to Him! Love it! Venerate it! It is the means God our Lord provides us with so we can travel along the road without fear of losing our way.6 The course of our life is perfectly charted. There is nothing that isn’t ... meticulously carved!7 It is carved like a precious masterpiece or a carefully crafted miniature to last for centuries to the praise and glory of God. And it is carved also as an indelible testimony, unaffected by the passage of time or the vicissitudes of climate or season. For ever, O Lord, thy word is firmly fixed in the heavens. Thy faithfulness endures to all generations.8

The spirit of the Work is ageless God has entrusted this treasure to us, and our primary obligation is to guard and defend it exactly as we have received it. We must be convinced once and for all that we are not engaged in a merely human pursuit, since ours is a divine enterprise, and as a consequence, it is not for us to cut out, change or yield in anything that belongs to the spirit and organization of the Work of God.9 On the contrary, it is up to us to become Opus Dei. To this end, we must root out any interior obstacles and steep ourselves in its spirit, assimilate its apostolic methods and put into practice down to the last detail the instructions

5 From our Father, Meditation, 12 April 1954 6 Ibid. 7 From our Father, Meditation, 2 October 1956 8 Ps 119:89-90 9 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 20 167 Universal and permanent

and Norms of behaviour our Father has left us. And we in turn must pass them on to others. “What has been entrusted to you ought to remain in you and be handed on to another as well. Gold you have received, pay back in gold.”10 Although all of us have the duty to guard and keep our internal Law, it applies particularly to the Directors, who “are obliged to foster the fulfilment of the spirit and law of the Work and prudently and effectively to ensure its implementation, in such a way that no contrary custom may ever arise or any of its precepts be left unfulfilled.”11 There will never come a time, either now or in the centuries to come, in which circumstances would advocate habitually abandoning some point of our internal Law. The universality and permanence of our spirit has a number of special characteristics. Not only will the Work never die, neither will it ever grow old. The Work will always be young, always new, always full of beauty and vigour. Like the Church’s doctrine, in whose womb it was born and lives, so is the spirit of Opus Dei: “like a precious liqueur contained in a noble vessel, it is constantly renewed by the power of the Holy Spirit, and itself renews the vessel which contains it.”12 The Work will never have any difficulty adapting to the world; it will never have to face the problem of bringing itself up to date. God brought his Work up to date once and for all by giving it the lay, secular characteristics which I have described for you in this letter. We will never need to adapt to the world, since we are of the world; nor will we ever have to follow in the wake of human progress for we are – you are, my daughters and sons – the ones who, together with the other citizens of this world, are bringing about this progress with your ordinary work.13

10 St Vincent of Lerins, Commonitorium, 22 11 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 342 12 St Irenaeus, Adversus haereses, 3, 24 13 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 92

Along the Path to Love 168

Years and centuries will pass, and human society will be enriched with new discoveries, new activities, new sources of progress. If we are faithful, someone of Opus Dei will have a role in all these events, because through our vocation we are present at the very birth of the upright innovations that take place in the life of society, and we also make our own the progress of each era. Down through the ages our outlook and our activity will always correspond fully to the needs and demands of the moment.14 Wherever fresh horizons dawn in the field of human endeavour, it will not be long before a soul appears there trying to do Opus Dei. We will never cease to identify with the yearnings that attract the minds and hearts of men (...) We feel profoundly the obligation not to abandon any upright field of human enterprise because ubicumque fuerit corpus, illic congregabuntur et aquilae: wherever the body is, there the eagles will be gathered together (Mt 24:28).15 Being permanently up to date with the mentality and tastes of each successive generation poses no problem for the Work. This adapting to new circumstances, which in Italian is called aggiornamento, is nothing other than identifying with the particular attitudes and way of life of each generation and thereby contributing effectively to it,16 as a living organism with a divine mission. Inanimate bodies react to changes in their surroundings in a passive way, their only limitation being the ability of their nature to sustain the change. Living creatures, on the other hand, operate quite differently: they select from what their environment offers, and above all they enter into it and modify it, imposing upon it the law of their own life processes. To conform to the environment would be to die or, at least, to acknowledge that one has no contribution to make. To adapt to it, on the other hand, means taking

14 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1950, 21 15 Ibid. 16 Ibid.

169 Universal and permanent

all its characteristics into account so as to avoid what is harmful, while employing its positive features to best advantage, in order to achieve one’s purpose.

Fidelity to the spirit of the Work In order to attain the particular goal of the Work, in all sectors and circumstances of society, our first concern must be to preserve integrally the specific spirit of our vocation.17 If anyone, inspired by a misguided zeal to introduce reforms, attempted to modify the apostolic methods to which, in the normal course of events, God has willed to harness the sanctifying effectiveness of the Work, the result would be utterly fruitless. It would be like trying to convert the Work of God into the work of man. A son or daughter of God in his Opus Dei is never afraid of getting ‘out of touch’ with the times, among other things, because your formation can never be considered finished. All life long you have to keep on perfecting your human, spiritual, doctrinal, apostolic and professional formation, all with a marvellous spirit of humility. Apart from other reasons, this is a consequence of your secularity, which requires you to embody the spirit our Lord has given us with great suppleness and without ever becoming immobile. This spirit, by its very nature, is not restricted by particular circumstances of time and space; down through the centuries it will always be able to respond to the most diverse changes and situations in society.18 Thanks to the superiority complex our Father spoke about so much, and which is not in any sense arrogant or obnoxious, we should be able to recognize when something is merely a misguided hankering after novelty or the latest ideological fashion. Daughters and sons of my

17 Ibid. 18 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 19

Along the Path to Love 170

soul, you need to be wide awake and take precautions, as one does during an epidemic. You see, it might occur to some people that unless we keep up to date with the latest trends it could hamper our activity, and they could then have a complex about losing our effectiveness if we maintain a discreet reserve with respect to these things and fail to adapt to them. Get this idea clear. In the Work we don’t have to make a special effort to keep up to date, because our secular vocation doesn’t allow us to lag behind. We are always abreast of those around us, our fellow men. Nevertheless, while we welcome and exalt all human values, we are intolerant with error. We love everything human, but we filter from it the dross of sin so as to offer it undefiled to God.19 In the Work we cultivate the individual’s free personality. Thus we are people who can think for ourselves and who don’t accept unquestioningly the cliches and catchphrases which are all the rage at the moment. Our formation teaches us to act as a sieve, which retains what is valuable and leaves the rest behind.20 As Tertullian said, “to live together with pagans does not mean sharing their customs. We live together with everyone. We rejoice with them because we share the same nature, but not their superstitions. We have the same soul, but not the same way of acting. We are co-owners of the world, but not of error.”21 A Christian is salt,22 leaven,23 and the light of the world,24 endowed with a mission to make this world divine by filling it with light, purifying it and making it ferment with love of God. The Gospel message is of its nature uncompromising. Our Lord

19 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 30 20 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 25 21 Tertullian, De idolatria, 1, 4, 5 22 Mt 5:13 23 Mt 13:33 24 Mt 5:14 171 Universal and permanent

has said: he who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters.25 Any supernatural undertaking which our Lord chooses to inspire on this earth necessarily has to encounter some opposition, just as a nail encounters resistance when it is driven into a wall. This opposition arises precisely because of the fact that the world has not yet been made divine. God wants to use us as instruments in this task. We have to allow for difficulties, not as something negative, but as one more factor we have to make use of to develop the apostolate. We shouldn’t exaggerate their importance, but neither should we pretend that they don’t exist. Years ago our Founder wrote: ‘And in a paganised or pagan environment when my life clashes with its surroundings, won’t my naturalness seem artificial? ’ you ask me. And I reply: Undoubtedly your life will clash with theirs; and that contrast – faith confirmed by works! – is exactly the naturalness I ask of you.26 The consistency between faith and conduct, without facile concessions to the surroundings or current fashions, is the best proof that we are convinced of the truth of what we teach, and consequently is a precondition of apostolic effectiveness above and beyond the fluctuations of time and place. You have to attract others above all by the integrity of your lives, with the humble but daring affirmation of living among your fellow men in a Christian manner, unostentatiously but consistently. We have to show our faith in deeds. This, with God’s help, will be the reason for our effectiveness. Don’t be afraid of the world; that’s where we belong. If we live our spirit in union with God, then nothing can harm us. At times our

25 Lk 11:23 26 The Way, 380

Along the Path to Love 172

Christian conduct may seem strange to people estranged from God. In that case, supported by divine omnipotence, you will have to have the courage to be faithful. For my children I pray for the fortitude of spirit which will enable them to carry their environment with them wherever they go. A son or daughter of God in his Work has to be like a burning coal: wherever it is, it sets things on fire, or at least raises the spiritual temperature, and leads people to live an intensely Christian life.27

Defending the spirit of the Work resolutely We have seen how we have to be strong and resolute in giving a supernatural tone to all environments. We are called upon to put this same virtue into practice in defending our spiritual makeup and the nature of our apostolic activity, as much now as when the Work was just a tiny seed. At that time the Work was so small that it was difficult for people who were unacquainted with its nature and the effects, which by God’s will it was destined to have, to perceive it at all. Even though circumstances change, we will always have to be just as resolute to defend the Work, because it is easier to want to be accepted by all than run the risk of falling out with anybody.28 Our Father used often say that we are not anti-anything.29 We will never put out a light that is lit in the name of Christ. Our only concern is to be faithful to our vocation and do God’s will. We will often have to go against the current, as we have nearly always done, in opening up new channels and new routes, not out of any desire for novelty, but because we want to be loyal to Jesus Christ and his teaching. The easy solution is to let oneself be carried along, but opting for the easy

27 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 11 28 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 25 29 Ibid.

173 Universal and permanent

solution is often a sign of lack of responsibility. It is true that you must always live among the people of your time, in accordance with their mentality and customs, but always be prepared to make a defence ... for the hope that is in you (1 Pet 3:15) in Christ Jesus. It should never happen that the fact that you don’t have to adapt to your surroundings, since you are already among your fellow men, means that you cannot be distinguished as disciples of Christ. What a great deal of wishful thinking, fear and cowardice there is in certain desires for adaptation!30 If we are strong and daring, and have a firm faith in God and an unshakeable confidence in his Work, we will always be able to say with the amazement of the disciples as they contemplated the firstfruits of the miracles being worked by their hands in Christ’s name: ‘There’s no denying our influence on environment!’31 Over and above our personal qualities, if we are faithful to the spirit of the Work and its apostolic activity, we will engender here on earth that Kingdom of Heaven which Christ compares to a leaven which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal, till it was all leavened.32 “Our Lord has mixed us, his believers, into the multitude,” says St John Chrysostom, “so that we make others share our faith. Let no one throw the blame on our small numbers. The power of the Gospel message is so great that once it has fermented it becomes fresh leaven for others ... If twelve men fermented the whole world, how evil we must be, now that we are so many, if we are unable to reform those who remain, because we ought to be enough, and more than enough, to be leaven for a thousand worlds. ‘But’, you will tell me, ‘they were Apostles.’

30 Ibid. 31 The Way, 376 32 Mt 13:33

Along the Path to Love 174

And what does that mean? Were they not men like you? Were they not educated in the cities? Didn’t they enjoy the same things you do? Didn’t they have jobs? Were they perhaps angels? Did they by any chance come down from heaven?”33 God does not ask us for anything which, with his grace, is not within our grasp. Out of pure goodness and love he has willed to associate us with his redemptive plan and provides us with more than enough means to be effective instruments. We, for our part, have to be like the faithful and prudent servant whom the master has set over his household to give them their food at the proper time.34 In the midst of the world, our Father wrote, which we love with all our soul, we have to be able to look upwards and attain that divine wisdom which makes us men with criteria and bestows true discernment, which makes us firm in faith and generous in charity, and which enables us, through love for truth and readiness to serve, to engage those around us in a dialogue of light and love. Incorporated in Christ, stay firm in the truth, because omnia in ipso constant (Col 1:17), all that is built on Him remains.35

33 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 46, 2-3 34 Cf Mt 24:45 35 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1965, 75

## LOVE FOR THE WORK AND COLLECTIVE HUMILITY {#cuaderno.8.14}

From the moment God’s call first made itself present in our lives, we began to acquire a growing awareness of the truth of these words of our Founder: My dearest children, in my conversations with you I have repeatedly pointed out that the enterprise we are engaged in is not something invented by men, but is rather a great supernatural enterprise, which from the beginning fulfilled exactly all the conditions to be truly called without boasting the Work of God ... The Work of God has not been dreamed of by a man to solve the pitiable situation of the Church in Spain since 1931. Many years ago God revealed it to a clumsy and deaf instrument, who saw it for the first time on the feast of the Guardian Angels, the 2nd of October 1928.1

God’s loving providence towards Opus Dei Our Father often used to say that the entire history of the Work is the history of God’s mercies.2 Our Lord personally supervised the construction of this supernatural edifice, using our Founder as a very faithful instrument; and now, by means of the Father, who always acts

1 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 1, 6, 7 2 From our Father, Letter 25 January 1961, 1

Along the Path to Love 176

in complete harmony with our Founder’s spirit, God continues to oversee its development and expansion throughout the world. This is how God does things: one at a time, guiding our steps, making use of secondary causes, getting human beings involved. See what the Acts of the Apostles tells us about Saul’s conversion. Wounded by divine grace, he says: Domine, quid me vis facere? Lord, what do you want me to do? And he hears the divine reply: surge et ingredere in civitatem et ibi dicetur tibi quid te oporteat facere: rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you are to do (Acts 9:6). Don’t you see? First God gives a grace, then he asks for something to be done, while it is he who decides the time, the manner and the circumstances. That’s what he did with his Work: first one section, then another, and afterwards – another gift – the priests. And at every step of our journey, in every campaign that had to be waged in this beautiful war of peace, our Lord always treated me like that: first do this, now do that. That’s why I ask you to join with me in giving thanks to Our Father God for his continuous loving providence towards us. The thought of all this divine goodness makes me really repentant for my failure to respond properly to his great mercy, and also because along the way I have been the cause of others having to suffer for mistakes that were my fault – I’m incapable of accepting injustice except under protest and with tears, irrespective of its cause or who suffers it. As I was saying, for mistakes that were my fault, and because God our Lord had to prepare me: it seemed that for each time he managed to strike the nail on the head he struck a hundred more blows on the horseshoe (...) perhaps it was because the suffering of others hurt me more. However, my children, along with contrition comes Love: none of these toils or troubles ever made me lose the gaudium cum pace, because God taught me to love, and nullo enim modo sunt onerosi 177 Love for the Work and collective humility

labores amantium (St Augustine, De bono vid. 21, 26); for the person who loves, toil is never a heavy burden. And so the important thing is to learn to love, because in eo quod amatur, aut non laboratur, aut et labor amatur (ibid.): where there is love, everything is happiness. God in his great mercy led me like a little child and taught me to love. When I was scarcely an adolescent he cast into my soul a seed of burning love, and today, my daughters and sons, that seed is a leafy tree with a graceful trunk, and refreshes in its shade a legion of souls.3

Love for the Work and fidelity to our vocation The years have passed – still not many, for an institution that has to last for as long as men live on this earth – and in many corners of the world the fruits of that divine implantation in our Founder’s heart are already abundant and mature. As time goes by we have more and more reasons for giving thanks to God, who leads us with his fatherly hand along the pathways of fulfilment of his most holy Will. When I reflect on how far we have come since 1928, my children, I see myself like a little child in the company of a very affectionate father. A little toddler is not asked to do a whole series of things at once. First he is given one job, and then another, and then another when he has finished the previous one. Have you ever seen a little child playing with his father? The child has a few wooden blocks of different shapes and colours, and his father says to him: put this one here, and that one there, and that red one over there ... and eventually, lo and behold, a castle! Well, my children, that’s how I see myself: God has been leading me along: ludens coram eo omni tempore, ludens in orbe terrarum: rejoicing before him always, rejoicing in his

3 Ibid., 2-3

Along the Path to Love 178

inhabited world (Prov 8:30-31), like in a divine game. And when the game is over, see the wonderful castle that has appeared! Opus sanctum, bonum, pulchrum, amabile! A Work that is his, with wonderful colours, and a great variety of shape and outline, which is all a reflection of God’s Goodness.4 As we get to know the Work we are enthralled to discover more clearly every day how attractive and beautiful it is; our own shortcomings, by comparison, become all the more glaringly obvious! However, God has chosen precisely us to do Opus Dei on earth: you did not choose me, but I chose you.5 If the thought of your mistakes and failings makes you feel inadequate – seeing what I’m like, how can I sanctify the world? – you should immediately hear a resounding assertion echo in your mind and heart: sufficit tibi gratia mea: my grace is sufficient for you (2 Cor 12:9).6 The grace of God, the divine vocation we received, is the surest guarantee we can have that we can bring God’s Work to completion, both in our own souls and among our fellow men in the world. United to our Lord, we can do all things: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat: (Phil 4:13); we can do all things in Him who will strengthen us, even though we make mistakes and commit errors, as long as we struggle not to.7 Thus, little by little, trying to respond to the help of grace, each of us gradually becomes Opus Dei; for what does love seek, except to adhere to the loved one, and if possible, to be just one whole?8 To become one, to be totally identified with the Work: each of us, through our life of dedication to the Church’s service, ought to be Opus Dei –

4 Ibid., 2 5 Jn 15:16 6 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1954, 27 7 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 58 8 St Augustine, De ordine, 2, 18, 48 179 Love for the Work and collective humility

that is, operatio Dei – work of God, in order to do Opus Dei on earth.9 By being faithful to our calling we become instruments in the hands of the divine Craftsman to polish this precious stone: God thereby accomplishes his Work on earth, sanctifying us, and making use of our littleness to sanctify the world. The soul becomes deified. Its life is a complete contrast with what it was before and what it so often encounters around it!10 This good deification is the fruit of response to grace and the humility of self-knowledge, and it ensures we never attribute to ourselves what belongs to God alone. Deification without humility? A bad business! our Father warned us. And if it is corporate, worse still! For you O Lord, save your humble people and humble the proud (Ps 17:28).11

Love for collective humility God has willed to adorn the Work with a characteristic, namely, collective humility, to keep us from being collectively proud. Our Father wrote: I always taught you that in our spirituality collective humility is fundamental. If John and Peter and Andrew, taken individually, are supposed to be humble, I cannot understand why together they can have the right to be as proud as peacocks.12 Collective humility has its roots in personal humility, which in turn is nourished by self-knowledge. You ... pride? About what?13 We are only God’s instruments: he is the one who works the miracles. That’s why we shun applause and praise. We do not seek people’s gratitude for our services to the Church and all mankind. The theological virtue of hope gives us such a great appreciation for the reward our Father

9 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1960, 4 10 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 3 11 Ibid., 4 12 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1954, 13 13 The Way, 600

Along the Path to Love 180

God has promised us, that we don’t wish to risk losing it through a lack of collective humility. We don’t wish to have applied to us, for having sought the applause of men, those words of our Lord: amen, dico vobis, receperunt mercedem suam: truly, I say to you, they have their reward (Mt 6:16). How sad it would be! That’s why we don’t want people to praise us or give us public recognition. We want to work silently and humbly, with interior joy – servite Domino in laetitia: serve the Lord with gladness (Ps 100:2)

– with an apostolic ardour that doesn’t fade precisely because it doesn’t try to show off or do anything spectacular. We want there to be, in all professions and all walks of life, select groups of men and women who, without waving flags or wearing identifying labels, live holy lives and influence their companions and society at large for the welfare of souls. This is the exclusive desire of the Work.14 We wish to pass unnoticed, working quietly, but without any mystique or secrecy, which, incidentally, we have never had any use for, nor will we ever have, because God has no use for them either; and besides, decent upright people find them quite off-putting anyway. So, work away quietly, with a personal humility that is so deep that it will lead you necessarily to practise collective humility, not to want to receive individually the esteem and appreciation which are due to the Work of God and to the holy lives of your brothers and sisters. This heroic degree of collective humility, which many people fail to understand, is what leads the members of the Work to pass unnoticed among their equals in the world, without being praised for the good seed they sow, because their companions hardly realize, nor are they entirely able to explain, that bonus odor Christi, the aroma of Christ (2 Cor 2:15) that should always be detectable in the lives of my children.15

14 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 65 15 Ibid., 64 181 Love for the Work and collective humility

It would be completely foreign to our spirit to want to attribute to ourselves, personally or collectively, what belongs exclusively to God. We cannot allow, even for an instant, any vain thoughts because of our service to God; for in that very moment we would cease to be supernaturally effective.16 In the apostolate we are not interested in statistics, or how many members we have, or measuring how successful we are, when it only serves to satisfy people’s curiosity or create a feeling of complacency. If we were to contract this weakness we would immediately begin to lose our collective humility and would be in danger of acquiring an esprit de corps, which is divisive and therefore not very ecumenical.17 The fact that we are the instruments of a wonderfully divine enterprise doesn’t mean that we should entertain any mistaken notions of our own importance, nor should we allow anyone else to do so either. The sight of the marvels God works through our hands should serve as an occasion for us to humble ourselves, to praise God, to acknowledge that everything comes from him and that we have done nothing more than get in the way, or at best are only poor instruments in his hands.18 The credit for all the success belongs to God. Neither he who plants nor he who waters is anything, but only God who gives the growth.19 In dedicating ourselves to God’s service, we are motivated in our selfgiving only by the desire to give God all the glory, and to serve the Church and all souls without seeking any credit for the Work or any personal gain.20

16 Ibid., 88 17 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1954, 13 18 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 89 19 1 Cor 3:7 20 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 21

Along the Path to Love 182

Making the truth known At the same time humility leads us to recognize and love the supernatural beauty of the Work, which is an instrument of salvation positively willed by God – in the bosom of his Church – and it ought to be known and loved for what it is, namely, an expression of God’s infinite mercy and a great benefit to mankind. And so we don’t wish to let error or ignorance succeed in obscuring its beauty. We want the greatest possible number of souls to get to know and love the Work so they can benefit from its spirit. It would be an insult to God’s grace and mercy if we were to keep quiet in the presence of false statements about the nature, means or aims of Opus Dei, or if we were to allow lies or misrepresentations to impede people from getting to know the Work and benefiting from its spirit. It would be a great sign of ingratitude. It would be an injustice, which, as St Jerome says, is the fruit of pride: “No one is as proud as the one who shows himself ungrateful.”21 “Humility leads man, in consideration of his defects, not to be proud. But to despise God’s gifts is not a sign of humility but of ingratitude.”22 Collective humility is not intended to restrict the supernatural love we should have for our Beautiful Mother the Work. It is impossible to go too far in this love, because God is both its source and its object. What collective humility does ensure is that it doesn’t become a disguised form of self-love, that is, love for oneself as an individual or as a part of a group. When faced with events that, in some people’s eyes, could reflect badly on the supernatural character and the divine features of the Work, it would be a sign of self-love and laziness to act as if nothing were amiss and remain silent or completely unruffled. Love for the truth, for Holy Church, and for the Work, at times

21 St Jerome, Letter 148 22 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.35, a.2 ad 3 183 Love for the Work and collective humility

leads us to get people to appreciate the characteristics of our spirit, while never wanting to be newsworthy.23 Thus, according to circumstances, we may tend to lay more or less stress on certain aspects: universality, respect for freedom, the exclusively supernatural nature of our aims, or our spirit of understanding and of living peacefully with everybody. This task must always be preceded and accompanied by prayer, because if the Spirit does not assist the hearts of the listeners, the word of the teacher is of no avail (St Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels, 30, 3). And, since it also requires us to be extremely prudent, I have repeatedly asked you to be very careful with terminology, because careless forms of expression can give rise to misunderstanding and do very definite, and possibly considerable, harm to our apostolate.24 To avoid this happening we need to learn the precise meaning of the expressions we use to describe the Work; they are the fruit of our Father’s prayerful toil, and express fully, and in a readily intelligible manner, the theological, ascetical and juridical nature of Opus Dei. We also need to ask our Lord for the ability to get them across to people with the ‘gift of tongues’. Our love for God and for souls may at times require us to react quite strongly. It is true that in putting things right we always have to act reasonably and amicably; we should be understanding even with those who do not understand us.25 We appreciate that some people don’t realize how much harm they do, so we have to forgive them. However, charity itself requires us to make them see the wrong they are doing and the consequences their actions can have, so as to avoid them committing serious sin and bringing about their own ruin. “Hatred of a person’s evil is equivalent to love of his good. Hence also this perfect

23 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1954, 3 24 Ibid. 25 From our Father, Crónica, May 1966, p.11

Along the Path to Love 184

hatred belongs to charity. Such hatred is perfect, of which the psalmist himself says, ‘I hate them with perfect hatred’ (Ps 139:22).”26 When people who are corrected eventually recognize the error of their ways and make amends, they feel understood in that former apparent lack of understanding. That is why “the just seem to be indignant but without indignation; they seem to be mistrusting, but do not mistrust; they seem to persecute, but they love.”27 Our love for the Work has to have practical expression. Collective humility requires us to recognize the truth and give all glory to God. By our word and example, and by following the guidelines we get from the Father and the Directors, we will succeed in revealing the beauty of our Beautiful Mother to all peoples. Our life of dedication to the service of God and souls helps men recognize God’s gifts, to give thanks for them and to derive benefit from them.

26 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.25, a.6 ad 1 27 St Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels, 2, 34, 2

## GOD’S INSTRUMENTS {#cuaderno.8.15}

“Since he wishes to continue his witness and his service through the laity also, the supreme and eternal priest, Christ Jesus, vivifies them with his spirit and ceaselessly impels them to accomplish every good and perfect work. To those whom he intimately joins to his life and mission he also gives a share in his priestly office, to offer spiritual worship for the glory of the Father and the salvation of man.”1 God was not compelled to involve us in his work of redemption, for “the soul benefits from the aid of human instruction when the one who acts to secure this benefit is God, but God could give the Gospel to men without the aid of any man.”2 Nevertheless he wanted to make use of us so as to reward us afterwards for certain accomplishments which are really his. For consider your call, brethren; not many of you were wise according to worldly standards, not many were powerful, not many were of noble birth; but God chose what is foolish in the world to shame the wise, God chose what is weak to shame the strong, God chose what is low and despised in the world, even things that are not, to bring to nothing things that are, so that no human being might boast in the presence of God. He is the source of your life in Christ Jesus, whom God made our wisdom, our righteousness and sanctification and redemption; therefore, as it is written, ‘Let him who boasts, boast of the Lord’.3

1 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 34 2 St Augustine, De doctrina christiana, 4, 17, 34 3 1 Cor 1:26-31

Along the Path to Love 186

Like a brush in the artist’s hand Left to himself man can only accomplish works that are proportionate to his nature. “And if our nature could not remain firm in doing good when it was healthy, how much less can it recover by itself once it was weakened?”4 If it was left so badly wounded by original sin that we cannot even avoid all venial sins without the special assistance of grace,5 how much less could we, without divine help, ever communicate supernatural life to others and so participate in Christ’s redemptive work? For the children of God were born, not of blood nor of the will of the flesh nor of the will of man, but of God.6 It is impossible for us, in our natural human condition, to communicate supernatural life to others. However, “the gift of grace raises man above the level of human nature”;7 his actions then acquire a divine efficacy, and share in the power of God who is able from these stones to raise up children to Abraham.8 In the apostolate “the Holy Spirit uses man’s tongue as a sort of instrument, and it is the same Spirit who completes the work inwardly. Hence St Gregory says: ‘Unless the Holy Spirit fills the hearts of the hearers, the master’s voice resounds in vain in their ears’.”9 This our Father has graphically taught us: you are but the brush in the hands of the artist. And nothing more.10 For a brush to be a useful and effective instrument, it must first of all be good enough to take up the pigment well and allow the artist to trace thick or thin lines, and strong or subdued colours, as he chooses.

4 St Bernard, Sermon on the Annunciation, 1, 1 5 Cf St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.109, a.8 c 6 Jn 1:13 7 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.171, a.2 ad 3 8 Mt 3:9 9 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.177, a.1 10 The Way, 612 187 God’s instruments

Secondly, the brush must subordinate its own quality to the use the painter wishes to make of it, for it is the painter who composes the figures and combines the colours, who plays with light and shadow, who combines darker tones with brighter ones, and who gives relief, depth and harmony to the work until it forms a coherent whole, full of elastic vigour. It is the artist who is the principal author of the work and who eventually signs it. Finally, the brush must be easy to hold and work with. If it doesn’t follow the painter’s every movement faithfully, then it cannot contribute to the work of art. This is basic for any useful instrument. Its quality and performance are of value only insofar as they are subordinated to the artist; otherwise they only get in the way. A good artist can produce a masterpiece even with a mediocre brush, but the best brush in the world can do nothing without a painter.

We are God’s instruments The image of the artist’s brush is only an inadequate description of our relationship with God, for even though the brush does not amount to much, it still has a being of its own, which it has not received from the artist. We, by contrast, are God’s creatures entirely; he has given us life and preserves us in being. Even in the natural order we need the divine concurrence for any action. As clay in the hand of the potter – for all his ways are as he pleases – so men are in the hand of him who made them, to give them as he decides.11 If it is true that we are God’s instruments in the natural order, it is far more the case in the supernatural sphere, where we are utterly in need of divine help, since by ourselves we could never achieve something so completely beyond us. In our supernatural activity the

11 Sir 33:13

Along the Path to Love 188

only essential component is the action of Almighty God, who out of sheer goodness chooses to use us as instruments merely to allow us share the glory of his divine work. For our spiritual life and apostolate to be truly instrumental, three elements are required: quality, subordination and union. In other words, we must be well prepared, so as to act responsibly, with all our initiative, and as perfectly as humanly possible. We must be humble, fully convinced that it is God who does everything. And we must be united to our Lord through interior life and obedience. This makes our action, which in itself is worth nothing, be a source of supernatural life. As St Paul said: I worked harder than any of them, though it was not I, but the grace of God which is with me.12 When God our Lord plans some work for the benefit of mankind, he first thinks of the persons whom he will use as instruments ... and gives them the necessary graces.13 God has chosen us to do his Work, and this divine vocation entitles us to receive the grace to carry out well the apostolic tasks he has entrusted us with. “‘His work is perfect’ (Deut 32:4). Consequently, any one who receives power from above also receives those things that render him competent to exercise that power.”14 When a person has to accomplish tasks of special responsibility, develop broader apostolates or take charge of them, then our Lord bestows his grace more abundantly. The Directors our Father writes ought to appreciate with complete certainty that they have a special grace from God, and be confident that our Lord will help them fulfil the duties of their position.15 We, for our part, have to act responsibly, because we are not inert

12 1 Cor 15:10 13 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 48 14 St Thomas, Supplementum, q.35, a.1 c 15 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 7 189 God’s instruments

and lifeless, but free, thinking beings who are meant to use our minds and hearts to give ourselves completely and be apt instruments of God’s action. We also need a great deal of humility. If we are not to hinder the action of grace, we have to be conscious of our own weakness and of God’s power and mercy. Together with a sense of responsibility and an awareness of our own weakness, we also need the union with God that we get from interior life and obedience, so that the love and vitality of divine wisdom can reach us effortlessly. This makes everything we do simultaneously our work and God’s work, and hence divinely effective in spite of our unworthiness.

In order to be useful, serve Responsibility: the instruments cannot be left to rust.16 We must always be ready to second the action of grace. Who then is the faithful and wise servant, whom his master has set over his household, to give them their food at the proper time?17 The servant left in charge of the Master’s household must be faithful and vigilant. “In the family of the just man who lives by faith and is as yet a pilgrim journeying on to the celestial city, even those who rule serve those whom they seem to command, for they rule not from a love of power, but from a sense of the duty they owe to others – not because they are proud of authority, but because they love mercy.”18 We have often heard our Father say: In order to be useful, serve. Unless we are ready to serve we cannot be good instruments. Not for nothing have I often said that I want to be ut iumentum, like a little donkey before God. This, though it is difficult, is how we have to be,

16 The Way, 486 17 Mt 24:45 18 St Augustine, The City of God, 19, 14

Along the Path to Love 190

you and I. Let us ask the Blessed Virgin for humility, since she called herself ancilla Domini. Service. How fervently do you say serviam every day? Is it just a word, or is it a cry that issues from the depths of your soul? 19 This eagerness to serve is shown in the use we make of all the practical means the Work provides for administering our apostolic tasks, among which collegial decision-making stands out as especially important. Our Father writes: It makes me very happy to see that you love collegiality and abhor tyranny. Our Lord has no reason to give inspiration to people who fail to train their colleagues properly. Collegiality is an expression of humility because it means that no one trusts his or her own judgment.20 Besides getting the opinion of those who work with us, we also feel obliged to harvest the experience of those who have preceded us, and not be so foolish as to want to revolutionise and tear down everything; what we have to do is improve on the legacy of past experience and hand it on to others. We can then be very proud of the fact that there are many more knowledgeable people around, who begin where we left off, and for whom we have acted as a pedestal.21 We also need to apply ourselves methodically and professionally to improve both our preparation and the way we carry out our apostolic tasks. Don’t you see, now that we are in the era of technology, how instruments are invented and developed to accomplish some purpose?
