We ought to do the same, except that we must take into account our vanity, our pride and our blindness, which get in the way and greatly hinder the progress of the pusillus grex our Lord has entrusted us with. What we need is collective humility at a very practical level, so

19 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 20 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 61 21 Ibid., 52 191 God’s instruments

we are always perfectly aware that we are not good instruments, and that we are lacking in mortification and many abilities we really should have because of all the grace our Lord showers upon us ... My children, if you ever get upset over something, consider that the real problem is not the incident itself or the person involved, but you and me, who have not prepared ourselves to be proper instruments.22 We have to be forever working at improving our formation. Bear in mind that God wants his instruments to do what they can to be properly prepared, so we have to make sure that we never lack this good disposition. As a result we will observe all around us how good effects multiply and men’s minds and behaviour fill with salt and light.23

The humility of instruments When we put all our thought and energy into the task that God gives us, and eventually begin to see its fruits appear, beware lest you say in your heart, ‘My power and the might of my hand have got me this wealth’.24 To behave like this would be preposterous, because the fruits are principally the work of the grace God grants his faithful servants, and if it were to become an occasion of pride he would withdraw it again. We are exposed to a very subtle and an almost imperceptible snare of the enemy who, when he sees us becoming more successful, redoubles his efforts to deceive us. This subtle danger, my daughters and sons, is a kind of hidden pride, and is often encountered in souls dedicated to working for God. It arises from the realization that we are the instruments of a wonderfully divine enterprise, and is a form of quiet self-satisfaction at seeing the miracles we perform in the apostolate. For we see minds that were blind recover their sight, wills that were

22 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 23 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 115 24 Deut 8:17

Along the Path to Love 192

paralyzed move once again, hearts of stone turn to flesh and become capable of supernatural charity and human affection, consciences covered with the leprosy of sin being cleansed, and completely dead and rotten souls recover supernatural life – iam foetet, quatriduanus est enim: by this time there will be an odour, for he has been dead four days (Jn 11:39). We also see difficulties being overcome, misunderstandings being straightened out, situations being turned around, and the apostolate becoming ever more extensive, more diverse and more effective ... All this, my children, at times can prove to be the occasion for an unjustified, but very real, form of conceit. Since we have to be on our guard against it ever happening, we need to have a very sensitive conscience and be able to react immediately. We cannot allow, even for an instant, any vain thoughts because of our service to God; for in that very moment we would cease to be supernaturally effective (...) The sight of the marvels God works through our hands should serve as an occasion for us to humble ourselves, to praise God, to acknowledge that everything comes from him and that we have done nothing more than get in the way, or at best are only poor instruments in his hands.25 We should never forget that all man’s merits are gifts from God, and consequently man is in greater debt to God than God to man.26 If there can be said to be any merit there, then the more effective the apostolate, the more indebted we are to our Lord. When you get the impression that you’ve worked hard in God’s service, repeat the words which He himself has taught us: servi inutiles sumus; quod debuimus facere, fecimus: we are unworthy servants; we have only done what was our duty (Lk 17:10).27 Rather, we should think: how good our Lord is!

25 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 87-89 26 St Bernard, Sermon on the Annunciation, 1, 2 27 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 90 193 God’s instruments

He has sought us out. He has made known to us this holy way of being effective, of loving all God’s creatures and bringing them peace and joy.28 If we don’t grow in humility, through the effectiveness of our work, we have reason to fear what St Jerome tells us: “To prophesy, to perform miracles and to cast out demons, at times is no credit to the one who does it. What achieves the result is the invocation of Christ. Sometimes it is done for the benefit of those who see or hear such marvels, and sometimes it is even for the condemnation of those who invoke him.”29

Being aware of our limitations The arrogance of attributing to oneself the supernatural effectiveness of the work may also have another expression, which is discouragement. I can’t understand how you want to give up that apostolic work – unless your motive is hidden pride: you think yourself perfect – just because God’s fire that attracted you and so often gives the light and warmth that arouse your enthusiasm, should also at times produce the smoke due to the weakness of the instrument.30 Sometimes our Lord lets us see our shortcomings a certain amount. And if we are not completely convinced that it is only God who gives the growth,31 and instead place our trust in our own power and not in his, when that happens we can pass from being over-confident to becoming totally discouraged. Our Father warns us: if any of you were to think you were not a very suitable instrument for this universal work, because your humility – perhaps I should say your pride – made you see your defects, I would like to remind you of these words of St

28 From our Father, Meditation, 29 March 1959 29 St Jerome, Commentary on St Matthew, 1, 7, 27 30 The Way, 485 31 1 Cor 3:7

Along the Path to Love 194

Paul: I think that God has exhibited us apostles – you are apostles

– as last of all, like men sentenced to death; because we have become a spectacle to the world, to angels and to men (1 Cor 4:9).32 To see one’s defects clearly is a grace from God. As St Paul said: I will all the more gladly boast of my weaknesses, that the power of Christ may rest upon me. For the sake of Christ, then, I am content with weaknesses, insults, hardships, persecutions, and calamities; for when I am weak, then I am strong.33 The awareness of our limitations gives us a better understanding of the need to be instruments of God. And since we want to be good instruments, the more insignificant and wretched humility makes us feel, the more our Lord will give us all we lack.34 He will make the work forge ahead even though we do nothing more than get in the way. For just as men write with a pen, our Lord writes with the leg of a table, just so it can be seen that it is He who is writing; that’s what makes it all so incredible and wonderful.35 We must be convinced that the light and warmth to attract souls comes from what we manage to reflect of the spirit God has given the Work. We have to work in spite of our defects and our mistakes, and strive continually to overcome them little by little by means of our interior struggle.36 If we realize that in some areas we have no ability at all and in others still have a lot to learn, that at times we are more of a hindrance than a help, that we do not always judge correctly, then it will be easier for us not to get in the way of God’s action and pass on the spirit of the Work without letting our defects distort it. We have to let the spirit get through without getting in the way ourselves, while

32 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 17 33 2 Cor 12:9-10 34 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 26 35 From our Father, Letter 14 September 1951, 4 36 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 18 195 God’s instruments

retaining as much of it as we need for our soul. We have to do and disappear. This is indeed difficult and yet easy at the same time! God has described us as salt, and he doesn’t like insipid instruments!37

Union with God through interior life and obedience If we struggle we will never be short of divine grace. “God so prepares and endows those whom he chooses for some particular office, that they are rendered capable of fulfilling it.”38 And since supernatural effectiveness all comes from God – what have you that you did not receive?39 – we need to be very united to him through piety and obedience, which are the normal channels through which we receive his grace. Tell me: of what use is a brush, if it won’t let the painter do his work? 40 The first thing a good instrument needs is humility. We must be humble. Humility, lowliness of heart, is the foundation and basis of all the virtues, for God opposes the proud, but gives grace to the humble (Jas 4:6). How it hurts haughty people, now and always, to be told they are instruments! But this Pauline doctrine is ancient. In the hands of God you and I are like an instrument that lets itself be used. Let us always place ourselves ... in our Lord’s hands so that he can mould us quasi lutum figuli in manu ipsius, like clay in the potter’s hands, to fashion as he pleases (Sir 33:13) ... You have to take into account all God’s blessings, and also your own desires, apostolic ambitions and difficulties. But don’t forget that among the difficulties we always include our lack of personal

37 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 38 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.27, a.4, c 39 1 Cor 4:7 40 The Way, 612

Along the Path to Love 196

holiness, yours and mine. We will be good instruments of God’s action if we improve every day.41 All this requires continual growth in humility. Without humility we can never serve effectively, because we wouldn’t feel compelled to abandon ourselves trustingly to the action of grace and to have constant recourse to God as our only source of strength; nor would we succeed in obtaining from him the favours he has reserved for us, for our own sanctification and that of our companions: quoniam excelsus Dominus, et humilia respicit: the Lord is high, he regards the lowly (Ps 138:6). Dearly beloved daughters and sons, I know you will struggle to be humble. I know that this is how you will be wonderfully effective, because you will be docile instruments in God’s hands, and thereby carry the salt and light of Christ to the whole world.42 We must have recourse to prayer, we must go to our Lord in the Eucharist and in the intimacy of our soul: we must be united to him. God introduces himself into the inmost recesses of our souls. And the reason he does so is so we have more salt, more light and more grace, and succeed in distributing his divine gifts, each of us in our own surroundings. And how do we manage to do this ... ? Through humility, piety and union. Do you remember the vine and the branches? Look how fruitful the branch is when it is united to the vine, and the ripe clusters of grapes it produces! And see how dead and withered it becomes when it is separated from the vine! Any grapes on it will be eaten by worms.43 Union with Christ is also sought through obedience. You are the

41 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 42 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 90 43 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 197 God’s instruments

body of Christ and individually members of it.44 How could we be good instruments if we were to evade what is the express will of God? Obey, as an instrument obeys in the hands of an artist, not stopping to consider the reasons for what it is doing, being sure that you will never be directed to do anything that is not good and for the glory of God.45 Those who are in charge are the ones who most need the holy virtue of obedience: first of all because they have to identify themselves with their immediate Directors, and furthermore because they have always to be guided by the spirit and norms of the Work.46 All the care and initiative we put into being good instruments are useful and worthwhile to the extent that they are subordinated to and second the directives of those in charge. We are above all ‘executors’, and so we need humility. Be ready to bow your head submissively. In these terrible moments the Church is going through we must be instruments, faithful and humble.47 Humility and obedience: two conditions which are indispensable in order to receive the doctrine, my spirit which God has given me to hand on to you.48 If we are happy to be instruments, then in spite of our failings we will always succeed in carrying out the duties we have been entrusted with. Rather, precisely because we do have defects and make mistakes, and because we are aware of our limitations and the need to have recourse to our Lord, we will be good instruments in his hands. When the poor and needy seek water, and there is none, and their tongue is parched with thirst, I the Lord will answer them, I the God of Israel

44 1 Cor 12:27 45 The Way, 617 46 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 35 47 From our Father, Meditation, 20 January 1967 48 From our Father, Meditation, 2 October 1955

Along the Path to Love 198

will not forsake them. I will open rivers on the bare heights, and fountains in the midst of the valleys; I will make the wilderness a pool of water, and the dry land springs of water. I will put in the wilderness the cedar, the acacia, the myrtle, and the olive; I will set in the desert the cypress, the plane and the pine together; that men may see and know, may consider and understand together, that the hand of the Lord has done this, the Holy One of Israel has created it.49 God will do all this, and more, in us, if we are docile and humble. Let us consider the example Our Lady offers us. She cooperated so fully with God that through her intercession she obtained for mankind the reward of eternal life.50 She was more closely united to God than any other creature, to the extent that she is uniquely co-redemptrix, and yet nevertheless her life was externally unremarkable. The few words of hers that Scripture records offer us an incomparable example of humility: my soul magnifies the Lord, and my spirit rejoices in God my Saviour, for he has regarded the low estate of his handmaiden. For behold, henceforth all generations will call me blessed; for he who is mighty has done great things for me, and holy is his name.51

49 Is 41:17-20 50 Cf Second Vatican Council, Lumen Gentium, 62 51 Lk 1:46-49

## THE NEW CREATURE {#cuaderno.8.16}

A human being is described as having personality when all the various aspects of his or her thoughts, affections and actions form a strongly harmonious whole and denote an individual who thinks, desires and acts in a clearly determined way. An essential characteristic of genuine personality is a sense of identity, of continuity, that is, a certain stability throughout the varying circumstances of life, not in the sense of always acting instinctively or predictably, like animals do, but logically and responsibly, and always capable of giving coherent reasons for their behaviour. Psychological personality consists essentially in the three qualities of unity, permanence and responsibility. It has nothing to do with being different from other people, which can easily be achieved by just being eccentric, and as a result being continually dependent on what others are doing in order to do something different and thus stand out. As sons and daughters of God in Opus Dei, we have received a divine vocation to serve God and all mankind. Our calling to holiness requires us to have a clearly defined personality. This can never be something vague or colourless, but rather has to be decidedly apostolic, because the Lord needs strong and courageous souls who refuse to come to terms with mediocrity but will be able to enter all kinds of environments with a sure step.1

1 Furrow, 416

Along the Path to Love 200

Personality: what it implies Real personality is always authentic, and is as far removed from mimicry as it is from wanting to show off. It is based on an interior principle which is conscious, free and responsible, and is expressed in actions which are positive and never just reactions to external events. If an individual acts in one particular way and not in another, it is because he or she has arrived at that conclusion after mature reflection, and not because of any purely formal and meaningless rule of behaviour. Authentic personality possesses an interior richness which is expressed in a series of essential, unchanging attitudes that have nothing to do with the stubborn rigidity of a simpleton. Personality is a consequence of the fact that we are spiritual beings, and therefore capable of directing our life – and to a certain extent finding self-fulfilment – in terms of goals which we choose and propose to achieve. Therefore in essence it does not depend upon temperament, heredity or environment, although all these factors do have a part to play in its development. The prime source of personality is knowledge. When one has a sure and universal rule of behaviour or outlook on life it gives unity to one’s actions and serves as a solid principle that can be applied in all circumstances of life. This is not possible without a constant, humble and painstaking application to the task of formation. That is why our Father pointed out that a terrible person is one who is ignorant but at the same time works tirelessly. Take care that even when you are old and decrepit, you keep on wanting to be better trained.2 On the other hand, since man possesses various appetites and tendencies and is subject to constantly changing attractions, each act of

2 Furrow, 538 201 The new creature

the will represents an act of mastery and coordination which expresses and at the same time forms the personality. The more powerful one’s will is, and the more it succeeds in imposing itself on the network of the individual’s tendencies, then the more perfect and effective is the unity of the personality. That is why inconstancy, fickleness, and illogical or capricious behaviour, are all signs of a lack of personality. What sort of Christian perfection do you expect to achieve if you are only following your whims and doing ‘what you like’? All your defects, unless you fight against them, will produce bad works as a natural consequence. And your will, untempered by a persevering fight, will be of no use to you when a difficult occasion arises.3 The combination of a sure and universal outlook on life with a will that is unwavering in its love, fixed on its ultimate end and firm in its decision, is a source of true effectiveness; it gives rise to behaviour which is consistent, freely assumed and clearly understood, and is a sign of unity of life and true character. In so far as one becomes accustomed to acting like this, it becomes progressively more pronounced and reliable, and more pleasant and effortless to follow, because character is a sort of victory over the initial anarchy left in us by original sin.

Dying to self in order to live in Christ As Christians we have been raised to the supernatural order and have become children of God by grace, and so our personality is not based on nature alone. Our natural character, which is the product of a series of natural virtues, acts as a preparation or basis for the full development of our personality as children of God, which has to be human and at the same time supernatural. A calm and balanced character, an inflexible will, deep faith and ardent piety: these are the

3 Furrow, 776

Along the Path to Love 202

indispensable characteristics of a son of God.4 Man’s natural and supernatural personalities are not as it were two distinct principles or opposing poles. One, the natural, has to be elevated and transformed to the other, but in the process nothing of what is perfect or positive is lost, because it is all contained in a superior fashion in the supernatural personality. The two aspects are not like two opposing forces that are reconciled only with difficulty; rather, one is present in the other as the less perfect is contained in the more perfect, something similar to what happens to man’s spiritual soul which, being one principle, at the same time possesses the perfections of his animal and vegetative natures. The road we Christians have to travel in order to attain, with the help of grace, this new supernatural personality, has two aspects. One consists in imitating Christ and reproducing in ourselves the features of his life: thinking like he thinks, loving like he loves, acting always like he would. The second consists in emptying ourselves, in dying to self, in order to let Jesus work in us. He who finds his life will lose it, and he who loses his life for my sake will find it.5 The loss is a necessary pre-condition for the gain. When the Word of God assumed human nature, he did so without any limitation, sanctifying it substantially; that is, he sanctified, we might say, the entire human nature common to all mankind. But it was still necessary to redeem and sanctify the individual members of the human race. This is done principally by means of the sacraments, which are channels whereby the grace Jesus won for us on the Cross is conveyed to each human being. Through Baptism we die to sin and are reborn in Christ; it is as if our sinful life were drowned in the baptismal water so that we might be reborn to a new life. Through Confirmation we reach

4 Furrow, 417 5 Mt 10:39 203 The new creature

maturity in this new life. Through the Eucharist, which is the noblest of the sacraments and their consummation, we are united to the very source of supernatural life, Christ himself, who makes us one with him. In this way Christ becomes the complete focus of our thoughts and actions. Our life really becomes his, because we are members of his Mystical Body and it is he who sustains our supernatural life. Like the allegory of the vine and the branches, we depend on him and exist in him; and, as he himself has told us, he is the source of our supernatural acts: apart from me you can do nothing.6 There is no other way open to us of participating in the divine life except by living in Christ, from whom we receive our sharing in divine filiation. And this life in Christ is what gives unity of life to the Christian, and makes him steadfast and consistent in the face of events so he can bend them to his will. Our entire supernatural life is Christ’s life in us: our intellect is his, because it is fully informed by faith; our will is his, because we want what he wants; and our acts are his, because we do what pleases him. To be a saint is to be another Christ, ipse Christus, Christ himself. Our personality as children of God depends upon really becoming saints, dying to ourselves in order to live in Christ. Like St Paul, each of us has to be able to say: to me to live is Christ.7 And also: I have been crucified with Christ; it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me; and the life I now live in the flesh I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me.8

Letting Christ live in us It is worth noting that, unless the effort to improve one’s personality is motivated by the desire to be identified with Christ, it usually ends

6 Jn 15:5 7 Phil 1:21 8 Gal 2:20

Along the Path to Love 204

up as a form of self-affirmation and implies a certain degree of selfsufficiency and detachment from others. My son, where do men find in you the Christ they are looking for? In your pride? In your desire to impose yourself on others? In those little character defects which you don’t wish to overcome? In your stubbornness? ... Is Christ to be found there? No, he is not! You need to have your own personality, agreed. But you should try to make it conform exactly to Christ’s.9 In the Gospel we often contemplate the divine personality of Jesus. We see him repeatedly in union with his Father, an uninterrupted union that for him is food and life, so close and intimate that he can tell the Apostles: I and the Father are one.10 A union of thought: my teaching is not mine, but his who sent me.11 A union of will: I seek not my own will but the will of him who sent me.12 A union of action: I always do what is pleasing to him.13 Jesus identifies himself with his Father to such an extent that it is God who acts through his human faculties. Through this identification with his Father’s will Jesus’ life becomes a ceaseless outpouring of love; his only concern is the salvation of mankind. The thought of the mission he received from his Father – the redemption – occupies him constantly. I have a baptism to be baptized with; and how I am constrained until it is accomplished!14 It is his immense love for us that leads him to the Cross and causes him to give himself and humble himself as he did. All that concerns him is to work, to pray, to intercede for us, and to teach us by word and example: the

9 The Forge, 468 10 Jn 10:30 11 Jn 7:16 12 Jn 5:30 13 Jn 8:29 14 Lk 12:50 205 The new creature

true light that enlightens every man was coming into the world.15 He seeks to attract all with his manner, with his sympathy, with his love. And the people sought him and came to him, and would have kept him from leaving them,16 until he wore himself out travelling through towns and villages, healing every disease and every infirmity,17 always forgiving, because it is he alone who takes away the sin of the world.18 The example Jesus gives us is one of renunciation, of dedication to his Father and to all mankind, and of forever seeking the glory of God and the salvation of souls. To become like Jesus, to become other Christs, we have to contemplate him: see how he thinks, how he desires and how he acts, so as to do the same ourselves. But we have to imitate him above all in his renunciation, and lose ourselves in order to gain possession of him. But as well as imitating Christ, we also have to live in him, by him and through him, to such an extent that he can be said to take the place of our ego and thereby make us all just one thing, his Mystical Body. Then each of us can say with St Paul, to whom God revealed this mystery at the start of his vocation, it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me.19 “Because Paul’s soul was constituted between God and the body. Paul’s body was certainly vivified and moved by his soul, but his soul by Christ.”20 Then may there be fulfilled the wish Jesus expressed in his prayer to his heavenly Father: I in them and you in me.21 This is the way: identify yourself with that Christ who is Life. Be ipse Christus.22

15 Jn 1:9 16 Lk 4:42 17 Mt 9:35 18 Jn 1:29 19 Gal 2:20 20 St Thomas, Super epistolam S. Pauli ad Galatas, 2, 20 21 Jn 17:23 22 From our Father, Meditation, 12 April 1954

Along the Path to Love 206

Jesus dwelling in us, in the inmost and deepest recesses of our being; then we will no longer say: I think, I love, I act; but: Jesus thinks, loves and acts in me. Thus, remaining in Jesus and Jesus in us, we shall live his life. We shall dwell with him, in tender, faithful, continuous conversation, our minds totally absorbed in contemplation of our heavenly Father. We shall completely identify our will, which will be Christ’s, with that of the Father: Lo, I have come to do your will.23 And we shall act with his power: Truly, truly, I say to you, he who believes in me will also do the works that I do.24

Apostolic formation and behaviour An apostle must not remain at the level of the mediocre. God calls him to be fully human in his actions, and at the same time to reflect the freshness of eternal things. That is why the apostle has to be a soul who has undergone a long, patient and heroic process of transformation.25 By means of constant formation we manage to improve our way of being and are able to begin working for Christ, in the lowest place in his army of apostles.26 Identification with Jesus leads directly to apostolate, as he himself expresses it so clearly in his last fervent prayer to his Father: that they may all be one; even as thou, Father, art in me and I in thee, that they also may be in us, so that the world may believe that thou hast sent me.27 Our union with our Lord is apostolic of its very nature, because it leads us to participate in his mission: as thou didst send me into the world, so I have sent them into the world.28 This mission determines the

23 Heb 10:9 24 Jn 14:12 25 Furrow, 419 26 The Way, 602 27 Jn 17:21 28 Jn 17:18 207 The new creature

entire Christian personality and makes it essentially apostolic. You know that our vocation – Christ’s call – brings us to identify ourselves with Him, and He came into the world ut omnes homines salvi fiant (1 Tim 2:4), to redeem all mankind. There isn’t a single soul that doesn’t interest Him. Every one of them has cost Him all the price of His Blood.29 This supernatural personality, which is a consequence of Christ living in the Christian, also gives the three elements of the natural personality a deeply apostolic flavour: in assessing situations one always does so in terms of their apostolic potential; the will is fortified with a tenacious and constant zeal for souls; and one’s way of acting involves a permanently apostolic approach to all situations. The first requirement, then, is an apostolic way of seeing people and situations, so that in all circumstances we are constantly seeking ways of bringing souls to God. We cannot be part-time apostles. Have you noticed how people generally, you and I included, have a sort of innate tendency to label things according to our particular background? When a doctor is going along the street and meets someone, without realizing it he says to himself: ‘that person has liver trouble’. And if instead of a doctor he’s a tailor, he says: ‘what awful clothes’, or maybe ‘what a well-dressed individual!’ And if he’s a shoemaker he notices the person’s footwear, and so on. You and I are God’s children, dedicated for love of him to serving our fellow men, so whenever we meet people we have to see them as souls, and think to ourselves: here comes a soul whom I have to help, whom I have to understand, with whom I have to get along, whom I have to save.30 A strong will, unshakeable in its resolve, gives rise to an ardent love

29 From our Father, Meditation, 16 April 1954 30 From our Father, Meditation A suit made to measure, 25 February 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 194

Along the Path to Love 208

for all souls without distinction. There isn’t a single soul we do not love, and try to help and understand.31 It is a zealous, burning love, a truly divine infatuation. We are interested in all souls.32 Through this outlook and will we possess a constant guiding principle and driving force that links all aspects of our behaviour and gives our life a clearly defined and permanent character, regardless of difficulties. No one who passes by our side can fail to experience the influence of our apostolate. Our personality thus gives rise spontaneously and necessarily to apostolic deeds: prayer and mortification for souls, readiness for sacrifice, doctrine, word and example, understanding, and affection that attracts all with a smile. Wherever the apostle goes there is salt and light, and these words of our Father come true: your relatives, colleagues and friends have noticed the change, and realised that it is not a temporary phase, but that you are no longer the same. Don’t worry, carry on. Vivit vero in me Christus – it is now Christ that lives in me – that’s what is happening.33 Just like St Paul, we should able to say I will most gladly spend and be spent for your souls;34 we must give our life and let it be poured out without seeking anything for ourselves. When we succeed in removing from our character everything that gets in the way, those around us at once behold the figure of Christ in us, and we thus bring to life these words of our Founder: the power to work miracles! How many poor dead, and even rotten, souls – quatriduanus est enim (Jn 11:39) – you will raise! And all those helpless young people to whom you will say: adolescens, tibi dico, surge: young man, I say to you, arise (Lk 7:14). Many of them you will see respond, because they are sincere,

31 From our Father, Crónica, March 1961, p.15 32 Ibid. 33 Furrow, 424 34 2 Cor 12:15 209 The new creature

perhaps better than yourselves. Our Lord passed by and they cried out to him without seeing him; Christ passed by, in you, and they sensed it, they implored his aid, and their eyes were opened to behold God’s wonders.35 When we truly renounce self, then Christ is formed in us; in a certain sense our human nature belongs to the person of Christ, and we become his instruments, just like the Humanity which the Word of God assumed, in Our Lady’s virginal womb. As we are other Christs, Christ himself, we have to be born of the Virgin Mary, who is the Mother of Jesus and our Mother too. The more we belong to Jesus, the more we are her children also; and the stronger our filiation to Mary, the more we are identified with Christ, until we become one with him. “Do you realize, brethren, do you appreciate, the full extent of what God has done for us? It is enough to fill you with admiration and joy. We have become Christ himself.”36

35 From our Father, Meditation, 19 June 1955 36 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus, 21, 18

## PRUDENCE OF THE SPIRIT

Our Lord once told his Apostles a parable to illustrate what they had to do to gain the kingdom of heaven: the parable of the faithful and wise steward whose master set him over his household.1 On another occasion he spoke to them about a nobleman who, on going to distant lands, gave each of his servants some talents to trade with until his return.2 And yet again, when speaking to them about responsibility, he lamented the fact that the sons of this world are wiser in their own generation than the sons of light.3 Jesus wants us to make use of all the intelligence and ingenuity at our disposal in the service of the divine Will. He commands us to love God with all our heart and with all our soul and with all our mind.4 It would be a very poor sign of love if we failed to place all our resources at his service, because love always finds a way: it reasons and searches until it finds the way to serve the loved one. “A person may be virtuous, without reasoning fully about all things, as long as he reasons well about those things which have to be done virtuously. In this sense every virtuous person reasons fully. Hence, those who seem to be

1 Cf Lk 12:42 2 Cf Mt 25:14-30 3 Cf Lk 16:8 4 Cf Mt 22:37 211 Prudence of the spirit

simple, through lack of worldly shrewdness, may possibly be prudent, in accordance with what we read in St Matthew: ‘be wise as serpents and simple as doves’ (Mt 10:16).”5

Acting prudently It is not enough to have a vague desire to please God in everything we do. Good will alone is not enough. Our Founder often liked to quote us those words of Scripture, discite benefacere: learn to do good,6 and he used to remark: it’s not enough just to want to be a doctor, you have to study medicine; it’s not enough just to want to be an engineer, you have to study in earnest.7 The same holds true for all the virtues we practise in the Work: it’s not enough just to want to acquire them, we have to actually learn how to do so. The prudence we are asked to have is not the prudence of the flesh, with its overt suspicion, excessive caution, and hidden motivations. The prudence of the flesh is death, but

## The prudence of the spirit {#cuaderno.8.17}

, life and peace.8 We have to acquire the habit of being supernaturally perceptive

– with the light of grace and the help of reason – to find the best way of serving God and souls in every situation. Happy is the man who finds wisdom, and the man who gets understanding, for the gain from it is better than the gain from silver and its profit better than gold. She is more precious than jewels.9 Prudence is the virtue which, in every concrete situation, helps us “to distinguish between what helps us to reach God and what could separate us from him.”10 It enables us to find the most suitable way to accomplish the good we desire.

5 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.58, a.4 ad 2 6 Is 1:17 7 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 20 8 Rom 8:6 9 Prov 3:14-15 10 St Augustine, De moribus Ecclesiae Catholicae, 1, 15, 25

Along the Path to Love 212

To be supernaturally prudent, therefore, we first of all have to ask God for it, because every good endowment and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights.11 Next, we need to know the theological principles – the truths of faith and morals. But even that is not enough. In addition to the universal and permanent truths of faith, we also need to have a good grasp of all the facts and circumstances of our everyday situation and the vagaries of time and place. Only then are we in a position to fulfil what God wants of us here and now, in each unrepeatable moment of life. “Man’s acts are always individual and specific. The prudent person, therefore, needs to know both the universal principles of reason and also the specific circumstances of his action.”12 Putting something into practice involves a process that ranges from the most remote and general principles to the most down-to-earth expertise. Thus any prudent decision “has three parts. The first is to seek advice. To this belongs information, for seeking advice implies asking questions (...) The second is to judge the established facts, and this terminates the speculative part of the process. But man’s practical reason, which is directed to accomplishing the action, proceeds finally to a third step which is to command, that is, to apply this advice and these judgments to the concrete situation.”13 A person must take all three of these steps, together with the necessary reflection, if the final decision is to be prudent, correct, and effective. In a word, in order to be prudent, we have to combine all these virtues. We have to be people who can think for themselves and who don’t accept unquestioningly the cliches and catchphrases which are fashionable at the moment. Our formation teaches us to act as a sieve,

11 Jas 1:17 12 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.47, a.3 c 13 Ibid., a.8 213 Prudence of the spirit

which makes use of what is valuable and leaves the rest behind.14

Prudent action: what it entails Acting prudently calls for knowledge of many things and requires a whole series of virtues,15 which are like different aspects or parts of the virtue itself, as they combine the requisites necessary to make that action prudent. Prudence is directed towards future actions. This looking forward cannot be achieved without first glancing at the past, which acts as a reference point for what we want to do now. And so we need experience, the knowledge of what happens in the majority of cases. This generalized knowledge, though, is still too imprecise, and so we need to sift out the data relevant to the particular situation; this calls for special keenness of mind. There are two ways of acquiring this analytical capacity – from ourselves or from someone else. Prudence needs to be accompanied, therefore, by two other virtues: firstly, a certain mental agility in reasoning; and secondly, docility, the virtue which enables us to learn from others by seeking their advice. However, the data acquired from our own or another’s experience would be useless unless we manage to correlate it. This requires being able to think rationally and judge clearly. Since prudence is geared towards action, it also presupposes the ability to see in advance the steps that need to be taken. Moreover, we also need to be watchful in case circumstances should oblige us to follow a particular course of action and to exclude others. Finally, we need to foresee possible emergencies, because it is not enough just to provide adequate resources: we must also be ready to grapple with difficulties as they arise.

14 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 25 15 Cf St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.49

Along the Path to Love 214

Since I’m talking about prudence, our Father writes, I feel I should underline the fact that prudence is not cowardice or inertia, it is not inactivity. Man’s inclination to the law of minimum effort – to laziness – has tended, in everyday language, to identify prudence and passivity. For us that sort of attitude would be a symptom of the prudence of the flesh which is death: prudentia carnis mors est; whereas the prudence of the spirit is life and peace: prudentia autem spiritus vita et pax (Rom 8:6). The cardinal virtue of prudence not only signifies the ability to judge shrewdly about what has to be done, but has as its principal act the actus imperandi whereby all one’s powers are mobilised decisively in the service of what is clearly seen to be God’s will. Therefore, once a person has considered things dispassionately in God’s presence, it is – and always will be – sinful to show any apathy or lack of diligence in carrying them out. Among the things that have nothing to do with real prudence one could mention, for example: being afraid to change one’s mind; being reluctant to begin something new; being defensive towards our brothers’ or sisters’ good ideas; a constant attitude of indecisiveness and holding back, which acts as a brake on all those holy promptings that need an outlet and which erects barriers to the impetuousness of faith.16

Seeking advice Among all the elements of prudent action, there is one which is particularly important because it is located at the very beginning of the decision-making process and its absence would invalidate the final outcome from the start; that element is a readiness to seek advice. Do nothing without deliberation, we read in the Book of Sirach, and when

16 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 50-51 215 Prudence of the spirit

you have acted, do not regret it;17 for reason is the beginning of every work, and counsel precedes every undertaking.18 Christ himself gives us an example of this virtue when, after he was lost, his parents found him in the temple, sitting among the teachers, listening to them and asking them questions.19 And St Gregory comments: “It needs to be thought about very carefully: when Jesus was found among the doctors he was not teaching them, but rather asking them questions (...) That child, through whose divine power the doctors received the voice of knowledge, wanted to learn by asking questions.”20 And the Gospel narrative continues: and all who heard him were amazed at his understanding and his answers.21 Though being perfect Man, our Lord Jesus Christ willed to be subject to this limitation of our human nature. He teaches us to ask questions, because man cannot learn everything singlehanded. This is especially true in matters of prudence. “For anything to be known with fair certainty about life’s eventualities, many conditions and circumstances have to be taken into account. This is not easily done by one person alone, but may be done with greater assurance by several persons together, because what escapes the notice of one may occur more directly to another.”22 We can reach the same conclusion about the need to turn to others if we consider how many courses of action are open to choice. “In human concerns the means to an end are far from being fixed; there is a manifold variety according to the variety of persons and matters.”23

17 Sir 32:19 18 Sir 37:16 19 Lk 2:46 20 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 3, 15 21 Lk 2:47 22 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.47, a.15 c 23 Ibid.

Along the Path to Love 216

Summarizing all this, our Father wrote: Don’t be too quick to trust your own judgment. Just as precious metal is put to the test – it needs a touchstone – so too do we have to see whether our judgment, both in human and in supernatural matters, is pure gold or not, by taking into account the opinion of others, especially those who have the grace of state to help us.24

Prudence in commanding and in obeying This manner of acting – genuine prudence – is seen in practice in how the Work is directed. All our houses and Centres, and all our activities, are supposed to be run collegially, for neither you nor I can trust our own judgment exclusively. And since this is so thanks to a very direct and special grace of God, it would be very serious indeed to disregard it.25 Even in unimportant decisions it is essential that everybody who has the right to intervene should in fact do so, especially when dealing with things in writing, even if it’s the wording of a telegram, even if it is only something small. Doing things this way gives one great peace of mind!26 It sometimes happens that all a person has to do is to carry out the allotted task exactly as indicated. But even in this case the virtue of prudence is still needed, because even if “the subject, insofar as he is the executor, does not need the prudence to govern, insofar as he is a rational being he participates to some degree in governing through the use of his free will, and to this extent he needs to be prudent.”27 This prudence is especially necessary in the Work, where we are supposed

24 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 50 25 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 28 26 Ibid., note 39 27 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.47, a.12 c 217 Prudence of the spirit

to obey like people who have a mind and a will of their own: that is, responsibly.28 And so, “when we find that some task is not easy to do or may even be inadvisable, we have to outline our difficulties or perplexity humbly and straightforwardly to the Director who asked us to do it. And after that we do whatever we’re told, the way we’re told.”29 On the other hand, we are seldom asked to do something that has been spelled out to the last detail. Normally we have considerable personal discretion about how best to carry out our apostolic tasks. You need to have lots of initiative, our Father tells us. Make suggestions. Make all the suggestions you think are appropriate and useful for helping the apostolate and the task you have in hand.30 Most of the time we are not given any specific orders, but rather just some general guidelines to keep in mind should the occasion arise to apply them. It is left completely up to us to guide and direct the task as we see fit in each case, and to take whatever measures we consider most suitable to determine the final outcome; this, “being closest to the goal of the practical reason, is the principal act of the virtue of prudence.”31 There is also the whole field of economic, social and political questions about which the Work will never give us any precept or ruling whatsoever. On you, our Father says, with your fellow citizens, falls the lot of courageously running the risk of finding honourable and Christian answers – according to your conscience, remembering that there isn’t just one solution – to the temporal issues you meet on your way. You’ll wait in vain for the Work to give them to you ready made: such a thing hasn’t ever happened, doesn’t happen nor shall it ever

28 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 91 29 Don Alvaro, note 120 to the Instruction, 31 May 1936 30 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 21 31 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.47, a.8 c Along the Path to Love 218

happen, because it would be contrary to our nature.32 In these situations it is entirely up to us to carry out the three acts of the virtue of prudence: to seek advice, to weigh up the information received, and to decide what has to be done. Conscious of our absolute freedom in temporal matters, we have to take the steps which allow us to exercise a supernatural prudence there too, because all Christians are obliged “to have the Gospel spirit permeate and improve the temporal order, going about it in a way that bears clear witness to Christ and helps forward the salvation of men.”33 Consequently, when in professional life one encounters ethical issues which are difficult or complex, or in which personal involvement can affect one’s objectivity, all Christians have the duty, arising from the obligation to act always with a right intention, to seek advice on what course of action to follow. This advice should be sought, naturally, from people who have a good grasp of ethical principles and who are well acquainted with the particular problems involved, so they are in a position to apply the principles of moral theology to the case in question. At times one may have to look for advice from someone who is an expert in the field and who has sound Christian principles. However, the advice one gets refers exclusively to evaluating the moral issues involved so as to assess the problem correctly; afterwards the individual concerned has to act in consequence, freely and on his or her own responsibility. The advice one gets, therefore, cannot be seen in any way as an interference in matters of opinion. In consultations of this sort, naturally, one is strictly obliged to adhere to the code of behaviour governing professional confidentiality.

32 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959, 36 33 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 2 219 Prudence of the spirit

An effective help God our Lord provides each of us with a particularly effective means to guide our steps – the fraternal talk. In the Confidence our Lord gives us light to know – to learn – what we have to do to act in a truly Christian manner in every circumstance.34 This is what Holy Scripture has to say on the matter: Stay constantly with a godly man whom you know to be a keeper of the commandments, whose soul is in accordance with your soul, and who will sorrow with you if you fail. And establish the counsel of your own heart, for no one is more faithful to you than it is. For a holy man’s soul sometimes discovers true things more than seven watchmen sitting high on a watchtower. And besides all this pray to the Most High that he may direct your way in truth.35 In the Church’s Tradition it has always been considered that “the more disguised is the leprosy of our own will and judgment, the more pernicious it is. What I refer to as our own will,” says St Bernard, “is that which does not conform to the will of God (...) , that is, when we act, not for God’s glory and for man’s good (...), but rather to satisfy the desires of our own heart (...) But even worse and more pernicious is the leprosy of one’s own counsel, because it is more hidden. And the more ingrained it is, the healthier it seems to the one who suffers it. This is the leprosy of those who are full of zeal for God, not in accordance with wisdom but in accordance with their own mistaken judgment, so obstinate that they do not want advice (...) Such men are enemies of unity. They do not live in harmony and are lacking in charity; they are puffed up with vanity. They are enamoured of themselves, considering themselves great in their own eyes; they ignore the justice of God and want to impose their own.”36

34 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 20 35 Sir 37:12-15 36 St Bernard, Easter Sermons, 3, 3-4

Along the Path to Love 220

Such is the consequence of being satisfied with one’s own opinion and of despising the judgment of others. Whereas if one has a friend to advise and guide, it facilitates the growth of one’s personality, the effective overcoming of one’s shortcomings, and the full development of one’s talents. Indeed, the more developed is a person’s discernment, personality, initiative and maturity of judgment, all the more keenly does he or she feel the need to seek advice. And St Thomas teaches that “it is a sign of excellence to be ready to count on the help of others.”37 The opposite tendency, as well as being a symptom of lack of humility, reflects a childish attitude that tries to apply simplistic solutions to very complex problems. For that reason “in the saints we find a prudence that is not so independent as to become pride: pride often misinterprets the words under the pretext of ‘holy freedom’. Nor does their humility degenerate into timidity, disguising as humility its own indecision and inhibiting the mind from speaking truthfully.”38 We always have to balance these two extremes: on the one hand, to seek advice and to listen to the opinion of others; and on the other, to be able to take decisions and embark on a course of action after having considered matters carefully, and being ready to accept responsibly all the consequences of our actions. We need to acquire the habit of consulting. Trust your brothers and sisters, our Founder preached. Also trust in the talents God has given them, and their love for God and for the Work. And mistrust your own judgment just a little: it will help you always to take other people’s opinions into account, and will make you more effective.39 If we want the advice we get to be satisfactory, we should present the query in the manner our Father describes: mention it in time,

37 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.129, a.9 ad 3 38 St Gregory the Great, In Ezechielem homiliae, 1, 7, 2 39 Alone with God, 156 221 Prudence of the spirit

without being insistent or impatient for an answer; distinguish what is important from what is not; be objective; study the matter in depth beforehand; and propose possible solutions ourselves if we can, but not so categorically as to determine the outcome in advance.

Personal responsibility A logical consequence of our freedom is that we are responsible for the consequences of our decisions. Responsibility has to be commensurate with freedom,40 our Father always taught. The advice we get can never be used as an excuse for not being personally accountable. We are as much in favour of freedom as anybody, always provided that we are talking about a responsible freedom that is prepared to answer for its actions. Nowadays one hears a lot of talk about freedom – in art, in politics, and everywhere – but little about accepting responsibility for one’s own actions; instead, one tends to hear all kinds of excuses.41 If a person asks for advice, it’s because he wants to get advice; and if he then follows it, he does so completely freely. The fact that the advice of a third party can help us to put into practice the acts of the virtue of prudence, doesn’t take from our own part in it in any way. In the conduct of human affairs, at times some virtues come into play, at other times others; but prudence is always necessary, because the virtues are so inter-related that it is not possible to regulate them if one is not prudent; nor is it possible to be truly prudent if one is not charitable, temperate, obedient or just.42 One specific way of progressing in all the virtues and improving one’s apostolic performance is to foster the desire to learn by consulting as often as necessary, for in “matters of prudence man needs to

40 From our Father, Crónica, August 1956, p.7 41 From our Father, Crónica, 1967, p.889 42 Cf St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.65, aa.1 & 2

Along the Path to Love 222

## rn from others”:43 he is never self-sufficient. The practice of virtue is something we have to learn, and not just theoretically but in the concrete circumstances of life. We need to be learning all the time if we are to acquire the maturity and equilibrium that make for sound judgment and wise decisions. You have to behave like adults,44 our Father wrote to all his daughters and sons. This practical virtue, which comes with age, will be ours if we learn to foster it by asking for advice at the various stages of life. “Age is not in years,” says St Augustine, “but in prudence and wisdom.”45

43 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.49, a.3 c 44 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 20 45 St Augustine, De moribus Ecclesiae Catholicae, 1, 10, 17

## FIDELITY IN LOVE {#cuaderno.8.18}

Thus says the Lord, he who created you ... ‘Fear not, for I have redeemed you; I have called you by name, you are mine’.1 Each of us has heard these words of the book of Isaiah echo in our soul when God called us to Opus Dei. Without taking us from our place in the world or from our surroundings – every one should remain in the state in which 2 – the divine call galvanized our whole being. Our Lord he was called has given himself completely to us prior to any merit on our part, and in return he asks us to give ourselves to him just as fully, for life, each according to his or her circumstances. In the case of Numeraries and Associates, one aspect of this selfgiving is the vocation to apostolic celibacy for the sake of the kingdom of heaven,3 with the precise aim of dedicating all their energies to the service of God and of souls with an undivided heart,4 that is, not through the medium of the love of another human being. In an analogous way Supernumeraries receive, through their vocation to the Work, which is the same for all members, the grace and strength to sanctify themselves in the exercise of their daily work and,

1 Is 43:1 2 1 Cor 7:20 3 Mt 19:12 4 Cf 1 Cor 7:32-35

Along the Path to Love 224

if they are married, in the fulfilment of their marriage duties.

Keeping our first love intact The road we have taken leads eventually to holiness. All life long we have to safeguard the commitment we made at the beginning of our vocation, and make it grow continually. We still retain power over what we have given our Lord, but we are now simply its custodians, because it has become divine property and no longer belongs to us. As simple guardians, God’s treasurers, we should pledge ourselves with a great sense of fidelity to safeguard the property which Christ himself has won from us. It is true that fidelity begets fidelity: the first decision helps establish a direction, and each subsequent victory further strengthens and tempers one’s weapons. Sometimes, though, progress is slow, and the struggle itself varies too: both the enemies without and the soul’s interior dispositions are constantly changing. Our nature requires continual acts of the will in order to reaffirm, in the constantly changing circumstances of life, the response we one day gave to God: ecce ego quia vocasti me!,5 here I am, for you called me. It could perhaps happen, as our Father pointed out, that in a moment of discouragement or tiredness, or after some big disappointment, or because of a seemingly insurmountable state of depression, someone might feel the attraction, not of sin, but of those noble earthly things which we have left behind for love of Jesus Christ, though without having lost the inclination towards them. Because we had that tendency, our self-giving was truly generous and detached. And because we preserve that self-giving, our fidelity is a continual offering: a love, an unselfishness, a magnanimity which lasts and

5 1 Sam 3:8 225 Fidelity in love

which is not just the result of inertia. St Thomas says: eiusdem est autem aliquid constituere, et constitutum conservare (S. Th., II- II, q.79, a.1 c). What gave rise to your self-giving in the first place, my son, also has to maintain it.6 Once we have committed ourselves we can no longer reformulate the question in the same way as we did at the beginning, because what we could give then, we gave. Now we are talking about looking after something that belongs to someone else, and other factors come into play: loyalty, fidelity, uprightness, family responsibilities, and similar virtues. And that applies just as much to those whose vocation is to apostolic celibacy as to those who are called to marriage.

The crisis of the forties At the onset of middle age, which is a time when attitudes to life tend to get fixed definitively, some people may go through a sort of psychological crisis. It can then happen that some people, not all, nor even the majority, tend to indulge in what I like to call mystical wishful thinking: if only I’d become a doctor instead of a lawyer, if only I hadn’t got married, if only ... anything at all, as long as it’s different from the actual situation. Along with that one tends to observe a certain change in character, or perhaps an excessive concern for one’s health, or the appearance of imaginary illnesses, or a certain loss of interest in one’s work. At the bottom of all this, and perhaps its most characteristic feature, is an interior attitude of striking a bargain with life. Humanly speaking, until then the person’s intellectual and physical life had been growing to maturity. From now on the human descent begins, and one has the impression that this bargain, which the

6 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 12

Along the Path to Love 226

prudence of the flesh invites us to make, has a certain definitive and irreversible character about it.7 In these circumstances, which could happen to anybody, if the person concerned were not forewarned they could start questioning their vocation unnecessarily. Many years ago our Father warned us about this psychological conflict, giving us the advice he had received from a friend of his when he was still a young priest: don’t forget that when people get to forty, married people want to be single, monks want to be curates, doctors lawyers, lawyers engineers and so on. It’s like a sort of spiritual earthquake. In fact things don’t happen quite like that religious said, or at least not as a general rule. But I want you to be aware of the possibility and be ready for it, although very few people may actually experience it. If any of your brothers or sisters are afflicted by it, you have to help them renew and strengthen their piety, treat them very affectionately, and give them something agreeable to do. It doesn’t necessarily happen at forty – it could equally be at forty five. Then we will have to ensure that they take things easy for a while; and we will do this not just to a few but in every case. Being children before God doesn’t mean that we have to become childish. We joined the Work when we were old enough to know that we have feet of clay and that we are made of flesh and bone. It would be ridiculous if this only dawned on us as we were getting on in years, like a few months old baby suddenly discovering to its surprise its own hands and feet. We have come to serve God, conscious of our insignificance and our weakness, but if we have given ourselves to him, his Love will not allow us to be unfaithful.8 And our Father adds: for a soul that has given itself completely, the sexual instinct should not be something

7 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 37 8 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 22-23 227 Fidelity in love

it discovers only in middle age. It is a perfectly normal instinct, but it is equally normal to keep it under control. To say ‘No’ requires a wellbalanced personality, a strong will, and the assistance of grace. Faithful in youth, my children, and also in maturity.9 Referring more specifically to his Numerary and Associate sons and daughters, our Founder also wrote: to be disloyal for the sake of a merely earthly love is the beginning of a very bitter life, full of sadness, shame and suffering. My children, strengthen your resolve never to sell your birthright, nor to exchange it with the passage of the years for a plate of lentils. It would be a great shame to throw away like that so many years of sacrificed love. Say I have sworn an oath and confirmed it, to obey your righteous ordinances (Ps 119:106).10 It is true that with the passage of time the effort to be chaste acquires a somewhat different emphasis than it had when one was younger. As one grows older the force of passion becomes less vehement and more deliberate, but also less idealistic and more pragmatic. And so if someone had imagined that apostolic celibacy meant renouncing only the satisfaction of an instinct – perhaps hitherto more easily overcome with the strong idealism of youth – then, when age and circumstances begin to highlight other aspects, they could experience a rude awakening. It can also happen in the lives of people irrevocably committed to marriage that the dreams and idealism of their early years slowly give way to new considerations which cause them to re-examine their freely assumed obligations. Both married and celibate persons, however, remain bound by the ties of a self-sacrificing fidelity whose roots are firmly anchored in the love of God and far removed from the shifting sands of sensuality.

9 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 72 10 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 23

Desires of the flesh

flesh and blood and not a pure spirit, is perfectly normal, and so too is the need to keep it under control. It is not a question of killing or annihilating it, but of overcoming it, keeping it subject to the judgment of reason and freely choosing to subdue its inclinations for the glory of God.

thesaurum istum in vasis fictilibus: we have this treasure in earthen vessels (2 Cor 4:7). I’m not telling you this for now, but just in case

moment I ask you, as from this moment, for a faithfulness which is seen in the way you make good use of time and overcome pride, in

safeguarding and demonstrating the quality of your self-giving.11

even years, a person experiences no particular difficulty in this area. Then if all of a sudden it becomes more prominent and requires a

mean anything particular, and on no account could it justify losing objectivity and confidence in grace. Before, it called for less effort or struggle; and now it requires more. Before, one offered something to God quite easily, and now one has to continue offering it to him laboriously, heroically if necessary and always with humility.

11 Ibid., 45

Along the Path to Love The presence of the sexual instinct in man, who is a creature of

Referring to the gift of celibacy, our Father wrote: if you ever feel that this grace which God has given us is in danger, don’t be surprised, because, as I already told you, we are made of clay: habemus autem at some stage in the future you feel your heart begin to waver. For that your decision to obey unassumingly, and in your effort to control your imagination. All these little details are very effective means of It may happen, and very often does, that for a long time, maybe greater effort, it shouldn’t be a cause for surprise, because it doesn’t

If the interior struggle ever becomes harder, it’s a good opportunity 229 Fidelity in love

to show that our Love is true. Once a soul has begun to savour its selfgiving to some degree, it would be a terrible swindle to succumb to defeat. Don’t forget that cry of St Paul’s: quis me liberabit de corpore mortis huius? (Rom 7:24). Who will deliver me from this body of death? And listen in your heart to the divine answer: sufficit tibi gratia mea (2 Cor 12:9). My grace is sufficient for you! The love which, with God’s grace, we have given him wholeheartedly in youth, is not something we can take back with the passage of time. Fidelity is the perfection of love: in the life of a soul dedicated to God one always finds, lurking behind any form of disenchantment, a source of corruption and impurity. Fidelity, when it is whole and entire, is always joyful and unconditional.12 But we have to be very sincere, first of all with ourselves. We have come to the Work to be saints. And we shouldn’t be surprised to discover that we are a long way from being so. This leads us to acknowledge our weaknesses without trying to gloss over them selfrighteously, thereby helping us avoid pride, which is so tremendously blinding and makes everything seem the opposite to what it is. My children, be sincere with yourselves, be objective. That’s how to make our dedication effective. It’s difficult: one has to open one’s heart completely and humbly in spiritual direction and ventilate all the corners of the soul.13 It is particularly necessary to be sincere whenever we feel tempted to disguise or justify something contrary to purity or to make something unacceptable appear respectable. ‘The flesh though dressed in silk’ ... This is my only comment when I see you waver before the temptation that hides its impurity under pretext of art, of science ... , of charity! This is my only comment set in the words of an old proverb: ‘The

12 Ibid., 45 13 Ibid., 38

Along the Path to Love 230

flesh is flesh though dressed in silk.’14 For some souls this could be a really important moment in their lives, an occasion of deeply reaffirming their self-giving to God and truly expressing their love. Be faithful! don’t be foolish! And anyway, if the occasion were to arise of offering God the small sacrifice of a handful of earth and we failed to do so, what sort of love would that be? Be faithful!15 The means are always within reach; the motives too are the same, and are now reinforced by considerations of loyalty and fidelity. And if it should happen to someone in the fulness of life, it is all the more reason to be faithful, bearing in mind those words of St Peter: the end of all things is at hand.16 In this context the Apostle is referring specifically to mortifying the desires of the flesh: so as to live for the rest of the time in the flesh no longer by human passions but by the will of God.17 I have noticed at times, our Father writes, how an athlete’s eyes light up at the sight of the obstacles he has to overcome. What a victory! See how he conquers the difficulties! God our Lord looks at us that way. He loves our struggle: we will win through always, because he will never deny us his all-powerful grace. Thus, it doesn’t matter if we have to fight, because he does not abandon us. It is a battle, but not a renunciation. We respond with a joyful affirmation, and give ourselves to him freely and cheerfully. Your conduct should not be limited to simply avoiding falls and occasions of sin. In no way should you let it come down to a cold and calculating negation. Are you really convinced that chastity is a virtue and that, as such, it ought to grow and become perfect? Then I insist

14 The Way, 134 15 From our Father, Crónica, September 1960, p.10 16 1 Pet 4:7 17 1 Pet 4:2 231 Fidelity in love

once again that it is not enough merely to be continent according to one’s state in life. We must practise, we must live chastity, to a heroic degree. This attitude involves a positive act whereby we gladly accept God’s summons when he says: praebe, fili mi, cor tuum mihi et oculi tui vias meas custodiant: ‘Son, give me your heart, and turn your gaze upon my ways of peace’ (Prov 23:26).18

Impulses of the heart Another factor we have to take into account in order to guarantee fidelity is the stirring of sentiment. Our Father wrote: How is that heart of yours getting along? Don’t worry: the saints – who were perfectly ordinary, normal beings like you and me – also felt those ‘natural’ inclinations. And if they had not felt them, their ‘supernatural’ reaction of guarding the heart – body and soul – for God, instead of giving it to creatures, would have had little merit. That’s why, once the way is seen, I think that the heart’s weaknesses should be no obstacle for a determined soul, for a soul in love.19 The heart has an absolute need of another’s love. Man generally seeks this fulfilment in the love of home and family, a love which God has blessed and made holy by means of a sacrament. But we cannot conclude from this that this is the only way open to the human heart, nor even that it is the ‘normal’ way. In the purely natural order that would indeed be the case; but, since the Christian soul has been raised by grace to the supernatural order, the ‘normal’ thing is to preserve the supernatural point of view in all its thoughts and affections and respond unreservedly to the specific demands of God’s call. A dry and arid heart is a good breeding ground for every type of vice. When it is not given something pure, clean and noble to love, it takes

18 Friends of God, 182 19 The Way, 164

Along the Path to Love 232

revenge and fills itself with squalor.20 How unfortunate, our Father adds, are those people who have never learned to love with tenderness! We Christians are in love with Love: our Lord does not want us dry and rigid, like inert matter. He wants us to be saturated with his love! People who, for the sake of God, say ‘No’ to a human love, are not bachelors or spinsters, like those sad, unhappy, crestfallen men and women who have despised the chance of a pure and generous love.21 A heart on fire with God’s love is practically invulnerable to the assaults that might lead it astray. The hearts of those who have been called to apostolic celibacy in Opus Dei have no need to fill themselves with any other love than that which is provided by their own selfgiving: in the first place God our Lord, and then our blessed fraternity and a universal and effective love for souls. We are not bachelors or spinsters who live without love. Divine Love has made us deny ourselves human love. And neither do we feel lonely. Love one another a lot. Let no son or daughter of mine on this earth ever feel lonely. This is a consequence of Divine Love, of noble love.22 Whenever we encounter temptation we have to keep pressing on with the help of grace. We’re not expected to behave like angels, because the angelic nature is quite different from ours; we have to behave like upright, strong and normal human beings! The sense of Christian fidelity which so many married couples have for each other, just like our parents had, we should have for the sake of the Love of Loves. Have a great regard for holy purity, invoke Mary the Mother of Fair Love, and we will persevere joyfully and be supernaturally fruitful in this divine Way of our Work.23

20 Friends of God, 183 21 Ibid. 22 From our Father, Crónica, September 1960, p.12 23 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 45 233 Fidelity in love

When one lives like that, one appreciates the full meaning of our Father’s words: Surely God’s Love is worth any love.24 To be faithful in love, everything depends on learning to love God.

Wanting to have children The desire to perpetuate oneself through having children may acquire a special importance in later years, at a time when children could appear to be the solution to one’s personal inadequacies and a sign of hope for the future. For some people also, children can come to be a means of justifying themselves and of justifying their own existence. However, children alone cannot give meaning to their parents’ lives; only God can do that. To want to give God children is something very noble, always provided it is sincere; and the first sign of sincerity is not to deny God what he asks for, which is the full and generous response to vocation, both in celibacy and in marriage. Anything that overlooks these commitments is just simply a pretext for indulging one’s baser instincts. The best aspect of paternity is bringing children into the world and educating them unstintingly to be good children of God while being ready to renounce any earthly recompense in return. This happens most supremely in the spiritual sphere, which is the situation of those who, for the love of God, have forsaken an earthly paternity. With their selfgiving and apostolate, full of prayer and sacrifice, they can truly exclaim with St Paul: My little children, with whom I am again in travail until Christ be formed in you!25 That is why winning new vocations, which more than satisfies this particular aspiration of the human soul, is so essential for us all. Who

24 The Way, 171 25 Gal 4:19

Along the Path to Love 234

does not hunger to perpetuate his apostolate?26 And who, on winning a new apostle, has not felt a deep human and supernatural joy at the birth of a new child in the spirit to grace, holiness and the apostolate? Longing for children? Children, many children, and a lasting trail of light we shall leave behind us, if we sacrifice the selfishness of the flesh.27 The life of apostolic celibacy has been given an explicit promise by God: I will give in my house and within my walls a monument and a name better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name which shall not be cut off.28 This is why we see Jesus correcting a certain woman in the crowd who sought to praise his Mother the Blessed Virgin: ‘Blessed is the womb that bore you, and the breasts that you sucked!’ But he said, ‘Blessed rather are those who hear the word of God and keep it!29

Heroic fidelity We have to be faithful both in youth and in maturity. If we ever go through a difficult patch we have to tackle it with a great sense of fidelity, to at least the same degree as those upright people who, having received from God a vocation to marriage, are faithful to their duties as spouses and parents. Supernatural fidelity is based on the natural virtue of loyalty, which God relies on to bring countless souls into the warmth of his light and his love. There are many souls around you, our Founder wrote, and we have no right to be an obstacle to their spiritual good. We are obliged to seek Christian perfection and be saints. Not only can we not disappoint

26 The Way, 809 27 Ibid., 28 28 Is 56:5 29 Lk 11:27-28 235 Fidelity in love

God for his having chosen us as his own, but neither can we fail all those people who hope for so much from our apostolic labours. There are other considerations to bear in mind too, and a sense of loyalty towards our fellow-men requires us to give good example. If we ever have the misfortune to do anything unworthy of a Christian, we have to ask God for the grace to rectify.30 If the circumstances were such as to call for real heroism, then we should remember that holiness does indeed consist of virtue practised to an heroic degree, and it would be a good opportunity really to show God how much we love him and want to be faithful. The following words of advice, which were valid in St Augustine’s time, continue to be relevant today: “Continue, saints of God, youths and girls, men and women, those of you who live in celibacy and you who have not again contracted marriage: persevere to the end! The more the Lord occupies your thoughts the more sweetly you praise him; the more faithfully you serve him the greater happiness you may expect; and the more eager you are to please him the more ardently you love him.”31 Those who are called to celibacy are asked for a lot, in one sense, but what they receive even in this life is incomparably greater. This is part of the hundredfold which our Lord promises to those who seek first the kingdom of God and his justice. It is a question of having faith in one’s vocation and never doubting it, trusting that he who began a good work in you will bring it to completion at the day of Jesus Christ.32 This degree of faith and fortitude calls for a great love of God if we are to preserve and complete “this really heavenly way of life”33 whereby we give ourselves to God body and soul in apostolic celibacy.

30 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 57 31 St Augustine, De sancta virginitate, 27 32 Phil 1:6 33 St Ambrose, De virginibus, 1, 3

Along the Path to Love 236

## refore we have been told from the beginning: What is the secret of perseverance? Love. Fall in Love, and you will not leave him.34 We have continually to ask the Blessed Virgin, Mother of Fair Love, to keep us forever steadfast in God’s service, to safeguard our hearts for him both in youth and in maturity, and make us always faithful and loyal in Love.

34 The Way, 999

## CITIZENS OF BOTH CITIES {#cuaderno.8.19}
