God created man as a social being, and willed that this aspect of human life would also be to his glory. Among other duties, this requires us to play our part in public affairs. Politics, in the best sense of the term, our Father wrote many years ago, is nothing other than the attempt to achieve the common good of the earthly city. This good is very extensive, and it is in the political forum where laws of the utmost importance are discussed and approved, for example those relating to marriage, the family, education, the necessary minimum of private property, and the dignity of the human person, with his rights and duties. All these matters, and others too, are in the first place of interest to religion, and an apostle cannot remain indifferent or apathetic in their regard.1

Civic duties The Church “exhorts Christians, as citizens of both cities, to perform their duties faithfully in the spirit of the Gospel. It is a mistake to think that, because we have here no lasting city, but seek the city which is to come (Heb 13:14), we are entitled to shirk our earthly responsibilities; this is to forget that by our faith we are bound all the more to fulfil

1 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 42

Along the Path to Love 238

these responsibilities according to the vocation of each one.”2 Our Founder always insisted that a dedicated and unselfish presence in public life offers immense opportunities to do good, to serve. Catholics cannot abandon this field – you, my children, cannot abandon it – and leave politics in the hands of people who don’t know or observe God’s law, or who are hostile to Holy Church. Man’s life, both individual and social, is inescapably linked with the law and spirit of our Lord Jesus Christ. Christians, therefore, naturally see the connection there is between apostolate and the regulation of society by the State, that is, political activity. As Christ said, render to Caesar the things that are Caesar’s, and to God the things that are God’s (cf Mt 22:21). Unfortunately, very often this clear principle isn’t adhered to: the terms are lost sight of and end up as two equally erroneous extremes: on the one hand, secularism, which ignores the legitimate rights of the Church; and on the other, clericalism, which overrides the equally legitimate rights of the State. My children, to combat these two abuses there is a great need of lay men and women who feel themselves, and who are, children of God and citizens of both Cities.3 This is even more urgent nowadays, because people generally are more aware of taking part in public life, and there are many individuals who make use of political power to promote customs contrary to the faith of Christ and Christian living, and to human dignity. They are the same people who honour a false liberty, and ask us Catholics ‘to do them the favour’ of going back to the catacombs.4 At the same time, in many countries one observes among believers a sort of apathy towards tackling social issues in the light of the

2 Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 43 3 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 41 4 Furrow, 301 239 Citizens of both cities

Christian message. It is quite common to find, even among apparently responsible and upright Catholics, the error of thinking that they are only required to fulfil their family and religious duties, and scarcely any mention is made of civic duties. It is not a question of selfishness. It is simply a lack of formation. Nobody has ever told them clearly that the virtue of piety, which is part of the cardinal virtue of justice, and the Christian sense of solidarity, are also expressed by being present in society, in being aware of and contributing to solve the problems affecting the whole community. Naturally, it would be unreasonable to expect everybody to get professionally involved in politics. Apart from anything else, because of the great specialization and full-time dedication which all work requires nowadays, politics included, it would be quite impossible to do so even in closely knit communities. But it is certainly both possible and desirable, in present-day circumstances, to have a basic level of awareness of the specific issues affecting the common good of the society in which one lives.5 Moreover, everybody participates in the political process in the broad sense of the term by exercising responsibly, in accordance with catholic teaching, the right and duty to vote in elections; by trying to bring a Christian influence to bear on the activities of the various political, cultural, professional, sporting and other social groupings in which one takes part; by making the truth known, or by opposing the falsehoods propagated by certain anti-Catholic sectors of the media. Your task as a Christian citizen is to help see Christ’s love and freedom preside over all aspects of modern life: culture and the economy, work and rest, family life and social relations.6 A very worthwhile, urgent and exciting task lies in store: to put an

5 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 46 6 Furrow, 302

Along the Path to Love 240

end to a widespread easygoing abstentionism and to get many of our fellow Catholics to have a sense of social awareness and become actively involved in public life, each one at his or her own level and in accordance with their vocation, because it is something we will all have to account to God for some day. Already in 1932 our Father put forward a series of practical measures in this regard: I would like to see elementary catechisms spelling out clearly (...) the obligation people have of getting involved in public affairs, and of not just abstaining, being ready to serve the common good faithfully and on their personal initiative (...) In the field of education and human formation it is also very desirable for educators, without imposing their personal theories in matters of opinion, to teach students the obligation they have to act freely and responsibly in fulfilling their duties as citizens.7

Freedom in matters of opinion As far as solutions to specific issues are concerned, social and political affairs are autonomous in their own spheres,8 but they still have to be directed to God as the ultimate end of all things. One has to start, therefore, from a series of principles which are true, and consequently not negotiable, regarding the nature of man and fundamental ethical values. And so it is “mistaken to think that we may immerse ourselves in earthly activities as if these latter were utterly foreign to religion (...) One of the gravest errors of our time is the dichotomy between the faith which many profess and the practice of their daily lives.”9 Nevertheless, once these fundamental common principles have been

7 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 45-46 8 Cf Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 36 9 Ibid., 43 241 Citizens of both cities

established, Christians “should recognize the legitimacy of differing points of view about the organization of worldly affairs and show respect for their fellow citizens, who even in associations defend their opinions by legitimate means.”10 This is only natural, because “the persons who go to make up the political community are many and varied; quite rightly, then, they may veer towards widely differing points of view.”11 You see, children of my soul, wrote our Founder many years ago, the great need there is to form Catholics with the specific aim of bringing them together on essentials, while at the same time leaving them quite free in temporal affairs, so they act charitably and considerately towards everybody. Freedom: no more dogmas in debatable matters. It is not in keeping with man’s dignity, nor with his psychological makeup, to be arbitrarily dogmatic about a whole series of things which every individual necessarily looks at from the viewpoint of his or her own interests and personal experience.12 In determined circumstances, nevertheless, whenever the common good and the good of the Church requires it, the Hierarchy, and they alone, have the right and the duty to lay down guidelines for Catholics in political matters, if they consider it necessary. And when the Hierarchy intervenes in this way, it is not a form of clericalism. All well-educated Catholics should know that it is part of the pastoral mission of bishops to issue directives in public affairs whenever the Church’s good requires it. And they should also know that, by divine right, this faculty belongs to bishops alone, because only they, in communion with the Roman Pontiff, have the public task of ruling the Church: Spiritus Sanctus posuit episcopos regere Ecclesiam Dei (Acts 20:28): the Holy Spirit

10 Ibid., 75 11 Ibid., 74 12 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 51

Along the Path to Love 242

appointed the bishops to rule the Church of God.13 Naturally, when fundamental principles are at stake – human life, religious freedom, the dignity of marriage and the family, Christian education – Catholics should always act on their own initiative in accordance with the principles of the moral law. This agreement on basic principles can in no sense be interpreted as having a common political policy or a one-party mentality, because the defence and promotion of fundamental human values is of its very nature on a completely different plane from the pragmatic decisions that are characteristic of politics, as that word is commonly understood. These supreme values belong in an untouchable sphere precisely because they are expressions of the divine law and the natural law, which are binding on all men. In this sense, for example, the defence of human life against arbitrary manipulation or elimination is not a political question, but rather a moral issue, and leaves no room for dissent or discussion about the Church’s position on the matter. Political debate, the free discussion of ways and means, arises at the moment of deciding the best way – programmes of legislation, information, education or welfare – to safeguard human life and to foster that ‘culture of life’ so insistently spoken about recently by the Roman Pontiffs. It is clear that here there is room for many different and equally acceptable opinions, which catholics don’t have to be in agreement about.

Unity and diversity God has entrusted the Church with a supernatural mission which is above the contingencies of politics. In this terrain the Church has the right and the duty to illuminate creation with the light of eternal Truth. “There is no better way to establish political life on a truly human basis,”

13 Ibid., 50 243 Citizens of both cities

says the Second Vatican Council, “than by encouraging an inward sense of justice, of good will, and of service to the common good, and by consolidating the basic convictions of men as to the true nature of the political community and the aim, proper exercise, and the limits of public authority.”14 This is exactly what the Church does for the faithful. In it we see a reflection of the clear awareness the first Christians had of being exemplary citizens: “For us it is not enough to be just, for justice consists in giving the same as one’s equals, but we are asked to be good and patient.”15 Proper doctrinal and spiritual formation has direct practical repercussions on people’s contribution to public life, while always respecting their freedom. That is how the Work behaves with its members. Opus Dei has nothing whatever to do with politics. It is absolutely foreign to any political, economic, ideological or cultural tendency or group (...) Its aims are exclusively spiritual and apostolic. The only thing it demands of its members is that they lead a Christian life, trying to live up to the ideal of the Gospel.16 Opus Dei aims at the sanctification of its members, each one in the state, profession or occupation they have in the world. You are bound together, and each one to the whole Work, only in the quest for personal holiness, and in the task – also exclusively spiritual – of carrying the light of Christ to your friends, to your families, to those around you.17 Our Father taught us that in the Work there is room, now and always, for all the options open to Christians, without the slightest compulsion on the part of the Directors.18 And he spoke very strongly

14 Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 73 15 Athenagoras, Legatio pro christianis, 34 16 Conversations, 28 17 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 44 18 From our Father, Crónica, February 1957, p.7

Along the Path to Love 244

about the blessed freedom the members of the Work enjoy in temporal affairs, especially in politics: if one of them ever tried to exert pressure on the others to make them accept his political opinions, or to use them for human interests, they would rebel and expel him without a second thought.19 And again: the Work has never intervened in politics and, with God’s help, it never will; but if it were to, I would be its number one enemy.20 And in his relations with us he was always extremely careful to abide by the same principle: I have never asked you what you think about these matters, nor will I ever do so, because I respect your rightful freedom, and the same applies to the Directors of the Work all over the world.21

Serving the Church without making use of her This attitude of the Work to temporal affairs has a number of practical expressions, the most important of which is our love for freedom. Our Founder used to say that this was the characteristic which, in the human sphere, he wished to leave us as an inheritance: we would be inconsistent if we didn’t respect other people’s opinions when they differ from those each of us has.22 And he used to state very strongly that if someone does not understand this, it may well be because he does not understand personal freedom.23 How sad it is to have the mentality of a Roman Emperor, and not to understand the freedom other citizens enjoy in the things God has left to the free choice of men.24 This mentality of a Roman Emperor can also be found among Catholics when, under the pretext of defending

19 Conversations, 28 20 Ibid. 21 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 48 22 Ibid., 49 23 Conversations, 28 24 Furrow, 313 245 Citizens of both cities

Christian faith or morality, or the Church, they attempt to maintain a monopoly of truth in matters of opinion. In the early years of the Work our Father wrote: there’s one more point about public affairs I’d like to make, my children, although maybe it’s superfluous, because if you have my spirit it is something you are hardly likely to fall into. The point is this: don’t ever become official Catholics, that is, Catholics who use religion as a springboard, not to leap towards God, but to rise to positions of importance and gain the things they covet, things like honour, wealth and power. It was of individuals like these that a certain reputable person said good-humouredly, perhaps exaggerating, that “their eyes are in heaven, and their hands in all sorts of places”.25 Precisely because we love our Mother the Church passionately, we recoil even at the thought of making use of her for material ends. The only use we can make of the Church is to obtain the sources of grace and salvation.26 We are not prepared to let anybody attribute our personal failings to the Church as a whole. It is not right to drag the Church into politics, because political assessments are only relatively accurate in their diagnosis, and always a matter of opinion. It is a very convenient approach to take, and very unfair.27 In any event, the formation we receive in the Work teaches us that, above and beyond the stirring of men’s passions, even the most just and noble, there is the supreme commandment of charity. Consequently, within the wide range of temporal opinions open to us, we try to promote the unity of Catholics in essential matters, in things the Church teaches authoritatively, because, among people of different political persuasions, harmony is often undermined by bickering. Our formation

25 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 52 26 Ibid., 55 27 Ibid., 47

Along the Path to Love 246

gives us a ‘holy intransigence’ towards ideas opposed to the Church’s teaching, and at the same time a ‘holy transigence’ towards the individuals who mistakenly defend them. It enables us to have always the good manners and sense of fairness to realize that, in resolving matters that God has left to man’s free scrutiny, we can easily make mistakes, and indeed do so frequently.

Availability for public office Like the immense majority of citizens, most of the members of Opus Dei work in jobs or occupations that have nothing to do with politics. Only a few, who have a vocation for public affairs, work professionally as politicians. And they do so completely freely and on their own responsibility. The Work has not got any political philosophy of its own: that’s not its aim. The only aim we have is spiritual and apostolic, and it has a divine seal: the love for freedom, which Christ obtained for us by his death on the Cross (cf Gal 4:31). And so the Work of God has never taken part in party politics, nor will it ever do so. For our supernatural family it is not just something praiseworthy, but rather a strict duty, to keep out of the debatable disputes that poison public life, for the simple reason that the aims of the Work – let me say once more

– are not in any way political, but apostolic. But you, my children, in your individual capacity, would not only make a mistake, as I have just said, but would betray our Lord’s cause, if you were to abandon the affairs of State to people who are unworthy or incapable, or to the enemies of Christ and his Church.28 It is a wholesome thing to be ready to accept public office, precisely in order to place Christ at the centre of civil life, and always completely

28 Ibid., 42 247 Citizens of both cities

## ely and on one’s own responsibility. The Second Vatican Council has the following words to say to those who feel called to this mission: “Those with a talent for the difficult yet noble art of politics, or whose talents in this matter can be developed, should prepare themselves for it, and, forgetting their own convenience and material interests, they should engage in political activity. They must combat injustice and oppression, arbitrary domination and intolerance by individuals or political parties, and they must do so with integrity and wisdom. They must dedicate themselves to the welfare of all in a spirit of sincerity and fairness, of love and the courage demanded by political life.”29 These are indeed lofty aspirations, both humanly and spiritually, that the Church proposes, not only to her children, but to all those who dedicate themselves professionally to public affairs. These goals of true service to the community are especially accessible to Christians, when they genuinely strive to follow, in personal, family and social life, the footsteps of that Master who came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many.30

29 Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 75 30 Mt 20:28

## COMMANDING AND OBEYING: SERVING AND LOVING {#cuaderno.8.20}

“The one Mediator, Christ, established and ever sustains here on earth his holy Church, the community of faith, hope and charity, as a visible organization through which he communicates truth and grace to all men. But, the society structured with hierarchical organs and the Mystical Body of Christ, the visible assembly and the spiritual community, the earthly Church and the Church endowed with heavenly riches, are not to be thought of as two realities. On the contrary, they form one complex reality which comes together from a human and a divine element.”1 The interior action of the Holy Spirit and the external exercise of authority are, by divine will, two inseparable elements of the Church’s life, and in a certain sense interdependent, so they cannot be seen as opposing forces. “It is not right to ignore the fact (...) that the Church is, by God’s will, a visible society with all the structures needed to guide it externally. Christ conferred the power of jurisdiction upon the Apostles and their successors. The Gospel itself confirms this for us when our Lord tells the Apostles: ‘All authority in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Go therefore and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,

1 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 8 249 Commanding and obeying: serving and loving

teaching them to observe all that I have commanded you’ (Mt 28:18-20).2 The Church, which is the Mystical Body of Christ, is a visible and perfect society, the People of God, and has need of a hierarchy. God has appointed in the Church first apostles, second prophets, third teachers, then workers of miracles, then healers, helpers, administrators, speakers in various kinds of tongues. Are all apostles? Are all prophets? Are all teachers?3 God arranged the organs in the body, each one of them, as he chose. If all were a single organ, where would the body be? As it is, there are many parts, yet one body,4 forming a single hierarchical whole.

Government and obedience in Opus Dei In the Work, which is a living constituent of Christ’s Mystical Body, God has also wanted some members to be in charge and others to obey them, so as to fulfil the purpose he had in mind when bringing it into existence. For “God so governs things that he makes some of them act as causes of others in government.”5 God has established that some human beings should direct others, for “in human affairs it is fitting that those above should move those below by a decision of their will, in virtue of a divine authority. Now to move by reason and will is to command. Wherefore just as in virtue of the divinely established natural order the lower natural things need to be subject to the movement of the higher, so too in human affairs, in virtue of the order of natural and divine law, inferiors are bound to obey their superiors.”6 To command and to obey are not opposed poles, but reciprocal

2 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 20 November 1965 3 1 Cor 12:28-29 4 1 Cor 12:18-20 5 St Thomas, S. Th., I, q.103, a.6 6 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.104, a.1

Along the Path to Love 250

expressions of the same love and the same activity. When Jesus was about to return to his heavenly Father he said to his disciples: if you love me, you will keep my commandments:7 if you love me, obey me. And our Father has written: to give formation and to govern is to love.8 In the Work the love of God fuses together the will of the person who commands and the one who obeys, making them just one thing: congregavit nos in unum Christi amor: the love of Christ gathers us together.9 The two roles, directing and obeying, are different but interdependent functions of the common mission of holiness and apostolate which God has entrusted to us all. In the Work the Directors exercise authority as an act of love, and we all obey out of love. No Director goes around flaunting his or her authority, and none of us obey as if we were not responsible for our actions. In government there is an element of submission, a mandate received from God; and in obedience there is an element of selfdetermination, whereby one applies things to one’s own situation. Christ “does not need our service, but we need to be under his dominion so that he works on us and protects us. Only he is the true Lord. We do not serve him for his good, but for our advantage and salvation.”10 In the Work we all have to obey. Those who are in charge are the ones who need to practise the holy virtue of obedience with utmost fidelity: first of all because they have to identify themselves with their immediate Directors, and furthermore because they have always to be guided by the spirit and norms of the Work.11 If the person who commands does not obey, he becomes incapable of directing others:

7 Jn 14:15 8 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 39 9 Hymn Ubi caritas 10 St Augustine, De Genesi ad litteram, 8, 11, 24 11 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 35 251 Commanding and obeying: serving and loving

revolt in turn breeds further revolt, so that the person who disobeys soon finds himself being disobeyed. Man experienced this reality in his own flesh when he rebelled against the divine precept and lost control of his faculties. “The whole order of original justice consists in man’s will being subject to God. This subjection, first and chiefly, was in the will, whose function it is to move all the other parts to the end. Hence when the will was turned away from God, all the other powers of the soul became deranged.”12 In disobeying God, man lost dominion over his passions and appetites. In one way or another, we all have a part to play in the work of government. Both in our apostolic tasks and in our daily work we often have to make decisions that affect others. And when we obey, we should always be in control of ourselves, and be fully at one with the will of the person in charge. We are members of a team, working together responsibly in a common project that concerns us all. You ought to feel the weight of your mission, writes our Founder, and appreciate how much God himself, through those in charge, expects of you.13 The obligation to command and to obey spring from the same root, namely, the love of God, and of others for God – holiness and apostolate. It leads some to command and others to obey, each one according to circumstances, in a single and complete act of love whereby we all serve our Mother the Church and the whole of mankind.

A common mission of service In a living body all the members are necessary. To sustain themselves some require the service of others. “The head is nothing without the feet, and likewise the feet are nothing without the head. Even the smallest members of our body are useful and necessary for the whole

12 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.82, a.3 13 From our Father, Letter 16 June 1960, 26

Along the Path to Love 252

body and all serve some purpose, and are ordained to preserve the whole body.”14 All the members serve the body, and the body its members. From the beginning of the Work this mission of service was to the forefront in our Founder’s mind. If the Work doesn’t serve the Church, it is good for nothing. That’s why it was born! That’s why God willed it!15 We have repeatedly heard the same words from his lips. As I have often told you, para servir, servir: in order to be useful, serve. This phrase encapsulates a great portion of our spirit: service to God, to his Holy Church, to the Roman Pontiff; service to all souls, especially to those our Lord has placed beside us and given a vocation to Opus Dei; and service to all those who, even though they don’t have a vocation, receive the influence of our example and doctrine, which is also an apostolic service.16 In the Work we all have to serve. To be in charge is to serve, and to obey is also to serve. The Director ought to be interiorly detached from his post. It is fitting that he should see himself before God as he is, so he can acknowledge that he is unworthy and better suited to obey. And with this humble disposition the Directors ought to appreciate with complete certainty that they have a special grace from God, and be confident that our Lord will help them fulfil the duties of their position. That is why the Director should have the habit of raising his heart to God many times a day: to ask for grace; to give thanks to God after resolving a matter of some importance with his Local Council; and, while advising others, to seek humbly his own sanctification. He should see himself as an instrument in the service of the Church. He should look upon the positions he holds as burdens: obligations rather

14 St Clement of Rome, Epistle to the Corinthians, 37, 4-5 15 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 6 16 Ibid., 9 253 Commanding and obeying: serving and loving

than rights. He should become all things to all men (cf 1 Cor 9:22), in the knowledge that the purpose of his earthly existence is a joyful, voluntary and unremitting servitude.17 To be in charge is to think about others and give oneself fully to them. This is why our Father tells us: I have taught you from the beginning to consider internal positions not as posts of honour or privilege, which they are not, but as yet another opportunity to serve. This explains why we do not congratulate people who receive internal appointments: to do so would be contrary to our spirit, because we do not think about the position but about the burden, joyfully borne, that is implied in serving our brothers or sisters.18 And so, to cease to hold a particular post is not a failure: it’s another way of serving.19 Just as to command is to serve, to obey is also to serve. The person who governs ought to serve, by the exercise of his authority, those whom he governs. And the person who obeys does likewise, for this is another valuable and necessary way of serving. There are people, though, who over-emphasise the idea of service in governing, while at the same time ignoring and dismissing obedience as something contrary to human dignity. This general attitude is even seen at times in the life of the Church. On a certain occasion Blessed Paul VI referred sorrowfully to this topic: “And what are we to say about obedience? In this regard too, what a great amount of restlessness, criticism and intolerance! Yet the reply is always the same: authority in the Church is something willed by Christ. Anyone who thinks that there will have to be a complete and total revision of Church discipline (...) is not on the right road. He is hurting the Church by tearing apart its spiritual and social fabric, and he is hurting himself by depriving himself of the merit of spontaneous, filial

17 Ibid., 6-7 18 Ibid., 11 19 Ibid., 12

Along the Path to Love 254

and robust docility, and of the comfort that comes from humility, good example and trust.”20 Our Father too pointed out to us the atmosphere of undisciplined anarchy one finds in certain environments, and which in any case is well-known. Today, when the atmosphere is filled with disobedience, gossip, deception, and confusion, we have to love obedience. My children, you must obey your Directors; and in this way, through the Directors, you are obeying your Father. If not, we won’t do anything in Opus Dei.21 As the Second Vatican Council expressly recalled, “to carry out the will of the Father, Christ inaugurated the kingdom of heaven on earth and revealed to us his mystery; by his obedience he brought about our redemption.”22

Learning to serve It is not enough, moreover, to want to obey: one must learn to obey. Our Father tells us: you have to be convinced that if you don’t learn to obey, you will not be effective.23 Learning to obey implies carrying out intelligently what we are asked to do, which means using our head and putting our heart into it. It implies an ardent desire to identify our will with that of our Directors, wanting what they want, and the way they want it. To obey like this, as the Work expects of us, our apprenticeship must be continuous. We should never consider it completed, because in the depths of each human being there lies a principle of disunity, the result of self-love, the heritage of original sin and the consequence of personal sin, which tends to inflame our ego and which we will have till the day we die. We must humbly acknowledge its existence and try

20 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 21 February 1965 21 From our Father, Meditation, 24 December 1963 22 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen Gentium, 3 23 From our Father, Meditation, 9 March 1962 255 Commanding and obeying: serving and loving

to uproot it. Our Father asks us: Why don’t you look inside yourself to see how you can be obedient? Humility and obedience: two conditions which are indispensable in order to receive doctrine, my spirit which God has given me to hand on to you.24 Not only do we need to learn to obey, but we must also learn to command: to know how to command with all the weight of authority, an authority that is service, but bearing in mind that those who obey do so using their intelligence and will, as free beings and not as corpses.25 Only the person who has learned to obey can command, for he has discovered that the true nature of obedience is love, and that the mission of governing is also an act of love. To learn to command, wrote our Father, means not being an insulator. It means being an instrument of communication and setting on fire with the spirit of Opus Dei the souls you have been entrusted with. It means putting into practice in turn the desire and will of your own Directors for the better service of God.26 That is why he never tired of repeating: my children, learn the art of governing by serving, so that your brothers or sisters can learn to obey freely, for supernatural reasons.27 The spirit of the Work is such that those who govern have to command by obeying: obeying their immediate Directors, obeying the spirit and statutes of Opus Dei, and God who directs all. Then too, the person who obeys has to do so with a spirit of self-mastery, so that in each case he can determine and put into practice the most suitable way to carry out what he has been asked to do. Furthermore, those who govern and those who obey must command and obey knowing that this way they are serving God, the Church, the Work, and all souls. And

24 From our Father, Meditation, 2 October 1955 25 From our Father, Crónica, November 1966, p.12 26 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 19 27 Ibid., 26

Along the Path to Love 256

## ays out of love, for this is the best reason of all. Only by acting like this will we be able to say to our Lord, when we are summoned to appear before him: I have obeyed the voice of the Lord my God, I have done according to all that you have commanded me.28 In commanding and obeying only out of love, we are assured of never losing our gaudium cum pace, the supernatural joy that is a foretaste of eternal happiness. Only by living like this “does obedience become assimilation to Christ, the divine obedient one. Thus it becomes the fundamental norm for our pedagogy of Christian formation, an indispensable element for the interior unity of the Church, the source and sign of her peace. Thus it becomes an effective cooperation in her evangelizing mission, and an ascetical and spiritual exercise of humility and charity (cf Phil 2:5-12). Thus it becomes communion with Christ and with him who is for us the apostle and representative of Christ.”29 My children, let no one outdo us in obeying, whether it is hot or cold, whether one is young or not so young. A person who is unable to obey will never learn to command. Obeying and commanding, but done with a lot of love.30

28 Deut 26:14 29 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 6 October 1966 30 From our Father, Get-together, 18 January 1965

## OUR PROFESSIONAL VOCATION {#cuaderno.8.21}

These are the generations of the heavens and the earth when they were created. On the day that the Lord God made the earth and the heavens ... no plant of the field was yet in the earth and no herb of the field had yet sprung up – for the Lord God had not caused it to rain upon the earth, and there was no man to till the ground.1 When God created the world he left it in a certain sense unfinished, for he willed man to play a part in its completion. The Lord God took the man and put him in the garden of Eden to till it and keep it.2 As the Latin text puts it, man was made ut operaretur, to work, to be master of the earth and direct it towards its goal, thereby making God’s perfections shine forth in the world. In undertaking this task man achieves his own perfection and draws closer to God.

Man’s natural duty to work The divine commandment to work was not imposed on our first parents as a punishment for sin, although it is true that their disobedience did add some punitive effects, namely, the effort and tiredness it entails. After original sin, wrote our Founder, work remains the same, although now, because of that sin, it has been joined by suffering and weariness. We read in Genesis: in the sweat of your face you shall

1 Gen 2:4-5 2 Gen 2:15

Along the Path to Love 258

eat bread (Gen 3:19). Work is not something accidental, but a law in man’s life. Sex diebus operaberis, septimo die cessabis: six days you shall do your work, but on the seventh day you shall rest (Ex 23:12).3 Work is a basic obligation for man precisely because “as the ‘image of God’ he is a person, that is to say, a subjective being capable of acting in a planned and rational way, capable of deciding about himself, and with a tendency to self-realization. As a person, man is therefore the subject of work,”4 and should not try to evade this law. “By his work a man ordinarily provides for himself and his family, associates with others as his brothers and renders them service; he can exercise genuine charity and be a partner in the work of bringing divine creation to perfection.”5 Since work is a necessity for man, its absence – idleness – has serious consequences. Idleness teaches much evil,6 because it prevents man from perfecting himself, it weakens the character, it arouses concupiscence and opens the door to many temptations. It seems – so you say – as if every imaginable sin were awaiting the first idle moment. Why, idleness itself must be a sin!7 The generic vocation to work is specified for each human being by a particular professional vocation. Since man is a limited being, he cannot do generic jobs but specific work for which he has a particular aptitude or natural inclination, or for which he has been specially trained, in the light of the various factors that determined his choice in the first place. When choosing a career a person is not free to select anything he or

3 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 3 4 St John Paul II, Encyclical Laborem exercens, 6 5 Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 67 6 Sir 33:27 7 The Way, 357 259 Our professional vocation

she feels like, but only something from what is realistically possible. If the chosen career is not really practicable in the circumstances, it means that the person is indirectly evading the basic human obligation to work. Now you will understand even better that if anyone among you didn’t love work, his own particular job; if he didn’t feel sincerely committed to some noble occupation in this world so as to sanctify it, or if he were to lack a professional vocation, then that person would never be able to understand the supernatural substance of what this priest is saying to you, for the very good reason that he would be lacking an indispensable condition for doing so: that of being a worker (...) Be convinced that our professional vocation is an essential and inseparable part of our condition as Christians. Our Lord wants you to be holy in the place where you are, in the job you have chosen for whatever reason. To me, every job that is not opposed to the divine law is good and noble, and capable of being raised to the supernatural plane, that is, inserted into the constant flow of Love which defines the life of a child of God.8

The Christian meaning of work This perspective on work and man’s professional vocation acquires fresh tones when the person doing it is a Christian. Jesus’ prayer to his heavenly Father, I do not pray that you should take them out of the world, but that you should keep them from the evil one,9 reminds us that as ordinary Christians we have to seek holiness and do apostolate precisely in the place God has placed us in society. And so our Christian calling, far from exempting us from the universal obligation to work, greatly reinforces it. As our Father affirmed since the 2nd of October 1928, ordinary work is – and it is extremely important to say

8 Friends of God, 58 & 60 9 Jn 17:15

Along the Path to Love 260

this very clearly – a means and a way to holiness, something to be sanctified and which sanctifies.10 The exercise of one’s occupation is “one of the most important means of sanctification, one of the most effective ways of responding to the divine Will and meriting heaven.”11 Christians, therefore, cannot do like the servant in the Gospel parable and bury their natural talents. God has placed some goods on deposit with us: to one he gave five talents, to another two, to another one, to each according to his ability.12 And if we wish to hear our Lord’s words of praise, well done, good and faithful servant,13 we have to go and sanctify ourselves by trading with the talents we have received. In addition to this, the Church teaches us that “through the homage of work offered to God, man is associated with the redemptive work of Jesus Christ, whose labour with his hands at Nazareth greatly ennobled the dignity of work.”14Jesus himself, who was known as the carpenter, the son of Mary,15 gives us a wonderful example in this sense: practically his entire life on earth was spent in the unspectacular daily monotony of a manual trade. For people who don’t appreciate the value of work – useful work, to earn a living – Christ’s life is necessarily enigmatic or absurd. Our Lord’s hidden life is an ideal which every Christian can and ought to imitate. I don’t understand how you can call yourself a Christian and lead such an idle, useless life, exclaims our Father. Have 16 Jesus’ life in Nazareth is the perfect you forgotten Christ’s life of toil? model of what Christian work should be like so as to be a genuine path

10 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 4 11 Venerable Pius XII, Address, 25 April 1950 12 Mt 25:15 13 Mt 25:21 14 Second Vatican Council, Pastoral Constitution Gaudium et spes, 67 15 Mk 6:3 16 The Way, 356 261 Our professional vocation

to holiness. As our Father wrote: ordinary work, similar to the daily occupation of countless millions of people, becomes in Jesus’ hands a divine undertaking, a redemptive task, a way of salvation.17 Work was originally intended to be for man something easy and pleasant. But when Adam disobeyed and was expelled from the earthly Paradise, God pronounced that harsh sentence which explains its subsequently toilsome character: in the sweat of your face you shall eat bread.18 Since then work means effort: we know it well, because it is our daily experience; and consequently weariness and tiredness. It is nevertheless evident that it offers us a constant opportunity to feel closer to Christ who has borne our griefs and carried our sorrows (Is 53:4).19 The difficulties involved in working well, “sweat and toil, which work necessarily involves in the present condition of the human race, present the Christian and everyone who is called to follow Christ with the possibility of sharing lovingly in the work that Christ came to do (cf Jn 17:4). This work of salvation came about through suffering and death on a Cross. By enduring the toil of work in union with Christ crucified for us, man in a way collaborates with the Son of God for the redemption of humanity. He shows himself a true disciple of Christ by carrying the cross in his turn every day (cf Lk 9:23) in the activity that he is called upon to perform.20 Moreover, the Christian feels compelled to restore all things in Christ: Jesus urges us on. He wants us to raise him up once more, not on the Cross, but in the glory of all human activities, so as to draw all things to himself (cf Jn 12:32).21 We are called upon to Christianize

17 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 3 18 Gen 3:19 19 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 4 20 St John Paul II, Encyclical Laborem exercens, 27 21 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 1

Along the Path to Love 262

all the institutions of mankind – the worlds of science, culture, civilization, politics, art and social relations. And in order to establish God’s kingdom everywhere, in all human activities there need to be sons and daughters of God who, identified with Christ, do their work as perfectly as possible and offer it “through him, with him, in him”22 to God the Father, in a sacrifice of adoration, thanksgiving, atonement and petition for all mankind. In those words of Scripture, and I, when I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all men to myself,23 our Father perceived in a new way the Will of God for many Christians, and specifically for his children in Opus Dei. I understood it perfectly. God was telling us: if you place me at the centre of all earthly activities, fulfilling the duty of each moment and being my witnesses at all times, in things both great and small, then ... omnia traham ad meipsum! My kingdom among you will indeed come to pass!24 And so, just as work is a path leading to holiness in the middle of the world, it also leads to apostolate. Moreover, nowadays more than ever before, because of the way society has evolved, work is one of the most important means that men have to relate to one another, and consequently to lead others to the Catholic faith and holiness of life. Our Father pointed this out to us when he wrote: professional work, whatever it is, becomes a lamp to enlighten your colleagues and friends. That is why I usually tell those who become members of Opus Dei, and the same applies to all of you who are now listening to me: ‘What use is it telling me that so and so is a good son of mine – a good Christian – but a bad shoemaker?’ If he doesn’t try to learn his trade well, or doesn’t give his full attention to it in carrying it out, he won’t

22 Roman Missal, First Eucharistic Prayer 23 Jn 12:32 24 From our Father, Meditation, 27 October 1963 263 Our professional vocation

be able to sanctify it or offer it to our Lord. The sanctification of ordinary work is, as it were, the hinge of true spirituality for people who, like us, have decided to come close to God while being at the same time fully involved in temporal affairs.25

A specific means of holiness To make the search for perfection and the exercise of apostolate in the middle of the world accessible to all souls, God has raised up his Work, which is operatio Dei, work of God. Opus Dei gathers together Catholics from all walks of life, men and women, celibate and married, who live in the middle of the world, because they are ordinary lay people. By divine vocation they aspire to attain Christian perfection and to take the light of Christ to their companions in the same environment through the sanctification of ordinary work.26 The entire spirituality of Opus Dei rests, like a door on its hinges, on ordinary work carried out in the middle of the world. Without a professional vocation a person cannot join Opus Dei: the raw material which has to be sanctified, and through which we have to sanctify ourselves and others, would be missing. God has given each of you particular talents, aptitudes and interests. The various events of your life have formed your personality, and you have come to perceive a certain field of activity as more properly yours. By working afterwards in that field, your outlook has been gradually transformed and you have acquired the specific characteristics of that occupation or profession. All that – your professional vocation – has to be looked after, because it also forms part of your vocation to holiness. I have told you a thousand times that the human vocation is a part, and an

25 Friends of God, 61 26 From our Father, Crónica, March 1960, p.9

Along the Path to Love 264

important part at that, of our divine vocation, because our life can be summarized by saying: we have to sanctify our work, we have to sanctify ourselves in our work, and we have to sanctify others through our work.27 For an ordinary Christian, daily work is a pathway to holiness. What all men have to do by nature, becomes an occasion for supernatural merit if it is done with a right intention in the state of grace and for love of God. “Dedicate yourself to working the land, if you are a farmer,” says an ancient ecclesiastical writer, “and while you till the ground, get to know God. Go to sea, if you are a sailor, but not without first invoking him who governs the heavens.”28 Or, as our Father puts it more concisely: add a supernatural motive to your ordinary work, and you will have sanctified it.29 Once more, as in the lifetime of the Apostles and the early Christians, the exercise of a career has become a powerful weapon of holiness and apostolate, one that has been recognized and blessed by the Church. “This Christian spirituality of work should be a heritage shared by all. Especially in the modern age, the spirituality of work should show the maturity called for by the tensions and restlessness of mind and heart.”30 The need to acquire professional prestige should never be used as a cloak for selfishness: our work has to be done with a right intention. This has some very definite characteristics, about which we should constantly examine ourselves and make good resolutions, so as to purify our professional vocation and perfect our self-giving. Everybody, young and old, writes our Father, should consider frequently the intention

27 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 6 28 Clement of Alexandria, Protrepticus, 10 29 The Way, 359 30 St John Paul II, Encyclical Laborem exercens, 25 265 Our professional vocation

with which they do their work, bearing in mind that the following are symptoms of the absence of a right intention: to be motivated by human considerations, not to be concerned about little things, to neglect family life or apostolic tasks, not to make use of one’s work situation, be it prestigious or not, to carry out a wide-ranging apostolate.31

The danger of ‘professionalitis’ We all love our chosen career, which God’s grace has raised to a higher plane and transformed into a means of holiness and of apostolate. If we don’t lose this outlook, we will always react promptly and willingly to aspects of self-giving which otherwise might be seen as unacceptable restrictions forcing us to deviate from the course our career had taken or was about to take. The professional vocation is something which gradually clarifies itself throughout life. It is not uncommon that a person who began studying a particular career realizes later on that he is more talented at something else, and starts working at that instead; or ends up specializing in a different area from which he originally intended; or discovers, when he is already well advanced in his chosen career, a new opening that enables him to support his family better or contribute more effectively to the good of the community; or he finds himself forced, for health reasons, to look for a change of climate and occupation. Someone who studied medicine ends up running a business; a person who started on the shop floor becomes manager of a little workshop; a person from the countryside moves to the city and works in a factory; a legal expert gets involved in administering property inherited from his parents or running a bank. My daughters and sons, writes our Father, the same happens to

31 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 19

Along the Path to Love 266

you, just like the rest of your colleagues: your life is subject to the same rules as theirs. And it is that life, with all the changes circumstances impose, which you have to sanctify.32 Our Founder gave us a very graphic example to help us react supernaturally if we are ever in danger of over-valuing our career by separating it from its divine aspect. We have all heard stories about ships laden with precious cargo which were saved from sinking because the crew cast over board all the treasure, all the bullion they were carrying. If one day it happens – it doesn’t have to happen, and it won’t if you are perceptive and well disposed – that you begin to see what seems like a conflict in your career between freedom and vocation, it’s the moment to follow the sailors’ example and throw everything that gets in the way overboard, saying to our Lord: ecce ego quia vocasti me: here I am, for you called me (1 Sam 3:6); ut iumentum (Ps 73:22), like a little donkey before God. Then we realize that we are freer than ever, and are all the happier for it. Moreover, I assure you, the cargo is not lost; it is recovered, once the stormy seas of passion die down, when one has a modicum of formation and speaks out sincerely: ut iumentum! We do whatever has to be done, with the docility, with the humility, with the perseverance and with the wisdom of the donkey. I repeat, our professional vocation is not lost, because, once our intention is purified, it materialises again on a higher level, clearer than before. It is always an anchor, a hook, a means of personal sanctification and a means of apostolate: and we perceive in a clearer light the divine unity between work and apostolate which, were it not present, would make it impossible for us to give ourselves to God in our state and occupation in the world.33 At times the danger can present itself under the guise of activism or

32 Ibid., 33-34 33 Ibid., 8 267 Our professional vocation

professionalitis. These two illnesses require the same treatment: piety, interior life, growth in our relationship with our Lord. With the same insistence with which I previously invited you to work, and work well, without fear of tiredness, wrote our Founder, I now invite you to have interior life. I will never tire of repeating it: our Norms of piety, our prayer, come first. Without ascetical struggle our life would be worth nothing, we would be ineffective, sheep without a shepherd, blind leading the blind (cf Mt 9:36; 15:14). My daughters and sons, if at any stage work – even disguised as apostolic zeal – were to prevent you fulfilling with loving fidelity the Norms of our plan of life, it would no longer be Opus Dei: it would have become the devil’s work, opus diaboli (...) We have to find our nourishment in the Mass, which is the centre of the interior life, in our encounter with Christ in the Gospel and in the Blessed Eucharist, in loving dialogue with the Blessed Virgin Mary, in docility to the inspirations of the Holy Spirit, in a filial relationship with our Father God. In a word: by fulfilling our Norms of life, in accordance with the spirit of the Work.34

Renouncing one’s career We always have to consider our career in the context of the needs of the apostolate. As our Founder explained, just as the father of a family, when thinking about his work, doesn’t take into account only his personal preferences, but the welfare of his children, neither can you lose sight of the good of the apostolate. If you can choose from a number of different possibilities, it is not contrary to your professional vocation, and is a sign of good spirit, to choose the one which offers you the best opportunity to do a more spiritually fruitful task.35

34 Ibid., 21 35 Ibid., 34

Along the Path to Love 268

It can also happen at some stage that we are asked to discontinue the career we had so assiduously pursued up to then, either temporarily or for the rest of our lives, in order to dedicate ourselves completely to internal work. In this context our Founder often said that all Numeraries and Associates have to be ready to abandon the most flourishing professional career, if the Directors request it, in order to dedicate themselves to the humblest of tasks. Let us take an example. If a son of mine, a chemist, has to add only three drops to a testtube in order to discover the philosophers’ stone, and just then is asked to do something else, if that son of mine has my spirit, he will stop to reflect and if, in spite of everything, he is told to put the test-tube aside, he will put it aside at once and proceed with his new assignment, for the rest of his life if necessary. And he’ll never discover the philosophers’ stone, because he has discovered the philosophers’ stone of the love of God: he has discovered holiness. Accompany me in asking our Lord that we may always be faithful, and have a right intention and a truly apostolic zeal.36 If we ever have to change our career for apostolic reasons, we shouldn’t think that we have thereby lost our professional vocation or have done something extraordinary. My daughters and sons, don’t forget that if our professional vocation is really part of our divine vocation, it is so to the extent to which our work, be it intellectual or manual, is a means of apostolate and sanctification: of use to God, and of use to all souls for God. If at any stage our career were to become an obstacle, then it must be cast aside, because it would have ceased to be a means. If it absorbs us to such an extent that it hinders our interior life or the faithful fulfilment of our duties of state; if it has ceased to be a hook to catch souls, it is no longer part of our divine vocation and I’m not interested in it any more: it is not a professional

36 Ibid., 36 269 Our professional vocation

## ation but a diabolical vocation. Only to the extent to which our professional vocation is a means of sanctification and of sanctifying others, as I said, is it part and parcel of our divine vocation.37 Our entire life is directed to this end. The means, no matter how noble and even indispensable they may be, cease to be good as soon as they cease to serve the purpose for which they were intended. And so when the exercise of our career, for whatever reason, ceases to serve our holiness, it has lost its entire reason for being. But, we might ask, is this not tantamount to burying our talents, like the worthless servant in the parable? This is our Father’s reply: Consider, my son, how grateful our Lord is for the incense burnt in his honour. Consider how little earthly things are worth: no sooner do they begin but they end. Think how insignificant human beings are: pulvis es, et in pulverem reverteris: you are but dust, and to dust you shall return (Ash Wednesday, Antiphon). But the marvellous thing is that, in spite of it all, we don’t live for this earth or for our honour, but for the honour and glory of God, and for his service. That’s what motivates us!38 If we find that we are reluctant to let go our professional ambitions, it is a sign perhaps that they are too worldly; but once we offer them generously to our Lord, we can then dedicate ourselves lovingly, disinterestedly and passionately to that new task which we know is God’s Will for us, because we are captivated by the thought of serving him as he wishes to be served. And for all that, we don’t lose our professional vocation: it lives on integrally, perfectly grafted on to obedience, in that new occupation which has become our career, and to which we henceforth devote all our energy, until God disposes otherwise.

37 Ibid., 7 38 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 19

## THE CONSEQUENCES OF POVERTY {#cuaderno.8.22}

“Christian doctrine teaches us that all good things come from the Creator. ‘Not only have we received spiritual and heavenly riches,’ St Leo the Great affirms, ‘but earthly and material goods have also come to us from his generosity’ (St Leo the Great, Homilies, 10, 1). Our Founder always taught that the world is not evil, because it is God’s handiwork, because it is his creation, because Yahweh looked at it and saw that it was good (cf Gen 1:7ff)1 ... Material goods are positively willed by God and we must use them as a means of sanctification and apostolate,in order to serve God and men.”2 Nevertheless, “the disorder introduced into the world by original sin, exacerbated by our personal sins, means that on many occasions man loses a sense of the proper use of material goods, and so inverts and twists the order established by God. The result is often the absurdity of attempting to make these goods the object and purpose of human destiny, as if we were going to reach a permanent dwelling place here below (cf Heb 13:14). We must be on our guard against that temptation, my daughters and sons, since it can entangle many creatures in its nets and can also ensnare us, who by divine vocation must walk and work in the world, using earthly goods to direct everything towards God.”3

1 Conversations, 114 2 Don Alvaro, Family Letters III, 1 3 Ibid., 2 271 The consequences of poverty

Detachment from the goods of this world makes it easier to have a close filial relationship with our Father God, who in his loving providence looks after those who trust in him.4 Love for poverty is a distinguishing feature of the disciples of Christ, who, as St Paul affirms, though he was rich, yet for your sake he became poor, so that by his poverty you might become rich.5 Love for poverty is an expression of trust in God and enables us to keep our eyes fixed on the true goods: blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.6 As long as we live on the earth, bearing the scars of sin, we need to be constantly watchful if we are to keep our hearts detached from earthly goods. This vigilance is so important that its presence is a sure sign that we love God sincerely. When we truly love our Lord we naturally love as well that self-sacrificing vigil which keeps our love awake. To love poverty, therefore, means to love its consequences, the daily effort to live up to our motivating ideal: not to aspire to anything other than God. It was expressed clearly by our Father: you don’t love poverty if you don’t love what poverty brings with it.7

Learning to be poor When we joined Opus Dei we undertook to live completely detached from earthly goods; poverty, therefore, means much more than observing certain rules of behaviour. It requires interior detachment, in thought, desire and imagination. It implies being fully identified with Christ’s spirit, as he himself, through our Father’s agency, has made known to us. Remember that what really matters is

4 Cf Lk 12:22-28 5 2 Cor 8:9 6 Mt 5:3 7 The Way, 637

Along the Path to Love 272

not whether you have this or lack that, but whether you are living according to the truth taught us by our Christian faith, which tells us that created goods are only a means, nothing more.8 To illustrate this point, our Founder told us, among other things, a very clear story from his own life. Many years ago, we read in one of his homilies, I used to visit an eating place run by a charitable group for the benefit of beggars who were so poor that their only food each day was the meal they were given there. There was a large canteen, looked after by a number of kind women. After the first meal was served, more beggars would come in to finish off the leftovers. Among this second group of beggars one man in particular attracted my attention. He was the proud owner of ... a pewter spoon! He would take it carefully out of his pocket, look at it covetously and, after he had downed his meagre ration, he would look at the spoon again with eyes that seemed to exclaim: ‘It’s mine!’ Next he would lick it a couple of times to clean it and then, with deep satisfaction, would hide it away again in the folds of his tattered garment. True enough, the spoon was his! Here was a wretchedly poor beggar who, among his companions in misfortune, thought himself to be rich. Around that same time I knew a titled lady who belonged to the Spanish aristocracy ... who lived in an ancestral mansion. But she spent next to nothing on herself. On the other hand she paid her servants very well and gave the rest of her money to the needy, while depriving herself of almost everything. This lady had many of the goods which so many people are anxious to obtain but she personally was poor, given to mortification and completely detached from everything. Am I making myself clear?9 It is not possible to be truly detached unless one really loves the

8 Friends of God, 118 9 Ibid., 123 273 The consequences of poverty

practical consequences of poverty, because human nature tends towards comfort, and to be poor isn’t comfortable. It’s not enough to want to be poor. One has to learn to be poor:10 one has to cooperate with formation and always stay spiritually fit. Even St Paul felt bound to follow the same routine, in order to be detached from everything at all times: I know how to be abased, and I know how to abound; in any and all circumstances I have learned the secret of facing plenty and hunger, abundance and want. I can do all things in him who strengthens me.11 The practical consequences of our poverty are the best school in which to acquire the virtue, because they are an accurate measure of what God expects of us. It would not be right for someone who is supposed to be in love, and who has left everything for the sake of the heavenly Kingdom, to hanker after situations in which the effects of poverty were different or less noticeable. That is what we learn in the psalm, as it places on the righteous man’s lips the following words: but as for me, my feet had almost stumbled, my steps had well nigh slipped. For I was envious of the arrogant, when I saw the prosperity of the wicked.12 True Christian poverty is incompatible, not only with longing for the luxuries of life, but even with being over-anxious about the necessities. If this should happen to someone who, in response to a divine vocation, has left all things to follow Christ closely, it would be a sign that he or she was becoming lukewarm. When the spirit of poverty begins to crack up, it’s a sure sign that the whole interior life is going badly.13

10 Alone with God, 50 11 Phil 4:12-13 12 Ps 73:2-3 13 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962

Along the Path to Love 274

Responsibility in financial matters A fundamental consequence of poverty in the spirituality of Opus Dei is that every member should be financially self-supporting: through our work we pay our own way and contribute to sustaining our apostolates. Each one of my children, said our Founder, has this idea very clear: the first and most important thing is to fulfil the Norms, which are a guaranteed way to holiness; and, simultaneously and inseparably, to be self-supporting, to be financially solvent, and to help maintain the house or Centre he or she lives in or depends on.14 On this point our Father was quite explicit, in terms that echo St Paul’s own teaching and behaviour.15 It would be a mistake, and a serious one at that, because its consequences would be serious, if any of my children were to act like spoiled little brats and allow themselves to be a burden on the Work. We can’t be juvenile, we have to be responsible adults, responsible children of God. I can see your good spirit, my daughters and sons. You know very well that in our life the divine and the human elements are intimately united. We imitate Jesus Christ, perfect God and perfect Man. And so, just as you can detect a lack of love of God behind some momentary carelessness in a simple material detail, you can also identify the same defect as the root cause of an insolvent financial situation.16 We don’t live on alms, nor are we like employees who couldn’t care about the ups and downs of the company they work for. Opus Dei is our family, and the Centres of the Work are our homes. What a wonderful reference point this is! It would never occur to a conscientious father or mother to be a burden in their own home; they are constantly trying to find ways of contributing to its upkeep and

14 From our Father 15 Cf 2 Thess 3:7-15 16 From our Father, Letter 29 September 1957, 74 275 The consequences of poverty

solving its problems. This is how every member of the Prelature should behave: we should be really determined to contribute, through the work we do, to support the apostolates we are responsible for. At the same time we have to call upon the generosity of Cooperators and friends, who appreciate the work Opus Dei is doing and are grateful for the spiritual benefits they receive. By asking them to contribute financially to our apostolates, we provide them with a focus for their generosity and help them to improve spiritually.

Parents of a large and poor family Another important aspect of the financial responsibility poverty entails has to do with the way we use money for personal expenses, either in our job, or when travelling, or in social life. The desire to be faithful to God and apostolically effective makes us conscious of acting with restraint and not spending any more than we have to. To this end, Numeraries and Associates always get approval for extraordinary expenses, even though they may be clearly necessary and even for negligible amounts, as a precaution against any form of impulse spending or self-indulgence. As regards ordinary everyday expenses, the decision to spend or not is normally up to the good spirit and interior refinement of the person concerned. Every week we are asked in the Circle to examine this aspect of our spirit of poverty. There are certain things which are objectively extravagant and inappropriate for an apostle of Christ, even though they may be quite widespread in society at large: gadgets, creature comforts, little forms of self-gratification; such things have no place in the expense account or the lifestyle of a person who wishes to follow our Lord closely, even though they may cost nothing at all. Nor should we be surprised if, in doing without them in a natural way, our behaviour should contrast with the environment, thereby acting as a spur to help Along the Path to Love 276

our companions shake off their materialistic attitudes. This is a positive and directly apostolic consequence of poverty and an expression of genuine love for souls, and ensures we are not just being attracted to the means themselves. Impulse spending is also completely contrary to a genuine spirit of self-denial and wanting to follow the Master’s footsteps, which are characterized by poverty and love for the Cross. Neither would it be a sign of authentic self-giving to indulge personal desires and whims simply because they are being financed by the company, the State, our family, or a hospitable friend, and neither we nor the Work have to pay for them. We don’t practise poverty just for financial considerations, but always for ascetical reasons, for the love of Christ. Pure convenience should never be an excuse for expenditure either. Before spending anything we should consider well if it is really necessary, make a budget, and ask for advice. As a reliable guide for all eventualities we have the good rule of thumb our Father gave us: as regards poverty let me say this. If in any situation you are unsure what to do, because you don’t know which course to follow and have no one to ask, don’t forget the clear principle I gave you: we are parents of a large and poor family. And you will always do the right thing.17 A natural consequence of this spirit of ours is that Numeraries and Associates keep an account of their ordinary expenses, and are humbly disposed to be corrected about them. What would you say about a person who keeps something back and doesn’t account for it, even though it’s only ten lira? Very bad. That person is going badly, very badly indeed. He is perverting the Work and destroying our corporate holiness. He is doing harm to his brothers, but above all to himself ... Our poverty has always been genuine, real, total.18

17 From our Father, Meditation, 8 February 1959 18 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962 277 The consequences of poverty

Signs of true poverty Our Father has given us a few simple guidelines to measure the quality of our detachment. He often summed it up as follows: signs of true poverty: not to have anything at all as one’s own; not to have anything superfluous; not to complain when one is short of essentials.19 In the first place, not to have anything as one’s own. We don’t want to have anything that could be considered our personal property: it would be like forcing our soul into a straight-jacket. Since we have renounced everything for the love of God, are we going to be attached to a watch, a pair of cuff-links, a few holy pictures or some bits of paper?20 These things would be our undoing; and the smaller they are, the more they cause the heart to shrivel up. Without doing anything which would lessen our efficiency at work or in apostolate, or which could look peculiar, we should be careful not to regard as ours the typical items which in any poor and united family are shared by all: car, camera, typewriter, computer, books, tape recorder and so forth. Anything else would be a clear sign of a bourgeois attitude, which would be improper to Opus Dei. In particular, Numeraries and Associates have to be on guard against ever consenting to anything even remotely resembling a peculium, the sort of personal pocket-money permitted in many religious communities. Wouldn’t it be just great, said our Father, if, in order to join the Work, you left everything you had, and your family, and your future prospects (because you came to the Work young), and at the end of it all wanted to hang on to that? My sons, rest assured that you’ll never be short of what you need. Wouldn’t it be silly if I had a few pence saved up to get myself some cuff-links or a pair of glasses, or to buy a

19 Ibid. 20 From our Father, Meditation, 4 April 1955

Along the Path to Love 278

cup of coffee?21 One expression of this voluntary renunciation is that among ourselves we avoid even the appearance of giving one another presents, and anything we get from others we give to the Director. We also try to look after the things we use so that they last. I once knew a certain individual who liked to dress well. He used to spend a fortune on clothes, but when he got home he used to leave them lying all over the place, and his explanation was as follows: ‘I haven’t been made for clothes, but clothes for me.’ You, my son, ought to think if at times something similar happens to you. Things have to be worn, that’s true, but that doesn’t mean they have to be mistreated. They have to last, because they’re not ours: they’re a means we use to reach holiness and do apostolate.22 This attitude implies a small but constant spirit of sacrifice, like the father or mother of a large and poor family. Far from seeing it as an imposition, we have to love it dearly, because it is a sure path to holiness and a consequence of real poverty, just like our Lord’s own. It is also eminently possible to do, provided our heart doesn’t become attached to the things we use, both personal belongings as well as things we use at work or in the apostolate. To be detached implies, in the first place, a spirit of self-restraint as regards essential items; and being able to do without the superfluous ones gracefully, both naturally and supernaturally. This brings us to the second rule of thumb our Founder gave us: not to have anything superfluous. Poverty is incompatible with wanting to provide a comfortable margin of safety against scarcity, the more or less disguised attempt, which could even go to ridiculous lengths, to safeguard the future. It would be, deep down, an unjustifiable lack of

21 Ibid. 22 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962 279 The consequences of poverty

confidence in the loving providence of our Father God. Consider the lilies, how they grow; they neither toil nor spin; yet I tell you, even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these. But if God so clothes the grass which is alive in the field today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, how much more will he clothe you, O men of little faith!23 To deny ourselves what is superfluous implies also another clear attitude, which is seen above all in the thousand and one little incidents of family life with our brothers or sisters: when you have to choose, take the poorest, the least attractive, for yourself.24 But it means above all not creating needs for ourselves. We have to make demands on ourselves in our daily lives. In this way we will not go about inventing false problems and ingenious needs which, in the last analysis, are prompted by conceit, capriciousness and a comfort-loving and lazy approach to life. We ought to be striding towards God at a fast pace, carrying no dead weight or impedimenta which might hinder our progress.25 This point is particularly relevant nowadays when, in many countries, the consumer mentality makes people feel they really need things which, although they may conceivably be good and useful to have, are in fact superfluous. There are things which may appear indispensable, but aren’t. I remember years ago, when, in all my youthful eagerness as a young priest, I used to visit the very worst districts of Madrid, I heard about some ladies who used to spend a lot of time every day providing charitable services to the unfortunate inhabitants of those suburbs. In the very depths of winter they came across a child who could hardly be described as being badly dressed, because he was going around practically naked.

23 Lk 12:27-28 24 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962 25 Friends of God, 125

Along the Path to Love 280

One of those good ladies, moved with Christian compassion, said: But my son, aren’t you cold? To which the little fellow replied: Is your face cold? Well, I’m all face. With this story I would like to remind you, so you never forget it, not to get bogged down with completely artificial needs, which in the last analysis are simply concessions to comfort.26 Not to have anything superfluous means learning to be content with what enables you to live a simple and sober life27 and not creating false needs for ourselves, being grateful to God who ordinarily allows us to enjoy sufficient means for our work, apostolate and recreation, while at the same time being ready to do without them if he allows it to happen. And finally, the third sign: not to complain when we are short of essentials. And when it happens, what do we do? Cry? No! Laugh, then? Well, maybe not either, but be happy inside, very happy. Nowadays we’re less likely to suffer real hardship. Formerly, for years and years we had to survive without the most basic essentials. And if some day it were to happen again, my children, be very happy. Rest assured that, if you abandon yourselves in God’s hands, then your apostolic endeavours, your brothers’ holiness, and your own too, will all work out well.28 In the Work we have no shortage of experience of how God rewards that abandonment. During the last twenty-six years, our Father said in 1955, on many occasions I have found myself alone, in the most complete penury and without the slightest glimmer of hope on the horizon, none at all. We didn’t have even the barest essentials. But, how happy we were! Because we knew that, by seeking the kingdom of God and his justice, all the rest would be given us as well. As long as

26 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, 25 27 The Way, 631 28 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962 281 The consequences of poverty

you have done what you should to provide for your needs, my children, be very happy if at some stage you have to do without something.29

Always cheerful Our poverty, my children, shouldn’t be a form of ostentatious destitution; it always hides itself behind a smile.30 It has the same unpretentious and natural appearance as the other aspects of our vocation. Ours is a poverty that doesn’t go around crying ‘I’m poor.’ We are happy living the way we do. In the very fact of not having, of not living beyond our means, we are divinely contented.
