We try to live austerely and yet keep smiling, in sickness and in health, so that people don’t notice.31 And this shouldn’t be seen as one more sacrifice on top of all we already have to do in order to live modestly, but a deep and sincere attitude that fills the spirit, part of the hundredfold our Lord promises to those who leave all things to follow him. It is not enough for us just to be ready to do without things willingly when the occasion arises. We have to lose our fear of over-extending ourselves and continually try to get new apostolic ventures under way. I can assure you, our Founder wrote, that not a single apostolic initiative had to be abandoned for lack of material resources. Whenever it was necessary our Father God, through his ordinary providence, would ensure in one way or another that we got what we required so that we could see that he is always ‘a generous paymaster’.32 To put a brake on the apostolate to keep the situation under control would be quite opposed to poverty, and even more to charity. It also means that in practice we would never incur the sort of scarcity that in theory we

29 From our Father, Meditation, 4 April 1955 30 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 28 31 From our Father, Meditation, 7 March 1962 32 Friends of God, 117

Along the Path to Love 282

## l ready to face. Nevertheless, we shouldn’t be surprised to find poverty hard to bear, and occasionally even quite difficult. If at times you feel the pinch of poverty, don’t get dejected and don’t rebel against it. I do however insist that you should try to use all the upright means available to get over such a situation, because to do otherwise would be to tempt God’s providence. But while you are so fighting, remember too the omnia in bonum!: all things, even scarcity and poverty, work together unto the good of those who love God (cf Rom 8:28). Get into the habit, from now on, of facing up cheerfully to little shortcomings and discomforts, to cold and heat, to the lack of things you feel you can’t do without, to being unable to rest as and when you would like to, to hunger, loneliness, ingratitude, lack of appreciation, disgrace ... 33 Daily discomforts and shortages can and should bring us closer to God. Ordinarily they are just the typical petty inconveniences of life which, in one way or other, the whole world has to put up with, and which in our case we have to view supernaturally and positively without losing interior peace. And when real difficulties arise, that is the moment to go deeper and make the directives our own, purifying our dispositions and recalling those words of Christ, which cannot fail: blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.34 There is still a little way to go before reaching true abandonment. If you have not attained it yet, do not worry: keep up the effort. A day will come when you won’t see any way other than Him – Jesus –, his Blessed Mother, and the supernatural means that the Master has left us.35

33 Ibid., 119 34 Mt 5:3 35 The Way of the Cross, Fourth Station, 4

## THE DUTY TO BE FAITHFUL {#cuaderno.8.23}

God our Lord, who saved us and called us with a holy calling, not in virtue of our works but in virtue of his own purpose and the grace which he gave us in Christ Jesus ages ago,1 is determined to complete his work of sanctification in our souls. He desires all men to be saved,2 and by his sovereign and absolutely free will has decreed his saving purpose once and for all. God is not man, that he should lie, or a son of man, that he should repent. Has he said, and will he not do it? Or has he spoken, and will he not fulfil it?3 On the contrary, all flesh is grass, and all its beauty is like the flower of the field ... the grass withers, the flower fades, but the word of our God will stand for ever.4 His decisions are immutable; they cannot change. Furthermore, besides being forever faithful and true to all he has decided, he is able to do what he had promised.5

A pact of love Nevertheless, God wants to count on man’s free response. In exchange for all the wonderful things he has prepared for those who

1 2 Tim 1:9 2 1 Tim 2:4 3 Num 23:19 4 Is 40:6-8; cf Ps 33:9-10 5 Rom 4:21

Along the Path to Love 284

love him,6 he asks man for the little he can freely give: his voluntary cooperation. The work of sanctification is like a pact between God, who is anxious to save the creatures he has adopted as his children, and man, who freely cooperates in his work. God bestows his grace through the Holy Spirit, which he poured out upon us richly through Jesus Christ our Saviour, so that we might be justified by his grace and become heirs in hope of eternal life.7 Man’s response is to say: ecce ego, quia vocasti me: here I am, for you called me.8 The model for this pact between God and the human soul is the covenant God made with the people of Israel. To prepare the coming of the Redeemer of the world, God chose Abraham and his descendants and made his pact with them. If you will obey my voice and keep my covenant, you shall be my own possession among all peoples; for all the earth is mine, and you shall be to me a kingdom of priests and a holy nation.9 God remained immutably faithful to his covenant; it was his chosen ones, a stiff-necked people,10 who repeatedly failed to keep it. But God enforced its fulfilment in order to prepare the coming of the Messiah, who has obtained a ministry which is as much more excellent than the old as the covenant he mediates is better, since it is enacted on better promises ... “The days will come, says the Lord, when I will establish a new covenant with the house of Israel and with the house of Judah; not like the covenant that I made with their fathers on the day when I took them by the hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt; for they did not continue in my covenant, and so I paid no heed to them, says the Lord.

6 1 Cor 2:9 7 Tit 3:6-7 8 1 Sam 3:5 9 Exod 19:5-6 10 Exod 32:9 285 The duty to be faithful

This is the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel after those days, says the Lord: I will put my laws into their minds, and write them upon their hearts, and I will be their God, and they shall be my people. And they shall not teach every one his fellow or every one his brother, saying, ‘Know the Lord,’ for all shall know me, from the least of them to the greatest. For I will be merciful toward their iniquities, and I will remember their sins no more.”11 Christ’s preaching is full of figures of people who are faithful to this pact with God: the faithful and wise servant,12 the man who is conscientious and honest in little things,13 the faithful steward.14 In contrast, there are also other images depicting unfaithfulness, for instance the vine dressers who, instead of fulfilling their contract, mistreated and murdered the messenger who came to collect the rent.15 The Gospel is so imbued with the idea of faithfulness that ‘faithful’ becomes synonymous with ‘Christian’, the follower of Christ. Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, who has blessed us in Christ with every spiritual blessing in the heavenly places, even as he chose us in him before the foundation of the world, that we should be holy.16 God’s call starts to act in our souls through the sacrament of Baptism, whereby we confess our faith in Christ our Saviour, promise to undertake a new way of life, and renounce the works of the ‘old man’. In return God adopts us as his children through grace, taking charge of our sanctification and imprinting on our souls once and for all the seal of the promised Holy Spirit, who is the guarantee of our inheritance until we acquire possession of it, to the praise of his

11 Heb 8:6-12 12 Cf Mt 24:45 13 Cf Mt 25:21 14 Cf Lk 12:42 15 Cf Mt 21:33-46 16 Eph 1:3-4

Along the Path to Love 286

glory.17 Whatever life may have in store thereafter, this seal is indelible: it remains forever as a sign of the soul’s commitment to God. An expression of freedom Another essential characteristic of the relationship between God and the human soul is that it is motivated exclusively by love, in the first place God’s love for us:18 out of the great love with which he loved us, even when we were dead through our trespasses, [he] made us alive together with Christ.19 God’s love is particularly evident in our divine filiation. See what love the Father has given us, that we should be called children of God; and so we are.20 When, besides that, God gives a soul a specific vocation, his love becomes a mark of very special favour. It is as if the soul, invited in this way to cooperate especially with God establishes with him a fresh contract of love. I heard the voice of the Lord saying, ‘Whom shall I send, and who will go for us?’ Then I said, ‘Here am I! Send me.’ And he said, ‘Go’.21 The greater the mission entrusted, the greater the need to correspond, but in this case the benefits received are also much greater. For the members of Opus Dei, God has asked us for everything: everything we had, and everything that youth promised us. Yet it is all nothing compared to what he gives us in return: a life of happiness on earth and the eternal, indescribable glory of heaven.22 We might well ask how a finite, limited creature is capable of making a life-long commitment once and for all. There are many examples of it in the history of salvation. The Blessed Virgin did so with her fiat.23 On

17 Eph 1:13-14 18 Cf 1 Jn 4:10 19 Eph 2:4-5 20 1 Jn 3:1 21 Is 6:8-9 22 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p.685 23 Lk 1:38 287 The duty to be faithful

the road to Damascus St Paul asked our Lord: What shall I do, Lord?24 Our Father began to say those aspirations – ut videam, ut sit! – from the moment he felt the first stirrings of his own vocation. And finally, each of us, with our serviam, have responded affirmatively to God’s call to serve him in the Work. Though we have the limitations of creatures of flesh and blood, we have also been made in God’s image and likeness.25 We have a spiritual soul. We are not like irrational creatures who are inexorably bound to the present moment. The faculty of memory enables us to recall the past; and our capacity to plan the future helps us to determine the events that lie ahead. We can propose long-term goals. We can evaluate our capabilities on the basis of past experience. We can foresee possible difficulties and opportunities and adopt different means in consequence. And we can decide on a particular course of action and keep to that decision definitively. Giving ourselves completely to God is more than a free and responsible act. It is the most perfect expression of our capacity to decide our future. There is no goal greater than God, and no nobler motive than divine love, nor can our freedom be put to better use than by dedicating our life entirely to him. Commitment! How I like that word! We freely commit ourselves to live completely dedicated to our Lord. We want him to reign over us completely and utterly.26 Once the soul makes use of its capacity to decide, and exercises its right to plan its own future, God accepts and confirms that commitment with his grace. It then acquires the corresponding duty to maintain that decision and be faithful to it.

24 Acts 22:10 25 Cf Gen 1:26 26 Alone with God, 319

Along the Path to Love 288

Man’s life is not a mere succession of independent and unconnected acts. Soul and body are constantly developing. We carry forward a past and shape our future. The duty to be faithful to God embraces everything we do. The single, all-encompassing, initial act we freely made continuously unfolds with every passing day. Faithfulness is not a matter of making new pacts with God each day, but of maintaining and perfecting what we agreed upon once and for all.

A sign of maturity First of all, faithfulness is a natural virtue. As such it presupposes the capacity to judge and decide, and to accept the consequences of our acts. It is a sign of maturity, as opposed to being like children, tossed to and fro and carried about with every wind of doctrine.27 It is the best guarantee of having developed a sense of responsibility and self-mastery. To a certain extent, the limitations of human nature have a part to play in a person’s fidelity. It is impossible to foresee every contingency. In time new factors arise which require a re-statement of the initial decision. For that reason the Lord advises prudence and would have matters considered calmly. For which of you, desiring to build a tower, does not first sit down and count the cost, whether he has enough to complete it? ... Or what king, going to encounter another king in war, will not sit down first and take counsel whether he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand?28 When decisions have to be made that affect one’s entire life, God gives us all the essential information and the graces we need to be faithful. From all eternity the Creator has chosen us for this life of complete dedication: elegit nos in ipso ante mundi constitutionem (Eph 1:4): he chose us before the creation of the world. None of us has the right,

27 Eph 4:14 28 Lk 14:28-32 289 The duty to be faithful

no matter what, to doubt that divine calling. God always offers his light and freely provides an inner strength, because he wants our weakness alongside his omnipotence and the darkness of our lowly nature next to his light. We cannot doubt that we have been deliberately chosen to be co-redeemers. Along with all the other reasons we have often considered, we have an external sign: the fact that we have been labouring so willingly in his Work for no earthly gain. If God hadn’t called us, all that self-effacing work in Opus Dei would have made us fit for a mental institution. But the fact that we are sane proves that there is something physical, something external, guaranteeing that our calling is divine: veni, sequere me: come, follow me (Lk 18:22).29 Everything else is merely accidental and shouldn’t affect our dedication substantially, although it should be judged in the light of what is essential so as not to lead us astray, which is what faithfulness implies in practice. In God’s sight fidelity is a most meritorious quality, and leads us to hope for that life which God will give to those that never change their faith from him.30

Obstacles to our fidelity The only thing that can undermine fidelity is a weakening of love. The love which, with God’s grace, we have given him wholeheartedly in youth, is not something we can take back with the passage of time. Fidelity is the perfection of love: in the life of a soul dedicated to God one always finds, lurking behind any form of disenchantment, a source of corruption and impurity. Fidelity, when it is whole and entire, is always joyful and unconditional.31 It is not a matter of enthusiasm but of self-sacrificing love. You have to work not out of enthusiasm but out

29 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 47 30 Tob 2:18 (Vg) 31 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 45

Along the Path to Love 290

of Love: conscious of duty, which means self-denial.32 Not infrequently the chief trials of fidelity spring from the more significant changes in life, even as one gets older: failures, falls, emotional or professional crises, disappointments, and so forth. When this happens, the soul may get the impression that it has lost its bearings in the ascetical struggle, and at times there may be objective grounds for thinking this. The danger, though, is that this can give rise to pessimism and cause the soul to conclude incorrectly that all its previous existence has been based on false premises. In these circumstances the important thing is not to lose one’s common sense, and especially one’s supernatural sense. To throw everything away just because one has lost one particular item is absurd and pointless; it’s the act of a madman. We have in our possession a wonderful treasure, and if for any reason we lose part or even most of it along the way, that’s no reason to despair and throw away what’s left. The obvious thing to do is to learn from that experience to make sure we don’t lose any more of it. In the affairs of the soul nothing is ever hopelessly lost. The humble and contrite effort to retain what remains will ensure the recovery of even more than what was lost. Now the intensity of the penitent’s repentance may sometimes be proportionate to a greater grace than that from which he fell by sinning (...) wherefore the penitent sometimes rises to a greater grace than that which he possessed before (St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.89, a.2 c).33 Another difficulty we have to overcome in continually reaffirming our initial decision stems from our evil inclinations and human weakness in general: to use St Paul’s phrase, the law of the flesh that resists

32 The Way, 994 33 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 46 291 The duty to be faithful

the law of the spirit.34 As our Founder has written, on the other hand, it is logical to feel the attraction, not of sin, but of those noble earthly things which we have left behind for love of Jesus Christ, though without having lost the inclination towards them. Because we had that tendency, our self-giving was truly generous and detached. And because we preserve that self-giving, our fidelity is a continual offering: a love, an unselfishness, a magnanimity which lasts and which is not just the result of inertia. St Thomas says: eiusdem est autem aliquid constituere, et constitutum conservare (S. Th., II- II, q.79, a.1 c). What gave rise to your self-giving in the first place, my son, also has to maintain it.35

Being loyal to God In every instance we must be at least as loyal to God, to the Church, to the Work, and to our brothers or sisters, as any honest person is in everyday life. Our Father says: in Opus Dei faithfulness is absolutely essential. It confirms our mission of being co-redeemers with Christ. We also have to appreciate that it is a virtue in the natural order too. There are people who are not good Catholics, who lead immoral lives, who misbehave in practically every area, but who will not allow anybody to criticise their mother. They defend her with all their strength. There are others who are loyal to their country or to their company, without being great models of virtue in other respects. I would like us to raise this natural virtue of fidelity to the supernatural plane and make it the basis for perseverance in serving the Church, souls, the Work and our vocation. That is why I ask you, my children, never to forget the noble virtue of loyalty, which is the

34 Cf Rom 7:23 35 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 12

Along the Path to Love 292

basis for fidelity, a fidelity that is happiness.36 We must recognize that every good endowment and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights with whom there is no variation or shadow due to change.37 Like the workers in the vineyard,38 God has called us to work not just for one hour but until the day is done. Some are called sooner and others later, but everybody has to work until the end of the day. It would be unfair to accept the invitation, while thinking inwardly: ‘count on me as long as it’s pleasant, but we’ll see what happens when it gets really hot.’ That would be tantamount to being on our guard in our relationship with God, who for his part has unreservedly called us and destined us to an eternal inheritance.39 My children, it is God who awaits us! So we must commit ourselves joyfully, lovingly and eagerly, even though it may mean going against the grain at times, or even indefinitely.40 It may be difficult to live up to our commitment, but even then fidelity is a joyful obligation that we must not try and get out of, even though it means giving up everything, even though it entails sacrifice and effort. God needs our fidelity.41 He needs good workers who bear the burden of the day and the scorching heat.42 When we have to rectify, we do so. For a person who has undertaken a divine commitment, nothing, no matter how apparently arduous or difficult, could recommend a solution not in accord with his or her obligations, since he who began a good work in you will bring it

36 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 60 37 Jas 1:17 38 Cf Mt 20:1-16 39 Cf Heb 9:15 40 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p.690 41 Alone with God, 319 42 Mt 20:12 293 The duty to be faithful

to completion at the day of Christ Jesus.43 The important thing is to grow in humility, to acknowledge our mistakes without trying to find excuses for them, and ask pardon for our sins. God will always take us back. He tells us: I the Lord do not change.44 His decision to sanctify us remains unaltered. My children, let us gaze with humble confidence at the way God has marked out for us, so we understand it correctly and follow it faithfully. A totally dedicated fidelity is a continual assurance that we have really found Christ, that with him we are fulfilling his Father’s will, and that our filial response to the vocation we received is authentic.45 “Of what good are those plants that blossom at first and then shortly afterwards wither? No. The Lord demanded of his followers a constant resistance. He did not want people to be able to say that he had done everything, and that it was no wonder that the Apostles were what they were since they had nothing difficult to suffer. Hence he told them that perseverance was necessary. Because if it is certain, as he seemed to tell them, that I will free you from the first dangers, it is because I am reserving other, more serious dangers for you, and these will be followed by still others. And as long as you live, you will never cease to be persecuted. This is what he wanted them to understand when he said: ‘he who endures to the end will be saved’ (Mt 10:22).”46

A daily conquest As our Father often said, our self-giving has to be fully deliberate.47 It has to be firm, steadfast, complete, free and fully conscious, an

43 Phil 1:6 44 Mal 3:6 45 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 4 46 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 33, 5 47 The Way, 293

Along the Path to Love 294

inalterable act of will, an ever greater conviction that for us there is nothing greater or more beautiful than our vocation. As St Paul wrote, I count everything as loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord. For his sake I have suffered the loss of all things.48 Renewing our dedication doesn’t mean reviving something that has been allowed to deteriorate. Where faith, hope and love are present, renewal means that, in spite of all our mistakes, falls and weaknesses, we remain in God’s hands and reaffirm our unswerving loyalty. Let me repeat: it means renewing our fidelity to what God wants of us, namely, love with deeds.49 From the moment our loving pact with God was sealed, we no longer had the right to make further revisions to it as if we were starting from scratch. No one who puts his hand to the plough and looks back is fit for the kingdom of God.50 On the contrary, we are convinced that our commitment of love for God and of service to the Church is not to be put on or taken off like an item of clothing. It encompasses our whole life and, with the help of grace, we want it always to stay that way.51 This is what Holy Scripture tells us: we are not of those who shrink back and are destroyed, but of those who have faith and keep their souls.52 Our good intentions of being faithful are more effective if we never forget that we have this treasure in earthen vessels, to show that the transcendent power belongs to God and not to us.53 Of itself the soul cannot hope to merit the unwavering perseverance that opens the gates of heaven, for it is a grace that God bestows out of pure mercy. For by grace you have been saved through faith; and this is not your own

48 Phil 3:8 49 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p.691 50 Lk 9:62 51 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 10 52 Heb 10:39 53 2 Cor 4:7 295 The duty to be faithful

doing, it is the gift of God – not because of works, lest any man should boast.54 But God will not deny the grace of final perseverance to the soul that faithfully fulfils its obligations. Well done, good and faithful servant; you have been faithful over a little, I will set you over much; enter into the joy of your master.55 Constant faithfulness in daily life receives from God the reward of eternal happiness in heaven. Fidelity has to be affirmed anew each day by a fresh act of will that is constantly renewed. We should ask for this grace for ourselves and for all our brothers and sisters without ever giving up.56 Though this reward is unmerited, we eagerly aspire to it with confidence, for as St Paul says, not that I have already obtained this or am already perfect; but I press on to make it my own, because Christ Jesus has made me his own.57 Let us strive to be faithful as long as we live. If at some stage we feel we are letting our Lord down, let us react and ask him for help. Then we are assured of victory, because God does not lose battles. Let us place all our wretchedness at Jesus’ feet, so he can triumph. Then you will see how high he is raised, and how he helps us divinize our earthly existence. Our weakness is always present, even in the most sublime moments of our lives. The Gospel teaches us that nothing should surprise us. At the Last Supper, amidst the outpouring of divine love and confidence in that gathering of intimate friends, of those who had received the most careful formation and were our Lord’s favourites, facta est autem contentio inter eos, quis eorum videretur esse maior (Lk 22:24): they began to argue and fight among themselves over which of them was the greatest.

54 Eph 2:8-9 55 Mt 25:21 56 Cf Lk 18:1 57 Phil 3:12

Along the Path to Love 296

## sequently, when we experience any weakness in ourselves or in others, we shouldn’t be surprised. Let us remember all those who, in spite of their undeniable failings, persevered and carried the word of God to all nations and became saints, and let us resolve to keep on fighting. What counts is perseverance.58 As long as we don’t hold back, we can always count on the help of God and of his most pure Mother, and difficulties will never manage to make us waver. Resist, firm in your faith, knowing that the same experience of suffering is required of your brotherhood throughout the world. And after you have suffered a little while, the God of all grace, who has called you to his eternal glory in Christ, will himself restore, establish, and strengthen you.59 Be faithful to your vocation, all you children of God in this Work of God. Together with the supernatural virtues, practise the natural virtues too. Bring the charity of Christ to all the paths of the earth, the divine paths of the earth. And spread throughout the world the hidden yet fruitful influence of your work as apostles, quasi fluvium pacis, like a river of peace (Is 66:12).60

58 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 48 59 1 Pet 5:9-10 60 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 175

## ON THE ROAD TO HOLINESS {#cuaderno.8.24}

During our Lord’s few years of public life we often find him surrounded by crowds, because he desires all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth.1 Much of his teaching, therefore, and most of his parables and major miracles, take place in broad daylight before the astonished gaze of his onlookers. Jesus’ relationship with his followers, however, cannot be reduced to some sort of impersonal mass encounter: in the pages of the Gospel it is clear that each soul gets to know the Master in a uniquely personal way. Christ compares himself to the Good Shepherd who leaves the ninety nine sheep in the fold and goes in search of the one that is lost.2 People like Nicodemus, the Samaritan woman, Zacchaeus, the rich young man, the good thief, and the Apostles, all had quite diverse experiences with our Lord. Of one he asks that he set aside his knowledge and be prepared to be born again; of the Samaritan woman, that she be cleansed of her past wickedness and raise her heart to God; and of another that he practise a justice that is tempered by charity. Like the sheep in the parable, Jesus seeks them out one by one and inspires them to open their heart in sincere confidence.

1 1 Tim 2:4 2 Cf Mt 18:12-13

Along the Path to Love 298

Confiding in God Our Lord’s approach responds fully to the human condition. We are God’s creatures and completely depend on his assistance; but, because we are thinking beings and capable of choosing freely, Jesus wants us to decide ourselves to seek his help, humbly acknowledging that personally we are worth nothing at all. And so, although your Father knows what you need,3 he urges us to make our needs known to him. Ask, and it will be given you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. For every one who asks receives, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened.4 To ask is to acknowledge our dependence on God, and thereby to render him completely freely the glory that is his due. But there is more to it than that. From the moment Christ redeemed us we became children of God, and if children, then heirs, heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ.5 Every time we are sincere with God and seek his help in one way or another, we behave like true children and welcome him into our lives and our affairs. The more we trust and confide in God the more we rely on his fatherly care. What man of you, if his son asks him for bread, will give him a stone? Or if he asks for a fish, will he give him a serpent? If you then, who are evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father who is in heaven give good things to those who ask him!6 This filial confidence, accompanied by an opening of the heart, is the logical outcome of the soul’s encounter with Jesus. Take heart, my son, he says to the unfortunate paralytic, your sins are forgiven.7

3 Mt 6:8 4 Mt 7:7-8 5 Rom 8:16-17 6 Mt 7:9-11 7 Mt 9:2 299 On the road to holiness

Our Lord wants us to have in abundance all the means we need to be closely united to him, namely, the sacraments – especially the Blessed Eucharist – and mental prayer. In the Work these feature as norms in our plan of life. Life flows forth from Christ in torrents: a divine power. My daughter, my son, every day you talk to him, you touch him, you eat him, you encounter him in the Eucharist and in your prayer: in the Bread and in the Word.8 But in addition, in Opus Dei we are provided with a very specific means of bringing to life again those personal conversations that Jesus had with his followers, namely, the Confidence or fraternal chat that each member has periodically with the Director, or with someone appointed by the Directors, to become more identified with the spirit of the Work and to improve one’s apostolate.9

The need for the fraternal chat The Confidence has its roots in the Gospel: this is seen both in the supernatural way we do it and in the fruits of holiness we get from it. The fraternal chat is one of the chief reasons we have for continually giving thanks to our Lord. We see this more and more clearly as we become more aware of its divine character. Its purpose is supernatural: to make us become Opus Dei, that is, to be saints according to the spirit God has willed for us. And it is also supernatural in its effects, which are totally disproportionate to our capacity as instruments. We have often reflected on, or have heard explained, how to do the Confidence in order to get the best results from it. It calls for a whole series of qualities, virtues like self-surrender, humility, obedience, fortitude and sincerity, all of which make us respond to God more readily. We can never do without the guidance of the Director’s judgment, because one’s own mind is a bad adviser, a poor pilot to

8 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 61 9 Catechism of the Work, 8th ed, 214

Along the Path to Love 300

steer the soul through the storms and tempests and among the reefs of the interior life.10 And it is marvellous to have someone to whom we can disclose our joys and sorrows, our hopes and disappointments, in order to receive a comforting and encouraging word in return. All of this occurs most wonderfully in the fraternal chat, and helps us to go to it in a spirit of complete trust and sincerity. But the most important reason we have for doing it, one we have engraved on our hearts, is that it is so very supernatural: it has been willed by God, and is absolutely indispensable to the process of making us every day holier and more Opus Dei. The Confidence, our Father has told us, is not just a whim: it’s a necessity.11 It is an essential element of our vocation and a very special gift from God.

How to make it fruitful When God speaks he expects his listener to pay attention: But you, son of man, hear what I say to you; be not rebellious.12 When he grants a grace he expects a personal response. The only seed which bore fruit was the seed that fell on good ground: those who hearing the word, hold it fast in an honest and good heart, and bring forth fruit with patience.13 The Confidence, precisely because it is something supernatural and not just human, is always fruitful if we approach it with the dispositions we see in the Gospel. In the first place, we have to be convinced that without it we cannot advance at all and that, irrespective of circumstances, it will always be necessary for us. This doesn’t mean that at times we mightn’t find it difficult to do, through tiredness,

10 The Way, 59 11 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 130 12 Ezech 2:8 13 Lk 8:15 301 On the road to holiness

sickness or frame of mind,14 or simply because at a given moment we find it harder to open our heart. Especially then we have to entrust ourselves unreservedly to the Directors and follow dutifully the things they tell us, to ask for a lot of help from our Lord, Our Lady and our Guardian Angel, and be prepared to struggle generously. And then Jesus will not deny us his grace to conquer. It is also possible to find it difficult because of differences of character, or even something we see as a defect in the person we chat with. In these circumstances especially, we need to remember that we don’t go to the Confidence for reasons of friendship, or personal considerations, but rather for supernatural motives; whoever receives the Confidence, it is your Father himself who receives it.15 And so we should always be sincere and open, in spite of any more or less objective reasons that might suggest otherwise. The fraternal chat is always efficacious if we don’t obstruct its action in any way. This is because the help we get doesn’t depend on the wisdom of the person we chat with, although ordinarily they will have it. Neither does it depend on their holiness, but rather on the mandate received from God through the Directors: the person who receives our Confidence becomes at that moment Christ’s instrument to improve our interior life. When we see the Custom of our fraternal conversation in this supernatural perspective – and we have to ask our Lord that we always do so – then it is much easier to acquire the other interior dispositions to really make it the clear, brief, sincere, supernatural and apostolic exchange it has to be if the soul is to be guided properly; man’s poor heart really needs an outlet.16 When one is absolutely sure of achieving a goal it is easier and more agreeable to take the steps to attain it.

14 Cf Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 132 15 Ibid. 16 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 20

Along the Path to Love 302

If at any stage we find it difficult to be sincere, could it not be that we are operating at a merely natural level? We have come to the Work to be saints, our Father reminds us, and we shouldn’t be surprised to discover that we are a long way from being so. This leads us to acknowledge our weaknesses without trying to gloss over them selfrighteously, thereby helping us avoid pride, which is so tremendously blinding and makes everything seem the opposite to what it is. My children, be sincere with yourselves, be objective. That’s how to make our dedication effective. It’s difficult: one has to open one’s heart completely and humbly in spiritual direction and ventilate all the corners of the soul.17 In short, we are talking about displaying the soul openly and plainly to God’s gaze. And before him no creature is hidden, but all are open and laid bare to the eyes of him with whom we have to do.18

Sincerity and simplicity In the fraternal talk one has to be very sincere if God’s light is to penetrate to the very depths of the soul. But being sincere is more than just telling the truth: it means telling the whole truth, without hiding any of the circumstances which make it easier to be known and helped. Otherwise the fraternal chat would be ineffective, just like someone who, “going to the doctor to be cured, forgot the reason why he had come and instead displayed the healthy members and hid the diseased ones. It is God,” says St Augustine, “who should bandage the sores, not you; because if you, through shame, choose to hide them with bandages, the doctor cannot cure you. You have to let him treat and bandage the sores and cure them with poultices. Whereas sores are cured by the doctor’s bandages, the patient’s bandages hide them. And from whom

17 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 38 18 Heb 4:13 303 On the road to holiness

do you propose to hide them? From him who knows all things?”19 Our Father often told us that in the Work there are two terrible signs of bad spirit: to be afraid of those who are in charge, and to be ashamed to speak in the chat.20 The awareness – the supernatural sense

– that God sees and knows us completely, helps us to open our heart. And the person who, in God’s name, listens to us, is not surprised at anything we have to say, no matter how enormous or unthinkable our weaknesses may appear. On the contrary, he or she understands us well, because we both have the same sinful nature, the same inclinations, and the same proximity to evil, from which we are preserved by the grace Christ has won for us. The strange thing would be that our wretchedness wouldn’t be in some way reflected in the chat. A person without defects would be an angel, not a human being, and since that is impossible, one would have to conclude that that soul either doesn’t know itself or doesn’t want to be known, and doesn’t want to be helped to become a saint. “The depth of the well of human wretchedness is great,” says St Augustine, “and if anyone falls into it, he falls into an abyss. However, if from there he confesses his sins to God, the well will not close its mouth on him ... In the opposite case the well will close its mouth. Why? Because he closed his first ... My brothers, we have to fear this greatly. You see a man who has committed some evil, who has fallen into the well. If, when you tell him, he replies, ‘I confess, I have sinned’, then the well will not close its mouth upon him. But if he should say to you, ‘Why, what evil have I done?’, then, in trying to justify his fault, he would cause the mouth of the well to close upon him, and leave no means whereby he could be freed. Scorning confession, there would be no place for mercy. If you then excuse yourself from

19 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos, 31, 2, 12 20 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 130

Along the Path to Love 304

your fault, how can God free you from it?”21 If we don’t acknowledge our defects our Lord cannot help us overcome them. The way to face up to our wretchedness is by being very sincere in the Confidence. Only by being truly sincere with God, with yourself, and with the people who look after your formation, can you achieve, insofar as it is possible with God’s help, your perfection as a Christian, your perfection as a human being, and perseverance in good works.22 Sincerity begets authenticity and simplicity; it is opposed to duplicity, whereby a man shows one thing outwardly while having another thing in his heart. So, from this point of view, simplicity is part of the virtue of sincerity.23 When one attempts to conceal some aspect of personality, there is a danger of pretending to be different from what one really is. This gives rise to a lack of authenticity and creates a gap between what one is and the image one tries to project. And this dichotomy, if not put right, so complicates the personality that it eventually becomes impossible to distinguish between the true and the false self. It would be difficult for this to happen if we do the fraternal chat well, because we go to the Confidence precisely to simplify our personality and make ourselves clear and transparent. Our asceticism has the simplicity of the Gospel. We shouldn’t complicate ourselves and leave our heart in darkness; we can’t obstruct the action of the Holy Spirit by creating in our lives a rupture which would rob us of our simplicity of heart and our sincerity before God (cf 2 Cor 1:12).24

21 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos, 68, 1, 19 22 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 41 23 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.109, a.2 ad 4 24 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 38 305 On the road to holiness

Clear motives Simplicity and sincerity go hand in hand with a right intention. The motives for our actions are not always clear even to ourselves. When we allow ourselves to be moved exclusively by our inclinations, we tend to look for self-gratification, consolation and acclaim. A supernatural outlook helps guard against this danger in the fraternal chat. Placing ourselves in the hands of the person who listens to our chat, as we would in the hands of Christ himself, guarantees our honesty. What we look for in the chat is neither praise nor compassion, but rather an effective remedy which we are ready to put into practice, without any concessions to those baser motives that arise from the prudence of the flesh which is death, while the prudence of the spirit is life and peace.25 Such is our wretchedness that we may perhaps find ourselves reacting like Naaman, the Syrian general who at first did not want to follow the very simple command given him by the prophet Elijah to cure his leprosy, which was to go and bathe in the river Jordan, because he considered that the Jordan was a far less significant river than those of his native land. However, like that good soldier, we must never lack the humility to rectify: in the end he went down and dipped himself in the Jordan, according to the word of the man of God; and his flesh was restored like the skin of a little child, and he was clean.26 If there is sincerity, supernatural outlook and good humour, then nothing is important: nothing is ever lost, our Father wrote. Barabbas was a murderer and a rebel, and Christ’s death – life for Life – saves him from death. Dimas was a thief, a delinquent, but just one humble word of repentance, a simple and confident prayer, and Jesus – life for Life – saves him from eternal death. Rectify, my son, it’s never too

25 Rom 8:6 (Vg) 26 2 Kg 5:14

Along the Path to Love 306

## e to rectify, but rectify at once!27 We always have to be careful that the human factors in the fraternal chat, which are good and necessary in themselves, never predominate and end up asphyxiating its supernatural character. Every effort we make in this direction is well worth while and is extraordinarily efficacious. You have the experience, just as I have, our Father told us, that after a Confidence well done, we find ourselves cum gaudio et pace, ready to set to work zealously and unruffled. In that Confidence our Lord gives us light to know – to learn – what we have to do to act in a truly Christian manner in every circumstance,28 in all the manifold situations in which we find ourselves throughout our life. In the fraternal chat we receive a fresh stimulus, optimism and advice; but above all we receive the security of being Opus Dei, of behaving like children of God who seek only their Father’s glory. And so, strong in our vocation, we experience the self-sacrificing and supernatural joy of seeing all our pettiness – all the wretchedness and weakness of our poor human nature, with all its frailties and defects – ready to be faithful to God’s grace and thereby being the instrument of great things.29

27 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 55 28 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, 20 29 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 62

## WITH THE IMPULSE OF LOVE {#cuaderno.8.25}

On the voyages of the interior life and of man’s spiritual toil, God sometimes grants his apostles spells of fair weather when all the elements, one’s own deficiencies and the external difficulties, are all as it were stilled: the soul rejoices, both inwardly and outwardly, in the beauty and power of the divine; it is filled with contentment and peace, and its vacillating faith is strengthened. God tends to lead souls, above all at the beginning, and at times perhaps for years, through less stormy seas, in order to consolidate them in their initial decision, without asking them at the beginning for what they cannot give, because they are still sicut modo geniti infantes (1 Pet 2:2), like newborn babes.1 When the soul begins to take its first steps in its vocation, it is not uncommon that its self-surrender is accompanied by a natural enthusiasm, by a detectable pleasure for the things of God and by an optimism that scarcely acknowledges the existence of difficulties. Those who are just beginning to follow our Lord don’t generally encounter many difficulties: God helps to consolidate their initial decision by enkindling them with fire: Fire goes before him, and burns up his adversaries round about.2

1 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 5 2 Ps 97:3

Along the Path to Love 308

Like a glowing ember I have told you that during our earthly voyage we sometimes encounter spells of fair weather, internal or external, at times prolonged; but only in heaven is peace definitive and tranquillity complete.3 With the passage of the years, even before we reach old age or even maturity, our initial impulses begin to moderate. Our God is always a consuming fire,4 but he doesn’t always cause the soul to be ablaze with great feelings of fervour or detectable satisfaction. Look, my children, said our Father on a certain occasion: I have been in Opus Dei for more than forty years. Have I enjoyed it? Have I had what people call personal satisfaction, sighing away like a devout old woman? There was a time when I used to think that all those old ladies who sigh in the darkness of a church were rather grotesque; now, however, they arouse in me a lot of devotion. However, in my years in Opus Dei I have had very little experience of the sighs of those devout and saintly old women. These forty years have been for the most part devoid of that sort of thing. I keep going because I know it is God’s way. And I don’t feel unhappy. It would be a pity if we were to do things simply because we liked them. We have to do them because it is our duty, and Love leads us on.5 With or without any personal satisfaction, with enthusiasm or with the mere determination to be loyal to God, every day we strive to gain ground and enkindle afresh in our hearts the fire of God’s love. As Christ himself said: I came to cast fire on the earth; and would that it were already kindled!6 But there are many ways of being on fire:

3 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 9 4 Heb 12:29 5 From our Father, Noticias, October 1968, p.48 6 Lk 12:49 309 With the impulse of love

blazing away superficially like a bundle of straw or a firework; or glowing solidly like an ember being slowly consumed in the hearth, silently radiating warmth to its surroundings. What we cannot do is let ourselves flicker out, to let our vocation grow cold and cease being that 7 that God lit in us to give light to all in the house.8 light of the world Our role is to be like lamps shining in a dark place;9 and to be so, we each know from experience how we have to rekindle the flame, to renew our self-surrender every moment, every day, and on occasions many times a day, having perhaps lost that naïve simplicity of the first moments. We have drawn close to Christ, and we have felt his Heart beating strongly, oh so strongly! And we have come to taste his delights, as we find him delighting in the sons of men (Prov 8:31). From all this we know the great value of God’s love. Yes, we have to renew our self-surrender; we have to pronounce once more those words, ‘Lord, I love you,’ and to say them with all our soul. Although our feelings don’t respond, we say it to him with the warmth of grace and with our will.10

With the passage of the years In order to light a great fire, it is first necessary to set fire to a small amount of dry leaves or paper. Then, once it is burning away, one has to add some more solid material, and although this may take a while to get going, once the fire takes hold it produces a mass of glowing coals that heat up the surroundings permanently. The coals don’t tend to generate great flames; just occasionally, if some particularly

7 Mt 5:14 8 Mt 5:15 9 Cf 2 Pet 1:19 10 From our Father, Meditation God’s way of reasoning, 6 January 1970, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 110

Along the Path to Love 310

inflammable material is thrown on or if a strong gust of wind blows, then the fire blazes out and a shower of sparks flies up. At times the coals scarcely give any light at all: they are a pile of embers, a source of heat hidden beneath the apparent coldness of the ashes. Something similar happens in the interior life. There are people who, if they don’t see flames, think that there isn’t any fire. And they are often mistaken. In order to give out heat and set things on fire, big flames aren’t needed: a red-hot coal is enough. Ordinarily, what one finds in young people is this more transient flame, the bonfire that will later leave a mass of glowing embers behind it. It is typical of the initial phase of self-surrender, the fervour of the first hour. Older people, by contrast, perhaps don’t blaze in the same way, but they do have embers which set on fire and burn everything around them (...) Young people can appear to be saints; and older people very often do not. However, normally, the former haven’t yet achieved the holiness they seek; while the latter, who have spent their lives in God’s service, and who humbly think that they are not saints at all and will never be so, but who do what they can to achieve it, these are really the ones who lead holy lives.11 With the experience and insight that comes from God, one gradually discovers aspects of the interior life that perhaps one didn’t appreciate quite as much at first. The ascetical struggle changes its pitch. It is like what happens when one sets out to climb a mountain, and at first thinks that it can be done in a few hours, only to discover on the way that it is going to take days, or even weeks, to get step by step to the summit. The height of the mountain doesn’t change, nor can it be said to require a greater effort than could be expected; it is just that the path is somewhat harder and longer than one envisaged at first. One can try just as hard,

11 From our Father, Brief Circle, 15 November 1964 311 With the impulse of love

with the same spirit of sacrifice, but the effort has to be properly harnessed, because in the last analysis the important thing is to get to the top. In the interior life, even allowing for the effect of grace, it is normal for one’s initial ardour to gradually disappear. But, as one’s effort becomes less externally visible, it becomes more supernatural and reflective; one experiences a greater need to trust in God, and begins to appreciate more that aspiration of St Paul’s: I will all the more gladly boast of my weaknesses, that the power of Christ may rest upon me.12 Perhaps one’s virtues don’t shine forth quite as much, nor does one’s apostolic zeal give rise to a bustling display of activity. Although, objectively speaking, there is indeed efficacy, one always has the impression that it is very little, that it is not at all in keeping with what God our Lord deserves. The conclusion I come to every day at the examination of conscience is always: pauper servus et humilis: a poor and humble servant! That is, when I don’t have to say instead: Lord, Josemaría is not very happy with Josemaría.13 This situation, then, is the mound of embers covered with ashes, the fire that has apparently gone out. But when the moment of adversity comes to test it, the breath of divine grace, which is never lacking, blows away the ashes and exposes a coal radiating with love, shining with faith, and glowing with a red-hot loyalty that can withstand all the storms and downpours. How many saints does one find on this earth? None! We are all capable of committing the greatest atrocities imaginable. You’ve heard me say again and again that holiness consists in struggling, in realizing we have defects and trying to avoid them. We’ll be like that till we die: en route to holiness. Otherwise, who would be able to put up with us? We wouldn’t be saints at all, we’d be totally unbearable. Holiness consists in

12 2 Cor 12:9 13 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 90

Along the Path to Love 312

having defects and fighting against them, but we’ll still die with them.14 Each step we take brings us closer to Christ and makes us live his life more fully; but the last step, the definitive one, the one which will crown God’s patient work in us, will be achieved only when this mortal body of ours is laid aside and the soul reaches its heavenly home.

The danger of becoming bourgeois All the same, we have to guard against trying to justify our failings by arguments such as: ‘Well, I’m no saint.’ We have to acknowledge our faults, ask for forgiveness and keep on fighting: Against thee, thee only have I sinned ... Behold, I was brought forth in iniquity, and in sin did my mother conceive me.15 We cannot shy away or justify ourselves. Even though we are not saints yet, and can never be totally so in this life, nevertheless we have to aspire to holiness with all our strength. The goal to which Jesus calls us is a high one: indeed, it will be inaccessible to us as long as we live. One can always tend to more, and the person who doesn’t advance, goes backwards; the person who doesn’t grow, diminishes.16 If someone were to lose this desire for constant improvement, which is not the same thing as being visibly enthusiastic, they would run the risk of becoming interiorly bourgeois and of losing, not only the flame, but also the embers. The divine ideal that first unsettled us and dragged us out of our mediocrity is very worth while indeed! It is worth while being faithful, it is worth while falling so deeply in love that there isn’t any room left in our life for the bourgeois mentality.17 Mediocrity in self-giving has a number of symptoms, among which

14 From our Father, Get-together, 13 July 1968 15 Ps 51:4-5 16 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 10 17 From our Father, Crónica, 1971, p.394 313 With the impulse of love

one could single out that of habitually being on the lookout for little compensations, a sign that one is trying, perhaps in a disguised way, to combine surrender to God with a worldly form of existence. This can give rise to carelessness in those aspects of mortification whose only purpose is to purify us and to offer God a concrete proof of our selfgiving, of wanting to be co-redeemers with him. One could fulfil the plan of life more or less up to a point, but perhaps with lots of little omissions that are hardly noticed. There mightn’t be any sign of special difficulties or struggles, but neither would there be that decisive firmness characteristic of someone who loves God above all things. And if difficulties do come along, they are often attributed to external causes – environment, climate, place of residence, working conditions – and one foolishly supposes that the mere passage of time, or a change of circumstances, is enough to solve everything. Deep down there could be a form of disillusionment when faced with the facts of the ascetical struggle, a disenchantment which is not openly acknowledged and which leads the soul to neglect remedies which are now considered as being useful only for beginners, whereas in fact we often have to recur to them time and time again: little reminders to help us keep presence of God; being daring in apostolate; the effort to fulfil very faithfully something we have been asked to do; making use of customary mortifications which prepare the soul to respond to the motions of grace. For it is here that one notices more clearly perhaps that not only has the bonfire has not only lost its initial flames, but that the embers have lost their heat. This interior coolness points to the loss of a personal relationship with God; and one detects a reluctance to react, even after more serious failures and sins, by raising one’s heart to God in prayer: I have taken upon myself to speak to the Lord, I who am but dust and ashes.18 My children, we cannot ever be content with what we are doing,

18 Gen 18:27

Along the Path to Love 314

just as an artist is never satisfied with the sculpture or painting he is working on. Everybody tells him: ‘It’s marvellous,’ but he thinks to himself: ‘No, it’s not what I aiming for; I wanted something better.’ We cannot be content either. Our Lord is never satisfied, he never comes to a standstill. We have to go at his pace. God is a lover who wants to possess his beloved entirely.19 And so, if at any stage someone were to be satisfied with the way they were, or at least resigned to a panorama with no great prospect of improvement, that soul would be quite right to think that it was still very far from God. It should therefore conclude that it needs to do a good examination of conscience, to ask for light and grace, to open itself more widely in spiritual direction and to renew the desire of beginning again to live more immersed in God. But living with God is indeed a risky business, for he will not share things; he wants everything. And if we move toward him, it means we must be ready for a new conversion, to take new bearings, to listen more attentively to his inspirations – those holy desires that he provokes in every soul – and to put them into practice.20

A deep examination One certainly shouldn’t confuse mediocrity with tiredness. We are human beings, and divine grace does not completely eliminate the fomes peccati,21 nor does it prevent us being exposed to temptations, or the body and soul getting exhausted working for God. Only someone who has no experience of the interior life could confuse physical or psychological exhaustion with a lack of holiness. The person who is simply tired is humble enough to abandon himself in the hands of the relevant person: the Directors or the doctor; at the very least, he really

19 From our Father, Get-together, 13 March 1969 20 Christ is passing by, 58 21 Cf Rom 7:23 315 With the impulse of love

does want to be docile. The same happens if one is deeply exhausted and scarcely has the energy to do what one is told. Those desires are not in vain, and God will not hesitate to come to the aid of that soul, as fire consumes the forest, as the flame sets the mountains ablaze.22 A person who has begun to consent to the temptation to be bourgeois, on the other hand, can at times get very actively involved in professional, social or cultural affairs. This tendency is a well-known method of escaping from the interior vacuum: if one doesn’t reach the goal in a particular area, one is compelled to apply one’s energies in a more attainable sphere. Deep down, what is really important is to ask oneself what exactly one is trying to achieve with all that activity. Rectitude of intention is not measured solely by subjective criteria, but above all by the docility with which one accepts timely advice. True docility begins well before one receives the advice: it is seen, for example, in the timing of the consultation; in the way in which it is put; and in the way in which one follows out conscientiously what one has been told. What God asks of us is to try to renew every day, to rejuvenate ourselves interiorly; and if, in our desire to serve him, the flames of superficial enthusiasm have been left behind, our heart doesn’t cease to glow like a red-hot coal for all that. Because the fire of God’s love needs to be fed. Every day it must grow further and gather strength in the soul: a fire keeps going by continually burning fresh material.23 So we must be ready to begin again, to rediscover the light and the stimulus of our first conversion in every new situation. And we prepare the way with a deep examination of conscience, asking our Lord for his help to know him, and ourselves, better. If we want to be converted again, there’s no other way.24

22 Ps 83:14 23 Christ is passing by, 58 24 Ibid.

Along the Path to Love 316

We also need to carry out a work of purification, of mortification, to cleanse our soul of the crust of ashes and to measure the real temperature of our love by means of a sincere examination of conscience. We may need to start using again those little reminders – the ones we used at the beginning – to rekindle the fire, and to dedicate all our efforts, night and day, to uniting our soul and our spirit to our Father God through prayer, through a ceaseless loving contemplation. When your senses, your imagination, the potencies of the soul, are all immersed in God, then you won’t have any personal problems, and, being ‘divinized’, you will be able to say: vivo autem iam non ego, vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20), it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. You will then acquire an unquenchable hunger and thirst for God, and will experience in your life the truth of those words: those who eat me will hunger for more, and those who drink me will thirst for more (Sir 24:21).25

The ‘Trinity’ on earth Every new dawn is another heaven-sent opportunity to correct life’s trajectory and draw closer to our final destination. Every event can bring with it an invitation to say ‘No’ to a disordered inclination, to straighten a twisted will, to carry out our apostolic task more lovingly. We all need to be born anew, especially if our soul’s interior fire has been allowed to die down. Every moment of life can be the big chance that God offers us to rectify the direction of our journey. My children, let us draw close to that group that we call the Trinity on earth: Jesus, Mary and Joseph. I imagine myself there, hiding in a corner; I don’t dare to draw near to Jesus, because all my wretchedness, past and present, rises up against me. I feel deeply ashamed, but I realize also that Jesus is casting an affectionate glance

25 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 28 317 With the impulse of love

in my direction. I then go to his Mother and to St Joseph, that man who was so neglected for so many centuries and who acted as his father here on earth. And I say to Jesus: Lord, I want to be really yours, so that my thoughts, my deeds, my entire life, are for you. But as you know, this poor wretch that I am has wandered around here and there so often ... I wish I had been yours from the very start, from the first beat of my heart, from the first moment my mind started to reason. I am not worthy to be – and without your help I will never be – your brother, your son and your love. You indeed are my brother and my love, and I am also your son. And if I can’t take Christ up and hold him against my breast, I will make myself small. That we can all do, and it is very much in keeping with the family nature of our spirit. I will make myself very small and will go to Mary. If she has her Son Jesus in her right arm, then I, who am her son too, will also find a place there. The Mother of God will embrace me with her other arm and will squeeze us both together against her breast. Forgive me, my children, if I say these things which may appear so silly. But isn’t it true that we are contemplatives? Considerations of this sort can help us, if necessary, to recover life, and can fill us with consolation and lots of fortitude.26

Renewing our love This is a good moment to see what our dispositions are like: if we really want to give ourselves completely, if we are prepared to clean out those ashes that hinder our progress towards holiness, if we are ready to be saints, even though perhaps the resolution is unaccompanied by feelings. Before God, and above all, before the Christ Child,

26 From our Father, Meditation God’s way of reasoning, 6 January 1970, in In Dialogue with the Lord, pp. 112-113

Along the Path to Love 318

helpless and needy, everything is purity; and I will see that, though I do have the savage possibility, like all men, of offending him and acting like an animal, it’s not a disgrace if it helps us to struggle, to express our love, and teaches us how to behave fraternally towards all mankind and all creation. We need to be always making acts of contrition, of reform, of improvement: continually advancing. Yes, Lord, who listens to us now: after the human race fell through the sin of our first parents, you permitted this creature called man to behave so brutally. And so, if at any stage I cannot be close beside you in your Mother’s arms, I will put myself next to the ox and the ass that were there with you in the stable. I will be the family dog. There I will be, looking at you tenderly, ready to defend that house. And at your side I will find the purifying warmth and the love of God to transform this animal we all bear within us into a son of God, something that cannot be compared with any earthly grandeur. This is our life, my children, the life of a good and honest little donkey, who perhaps at times rolls on the floor and brays away with his legs in the air. But he is ordinarily faithful and carries whatever load is placed upon him; and he puts up with a simple diet, not very plentiful and always the same; and he has a tough skin for working. I am moved by the image of the loyal little donkey who doesn’t buck his load. Lord, I’m just a little donkey, here you have me. Don’t think, my children, that this is nonsense; it isn’t. I’m showing you the way I pray myself, and it works well.27 This trusting dialogue with God restores the heart to its youthful vigour and turns it into a burning coal. My children, we are close to Christ. We are bearers of Christ, we are his little donkeys, like that donkey in Jerusalem, and as long as we don’t drive him out, the Father,

27 Ibid.

319 With the impulse of love

the Son and the Holy Spirit, the Blessed Trinity, are all with us. We are bearers of Christ and we have to be light and warmth, we have to be salt, we have to be spiritual fire, we have to be constant apostolate, we have to be alive and animated, we have to be the impetuous wind of Pentecost.28 We will truly be Christ-bearers, with all its consequences, if we try with all our might to fulfil the Norms with more and more love every day. We cannot dash our Founder’s expectations for us: I know your good spirit and I can see how you fight to make flesh of your flesh, a living witness, the spiritual formation the Work gives you. I know that, through the help of our Norms, you try to find that intimacy with God which comes from the union of his grace and your own effort. And I also know that you have the experience that the Norms, when they are done with love – and they can be done anywhere – will rescue you from all dangers, will launch you into the attack and will spur you on, so that you never become spiritually soft.29 I am very happy with all my children, with all of you: I have to say it. But every day there has to be a new conversion in our lives. We cannot think that we are already definitively directed towards God; we continually need new conversions to bring us closer to holiness.30 Renewing daily our interior struggle, rejuvenating our self-surrender with frequent acts of atonement, is the way to keep the soul forever vibrant and to facilitate the growth of that fire God enkindled with his calling, the fire which never says, ‘Enough’.31 And so, with the passage of the years, the embers will always be alive, and we will be glowing coals that give heat to all who pass by us.
